### [1] [Walshness: an intrinsic neural-network representability metric for quantum states](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00505)
*Nisarga Paul,Kehuang Chen,Jessica K. Jiang,Haimeng Zhao,Di Luo*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces "Walshness," a complexity metric quantifying when a quantum many-body state admits a compact neural quantum state (NQS) representation, proves an if-and-only-if relationship between bounded Walshness and efficient NQS representability, and provides an energy-minimization algorithm to find optimal bases that markedly improve learnability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding when neural networks can efficiently represent physical quantum states remains limited, due to the nonlinear parameterization of NQS, the intricate sign structure of quantum states, and the sensitive dependence on basis choice. An intrinsic complexity metric with provable ties to neural-network representability is needed.

Method: The authors define Walshness as a complexity metric of a quantum state, quantifying whether it admits a compact neural-network representation. They rigorously prove that a general quantum many-body state admits an efficient NQS representation if and only if it has bounded Walshness, for various NQS architectures. They also devise an algorithm to find the optimal basis by minimizing the energy of a corresponding classical spin model.

Result: Minimizing Walshness via the classical spin model improves learnability of various physical quantum states, including ground states of the transverse-field Ising model and the mixed-field toric code, often reducing infidelity by orders of magnitude. It also recovers and generalizes the basis choice given by the well-known Marshall sign rule in frustrated antiferromagnets.

Conclusion: Walshness provides an intrinsic complexity metric for quantum states with provable connections to their neural-network representability, enabling principled basis selection that substantially enhances NQS learnability.

Abstract: Neural quantum states (NQS) have emerged as powerful representations of quantum states with rapidly expanding applications across quantum many-body physics. Yet our understanding of when neural networks can efficiently represent physical quantum states remains limited, in part due to the nonlinear parameterization of NQS, the intricate sign structure of quantum states, and the sensitive dependence on basis choice. We introduce Walshness, a complexity metric of a quantum state which quantifies whether it admits a compact neural-network representation. We rigorously prove that a general quantum many-body state admits an efficient NQS representation if and only if it has bounded Walshness for various NQS architectures. We provide an algorithm for finding the optimal basis by minimizing the energy of a corresponding classical spin model. This allows us to empirically improve the learnability of various physical quantum states, including ground states of the transverse-field Ising model and mixed-field toric code, often reducing infidelity by orders of magnitude, and recovers and generalizes the basis choice given by the well-known Marshall sign rule in frustrated antiferromagnets. Our results provide an intrinsic complexity metric for quantum states with provable connections to their neural-network representability.

</details>


### [2] [Beyond Light Cones: State Preparation Complexity in Quantum Spin Glasses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02166)
*Omar Al-Ghattas,David Gamarnik,Bobak T Kiani*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces "effective profile complexity" (from metric entropy of Pauli profiles) to bound state-preparation complexity in dense quantum p-spin Hamiltonians beyond lightcone arguments, proving Ω(n²/log n) gate lower bounds for near-ground-state energy, depth-width tradeoffs, MPS bond-dimension bounds, and obstructions across Parham's magic hierarchy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing circuit-lightcone bounds cannot handle circuits whose structure (e.g., unrestricted Clifford tails or arbitrary ancillas) spreads local observables across the system, so a complexity measure capturing the class's effective profile structure is needed to lower-bound ground-state preparation cost.

Method: Defines effective profile complexity via metric entropy of Pauli profiles (expectations on exactly p-qubit Pauli operators). Combines a nonsymmetric quantum de Finetti theorem (Berta et al., arXiv:1810.12197) with Gaussian process entropy bounds to separate classes from ground-state energy by Ω(√n) at bounded quadratic complexity, and to give leading-order benchmarks via product states at subquadratic complexity.

Result: Near-ground-state energy requires Ω(n²/log n) one- and two-qubit gates even with discardable ancillas; depth-width tradeoffs, entanglement-depth and MPS bond-dimension lower bounds; obstructions at every fixed level of Parham's magic hierarchy at total width O(n); Clifford+T circuits with o(n) T-gates have no leading-order energy advantage over product stabilizer states.

Conclusion: A profile-complexity framework yields broad, structure-agnostic lower bounds on dense p-spin ground-state preparation, covering gate counts, depth-width, entanglement, and magic-hierarchy obstructions, including cases where Clifford tails and clean ancillas defeat naive lightcone arguments.

Abstract: We introduce a method for studying state preparation complexity in dense quantum $p$-spin Hamiltonians on $n$ qubits, going beyond bounds based only on circuit lightcones. The key input is the class's effective profile complexity, which is derived from the metric entropy of its Pauli profiles. These profiles record expectations of all Pauli operators supported on exactly $p$ qubits. Classes with uniformly bounded quadratic effective profile complexity remain separated from the ground-state energy by a positive multiple of $\sqrt n$ for sufficiently large fixed $p$. At subquadratic effective profile complexity, the class cannot outperform a suitable benchmark class at leading order, with product states providing a universal benchmark. The proof combines an adaptation of a nonsymmetric quantum de Finetti theorem of Berta et al. (arXiv:1810.12197) with Gaussian process entropy bounds.
  Applying this framework, we show that attaining near-ground-state energy requires $Ω(n^2/\log n)$ one- and two-qubit gates, even with arbitrary discardable ancillas. We also obtain depth-width tradeoffs, entanglement-depth and matrix product state bond-dimension lower bounds, and obstructions for both orientations at every fixed level of Parham's magic hierarchy (arXiv:2504.19966), with total circuit width $O(n)$. In first-level reverse magic, a shallow circuit is followed by an unrestricted Clifford circuit. The latter can spread local observables across the system, preventing a direct application of small-lightcone bounds. For this first-level class, our bounds also allow arbitrarily many clean ancillas at fixed shallow-circuit depth. A sharper benchmark shows that Clifford+$T$ circuits with $o(n)$ $T$-gates have no leading-order energy advantage over product stabilizer states, even with unrestricted Clifford operations and arbitrary discardable ancillas.

</details>


### [3] [Beyond Lie Algebras: Lie-Wedge Stratification and Pure-State Stabilizability in Single-Channel Qubit Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00046)
*Yibin Wang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Lie wedges retain the reversible/irreversible distinction that Lie algebras erase in open systems; for drift-free single-qubit systems with bidirectional Hamiltonian control and at most one traceless dissipator, the authors classify 13 structural strata and analyze pure-state stabilizability per stratum.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Lie algebras capture how control generators combine but lose the distinction between reversible control and irreversible dissipation in open systems. Lie wedges preserve this information, motivating their use to characterize control capabilities.

Method: Study drift-free single-qubit systems with bidirectional Hamiltonian controls and at most one independently actuated dissipator generated by a traceless jump operator. Classify structures via the generated algebra and its Lie-wedge geometry, using the control algebra and four channel properties to define strata. Determine whether each wedge contains a generator with a unique globally attractive pure state.

Result: The systems fall into thirteen structural strata determined by the generated algebra and Lie-wedge geometry. In each wedge, reversible directions are exactly the coherent controls. Five strata have stabilizability depending on additional channel data. One exceptional stratum lacks any single control-rotated dissipative direction that suffices, yet averaging two such directions in the wedge yields stabilization.

Conclusion: Lie wedges reveal control capabilities invisible to the generated Lie algebra, enabling finer stratification and stabilizability analysis of open quantum control systems.

Abstract: Lie algebras describe how control generators combine, but in open systems they erase the distinction between reversible control and irreversible dissipation. Lie wedges retain this information. We study drift-free single-qubit systems with bidirectional Hamiltonian controls and at most one independently actuated dissipator generated by a traceless jump operator. In this work, we prove that they fall into thirteen structural strata defined by the generated algebra and its Lie-wedge geometry. The control algebra and four channel properties determine the stratum; in each wedge, the reversible directions are precisely the coherent controls. We then use the stratification to determine whether a wedge contains a generator with a unique globally attractive pure state. In five strata, stabilizability depends on additional channel data. In one exceptional stratum, no single control-rotated dissipative direction suffices, but averaging two such directions in the wedge yields stabilization. Lie wedges therefore reveal control capabilities that the generated Lie algebra misses.

</details>


### [4] [Sharp Target-Domain Certificates for Quantum-Kernel Advantage under Distribution Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00080)
*Roberto Fernández-Barrios,Iker Pastor-López,Asier González-Santocildes,Pablo García Bringas*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A sharp, assumption-free interval is derived for the finite-batch predictive advantage of a fixed candidate over the best classical-kernel baseline under unaudited label completions, with retrospective and prospective audits showing the advantage is eliminable with few labels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Claims of quantum predictive advantage under distribution shift typically rely on known target labels, which is unrealistic and confounds the comparison with classical baselines. The paper seeks an assumption-free, honest assessment that does not presuppose label availability.

Method: Derive the sharp identified interval (worst- and best-case bounds) for the finite-batch advantage of a fixed candidate over the best member of a prespecified fixed classical-kernel family, valid under any bounded loss and over unrestricted completions of unaudited labels; special-case zero-one accuracy to exact closed-form partial-label updates. Apply across eight security shifts against 115 classical kernels, then perform retrospective auditing and prospective corroboration on eligible tasks with predefined criteria and protocol controls.

Result: Zero-label upper endpoints of advantage span 0.002-0.088 across the eight shifts. Retrospective auditing shrinks every endpoint to 0.010 using 0-33 of 500 labels, including for entangling-ZZ models. Prospectively, on two eligible tasks, four task-classifier medians require 0-3 labels (overall median 0; maximum 76) and all 20 realized effects are negative; a third task fails its feature gate. Protocol controls reverse an apparent advantage, and finite-shot noise can raise predictive distinctness without yielding useful advantage.

Conclusion: The framework cleanly separates protocol validity, target predictive indispensability, and quantum relevance, showing that reported shift-based predictive advantages are fragile and typically vanish under small amounts of label auditing.

Abstract: Quantum predictive advantage under shift usually requires known target labels. We derive the assumption-free sharp identified interval for the finite-batch advantage of a fixed candidate over the best member of a prespecified fixed classical-kernel family under any bounded loss and unrestricted completions of the unaudited labels; zero-one accuracy has exact closed-form partial-label updates. Across eight security shifts, zero-label upper endpoints against 115 classical kernels span 0.002-0.088. Retrospective auditing reduces every endpoint to 0.010 with 0-33 of 500 labels, including entangling-ZZ models. Prospective corroboration on two eligible tasks met predefined criteria: four task-classifier medians require 0-3 labels (overall median 0; maximum 76), and all 20 realized effects are negative; a third task fails its feature gate. Protocol controls reverse an apparent advantage, while finite-shot noise can increase predictive distinctness without useful advantage. The framework separates protocol validity, target predictive indispensability, and quantum relevance.

</details>


### [5] [Exact Posterior Prediction from Product Haar Measurements and a Randomized-Mesh Maximum-Likelihood Bridge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00117)
*Naqueeb Ahmad Warsi,Ayanava Dasgupta,Masahito Hayashi*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper analyzes Bayesian prediction of an unmeasured copy of a finite-dimensional pure quantum state after independent one-copy Haar POVM measurements, computing the relative-entropy-optimal posterior mean exactly via permanents of minors, proving finite-sample guarantees, and establishing an asymptotic refinement from fixed-mesh and collective benchmarks to the sharper scale (d/ε)log(d/ε).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Predicting a further unmeasured copy of an unknown pure quantum state from independent single-copy Haar POVM data is a fundamental information-theoretic task, but the exact structure of the Bayes-optimal predictive state under relative-entropy loss and its achievable sample complexity were not fully resolved. A principled bridge was also needed between the fine product experiment and a regular ray-valued model without invoking deterministic finite-sample MLE uniqueness or unspecified mesh schedules.

Method: For the fixed separable observation, the full-rank posterior mean is evaluated exactly through permanents of minors of the outcome Gram matrix; positivity and normalization of the permanent representation are proved, and minimaxity among decision rules based on the fixed outcomes is established. Hilbert–Schmidt projection of the posterior mean and an exactly analyzed linear-inversion competitor yield a finite-sample posterior-purity bound and an explicit relative-entropy guarantee. The fine product data are connected to a regular ray-valued experiment via a single random orientation of a finite projective mesh, retained across the sample, with a selected finite-alphabet MLE; a nested recovering mesh recovers the fine Fisher information.

Result: An explicit relative-entropy guarantee with sample count O((d²/ε)log(d/ε)) is obtained. Entropy and posterior-purity comparisons follow from exact collective benchmarks. Every sufficiently fine fixed mesh yields a regular finite-alphabet model whose selected MLE has Haar-averaged inverse-Fisher coefficient \bar{a}_k, with refining the mesh after the fixed-mesh large-sample limit driving \bar{a}_k toward d−1, giving epsilon-optimal leading relative-entropy and posterior-overlap bounds and the sharper fixed-dimensional asymptotic scale (d/ε)log(d/ε).

Conclusion: The Bayes predictive state for the one-copy Haar POVM observation admits an exact permanent-of-minors representation that is positive, normalized, and minimax among fixed-outcome rules. Finite-sample guarantees at scale O((d²/ε)log(d/ε)) are proved, while a random-orientation mesh construction with selected MLE yields, in the fixed-dimensional asymptotic limit, the sharper (d/ε)log(d/ε) scale. The latter is an asymptotic result, not a uniform finite-sample guarantee; no deterministic finite-sample MLE uniqueness or triangular mesh schedule is required.

Abstract: We study prediction of one unmeasured copy of an unknown finite-dimensional pure quantum state after independently measuring the observed copies with the one-copy Haar POVM. For the resulting fixed separable observation, the Bayes predictive state under quantum relative-entropy loss is the full-rank posterior mean. We evaluate it exactly through permanents of minors of the outcome Gram matrix, prove positivity and normalization of the permanent representation, and establish minimaxity among decision rules based on these fixed outcomes. Using Hilbert--Schmidt projection of the posterior mean and an exactly analyzed linear-inversion competitor, we prove a finite-sample posterior-purity bound and an explicit relative-entropy guarantee with sample count $O((d^2/ε)\log(d/ε))$. Using the exact collective benchmarks proved in a companion paper, we also obtain entropy and posterior-purity comparisons. To connect the fine product data to a regular ray-valued experiment without assuming deterministic finite-sample uniqueness, we draw one random orientation for a finite projective mesh, retain it for the entire sample, and apply a selected finite-alphabet MLE in the resulting reference experiment. A nested recovering mesh uniformly recovers the fine Fisher information. Consequently every sufficiently fine fixed mesh gives a regular finite-alphabet model. Its selected MLE has Haar-averaged inverse-Fisher coefficient $\overline a_k$, and refining the mesh after the fixed-mesh large-sample limit makes $\overline a_k$ approach $d-1$. This yields epsilon-optimal leading relative-entropy and posterior-overlap bounds for the fine posterior and the sharper fixed-dimensional asymptotic scale $(d/ε)\log(d/ε)$. The latter is not asserted as a uniform finite-sample guarantee. No deterministic finite-sample MLE uniqueness or triangular mesh schedule is used.

</details>


### [6] [Global and nonlocal magic of quantum many-body scars](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00129)
*Lukas Ebner,Giovanni Cataldi,Kaidi Xu,Jad C. Halimeh,Emanuele Tirrito*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work uses global and nonlocal measures of nonstabilizerness (magic) to characterize quantum many-body scars in (1+1)D Abelian Z2 and U(1) lattice gauge theories, deriving an exact closed-form expression for nonlocal trace-distance magic from the Schmidt spectrum.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nonergodic features of chaotic quantum many-body systems are typically probed via local observables, fidelity, and entanglement entropy. The authors aim to extend this characterization by employing global and nonlocal measures of nonstabilizerness (magic), which capture quantum computational resources beyond entanglement.

Method: Analytical and numerical study of quantum many-body scars near infinite temperature in (1+1)-dimensional Abelian Z2 and U(1) lattice gauge theories. Derivation of an exact closed-form expression for the nonlocal trace-distance magic from the Schmidt spectrum, reducing the optimization over local unitaries to a finite maximization over stabilizer-compatible Schmidt ranks.

Result: Scar eigenstates can exhibit extensive global magic despite anomalously low entanglement, while retaining anomalously large nonlocal magic compared with ergodic states. Constrained Hilbert spaces can generate irreducible nonlocal magic through stabilizer-incompatible Schmidt ranks, even for a flat entanglement spectrum.

Conclusion: Global and nonlocal magic provide a complementary diagnostic of nonergodicity in gauge theories, revealing that scars are distinguished not only by low entanglement but by anomalous, potentially irreducible, nonlocal magic arising from Hilbert-space constraints.

Abstract: Nonergodic features of chaotic quantum many-body systems are commonly characterized through local observables, fidelity, and entanglement entropy. Here, using global and nonlocal measures of nonstabilizerness (magic), we study quantum many-body scars near infinite temperature in $(1+1)$-dimensional Abelian $\mathbb{Z}_2$ and U$(1)$ lattice gauge theories, both analytically and numerically. We derive an exact closed-form expression for the nonlocal trace distance magic solely from the Schmidt spectrum, reducing the optimization over local unitaries to a finite maximization over stabilizer-compatible Schmidt ranks. We find that scar eigenstates can exhibit extensive global magic despite their anomalously low entanglement, while retaining anomalously large nonlocal magic compared with ergodic states. We further show that constrained Hilbert spaces can generate irreducible nonlocal magic through stabilizer-incompatible Schmidt ranks, even for a flat entanglement spectrum.

</details>


### [7] [Stellar rank under the contraction of SU(1,1) to the Heisenberg-Weyl group](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00133)
*Chon-Fai Kam*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper studies how the zero set of the Husimi function of a single-mode bosonic pure state behaves under the contraction of $SU(1,1)$ to the Heisenberg-Weyl group, showing the stellar rank equals the number of zeros and that the whole effect is governed by one weight sequence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The stellar rank (number of Husimi zeros) characterizes Gaussian states by its vanishing, but this characterization fails on the Poincaré disk for the discrete series of $SU(1,1)$: the zero set leaves a zero-free factor undetermined. Hence the paper asks how the zero structure behaves in the contraction limit instead of classifying it at fixed Bargmann index.

Method: Under the $SU(1,1) \to$ Heisenberg-Weyl contraction, zeros redistribute on a single scale. The analysis reduces to the weight sequence $w_k(m)=(2k)^m/(2k)_m$, whose monotonicity in $k$ controls all estimates. Tools include normal families for rescaled states, Segal-Bargmann space membership, semicontinuity of the rank, a two-sided Harnack estimate, and a semiclassical Husimi potential.

Result: Rescaled states form a normal family; every nonzero limit point lies in the Segal-Bargmann space, and those with finitely many zeros are exactly the finite-rank Fock states. The rank is upper semicontinuous, with equality precisely when all zeros scale as $O(1/\sqrt{2k})$, the coherent-state scale. A family of rank $r$ degenerates iff the amplitudes on the lowest $r+1$ weight vectors tend to zero. Mass escapes at the disk edge via a first-order transition of the semiclassical Husimi potential at an explicit critical coupling. The contraction limit retains exactly the lowest $2r+3$ amplitudes, and this number cannot be lowered.

Conclusion: The contraction limit provides the correct framework for understanding Husimi zero structure, replacing fixed-Bargmann-index classification. The stellar rank is recovered as an upper semicontinuous quantity, Gaussian states are characterized by rank vanishing, degeneracy is controlled by finitely many low-lying amplitudes, and the minimal number of amplitudes needed to capture rank $r$ in the limit is exactly $2r+3$.

Abstract: Under the contraction of $SU(1,1)$ to the Heisenberg-Weyl group, the zeros of the Husimi function of a state redistribute according to a single scale, and we determine which of them survive. The stellar rank of a single-mode bosonic pure state, the number of these zeros, characterises the Gaussian states on the plane through the vanishing of the rank. On the Poincaré disk, where the same construction applies to the discrete series of $SU(1,1)$, it does not, because the zero set leaves a zero-free factor undetermined. We therefore ask how the zero structure behaves in the contraction limit rather than how it should be classified at fixed Bargmann index. The entire difference between the two Hilbert spaces reduces to one weight sequence $w_k(m)=(2k)^m/(2k)_m$, whose monotonicity in $k$ drives every estimate below. The rescaled states form a normal family, every nonzero limit point lies in the Segal-Bargmann space, and those with finitely many zeros are exactly the finite-rank Fock states. The rank is upper semicontinuous, with equality precisely when all zeros scale as $O(1/\sqrt{2k})$, the scale of the coherent-state labels. A two-sided Harnack estimate pins the profile on compact sets. Separately, a family of rank $r$ degenerates if and only if the amplitudes on the lowest $r+1$ weight vectors tend to zero. Mass escapes at the edge of the rescaled disk instead, by a first-order transition of the semiclassical Husimi potential at an explicit critical coupling. Finally, the contraction limit retains exactly the lowest $2r+3$ amplitudes, and this number cannot be lowered.

</details>


### [8] [Symmetry Discovery in Quantum Learning: Observable-Level and Task-Level Inference from Finite Measurements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00157)
*Zeyu Chen*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper develops a finite-measurement theory for inferring which symmetry group a quantum learning model actually supports, identifying invisible transformations with the stabilizer of a projected state, and providing statistical (shadow-statistic) and geometric (Fisher-orthogonality, projection bias, l1 readout) tools for symmetry recovery and task validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Symmetry reduces the capacity of a quantum learning model, but an imposed group is only valid if it matches both the measured information and the label transformation. Determining whether a candidate symmetry is genuinely supported by finite measurements—rather than assumed—is therefore essential, motivating a theory that infers the correct group from candidate transformations under finite data.

Method: The authors build a finite-measurement inference framework: a structural result equating observable-invisible transformations with the stabilizer of a projected state (given invariant probe span); recovery of generators forming a valid subgroup, with continuous invisible space identified as its Lie algebra. For finite dictionaries, an unbiased shadow statistic distinguishes zero from positive squared-expectation discrepancies at an inverse-gap rate. Task validation uses a characteristic kernel on the joint distribution or a classical--quantum discrepancy on its encoded mean, linked by an exact group-average identity to joint-state asymmetry and binary task breaking mass. Capacity trade-offs are quantified via projection bias and an l1 readout bound, with Fisher-orthogonality of retained and breaking sectors at an invariant pure-state backbone. Ising-chain calculations illustrate finite-shot recovery and sector drift.

Result: Observable-invisible transformations are shown to equal the stabilizer of a projected state; recovered generators form a valid subgroup whose Lie algebra is the continuous invisible space. The shadow statistic achieves an inverse-gap measurement rate, improving on the inverse-square-gap rate of uniform discrepancy estimation, and a commuting qubit lower bound proves this gap dependence optimal at fixed snapshot scale, with simultaneous intervals supporting data-dependent tolerances. An exact group-average identity relates the classical--quantum discrepancy to joint-state asymmetry and specifies conversion to binary task breaking mass. Projection bias and the l1 readout bound quantify the cost of excessive symmetry and the capacity gained by relaxing it. At an invariant pure-state backbone, retained and nontrivial breaking sectors are Fisher-orthogonal. Ising-chain calculations connect finite-shot recovery, label-dependent symmetry, and physical sector drift.

Conclusion: The framework determines which symmetry the finite measurements actually support and supplies the statistical and geometric basis for a subsequent release decision, i.e., deciding whether to keep or relax the imposed group.

Abstract: Symmetry reduces the capacity of a quantum learning model, but the imposed group must match both the measured information and the label transformation. We establish a finite-measurement theory for inferring this group from candidate transformations. The central structural result identifies observable-invisible transformations with the stabilizer of a projected state whenever the probe span is invariant. It turns recovered generators into a valid subgroup and identifies the continuous invisible space with its Lie algebra. For finite dictionaries, an unbiased shadow statistic distinguishes zero from positive squared expectation discrepancies with an inverse-gap measurement rate, improving the inverse-square-gap rate of uniform discrepancy estimation. A commuting qubit lower bound proves the gap dependence optimal at fixed snapshot scale, and simultaneous intervals support data-dependent tolerances. Task validation then tests either the joint distribution through a characteristic kernel or its encoded mean through a classical--quantum discrepancy. An exact group-average identity relates the latter to joint-state asymmetry and specifies its conversion to binary task breaking mass. Projection bias quantifies the cost of excessive symmetry, while an $\ell_1$ readout bound quantifies the capacity gained by relaxing it. At an invariant pure-state backbone, retained and nontrivial breaking sectors are Fisher-orthogonal. Ising-chain calculations connect finite-shot recovery, label-dependent symmetry, and physical sector drift. These results determine which symmetry the measurements support and provide the statistical and geometric basis for a subsequent release decision.

</details>


### [9] [Projector Form of the Quantum Brachistochrone and Its Relation to Two-Boundary Quantum Algorithm Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00169)
*Masayuki Ohzeki*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper establishes a connection between the time-optimal quantum evolution framework of Carlini et al. and the coherent two-boundary optimal-control formulation for quantum algorithm design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Two distinct approaches—time-optimal quantum evolution (Carlini et al.) and coherent two-boundary optimal control—have been developed for quantum algorithm design, but their relationship remained unclear, motivating a unified understanding.

Method: A theoretical/comparative analysis relating the two formalisms: the time-optimal quantum evolution of Carlini et al. and the coherent two-boundary optimal-control formulation.

Result: The relation between the two formulations is clarified, showing how they connect.

Conclusion: The time-optimal evolution of Carlini et al. and the coherent two-boundary optimal-control framework are shown to be related, providing a unified perspective for quantum algorithm design.

Abstract: We clarify the relation between the time-optimal quantum evolution of Carlini et al. and the coherent two-boundary optimal-control formulation recently proposed for quantum algorithm design.

</details>


### [10] [Entanglement cost of quantum depolarization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00187)
*Kun Fang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work determines the exact entanglement cost of every two-qubit Bell-diagonal state (including all qubit isotropic states) by converting tuned qubit semigroup log-Sobolev bounds into supporting lines of Wootters' function, tensorizing via a recent exact tensorization theorem to obtain matching lower bounds, and extends near-tight bounds and channel results to qudit isotropic states and depolarizing channels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Entanglement cost is defined via a regularized (asymptotic) quantity, making exact evaluation notoriously difficult. Even the seemingly simple family of isotropic states (depolarized maximally entangled states) has resisted exact determination. The paper aims to close this gap by computing exact values for qubit isotropic and general two-qubit Bell-diagonal states, and to provide strong bounds for the qudit case and for channel preparation.

Method: The proof uses a family of suitably tuned qubit semigroups to transform a general log-Sobolev entropy bound into supporting lines of Wootters' function. A recent exact tensorization theorem of Dong et al. extends these single-copy bounds to arbitrarily many copies, yielding a general lower bound on entanglement cost. Matching this bound with the entanglement of formation gives the exact cost for a broader class of two-qubit states. For qudit isotropic states, a general lower bound is derived from the full depolarizing 2-to-3 norm. The qubit results and qudit bounds are then extended to the entanglement cost of preparing quantum depolarizing channels under both parallel and sequential strategies.

Result: The entanglement cost is exactly determined for every qubit isotropic state and, more generally, every two-qubit Bell-diagonal state; the lower bound matches the entanglement of formation, also determining the exact cost for a broader class of two-qubit states. For qudit isotropic states, the depolarizing 2-to-3 norm bound substantially improves on the PPT-relative entropy of entanglement and nearly matches the entanglement of formation, leaving a gap of at most 2% of log d that vanishes as d grows. These results transfer to the entanglement cost of preparing quantum depolarizing channels under parallel and sequential strategies.

Conclusion: The regularized entanglement cost is shown to be exactly computable for all two-qubit Bell-diagonal/isotropic states, resolving a long-standing evaluation problem in that regime, while for qudit isotropic states the new norm-based bound is near-tight (gap at most 2% of log d, vanishing with growing d) and improves upon PPT-relative entropy of entanglement. The framework also yields corresponding channel-preparation costs for depolarizing channels.

Abstract: Entanglement cost is the asymptotic rate of Bell pairs required to prepare a quantum state. Its regularized definition has made exact evaluation difficult, even for isotropic states (i.e., depolarized maximally entangled states). In this work, we determine the entanglement cost of every qubit isotropic state and, more generally, every two-qubit Bell-diagonal state. Our proof uses a family of suitably tuned qubit semigroups to transform a general log-Sobolev entropy bound into supporting lines of Wootters' function. A recent exact tensorization theorem of Dong et al. [arXiv:2606.17729] extends these bounds to arbitrarily many copies, yielding a general lower bound on entanglement cost. Matching this bound with the entanglement of formation also determines the exact cost for a broader class of two-qubit states. For qudit isotropic states, we derive another general lower bound from the full depolarizing $2\to 3$ norm. This bound substantially improves on the PPT-relative entropy of entanglement and nearly matches the entanglement of formation, leaving a gap of at most $2\%$ of $\log d$ that vanishes as $d$ grows. Finally, the exact qubit results and qudit bounds extend to the entanglement cost of preparing quantum depolarizing channels under both parallel and sequential strategies.

</details>


### [11] [Simultaneous Perturbation as a Spectral Filter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00201)
*Masayuki Ohzeki*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper analyzes the random perturbation used in SPSA for parameterized quantum circuits, deriving the exact mode-by-mode response of the mean Rademacher estimator on structured Fourier landscapes, characterizing its non-conservative mean field, sampling variance, and resulting stochastic dynamics via a Fokker-Planck equation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Parameterized quantum circuits require repeated measurements to estimate objective gradients, making the number of circuit evaluations a central resource. SPSA is widely used because it obtains a full gradient estimate from only two cost evaluations, independently of the number of parameters, but the behavior of its random perturbation on the structured landscapes produced by quantum circuits is not well understood.

Method: The authors analyze the random perturbation for structured Fourier landscapes generated by quantum circuits. For a finite update width, they derive the exact mode-by-mode response of the mean Rademacher estimator. They then analyze general generators by examining the filtered mean field, deriving its sampling variance, comparing it with Gaussian random-direction smoothing, and formulating the resulting stochastic dynamics through a Fokker-Planck equation.

Result: The mean Rademacher estimator selectively suppresses modes involving many parameters. For general generators, the filtered mean field need not be conservative. The non-conservative field generates acceleration to the steady state and the global minimum of the cost function as the parameters in SPSA are gradually decreased.

Conclusion: SPSA's random perturbation induces a non-conservative filtered mean field on structured Fourier landscapes, which drives acceleration toward the steady state and global minimum when parameters are annealed, providing a theoretical account of why SPSA is effective for parameterized quantum circuits.

Abstract: Parameterized quantum circuits require repeated measurements to estimate objective gradients, making the number of circuit evaluations a central resource. Simultaneous perturbation stochastic approximation (SPSA) is widely used because it obtains a full gradient estimate from only two cost evaluations, independently of the number of parameters. We analyze the random perturbation for the structured Fourier landscapes generated by quantum circuits. For a finite update width, we derive the exact mode-by-mode response of the mean Rademacher estimator and show that it selectively suppresses modes involving many parameters. For general generators, the filtered mean field need not be conservative. We derive its sampling variance, compare it with Gaussian random-direction smoothing, and formulate the resulting stochastic dynamics through a Fokker--Planck equation. The non-conservative field generates acceleration to the steady state and the global minimum of the cost function by gradually decreasing the parameters in SPSA.

</details>


### [12] [Minimax Quantum State Tomography with Periodic Clifford Measurements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00210)
*Hongru Zhao*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper characterizes the minimax expected trace norm risk of quantum state tomography over polynomial spectral decay classes using randomized nonadaptive single-copy periodic Clifford measurements, establishing adaptive oracle inequalities and matching minimax rates for two estimators.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum state tomography underpins state characterization and outcome prediction, yet optimal risk rates and structure-adaptive estimation over realistic spectral decay classes remain insufficiently understood. The work seeks minimax-optimal, prior-free estimators with low-depth measurements.

Method: Under an explicit sufficient block size condition for the two-layer periodic Clifford ensemble, the authors analyze operator norm minimum distance and measurement weighted projected least squares, deriving statewise expected trace norm oracle inequalities via covariance concentration, positivity, and a sharp uniform fourth-moment bound on ensemble projectors; lower bounds follow from a shared rotation information argument on spectral tail packings.

Result: Both estimators attain minimax expected trace norm rates up to constants over polynomial spectral decay classes and every fixed rank constrained class, adaptively with respect to unknown rank, eigenbasis, and spectral structure. Admissible logarithmic block sizes yield these guarantees with logarithmic elementary gate depth. Polynomial time reconstruction algorithms, numerical comparisons, and Lean formalizations are provided.

Conclusion: Structure-adaptive, prior-free quantum state tomography is achievable at minimax trace norm rates using low-depth periodic Clifford measurements, with matching lower bounds confirming optimality and the fourth-moment projector bound being sharp in dimension for arbitrary entangled test states.

Abstract: Quantum state tomography provides a foundation for characterizing state preparation and predicting measurement outcomes. We characterize the minimax expected trace norm risk of quantum state tomography over classes with polynomial spectral decay under randomized nonadaptive single copy measurements. Under an explicit sufficient block size condition for the two layer periodic Clifford ensemble, we establish statewise expected trace norm oracle inequalities for operator norm minimum distance and measurement weighted projected least squares, with bounds that depend on each state's actual spectral tail, while the estimators require no prior knowledge of the state's structural characteristics, including its rank, eigenbasis, or spectral structure. Over these polynomial spectral decay classes, both estimators attain the minimax expected trace norm rates up to constants. As a direct consequence, the same estimators attain the minimax rate over every fixed rank constrained class, while remaining adaptive to the unknown structural characteristics of the state. For admissible logarithmic block sizes, the periodic Clifford measurements attain these guarantees with logarithmic elementary gate depth. The main technical ingredient is a uniform bound on the fourth moment of projectors for the periodic Clifford ensemble. The bound is sharp in dimension and holds for arbitrary test states, including those entangled across blocks. Covariance concentration and positivity yield adaptive oracle bounds, while a shared rotation information argument on spectral tail packings establishes matching lower bounds. We also provide polynomial time reconstruction algorithms, numerical comparisons, and Lean formalizations of all labeled theoretical results.

</details>


### [13] [Physical-Work Fluctuation Relations from Accessible Quantum Macrostates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00246)
*Borhan Ahmadi*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Endpoint thermodynamic information can select an exact fluctuation relation that controls the Jarzynski estimator, lowering sampling cost without changing the free-energy target.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Jarzynski's equality recovers equilibrium free-energy differences from nonequilibrium work trajectories, but its exponential average converges slowly because rare trajectories carry large weight, creating a heavy sampling burden.

Method: Coarse thermodynamic information measured at the nonequilibrium endpoint—specifically the endpoint mean energy and a coarse spatial record—is used to define a maximum-entropy state and select one member of an exact family of fluctuation relations. This selected member provides exact statistical control for the ordinary Jarzynski estimator, even when the final energy and retained record do not commute. The approach is tested in a finite Bose–Hubbard system.

Result: In the finite Bose–Hubbard system, the control substantially lowers the finite-confidence sampling cost. A small controlled departure from exact endpoint matching yields a square-root information–sampling tradeoff.

Conclusion: Coarse endpoint thermodynamic information can reduce the sampling burden of Jarzynski estimation while preserving the same microscopic trajectories and free-energy target, and controlled deviations from exact endpoint matching give rise to a square-root tradeoff between information and sampling.

Abstract: Jarzynski's equality recovers an equilibrium free-energy difference from nonequilibrium work trajectories, but its exponential average can converge very slowly because rare trajectories carry large weight. We show that coarse thermodynamic information measured at the nonequilibrium endpoint can reduce this sampling burden while keeping the same microscopic trajectories and the same free-energy target. The endpoint mean energy and a coarse spatial record define a maximum-entropy state and select one member of an exact family of fluctuation relations. That physically selected member provides an exact statistical control for the ordinary Jarzynski estimator, even when the final energy and retained record do not commute. In a finite Bose--Hubbard system, this control substantially lowers the finite-confidence sampling cost, while a small controlled departure from exact endpoint matching produces a square-root information--sampling tradeoff.

</details>


### [14] [Exact replica-sector hierarchy of multi-resolvent correlations in random free fermions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00261)
*Zhiqiang Huang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper constructs a systematic replica-sector algebra organizing the higher-order (connected) cumulants of channel-resolved spectral functions in random free fermions, giving an exact microscopic benchmark for higher-order fluctuation sectors of eigenstate thermalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The spectral weight of a many-body eigenstate in a given channel is not fixed by the smooth one-point ETH envelope, and the higher-order correlations—connected cumulants of three or more channel-resolved spectral functions—lacked a systematic organization. The work aims to supply that missing organization.

Method: For random free fermions, where each channel overlap is a squared Slater minor of a Haar-orthogonal one-body eigenvector matrix, the r-resolvent moment is given an exact replica-sector decomposition: a sum over double-coset sectors of the orthogonal Weingarten algebra, with permutation sectors as replica contraction classes. Sector coefficients are obtained via an exact occupancy-matrix assembly—signed convolution over slot permutations, decomposition into connected blocks, and a finite-state chain transfer matrix with local transition rules independent of r and k.

Result: The sector theorem holds at arbitrary finite multiplicity, verified exactly at r=3 (k=2,3,4), r=4 and r=5 (k=2). The structure reproduces the physical hierarchy: the fully coincident sector yields the Selberg moment ratios q_r, the levels are nested by exact marginalization, and the sectors are the multi-energy spectral cumulants behind resolvent cumulants, with irreducible self-energy vertices of the Feshbach ladder as the connected kernel.

Conclusion: Multi-resolvent correlations of random free fermions are organized level by level by a single replica-sector algebra, providing an exact microscopic benchmark for the higher-order fluctuation sector of eigenstate thermalization with explicit microscopic sector geometry.

Abstract: The spectral weight with which a many-body eigenstate contributes to a given channel is not fixed by the smooth one-point ETH envelope, and its higher-order correlations---the connected cumulants of three and more channel-resolved spectral functions---have lacked a systematic organization. We construct one. In random free fermions, where every channel overlap is a squared Slater minor of a Haar-orthogonal one-body eigenvector matrix, the $r$-resolvent moment admits an exact replica-sector decomposition: a sum over the double-coset sectors of the orthogonal Weingarten algebra, in which the permutation sectors are the replica contraction classes and each sector value is determined by the overlap pattern of the channels and of the eigenstates. The sector coefficients follow from an exact occupancy-matrix assembly---a signed convolution over the slot permutations, a decomposition into connected blocks, and a finite-state chain transfer matrix whose local transition rules are independent of $r$ and $k$---so the sector theorem holds at arbitrary finite multiplicity, realized exactly at $r=3$ ($k=2,3,4$), $r=4$ and $r=5$ ($k=2$). The structure carries the physical hierarchy: the fully coincident sector is the projection that yields the Selberg moment ratios $q_r$, the levels are nested by exact marginalization, and the sectors are the multi-energy spectral cumulants behind the resolvent cumulants, with the irreducible self-energy vertices of the Feshbach ladder as the connected kernel. The multi-resolvent correlations of random free fermions are therefore organized, level by level, by one replica-sector algebra---an exact microscopic benchmark for the higher-order fluctuation sector of eigenstate thermalization, with its microscopic sector geometry made explicit.

</details>


### [15] [Exact Critical Curve for Uniform Stabilizer-State Identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00266)
*Masahito Hayashi,Yimin Lu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper resolves the exact sample-complexity crossover for identifying a uniformly random pure n-qubit stabilizer state using collective measurements and no side information, showing that for k=n+c, the optimal success probability converges to an explicit positive series P∞(c), with P∞(0)=0.1760837896..., and that the pretty-good measurement is exactly optimal for all finite n and k.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The sample complexity of identifying an unknown pure stabilizer state has been a longstanding open problem in quantum information. Prior work lacked an exact characterization of the critical crossover—the precise number of copies needed for reliable identification—for the uniform ensemble of stabilizer states under arbitrary collective measurements without side information.

Method: The proof diagonalizes the stabilizer Gram operator by exploiting Clifford tensor-power duality together with a defect filtration, including a careful treatment of the exceptional binary sectors. This yields an exact spectral/integer-scale analysis of the optimal measurement problem.

Result: For k=n+c with fixed integer c, the optimal success probability converges to an explicit positive series P∞(c); notably P∞(0)=0.1760837896.... The two tails follow a quadratic-exponential law below threshold and satisfy 1−P∞(c)∼2^−c above threshold. The pretty-good measurement is shown to be exactly optimal for every finite n and k.

Conclusion: The paper completely determines the exact critical crossover and quantifies the n+O(1) copy threshold for stabilizer-state identification, establishing the pretty-good measurement's exact optimality and providing a closed-form limiting success probability with explicit threshold behavior.

Abstract: The sample complexity of identifying an unknown pure stabilizer state has been a longstanding problem in quantum information. In this paper, we resolve the entire exact critical crossover for the uniform ensemble of pure $n$-qubit stabilizer states under arbitrary collective measurements and without side information. For $k=n+c$, with fixed integer $c$, the optimal success probability converges to an explicit positive series $P_{\infty}(c)$. In particular, $P_{\infty}(0)=0.1760837896\ldots$, while its two tails obey a quadratic-exponential law below threshold and $1-P_{\infty}(c)\sim2^{-c}$ above threshold. The pretty-good measurement is exactly optimal for every finite $n$ and $k$. The proof diagonalizes the stabilizer Gram operator through Clifford tensor-power duality and a defect filtration, including the exceptional binary sectors. The resulting integer-scale law quantifies the $n+O(1)$ copy threshold.

</details>


### [16] [A Frame-Spread Lower Bound for Quantum Entropy Estimation under Fixed Rank-One Measurements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00270)
*Xinyu Song*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper establishes minimax lower bounds for estimating von Neumann entropy of a d-dimensional quantum state from a fixed rank-one measurement, showing that Ω(d²) observations are necessary for fixed uniform accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Estimating von Neumann entropy is a fundamental problem in quantum information, but existing tomography bounds are loose. The paper aims to characterize the fundamental sample complexity of entropy estimation under a fixed rank-one measurement by analyzing the minimax mean-squared error.

Method: The authors use the normalized trace-zero frame spread κ_ν ∈ [1, d+1] (with κ_ν = 1 for exact complex projective 2-designs) to parameterize the measurement. They derive minimax mean-squared error lower bounds for any fixed rank-one measurement when n ≤ c₂d²/κ_ν, and analyze the bounded frame spread regime.

Result: For any fixed rank-one measurement and n ≤ c₂d²/κ_ν, the minimax MSE is at least c₁log²{d²/(κ_νn)}. For bounded frame spread and every fixed 0 < a < 2, the minimax risk is of order log²d throughout n ≲ d^{2−a}, implying Ω(d²) observations are required for fixed uniform accuracy. Existing tomography bounds give bounded risk from O(d³log³d) observations for finite equal-weight exact designs and the Haar covariant measurement.

Conclusion: The sample complexity of entropy estimation with a fixed rank-one measurement is Θ(d²) for fixed uniform accuracy, significantly improving over prior O(d³log³d) tomography bounds. The intermediate minimax regime remains open, and adaptive, collective, or common-randomness-reusing protocols are not covered.

Abstract: We study estimation of the von Neumann entropy of a $d$-dimensional quantum state from independent outcomes of a fixed rank-one measurement. Let $κ_ν\in[1,d+1]$ denote the normalized trace-zero frame spread; $κ_ν=1$ for exact complex projective $2$-designs. For any fixed rank-one measurement and $n\le c_2d^2/κ_ν$, the minimax mean-squared error is at least $c_1\log^2\{d^2/(κ_νn)\}$. For bounded frame spread and every fixed $0<a<2$, the minimax risk is of order $\log^2 d$ throughout $n\lesssim d^{2-a}$; hence fixed uniform accuracy requires $Ω(d^2)$ observations. Existing tomography bounds give bounded risk from $O(d^3\log^3 d)$ observations for finite equal-weight exact designs and the Haar covariant measurement, while the intermediate minimax regime remains open. The results concern a fixed measurement repeated independently across copies; adaptive and collective protocols, and protocols that reuse common randomness across copies, are not covered.

</details>


### [17] [Frame dependence of Kochen-Specker contextuality for relativistic spin systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00274)
*Jonte R. Hance,Ruben Campos Delgado*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Kochen-Specker contextuality is Lorentz invariant on the full Hilbert space (boosts act unitarily) but not for spin alone, where momentum acts as an environment applying momentum-dependent Wigner rotations that unsharpen spin observables and break the operator identities underlying state-independent contextuality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper asks whether Kochen-Specker contextuality survives a Lorentz boost, probing the compatibility between quantum contextuality and relativistic kinematics.

Method: Analyze boosts restricted to the spin subsystem, treating momentum as an environment that induces momentum-dependent Wigner rotations via a decoherence-like channel; quantify degradation with the packet average Δ of sin²(θ_W/2) for spin 1/2, and via spin variance transverse to the boost for general spin; propose a q-plate implementation on photon polarisation.

Result: For spin 1/2 a single scalar Δ governs the loss. The Free Will Theorem's SPIN axiom fails for every Δ>0; the Yu-Oh set loses contextuality between Δ≈0.008 and 0.016 (state independence first); the Peres-Mermin square loses contextuality at Δ≈0.059 or 0.074 depending on orientation; singlet CHSH loses nonlocality for Δ≥0.159; a GHZ-state OR gate loses Raussendorf's contextuality certificate at Δ≈0.206.

Conclusion: Contextuality is not Lorentz invariant in the spin-only account, with well-defined, experimentally accessible thresholds for its breakdown. A q-plate realizes the same channel exactly on photon polarisation (retardation replacing rapidity), making the nonlocality, GHZ, and aimed-source predictions testable without any motion.

Abstract: We ask whether Kochen-Specker contextuality is Lorentz invariant. On the full Hilbert space, it is, since boosts act unitarily. Restricted to spin, it is not: momentum acts as an environment applying momentum-dependent Wigner rotations, leaving spin observables unsharp and breaking the operator identities behind state-independent contextuality. For spin $1/2$ one scalar governs the loss, the packet average $Δ$ of $\sin^2(θ_W/2)$ (where $θ_W$ is the Wigner angle); for any spin, a ray degrades at a rate set by its spin variance transverse to the boost. In the spin-only account, the Free Will Theorem's SPIN axiom fails for every $Δ>0$; the Yu-Oh set loses contextuality between $Δ\approx0.008$ and $0.016$, with state independence going first; the Peres-Mermin square loses contextuality at $Δ\approx0.059$ or $0.074$ by orientation; singlet CHSH loses nonlocality for $Δ\geq0.159$; and a GHZ-state OR gate loses Raussendorf's contextuality certificate at $Δ\approx0.206$. A $q$-plate implements the channel exactly on photon polarisation, with a retardation in place of the rapidity, so the nonlocality, GHZ and aimed-source results are testable without motion.

</details>


### [18] [Nonlinear collective-spin dynamics for quantum-enhanced sensing in solid-state platforms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00286)
*Le Bin Ho*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This tutorial presents an intuitive, materials-oriented framework for quantum-enhanced sensing via nonlinear collective-spin dynamics in solid-state systems, linking idealized collective states to microscopic interactions and real platforms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Collective spin systems in solid-state materials (e.g., NV centers, donor spins in silicon, magnetic materials) offer platforms for quantum-enhanced sensing, where spin interactions can generate collective quantum correlations that surpass the standard quantum limit. A materials-oriented understanding of how such nonlinear collective dynamics enable metrological gains is needed.

Method: The tutorial develops a framework based on nonlinear collective-spin dynamics, showing how nonlinear interactions reshape collective spin states in phase space, redistribute quantum fluctuations, and generate metrologically useful states such as spin-squeezed and entangled states. It connects idealized collective dynamics to microscopic interactions and realistic solid-state systems, examining effects of interaction strength, disorder, decoherence, and experimental control on sensing performance, and compares platforms and experiments.

Result: Nonlinear interactions are shown to reshape collective spin states in phase space and redistribute quantum fluctuations, generating spin-squeezed and entangled states useful for metrology. Platform comparisons highlight key material properties, advantages, and limitations governing sensing performance.

Conclusion: The tutorial provides a materials-oriented understanding of nonlinear collective-spin sensing, rather than a comprehensive comparison of experimental platforms or a practical guide to sensing experiments.

Abstract: Collective spin systems in solid-state materials are promising platforms for quantum-enhanced sensing. In systems such as nitrogen-vacancy centers in diamond, donor spins in silicon, and magnetic materials, interactions among many spins can generate collective quantum correlations that improve measurement precision beyond the standard quantum limit. This tutorial develops an intuitive, materials-oriented framework for understanding these effects through nonlinear collective-spin dynamics. We show how nonlinear interactions reshape collective spin states in phase space, redistribute quantum fluctuations, and generate metrologically useful states such as spin-squeezed and entangled states. We then connect these ideal collective dynamics to microscopic interactions and realistic solid-state systems, discussing how interaction strength, disorder, decoherence, and experimental control affect sensing performance. Different platforms and experiments are compared to highlight key material properties, advantages, and limitations. The tutorial focuses on a materials-oriented understanding of nonlinear collective-spin sensing, rather than a comprehensive comparison of experimental platforms or a practical guide to sensing experiments.

</details>


### [19] [Quantum gates from the middle convolution of twisted Burau representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00293)
*Haru Negami*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper analyzes braid representations arising from character-twisted Burau inputs via the Katz-Long-Moody construction (multiplicative middle convolution for KZ-type equations). It determines the spectra and invariant summand structure of the output representation, establishes non-Clifford and entangling properties of the generators, shows the braid image is not projectively dense, and constructs an explicit orthonormal basis yielding a controlled block decomposition with efficient gate-level realizations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Character-twisted Burau inputs under the KLM construction yield unitary braid representations relevant to topological quantum computation and quantum information. Understanding their spectral structure, reducibility, and gate-theoretic properties is necessary to assess their computational utility and to compare them against existing quantum compilation schemes.

Method: For parameters q=e^{2πia}, τ=e^{2πic} with a,c>0 and nc+(n+1)a<1, and λ=e^{2πil}, 0<l<1-nc-(n+1)a, the authors use known form and signature results to obtain a positive-definite invariant form. They compute generator spectra to recover (q,τ), decompose the M=n(n+1)-dimensional output into invariant summands, analyze irreducibility for n=3,4, and study Weyl-Heisenberg frame and entangling properties of generators. They construct an explicit orthonormal basis realizing a block decomposition and express each generator as products of rank-one phase reflections, then as O(M)-length words in one-qubit unitaries and CNOTs in a binary encoding.

Result: The generator spectrum recovers (q,τ). The output decomposes into two trivial summands, two reduced Burau summands, and an n(n-1)-dimensional summand isomorphic to Long's reduced construction, generically irreducible for n=3,4. If a or c is irrational, every generator is non-Clifford in every finite-dimensional Weyl-Heisenberg frame; if 1,a,c are Q-linearly independent, every generator is entangling for every nontrivial bipartition. The full braid image is not projectively dense. For general positive-definite unitary inputs, irreducibility at a nonexceptional parameter forces the free-group input operators to commute. An orthonormal basis is constructed giving each generator one block of size 2(n+1), n-2 blocks of size two, and identity elsewhere. Each generator is a product of 2n rank-one phase reflections, realizable as an O(M)-length word in one-qubit unitaries and CNOTs without auxiliary qubits, including direction preparation and its inverse. Finite-alphabet approximation, conditioning, and error bounds are given, along with a matched-resource comparison with a Quantum Shannon reference compiler.

Conclusion: The KLM construction from character-twisted Burau inputs yields braid representations with strongly constrained structure: a specific invariant summand decomposition, generically irreducible central summand for small n, non-Clifford and entangling generators under irrationality/linear independence conditions, and a non-dense braid image. An explicit orthonormal basis gives a fixed block form, enabling O(M)-length circuit realizations by one-qubit unitaries and CNOTs without ancillas. The gate bound is exponential in the encoded qubit count and excludes classical preprocessing.

Abstract: We study braid representations obtained from character-twisted Burau inputs by the Katz-Long-Moody (KLM) construction, which corresponds to multiplicative middle convolution for KZ-type equations. For $n\ge2$, let $q=e^{2πia}$, $τ=e^{2πic}$, where $a,c>0$ and $nc+(n+1)a<1$. Known form and signature results give a positive-definite invariant form for $λ=e^{2πil}$, $0<l<1-nc-(n+1)a$. For fixed input, unitary realizations are unique up to simultaneous unitary conjugation. We address two questions.
  First, we determine spectra and invariant summands of the $M=n(n+1)$-dimensional output. The generator spectrum recovers $(q,τ)$. The output decomposes into two trivial and two reduced Burau summands, and an $n(n-1)$-dimensional summand isomorphic to Long's reduced construction, generically irreducible for $n=3,4$. If $a$ or $c$ is irrational, every generator is non-Clifford in every finite-dimensional Weyl-Heisenberg frame; if $1,a,c$ are linearly independent over $\mathbb{Q}$, every generator is entangling for every nontrivial bipartition. The full braid image is not projectively dense. For general positive-definite unitary inputs, irreducibility of the full braid output at a nonexceptional parameter forces the free-group input operators to commute.
  Second, we construct one orthonormal basis giving each generator one block of size $2(n+1)$, $n-2$ blocks of size two, and identity elsewhere. Each generator is a product of $2n$ rank-one phase reflections and, in a fixed binary encoding, an $O(M)$-length word in one-qubit unitaries and CNOTs, without auxiliary qubits and including direction preparation and its inverse. We give finite-alphabet approximation, conditioning and error bounds, and a matched-resource comparison with a Quantum Shannon reference compiler. The gate bound is exponential in the encoded qubit count and excludes classical preprocessing.

</details>


### [20] [Hidden-State Updates and observable-record composition in retrocausal models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00357)
*D. M. Theshan N. Weerasinghe*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A hidden-variable model can match context-independent individual readouts yet fail to preserve observable records when devices are connected or reused. Two composition requirements are distinguished: an invariant space from normalization defects characterizes admissible positive cones closed under outcome-conditioned updates, and a separate observation space characterizes indistinguishability under joint readout. Examples, including a wrapped-Cauchy spin source and a finite-state policy case, show failure under outcome-dependent stopping and setting-dependent agreement, though the criteria apply only to specified instrument families and accessible records.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hidden-variable models may reproduce context-independent readouts individually but break down under device composition or reuse, so there is a need to formalize when observable records are preserved under composition.

Method: The paper distinguishes two composition requirements using invariant spaces generated by normalization defects for finite families of nonnegative instrument matrices, with an annihilator defining a maximal admissible positive cone closed under outcome-conditioned updates. It also defines a separate observation space for indistinguishability of context-dependent hidden preparations under joint readout. Examples are tested via exact rational calculations, exhaustive finite-policy experiments, continuous source sampling, and quadrature, including a finite-state case and a wrapped-Cauchy spin source with passive extension.

Result: A finite-state example passes every fixed sequence but fails under outcome-dependent stopping. For a wrapped-Cauchy spin source under passive extension, two individually fair probes acquire a setting-dependent agreement probability with contrast ε² sech(2g)/4. Protection under one and two identical uses forces a continuous binary passive response to be constant, while measurable two-valued exceptions remain possible.

Conclusion: The criteria apply to specified instrument families and accessible records; they do not construct a microscopic detector or establish consistency under unrestricted network composition.

Abstract: A hidden-variable model can reproduce context-independent individual readouts while failing to preserve observable records when devices are connected or reused. We distinguish two composition requirements. For a finite family of nonnegative instrument matrices, an invariant space generated by normalization defects characterizes unit continuation mass under every finite adaptive policy. Its annihilator defines the maximal admissible positive cone closed under outcome-conditioned updates. A separate observation space characterizes indistinguishability of context-dependent hidden preparations under joint readout. A finite-state example passes every fixed sequence but fails under outcome-dependent stopping. For a wrapped-Cauchy spin source with an explicitly stipulated unchanged-source passive extension, two individually fair probes acquire a setting-dependent agreement probability with contrast $ε^2\operatorname{sech}(2g)/4$. More generally, protection under one and two identical uses forces a continuous binary passive response to be constant; measurable two-valued exceptions remain possible. Exact rational calculations, exhaustive finite-policy experiments, continuous source sampling, and quadrature test the stated examples. The criteria apply to specified instrument families and accessible records; they do not construct a microscopic detector or establish consistency under unrestricted network composition.

</details>


### [21] [Finite-blocklength classical communication over the quantum erasure channel with and without classical feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00440)
*Mark M. Wilde*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper determines the exact optimal success probability for transmitting M classical messages over n uses of a quantum erasure channel with classical feedback and no shared entanglement, given by E[min{1,d^K/M}] with K~Binomial(n,1-p).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding the fundamental limits of classical communication over quantum erasure channels, particularly the role of noiseless classical feedback and whether it improves achievable performance, is a core question in quantum information theory. Prior work lacked exact finite-blocklength success probabilities and a full characterization of feedback's benefit.

Method: Derives the optimal success probability for arbitrary adaptive quantum encoders, quantum memories, and receiver instruments. The converse uses an elementary dimension bound for noiseless quantum communication with classical feedback, obtained by fixing classical controls without conditioning the sender's state on the transcript. Achievability uses a classical protocol transmitting base-d digits with acknowledgment-driven repetition. Also establishes a relative-majorization property of erasure-channel outputs and an exact hypothesis-testing converse.

Result: The optimal success probability equals E[min{1,d^K/M}] with K binomial(n,1-p), achieved by a simple digit-repetition protocol for every integer M. A no-feedback hypothesis-testing bound matches numerically but is not always achievable: four uses of the qubit erasure channel with four messages show a strict gap. A product-state tetrahedral qubit code beats all classical binary erasure codes. Explicit message-size formula given; bounded-remainder normal approximation and average-success strong-converse exponent are unchanged without feedback. Feedback-assisted error exponent below capacity is determined and matches the no-feedback exponent above a critical rate.

Conclusion: Classical feedback yields an exact, achievability-matching characterization of optimal success probability for quantum erasure channels, but its benefit over no-feedback protocols is subtle: identical numerical bounds can be non-achievable without feedback, and the error exponent coincides with the no-feedback exponent above a critical rate.

Abstract: We determine the optimal success probability for transmitting a fixed number of classical messages through a finite number of uses of the quantum erasure channel, assisted by noiseless classical feedback and without initial shared entanglement. For an input dimension $d$, an erasure probability $p$, a blocklength $n$, and $M$ equiprobable messages, the optimal success probability is equal to $E[\min\{1,d^K/M\}]$, where $K$ is binomial with parameters $n$ and $1-p$. The converse allows arbitrary adaptive quantum encoders, quantum memories, and receiver instruments. Its main ingredient is an elementary dimension bound for noiseless quantum communication with classical feedback, proved by fixing the classical controls without conditioning the sender's state on the observed transcript. A classical protocol that transmits the base-$d$ digits of an integer representing the message, repeating each digit until the receiver acknowledges its reception or the prescribed blocklength is reached, attains the bound for every integer $M$. We also establish a relative-majorization property of erasure-channel outputs and exactly evaluate a hypothesis-testing converse, recovering the same numerical bound without feedback. That converse need not be achievable without feedback: four uses of the qubit erasure channel and four messages give a strict gap. A product-state code employing tetrahedral qubit states nevertheless outperforms every classical binary erasure code with these parameters. We give an explicit message-size formula and show that the bounded-remainder normal approximation and the average-success strong-converse exponent are unchanged without feedback. We also determine the feedback-assisted error exponent below capacity, prove that it agrees with the no-feedback exponent above a critical rate, and give no-feedback bounds at lower rates.

</details>


### [22] [Quantum Secret Sharing and Error Correction vs No-Cloning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00441)
*Steven Chien,Ishani Mukherjee,Mark Zhandry*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces multi-copy quantum secret sharing (QSS), where k copies of the input state are given but only one need be recovered. It shows no-cloning does not fully characterize feasibility in the many-copy setting: for thresholds, QSS is achievable when t > (n-k+1)/2, while a new Clique Path obstruction makes it impossible when t ≤ (n-1)/k. Bounds match exactly for k=2.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical secret sharing allows all access structures and efficient threshold protocols, but in the quantum setting the no-cloning theorem makes many access structures impossible. For single-copy threshold QSS, feasibility is exactly characterized by no-cloning: possible iff t > n/2. The paper asks whether this clean characterization extends to a many-copy variant, and aims to understand the true feasibility boundary in that regime.

Method: The authors define k-copy QSS, in which k identical copies of the input state are provided but the output only needs to recover one copy. They connect feasibility to the k-colorability of certain graphs derived from the access structure. Using these graph-theoretic connections, they construct positive protocols for thresholds and derive a new general obstruction, the Clique Path obstruction, applicable to arbitrary access structures.

Result: For k-copy threshold QSS, no-cloning implies impossibility when t ≤ n/(k+1). The paper proves a positive result: QSS is constructible whenever t > (n-k+1)/2. It also proves a new Clique Path obstruction showing impossibility whenever t ≤ (n-1)/k. For k=2 the upper and lower bounds coincide exactly, fully characterizing feasibility. A gap remains for k ≥ 3.

Conclusion: No-cloning does not exactly characterize the many-copy case of quantum secret sharing; multiple copies permit going slightly beyond the single-copy obstruction. The Clique Path obstruction is a novel general impossibility tool. The results extend to quantum error correction for erasure channels, yielding analogous obstructions. Closing the gap for k ≥ 3 copies is left as an open problem.

Abstract: Secret sharing is ubiquitous throughout cryptography. All possible access structures are classically feasible, and in the case of threshold access structures, the protocols are even very efficient. However, when moving to the quantum setting, the no-cloning theorem shows that many access structures are impossible. In fact, no-cloning exactly characterizes feasibility: for thresholds, quantum secret sharing is possible if and only if the threshold $t$ is strictly more than $n/2$.
  In this work, we propose a variant of quantum secret sharing (QSS) where two or more identical copies of the input state are provided, but the output is only required to recover one copy. This notion circumvents the simple one-copy no-cloning obstruction, though the natural $k$-copy generalization still gives much milder obstructions. For thresholds using $k$ copies, no-cloning implies that QSS is impossible whenever $t\leq n/(k+1)$.
  It is tempting to hypothesize that no-cloning continues to exactly characterize the many-copy case. However, we show that this is not the case. We give positive results showing that multiple copies allow for going slightly beyond the single-copy obstruction: for thresholds, we construct QSS whenever $t>(n-k+1)/2$. On the other hand, we give a novel obstruction we call the Clique Path obstruction, which applies to arbitrary access structures. For thresholds, it shows that QSS is impossible whenever $t\leq (n-1)/k$. Our upper and lower bounds exactly match for $k=2$. Both our results leverage connections to the $k$-colorability of certain graphs derived from the access structure. We leave closing the gap for $k\geq 3$ copies as a fascinating direction for future work.
  Secret sharing is closely related to error correction, which can also be considered in the many-copy setting. Our results imply similar obstructions for quantum error correction for erasure channels.

</details>


### [23] [Robust classical and quantum communication over almost-iid channels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00442)
*Liuhang Ye,Bjarne Bergh,Nilanjana Datta*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The asymptotic capacities of a quantum channel Φ are shown to persist for sequences of Choi-almost-iid channels that are MSR almost-iid along Φ with defect parameters r_n = o(n); both universal unassisted classical and quantum capacities coincide with those of Φ, with a single code sequence (depending only on Φ and the rate) achieving vanishing error across the whole class.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The standard iid paradigm models n uses of a channel Φ as the tensor power Φ^{⊗n}, but exact tensor-power behaviour is an idealization. The paper asks whether Φ's asymptotic capacities remain achievable for channel sequences that are only approximately iid along Φ.
