The MSR almost-iid class is motivated by its appearance in the approximating mixture of Renner's exponential de Finetti theorem for quantum states, with Choi-almost-iid channels playing the analogous role in an exponential de Finetti theorem for quantum channels developed in a companion paper.

Method: The authors define Choi-almost-iid channels: sequences whose normalized Choi states are Mazzola-Sutter-Renner (MSR) almost-iid along the normalized Choi state of Φ, with defect parameters r_n = o(n). They then analyze the universal unassisted classical and quantum capacities of this class, demanding a single code sequence that depends on Φ and the target rate but not on the specific channel sequence, and that yields vanishing error for every sequence in the class.

Result: The universal unassisted classical and quantum capacities of the class of Choi-almost-iid channels along Φ equal those of Φ itself. Universality holds within the class: for every rate below the relevant capacity, one code sequence (depending on Φ and the rate, but not on the particular channel sequence) achieves vanishing error for every sequence in the class.

Conclusion: Exact tensor-power iid behaviour is not necessary for achieving the asymptotic capacities of Φ; approximate iid structure (Choi-almost-iid / MSR almost-iid with r_n = o(n)) suffices. This establishes robust, universal coding for approximately iid channel sequences, complementing the exponential de Finetti theorem for quantum channels in the companion paper.

Abstract: The independent and identically distributed (iid) paradigm for asymptotic communication through quantum channels describes $n$ uses of a channel $Φ$ by $Φ^{\otimes n}$. Since exact tensor-power behaviour is an idealization, we ask whether the asymptotic capacities of $Φ$ remain achievable for channel sequences that are almost-iid along $Φ$. We consider sequences of Choi-almost-iid channels whose normalized Choi states are Mazzola-Sutter-Renner (MSR) almost-iid along the normalized Choi state of $Φ$, with defect parameters $r_n=o(n)$. In Renner's exponential de Finetti theorem for quantum states, almost-iid states appear in the approximating mixture and motivate the MSR almost-iid class. Choi-almost-iid channels play the corresponding role in an exponential de Finetti theorem for quantum channels established in a companion paper. In this paper, we prove that the universal unassisted classical and quantum capacities of the class of Choi-almost-iid channels along $Φ$ equal those of the channel $Φ$. Here universality is within this class: for each rate below the relevant capacity, a code sequence depending on $Φ$ and the rate, but not on the particular channel sequence, achieves vanishing error for every sequence in the class.

</details>


### [24] [Flexible rigidity: Compiling generic trusted-preparation protocols into sequential device-independent protocols](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00444)
*Amir Arqand,Srijita Kundu,Ernest Y. -Z. Tan*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents a generic compiler that converts a broad class of device-dependent protocols with trusted state preparation into sequential device-independent protocols, applied to uncloneable encryption, copy-protection of point functions, and blind randomness expansion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Device-independent protocols offer strong security guarantees but often suffer from poor rates. Previous rigidity theorems based on trace distance or fidelity face a fundamental scaling obstacle, limiting the efficiency of sequential device-independent protocols. The motivation is to overcome this rate limitation by developing new rigidity tools that enable constant-rate, noise-tolerant compilation.

Method: The compiler requires the trusted-preparation protocol to admit an entanglement-based realization corresponding to the optimal strategy of any non-local game satisfying a weak rigidity condition. Technically, the authors introduce a new class of rigidity theorems based on Rényi divergences. For the generic sequential case, the proof relies on the sharp-divergence chain rule of Fawzi and Fawzi (2021). For memoryless devices, they derive a novel subadditivity property for a modified channel sandwiched divergence to improve rates. They also prove a chain rule for iterated-mean divergences.

Result: The compiler yields sequential device-independent protocols for uncloneable encryption, copy-protection of point functions, and blind randomness expansion. It achieves a constant rate (ratio of trusted states prepared to device uses) while tolerating a constant level of noise per device usage, overcoming the scaling obstacle of prior trace-distance or fidelity-based rigidity theorems.

Conclusion: The work provides a general framework for compiling trusted-preparation protocols into sequential device-independent ones with constant-rate and noise-tolerant performance, enabled by Rényi-divergence-based rigidity theorems. The subadditivity result for a modified channel sandwiched divergence may be of independent interest, and the chain rule for iterated-mean divergences offers a prospect for future rate improvements.

Abstract: We give a generic compiler for turning a wide class of device-dependent protocols with trusted state preparation into sequential device-independent protocols. In order for our compiler to apply, the trusted-preparation protocol needs to satisfy certain minimal conditions: having an entanglement-based realization in the first place, and the entanglement-based realization corresponding to the optimal strategy of any non-local game that satisfies a fairly weak rigidity condition. We apply our generic compiler to give sequential device-independent protocols for uncloneable encryption, copy-protection of point functions, and blind randomness expansion.
  Our technical contribution is a new class of rigidity theorems based on Rényi divergences, with flexible cryptographic applications. They allow our compiler to achieve a constant rate (i.e., the ratio of trusted states prepared to device uses), while tolerating a constant level of noise per device usage. This overcomes a fundamental scaling obstacle faced by previous rigidity theorems for trace distance or fidelity. For the generic sequential case, our proof is based on the sharp-divergence chain rule by Fawzi and Fawzi (2021), which suffices for a constant but fairly low rate. In the special case when the devices retain no memory, we improve the rates by deriving a novel subadditivity property for a modified channel sandwiched divergence, which may be of independent interest. Finally, as a prospect for future improvements, we also prove a chain rule for the iterated-mean divergences from Brown, Fawzi and Fawzi (2021).

</details>


### [25] [Learning Local Fermionic Lindbladians under Parity Superselection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00448)
*Tim Möbus,Daniel Stilck França,Cambyse Rouzé*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work develops sample-efficient protocols to learn parity-covariant, k-mode-local Lindbladians on m fermionic modes despite parity superselection, using even preparations/measurements, internal markers, signed Fierz inversion, and semidefinite fitting, with provable sample and diamond-norm complexity bounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Parity superselection forbids direct measurement of odd Majorana observables, which obstructs standard learning of the full Lindbladian generator. The paper aims to overcome this restriction and learn time-independent, parity-covariant, k-mode-local Lindbladians using only physically allowed even preparations and measurements, without external modes or known nonzero coefficient locations, and to establish optimal sample complexity including diamond-norm guarantees and lower bounds.

Method: The protocol uses uninterrupted short-time evolution with even preparations and measurements. Internal markers convert odd probes into even observables, and pair measurements among three separated markers calibrate marker dynamical contributions. A signed Fierz inversion recovers canonical coefficients, while semidefinite fitting yields a valid generator. For the graph-free setting, one idle ancillary mode per system mode and potentially nonlocal pair operations are used, under a supplied approximate coefficient-degree bound and controlled weak-coefficient tails. Geometric models exploit fixed-support even observable prediction.

Result: For finite-range models on known bounded-degree graphs with suitable marker access and weighted-strength bound ᾱ, entrywise error ε is achieved with Õ(ᾱ²ε⁻²log(m/δ)) samples without external modes or known nonzero coefficient locations. Recovery to diamond-norm error ε costs an additional m² factor, matching lower bounds for short-time experiments on fresh systems up to logarithmic and fixed geometric factors. Without a supplied graph, total absolute coefficient error ≤ ε per mode is achieved with Õ_k(ᾱ²d²m^{⌊k/2⌋}ε⁻²log(m/δ)) samples under an approximate coefficient-degree bound d. For geometric models, fixed-support even observables are predictable with logarithmic system-size sample complexity. All guarantees hold with probability ≥ 1−δ. Finite-volume and exponential-tail extensions are provided.

Conclusion: Parity superselection can be circumvented for Lindbladian learning via even-only resources combined with marker-based calibration and Fierz inversion, yielding provably sample-efficient, near-optimal protocols. The approach extends to graph-free and geometric settings, attains diamond-norm lower bounds for short-time experiments, and generalizes to finite-volume and exponential-tail regimes.

Abstract: Parity superselection forbids direct measurement of odd Majorana observables. We learn time-independent, parity-covariant, $k$-mode-local Lindbladians on $m$ modes using even preparations and measurements with uninterrupted short-time evolution. Internal markers turn odd probes into even observables, and pair measurements among three separated markers calibrate their dynamical contributions. Signed Fierz inversion recovers canonical coefficients, while semidefinite fitting yields a valid generator. For finite-range models on known bounded-degree graphs with suitable marker access and a supplied weighted-strength bound $\barα$, entrywise error $\varepsilon$ is achieved using $\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\barα^2\varepsilon^{-2}\log(m/δ))$ samples, without external modes or known nonzero coefficient locations. Recovery to diamond-norm error $\varepsilon$ costs an additional factor $m^2$, matching lower bounds for short-time experiments on fresh systems up to logarithmic and fixed geometric factors. Without a supplied graph, one idle ancillary mode per system mode and potentially nonlocal pair operations give total absolute coefficient error at most $\varepsilon$ per mode using $\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_k(\barα^2\mathsf d^2m^{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}\varepsilon^{-2}\log(m/δ))$ samples, under a supplied approximate coefficient-degree bound $\mathsf d$ and controlled weak-coefficient tails. All guarantees hold with probability at least $1-δ$. For geometric models, fixed-support even observables can be predicted with logarithmic system-size sample complexity at fixed time and accuracy. We also give finite-volume and exponential-tail extensions.

</details>


### [26] [Clifford-hierarchy stabilizer formalism with applications to twisted quantum doubles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00464)
*Christopher Fechisin,Kieran Cooney,Mathi Raja,Victor V. Albert,Dominic J. Williamson,Kyle Kawagoe,Seth Musser*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces the $X\mathcal C^{(k)}_d$ stabilizer formalism, generalizing Pauli stabilizers by mixing Pauli-$X$ with diagonal operators from level $k$ of the Clifford hierarchy. It classifies these states, relates stabilizer level to wavefunction phase gates, realizes 2D twisted quantum doubles with Clifford stabilizers, conjectures the level needed for type-I/II cocycles, and shows deterministic logical magic-state preparation of qudit toric codes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum double models can circumvent the Bravyi-Koenig bound via code-switching protocols where non-Pauli stabilizer phases appear as intermediate codes. This motivates a concrete stabilizer framework for beyond-Pauli topological codes and their logical gates.

Method: Define the $X\mathcal C^{(k)}_d$ stabilizer formalism on prime-dimensional qudits, combining Pauli-$X$ operators with diagonal operators from a fixed level $k$ of the Clifford hierarchy. Classify the resulting stabilizer states, analyze the relationship between stabilizer level and diagonal phase gates, and construct realizations of 2D twisted quantum doubles ${\cal D}^\omega(\mathbb{Z}_p^n)$.

Result: $X\mathcal C^{(k)}_d$ stabilizer states are uniform superpositions over affine-linear subspaces with diagonal phases. States whose stabilizers need level-$k$ Clifford operators require phase-producing diagonal gates one level higher. Every 2D twisted quantum double ${\cal D}^\omega(\mathbb{Z}_p^n)$ with $\omega$ a product of type-III cocycles and $p$ odd admits a Clifford ($k=2$) stabilizer realization; phases with type-I or type-II cocycles are conjectured to require level-$p$ stabilizers. A pair of qudit toric codes can be deterministically prepared in a logical magic state by measuring $X\mathcal C^{(k)}_d$ stabilizers.

Conclusion: The $X\mathcal C^{(k)}_d$ formalism provides a concrete stabilizer framework for topological codes beyond Pauli stabilizer models and for their logical gates.

Abstract: Recent work has shown that quantum double models can be used to circumvent the Bravyi-Koenig bound. In particular, this is achieved through code-switching protocols in which non-Pauli stabilizer phases appear as intermediate codes. We introduce the $X\mathcal C^{(k)}_d$ stabilizer formalism, which generalizes the Pauli stabilizer formalism by combining Pauli-$X$ operators with diagonal operators drawn from a fixed level $k$ of the Clifford hierarchy on prime-dimensional qudits. This yields a natural class of beyond-Pauli stabilizer models, including many twisted quantum double phases. We classify a large class of these $X\mathcal C^{(k)}_d$ stabilizer states, showing that they are uniform superpositions over affine-linear subspaces with diagonal phases. In particular, when the stabilizers of a state require operators from level $k$ of the Clifford hierarchy, we show that the diagonal gates required to produce the phases in the wavefunction sit one level higher. We further show that every 2D twisted quantum double ${\cal D}^ω(\mathbb{Z}_p^n)$ with $ω$ a product of type-III cocycles and $p$ an odd prime admits a realization using only Clifford ($k=2$) stabilizers, and we conjecture that phases where $ω$ contains a type-I or type-II cocycle require level-$p$ Clifford-hierarchy stabilizers for $p$-dimensional qudits. Finally, we demonstrate that a pair of qudit toric codes may be deterministically prepared in a logical magic state by measuring $X\mathcal C^{(k)}_d$ stabilizers. Together, these results provide a concrete stabilizer framework for topological codes beyond Pauli stabilizer models and their logical gates.

</details>


### [27] [Factor Code Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00469)
*Abhisek Sahu,Jeremy van der Heijden,Mark Van Raamsdonk,Rana Zibakhsh*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces "factor codes" and "factor code networks," where isometries between Hilbert spaces are promoted to unital *-homomorphisms. It determines when such promotion is possible, showing divisibility conditions suffice for tree-structured networks but not in general, and gives a complete characterization for diamond networks via multiplicities of principal angles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum codes are naturally modeled by isometries T from a logical to a physical Hilbert space, and one wants to extend this to a consistent operator map between the corresponding operator algebras. When multiple codes are related hierarchically, the question arises whether the underlying isometry network can be coherently lifted to a network of operator-algebra maps preserving composition. This promotion problem is fundamental for composing, nesting, and compatibility of quantum codes, but the precise conditions under which it is possible were unclear.

Method: The authors formalize operator maps φ as *-homomorphisms satisfying φ(O)T = TO, and call a pair (T,φ) a factor code when φ is unital (equivalently dim H_code divides dim H_phys), so that φ(B(H_code)) is a von Neumann factor subalgebra. They define code networks as families of Hilbert spaces indexed by a poset with composable isometries T_kj T_ji = T_ki, and define factor code networks by requiring compatible unital maps φ_kj φ_ji = φ_ki. They analyze existence of such lifts in terms of Hilbert space dimension divisibility, tree structures, diamond-shaped networks (using principal angles between subspaces and their multiplicities), and algebraic relations—commutation, generation of B(H_j), and intersections—among images φ(B(H_i)) for comparable and incomparable indices. They also analyze nondegenerate commuting squares formed by these algebras.

Result: Divisibility conditions on Hilbert space dimensions are sufficient for promoting any code network with a tree structure, but not sufficient in general. For a diamond-shaped network, promotion is possible if and only if the principal angles between T_42 H_2 and T_43 H_3 all have multiplicity divisible by dim H_1. For incomparable i_1, i_2 < j, the paper characterizes when φ_j i_1(B(H_i1)) and φ_j i_2(B(H_i2)) commute, when they generate all of B(H_j), and when they intersect only in multiples of the identity. For i < j_1, j_2 < k, it characterizes when φ_k j_1(B(H_j1)) ∩ φ_k j_2(B(H_j2)) = φ_k i(B(H_i)) and when these algebras together with B(H_k) form a nondegenerate commuting square.

Conclusion: The operator-map promotion of code networks is governed by both dimension divisibility and finer geometric data (principal angles with prescribed multiplicities). Tree networks are always promotable under divisibility, whereas general and diamond networks require additional angle-multiplicity conditions. The relative position of the image algebras is fully characterized in terms of commutation, generation, and intersection, yielding criteria for nondegenerate commuting squares, which connect factor code networks to the theory of inclusions of von Neumann algebras and subfactors.

Abstract: For a quantum code defined by an isometry $T:H_{\rm code} \to H_{\rm phys}$, an operator map is a *-homomorphism $\varphi: B(H_{\rm code}) \to B(H_{\rm phys})$ from the space of operators on the logical Hilbert space to the space of operators on the physical Hilbert space satisfying $\varphi({\cal O}) T = T {\cal O}$ for every operator ${\cal O}$ on $H_{\rm code}$. This can additionally be taken to be unital $\varphi(I_{code}) = I_{phys}$ if and only if $\dim H_{\rm code}$ divides $\dim H_{\rm phys}$. In this case, $\varphi(B(H_{\rm code})$ is a von Neumann factor subalgebra of $B(H_{\rm phys})$ and we call the pair $(T,\varphi)$ a {\it factor code}.
  A code network is a family of Hilbert spaces $\{{\cal H}_i\}$ indexed by a partially ordered set with isometries $T_{ji}:{\cal H}_i \to {\cal H}_j$ for $i < j$ satisfying $T_{kj} T_{ji} = T_{ki}$ for $i < j < k$. In this paper, we ask when this can be promoted to a {\it factor code network} by defining unital operator maps $\varphi_{ji}$ so that $(T_{ji}, \varphi_{ji})$ is a factor code and $\varphi_{kj} \varphi_{ji} = \varphi_{ki}$. We show that the divisibility conditions on Hilbert space dimensions are sufficient for any code network with a tree structure but not in general. For a diamond-shaped network, we show that promotion is possible if and only if the principle angles between $T_{42} H_2$ and $T_{43} H_3$ all have multiplicity divisible by $H_1$.
  For incomparable $i_1,i_2 < j$, we characterize when $\varphi_{j i_1} (B(H_{i_1}))$ and $\varphi_{j i_2} (B(H_{i_2}))$ commute, when they generate all of $B(H_j)$, and when they intersect only on multiples of the identity. For $i < j_1,j_2 < k$, we characterize when $\varphi_{k j_1} (B(H_{j_1})) \cap \varphi_{k j_2} (B(H_{j_2})) = \varphi_{k i} (B(H_{i}))$ and when these algebras together with $B(H_k)$ form a nondegenerate commuting square.

</details>


### [28] [Mixed-state phases induced by power-law quantum channels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00480)
*Christopher Fechisin,Tsung-Cheng Lu,Zhi-Yuan Wei,Jeet Shah,Yu-Xin Wang,Alexey V. Gorshkov,Cheng-Ju Lin*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A long-range two-body dephasing channel circumvents the no-go result for finite-depth local channels in 1D, producing a phase diagram with trivial, mixed SPT, and SW-SSB phases for matrix product states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inherently mixed quantum orders such as strong-to-weak spontaneous symmetry breaking (SW-SSB) lack pure-state analogues. Finite-depth local quantum channels are generally expected to be unable to induce a transition to SW-SSB in one spatial dimension, so a new route is needed to access such mixed phases.

Method: Introduce a long-range quantum channel implementing two-body dephasing on site pairs with power-law-decaying probability in site separation. Apply it to a one-parameter family of matrix product states interpolating between trivial and SPT phases. Use Rényi-2 diagnostics and map the system to a classical statistical mechanics model to link loss of logical information to SW-SSB onset.

Result: Constructs a phase diagram hosting trivial, mixed SPT, and SW-SSB phases under Rényi-2 diagnostics. The mixed SPT phase differs from previously studied decohered SPT phases: it hosts two logical qubits and exhibits nonvanishing Rényi-2 string order despite vanishing conventional string order. The mapping establishes a direct connection between loss of logical information and SW-SSB onset.

Conclusion: Long-range dephasing enables mixed quantum orders beyond the reach of finite-depth local channels. The states can be tuned to high-purity regimes, reducing measurement overhead and enabling experimental realization and detection on near-term quantum hardware.

Abstract: Open quantum systems can exhibit inherently mixed quantum orders, such as strong-to-weak spontaneous symmetry breaking (SW-SSB), which have no analogue in pure states. It is generally expected that a finite-depth local quantum channel cannot induce a phase transition to SW-SSB in one spatial dimension. We circumvent this obstruction by introducing a long-range quantum channel, which implements two-body dephasing on pairs of sites with probability falling off according to a power-law in the site separation. Applying this channel to a one-parameter family of matrix product states interpolating between trivial and symmetry-protected topological (SPT) phases, we construct a phase diagram that hosts trivial, mixed SPT, and SW-SSB phases with respect to Rényi-2 diagnostics. Notably, the mixed SPT phase is distinct from previously studied decohered SPT phases: it hosts two logical qubits and exhibits nonvanishing Rényi-2 string order, despite vanishing conventional string order. Through a series of mappings to a classical statistical mechanics model, we establish a direct connection between the loss of logical information and the onset of SW-SSB in this model. Finally, we consider the feasibility of experimental realization of the phenomena we have studied. We emphasize that our family of mixed states can be tuned to regimes of high purity, substantially reducing measurement overhead and enabling their experimental realization and detection on near-term quantum hardware.

</details>


### [29] [Causal Trade-Offs in Superluminal Extensions of Relativity. A no-go theorem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00494)
*Amrapali Sen,Matthias Salzger,V. Vilasini*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper develops a causal modelling framework to analyze Dragan and Ekert's proposal that superluminal reference frames can reconcile relativistic symmetry with quantum uncertainty, proving a no-go theorem that such theories can avoid superluminal signalling only under severe constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extensions of relativity including superluminal reference frames have been proposed by Dragan and Ekert to reconcile relativistic symmetry with quantum uncertainty. The paper aims to formally examine whether this proposal works and to clarify the causal constraints involved.

Method: The authors develop a causal modelling framework to formally study the proposal and prove a no-go theorem regarding theories that permit superluminal reference frames.

Result: A theory permitting superluminal reference frames can avoid superluminal signalling only if it severely constrains which spacetime regions are accessible to operational protocols, if the choice of reference frame has observable consequences, or if free variables do not exist or their existence depends on the reference frame. The no-go result shows that Dragan and Ekert's objective uncertainty does not by itself eliminate the conflict; relaxing other fundamental assumptions may avoid superluminal signalling instead, rendering the proposed objective uncertainty possibly unnecessary.

Conclusion: The results clarify the limitations of uncertainty-based resolutions and the causal trade-offs faced by superluminal extensions of relativity.

Abstract: Extensions of relativity that include superluminal reference frames have recently been proposed by Dragan and Ekert as a way to reconcile relativistic symmetry with quantum uncertainty. In this work, we develop a causal modelling framework to formally study this proposal and prove a no-go theorem demonstrating that a theory permitting superluminal reference frames can avoid superluminal signalling only if it severely constrains which spacetime regions are accessible to operational protocols, if the choice of reference frame has observable consequences or if free variables do not exist or their existence depends on the reference frame. Crucially, our result goes beyond the familiar intuition that superluminal transformations may lead to superluminal signalling; our no-go result shows that Dragan and Ekert's proposal of introducing objective uncertainty does not, by itself, eliminate this conflict. In fact, because superluminal signalling might instead be avoided by relaxing other fundamental assumptions in the presence of superluminal reference frames, the proposed objective uncertainty may not be necessary at all. Our results clarify the limitations of uncertainty-based resolutions and the causal trade-offs faced by superluminal extensions of relativity.

</details>


### [30] [Quantum Algorithms for OPI Variants Beyond Locality and Classical Decodability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00502)
*Seyoon Ragavan,Noah Shutty*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper extends Regev's quantum reduction framework by removing two prior restrictions separately: it enables decoding with a quantum decoder for codes with a two-fold multiplication property, and it supports global histogram-local constraints (allowed symbol frequencies) via a resampling stability argument, yielding efficient quantum algorithms for optimal polynomial intersection variants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Regev's reduction is a quantum framework for finding codewords satisfying nonlinear constraints via dual-code decoding. Prior non-dequantized applications relied on (i) efficient classical decoders and (ii) coordinate-wise constraints specifying allowed values per coordinate. This work aims to push past both restrictions.

Method: First contribution: use a quantum decoder to solve for y in (F_q\{0})^m with By=0, adapting the Chen–Liu–Zhandry template to codes spanned by rows of B satisfying a "two-fold multiplication property" (coordinate-wise products of pairs of codewords span a space of dimension much smaller than m), for fixed prime q>2. Second contribution: keep classical decoding but allow global constraints, studying "histogram-local" constraints that specify allowed occurrence counts per symbol; show that for broad families, a uniformly random satisfying vector stays satisfying with constant probability after resampling a uniformly random coordinate.

Result: The quantum-decoder approach extends solvability beyond the random-matrix regime m=Ω(n^2) previously covered. The histogram-local stability property guarantees sufficient Fourier mass at decodable Hamming weights, enabling efficient quantum algorithms for variants of optimal polynomial intersection (OPI).

Conclusion: Regev's reduction can be generalized along two independent axes—quantum decoding on structured codes and global frequency constraints—broadening its applicability to new problems such as histogram-constrained OPI variants.

Abstract: Regev's reduction is a quantum algorithmic framework for finding codewords satisfying nonlinear constraints by decoding the dual code. To date, applications that have not been dequantized have relied on efficient classical decoders and coordinate-wise constraints specifying a set of allowed values for each coordinate. We push past these two restrictions separately via two separate contributions.
  Our first contribution uses a quantum decoder to find solutions $\mathbf{y}\in(\mathbb{F}_q\setminus\{0\})^m$ to $\mathbf{B}\mathbf{y}=0$, where $\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{F}_q^{n\times m}$. For fixed prime $q>2$, Chen, Liu, and Zhandry (Eurocrypt 2022) solve this problem for random matrices with $m=Ω(n^2)$, a regime now covered by classical algorithms. We adapt their template to codes (spanned by the rows of $\mathbf{B}$) with a ``two-fold multiplication property'': the coordinate-wise products of pairs of codewords span a space of dimension much smaller than $m$.
  Our second contribution retains classical decoding but allows global constraints on symbol frequencies. We study ``histogram-local'' constraints, which specify the allowed numbers of occurrences of each symbol. For broad families of these constraints, we show that a uniformly random satisfying vector remains satisfying with constant probability after resampling a uniformly random coordinate. This stability provides sufficient Fourier mass at decodable Hamming weights, yielding efficient quantum algorithms for variants of optimal polynomial intersection (OPI).

</details>


### [31] [Graph Neural Post-selection for Quantum Error Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00504)
*Conor Carty,Tamas Noszko,Joschka Roffe,Roberto Bondesan*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Graph neural networks predict quantum decoder failure directly from syndromes (and belief-propagation posteriors for qLDPC codes), enabling post-selection without extra decoder runs, achieving large logical error reductions on BB and surface codes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Post-selection improves logical reliability by discarding failure-prone shots, but existing selectors typically require additional decoder executions, which is costly. The paper aims to predict decoder failure cheaply, using only syndrome data, to enable efficient post-selection for high-rate qLDPC codes.

Method: The authors introduce graph neural networks that predict decoder failure without additional decoder executions, leveraging only syndromes and, for high-rate qLDPC codes, existing belief-propagation posteriors. They evaluate rotated surface codes and bivariate bicycle (BB) code memories under uniform depolarising circuit-level noise.

Result: Retaining 90% of shots yields approximately 3700x and 740x logical error rate reductions for [[72,12,6]] and [[144,12,12]] BB codes at realistic physical error rates. For a distance-5 surface code at physical noise p=0.003, the neural post-selector achieves logical error suppression comparable to the leading complementary gap method without running the decoder.

Conclusion: GNN-based failure prediction enables decoder-free post-selection that substantially suppresses logical errors for both BB qLDPC and surface codes, matching a leading gap-based method at a fraction of the cost.

Abstract: Post-selection improves the logical reliability of quantum computation by rejecting shots which are likely to result in logical failure. We introduce graph neural networks that predict decoder failure without additional decoder executions, using only syndromes, and for high-rate quantum low-density parity-check (qLDPC) codes, existing belief-propagation posteriors. We evaluate rotated surface and bivariate bicycle (BB) code memories under uniform depolarising circuit-level noise. Retaining 90% of shots gives approximately 3700x and 740x reductions in logical error rates for [[72,12,6]] and [[144,12,12]] BB codes at realistic physical error rates. For the distance 5 surface code at physical noise p=0.003, our neural post-selector achieves logical error suppression comparable to the leading complementary gap method without running the decoder.

</details>


### [32] [Noisy Quantum Query Complexity via Fractional Block Sensitivity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00506)
*Mehil Agarwal,Shravas Rao,Fang Song*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper studies quantum query complexity under imperfect oracle access, developing lower bounds via a common framework based on fractional block sensitivity (fbs), and characterizes quantum speedups and tradeoffs under negligence, hybrid classical-coherent queries, and time-varying dephasing noise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum algorithms typically assume perfect oracle access, but real oracles may be faulty, noisy, or hybrid (mixing classical and coherent queries). Understanding how such imperfections affect quantum query complexity, and whether quantum speedups survive, requires a unified lower-bound framework.

Method: The authors build lower bounds on fractional block sensitivity (fbs). They analyze three imperfect oracle models: (1) negligent oracles applying the correct query with probability 1-p; (2) hybrid algorithms with Q coherent and C classical queries; (3) IID dephasing noise dephasing the query-index register at rate p per query. They also introduce a broader family of time-varying dephasing models and reduce the corresponding variational resource to a convex optimization problem.

Result: For negligent oracles, a lower bound in terms of fbs(f,0^n) is proved. Surprisingly, negligence need not destroy quantum speedups: any f can be transformed into a partial function f' preserving the quantum query complexity of f, yielding exponential quantum speedups under negligent queries and ruling out a general fbs(f) lower bound for partial functions in this model. For hybrid algorithms, the tradeoff C + Q^2 = Ω(fbs(f)) is established. For IID dephasing, Ω(p·fbs(f)) queries are necessary. A variational resource lower bounded by fbs(f) is identified for time-varying dephasing, computable via convex optimization, recovering the hybrid and IID bounds and determining the query complexity of unstructured search when the dephasing rate grows over time.

Conclusion: Fractional block sensitivity provides a unifying framework for lower bounds under imperfect oracle access. Quantum speedups can persist even under negligent queries for partial functions, while hybrid and dephasing models admit general fbs-based lower bounds. Time-varying dephasing is captured by a convex-optimization variational resource, enabling systematic lower bounds for new noise schedules.

Abstract: We study quantum query complexity under several models of imperfect oracle access, and develop lower bounds through a common framework based on fractional block sensitivity (\(\fbs\)).
  For a negligent oracle that applies the correct query with probability \(1-p\), we prove a lower bound in terms of \(\fbs(f,0^n)\). We also show, perhaps surprisingly, that negligence need not destroy quantum speedups: any function \(f\) can be transformed into a partial function \(f'\) whose negligent query complexity essentially preserves the quantum query complexity of \(f\). This gives partial functions with exponential quantum speedups even under negligent queries, and in particular rules out a general lower bound in terms of \(\fbs(f)\) for partial functions in this model.
  For two other models, we obtain general lower bounds in terms of \(\fbs(f)\) for all Boolean functions. For hybrid algorithms using \(Q\) coherent and \(C\) classical queries, we prove the tradeoff \(C+Q^2=Ω(\fbs(f))\). For an IID dephasing noisy model where each query dephases the query-index register at rate \(p\) independently, we prove \(Ω\!\left(p\,\fbs(f)\right)\) queries are necessary.
  Finally, we introduce a broader family of time-varying dephasing models and identify a variational resource that is always lower bounded by \(\fbs(f)\). Computing this resource reduces to a convex optimization problem, providing a simple way to derive lower bounds for new noise schedules. As applications, we recover the hybrid and IID dephasing bounds and determine the query complexity of unstructured search when the dephasing rate grows over time.

</details>


### [33] [Blind Unforgeability implies Plus-One Unforgeability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00517)
*Mehil Agarwal,Calliope S. Reimann,Fang Song*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper proves that quantum blind-unforgeability (BU) implies quantum plus-one unforgeability, showing BU is strictly stronger in the quantum setting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Blind-unforgeability and plus-one unforgeability are two quantum security notions for message authentication, and it was an open question whether the former implies the latter. Since the converse implication is known to fail, resolving this would pinpoint the exact relative strength of the two notions.

Method: The authors introduce a new smoothing technique that coherently rescales the query histories of a quantum-query algorithm in a black-box manner. This transforms any successful plus-one attacker into a blind-unforgeability attacker with only inverse-polynomial loss, replacing the earlier approach of merely extracting a useful query history.

Result: Blind-unforgeability is proven to imply plus-one unforgeability. Combined with the known fact that plus-one unforgeability does not imply blind-unforgeability, blind-unforgeability is strictly stronger than plus-one unforgeability in the quantum setting.

Conclusion: The open question is settled: quantum blind-unforgeability strictly implies quantum plus-one unforgeability, enabled by a conceptual shift toward coherently reshaping query-history interference rather than extracting a single useful history.

Abstract: We prove that quantum blind-unforgeability implies quantum plus-one unforgeability, settling an open question in the literature. Since plus-one unforgeability is known not to imply blind-unforgeability, our result establishes that blind-unforgeability is \emph{strictly stronger} than plus-one unforgeability in the quantum setting.
  The implication is proven using a new \emph{smoothing} technique that coherently rescales the query histories of a quantum-query algorithm in a black-box manner. This enables us to transform any successful plus-one attacker into a blind-unforgeability attacker with only inverse-polynomial loss. Our proof represents a conceptual shift from merely extracting a useful query history as in earlier attempts, which we show inevitably fails in general, to first coherently reshaping how query histories interfere.

</details>


### [34] [Near-Optimal Ground-State Preparation without Controlled Hamiltonian Evolutions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00520)
*Dhrumil Patel,Steven T. Flammia,Raul Garcia-Patron*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper shows that ground-state preparation, energy estimation, and property estimation can be achieved with near-optimal complexity using only uncontrolled Hamiltonian evolution, via a "spectral descent principle."


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing ground-state preparation algorithms attain near-optimal complexity but rely on block encodings or controlled Hamiltonian evolution. The open question is whether controlled Hamiltonian evolution is necessary or whether the same scaling is achievable in the weaker model with access only to uncontrolled evolution U_H and U_H†.

Method: Two algorithms are proposed that query H only through U_H and U_H† (with ‖H‖≤α_H and τ=Θ(1/α_H)). The first uses fresh copies of the input state, requiring Õ(α_H/(γη)) queries given ground-state weight ≥η and spectral gap ≥γ. The second uses coherent access to the input-state preparation unitary and its inverse, improving to Õ(α_H/(γ√η)). Both rely on the spectral descent principle, progressively descending the energy spectrum from the input state until target fidelity is reached.

Result: The second algorithm's Õ(α_H/(γ√η)) bound matches the known lower bound up to logarithmic factors, demonstrating that access to controlled Hamiltonian evolution cannot improve query complexity for ground-state preparation beyond logarithmic factors. The framework is extended to ground-state property and energy estimation with explicit complexity guarantees.

Conclusion: Controlled Hamiltonian evolution is not necessary for near-optimal ground-state preparation; uncontrolled evolution suffices, and the spectral descent principle provides a unified framework for ground-state preparation, property estimation, and energy estimation.

Abstract: Ground-state preparation, energy estimation, and property estimation are fundamental tasks in quantum simulation. Existing ground-state preparation algorithms achieve near-optimal complexity using block encodings or controlled Hamiltonian evolution, raising a natural question: is controlled Hamiltonian evolution necessary, or can the same scaling be attained in the weaker access model that provides access to only uncontrolled Hamiltonian evolution? We resolve this question by giving two algorithms that access the input Hamiltonian $H$ only through queries to $U_H$ and its inverse $U_H^\dagger$, where $\|H\|\leα_H$ and $τ=Θ(1/α_H)$. Given an input state with ground-state weight at least $η$ and a spectral gap of $H$ of at least $γ$, our first algorithm uses fresh copies of the input state and requires $\widetilde O(α_H/(γη))$ queries to $U_H$ and $U_H^\dagger$, while our second algorithm uses coherent access to the input-state preparation unitary and its inverse to improve this complexity to $\widetilde O(α_H/(γ\sqrtη))$. The latter bound matches the known lower bound up to logarithmic factors, showing that algorithms with additional access to controlled Hamiltonian evolution cannot improve the query complexity for ground-state preparation beyond logarithmic factors. Both algorithms are based on a simple mechanism that we call the spectral descent principle, in which the algorithm starts from the input state $|ψ\rangle$ and progressively descends through the energy spectrum, moving closer to the ground state until the desired fidelity is reached. We also extend this framework to ground-state property and energy estimation and give explicit complexity guarantees for both tasks.

</details>


### [35] [First-quantized quantum simulation with non-local potentials by matrix-product-state encoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00521)
*Kazuki Tsuoka,Taichi Kosugi,Masari Watanabe,Hirofumi Nishi,Yu-ichiro Matsushita*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes encoding non-local pseudopotentials via matrix product states (MPS) to efficiently implement Trotter step operators in first-quantized quantum simulation, achieving reduced circuit depth demonstrated on a 1D hydrogen ionization model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: First-quantized quantum simulation offers lower space and gate complexity than second-quantized approaches, and pseudopotentials further reduce the number of simulated electrons. However, implementing pseudopotentials in quantum circuits is difficult because they are non-local.

Method: MPS encoding is used to efficiently implement non-local potentials, allowing a Trotter step operator to be realized with circuit depth O(deg(V_loc) n^deg(V_loc) + 8L n n_atom χ² + 2n²), where deg(V_loc) is the degree of the polynomial local potential, n_atom is the number of atoms, L is the maximum number of projector functions per atom, and n is the number of qubits for the binary-encoded 1D wave function.

Result: Applied to a model of ionization of a one-dimensional hydrogen atom driven by an ultrashort laser pulse, MPS encoding significantly reduced the required circuit depth compared to direct unitary decomposition.

Conclusion: MPS encoding provides an efficient pathway for practical first-quantized quantum simulations with non-local pseudopotentials.

Abstract: Quantum simulations based on first-quantized approaches can offer lower space and gate complexities than second-quantized approaches. The gate requirements for these simulations can be further reduced by employing pseudopotentials, a technique widely used in classical quantum chemistry calculations to decrease the number of simulated electrons. However, implementing pseudopotentials in quantum circuits remains challenging due to their non-local nature. In this study, we proposed a method to efficiently implement non-local potentials using matrix product state (MPS) encoding. MPS encoding allows us to efficiently implement a Trotter step operator with a circuit depth of $O(\mathrm{deg}(V_{\mathrm{loc}}) n^{\mathrm{deg}(V_{\mathrm{loc}})} + 8Lnn_{\mathrm{atom}}χ^2 + 2n^2)$, where $\mathrm{deg}(V_{\mathrm{loc}})$ is the degree of the polynomial local potential, $n_{\mathrm{atom}}$ is the number of atoms in the system, $L$ is the maximum number of projector functions across all atoms, and $n$ is the number of qubits used to represent the binary-encoded wave function in one-dimensional space. We demonstrated the effectiveness of this approach by applying it to a model of the ionization of a one-dimensional hydrogen atom driven by an ultrashort laser pulse. Our results showed that MPS encoding significantly reduces the required circuit depth compared to a direct unitary decomposition, providing a pathway for practical first-quantized simulations.

</details>


### [36] [Good Quantum Locally Testable Codes from Lossless Cubical Complexes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00525)
*Itay Cohen,Itai Leigh,Assaf Reiner,Amnon Ta-Shma,Elad Tzalik*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper develops a higher-dimensional lossless expansion approach for constructing asymptotically good quantum locally testable codes (qLTCs), proving a local-to-global theorem for level chain complexes derived from high-dimensional cubical complexes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sipser and Spielman built LDPC codes from bipartite spectral or one-sided lossless expanders, and spectral expansion has been central to higher-dimensional constructions of good classical LTCs and qLDPC codes. Lin and Hsieh used two-dimensional lossless cubical complexes instead. This work aims to extend that lossless approach to higher dimensions, not by constructing the needed complexes but by studying what their existence would imply.

Method: The authors associate a level chain complex with a high-dimensional cubical complex, where chain groups are supported on level sets of the Boolean cube rather than on its cells. They then prove a clean local-to-global theorem: suitable one-dimensional lossless expansion in the directional graphs implies small-set coboundary expansion of the global level complex.

Result: Sufficiently imbalanced, two-sided lossless four-dimensional cubical complexes yield asymptotically good quantum locally testable codes. The main technical tool is the local-to-global principle.

Conclusion: The local-to-global principle developed here is expected to have further applications beyond the qLTC construction presented.

Abstract: Sipser and Spielman constructed LDPC codes from either bipartite \emph{spectral} expanders or one-sided \emph{lossless} expanders. In higher dimensions, \emph{spectral} expansion similarly played a central role in the constructions of asymptotically good classical LTCs and qLDPC codes by Dinur, Evra, Livne, Lubotzky, and Mozes and by Panteleev and Kalachev. Alternatively, Lin and Hsieh constructed classical LTCs and qLDPC codes from two-dimensional \emph{lossless} cubical complexes.
  In this work we develop the higher-dimensional \emph{lossless} approach. We do not construct the required high-dimensional lossless cubical complexes; rather, we investigate what their existence would imply. We associate with a high-dimensional cubical complex a \emph{level chain complex}, whose chain groups are supported on the level sets of the Boolean cube rather than on its cells. Our main technical contribution is a clean local-to-global theorem for this structure: suitable one-dimensional lossless expansion in the directional graphs implies small-set coboundary expansion of the global level complex. As a consequence, sufficiently imbalanced, two-sided lossless four-dimensional cubical complexes give rise to asymptotically good quantum locally testable codes. We expect the local-to-global principle developed here to have further applications.

</details>


### [37] [Polynomial-time local-unitary equivalence of graph states](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00527)
*Yuxuan Zhang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents a deterministic polynomial-time algorithm deciding local-unitary (LU) equivalence of graph states, resolving a decade-old open problem, with bit complexity Õ(n^6.38).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LU equivalence of graph states—whether two states differ only by independent single-qubit basis changes—had resisted a polynomial-time decision procedure for over ten years. Only a quasipolynomial algorithm (Claudet and Perdrix) was known, leaving the complexity of this fundamental equivalence problem unresolved.

Method: The authors build on Claudet and Perdrix's quasipolynomial algorithm, replacing the enumeration of vertex subsets with a compact constraint system generated from pairs and triples of vertices. The remaining graph transformation is recovered by solving linear equations over the binary field. These new steps cost Õ(n^5) bit operations, while the inherited graph preprocessing dominates the overall bound of Õ(n^6.38).

Result: The algorithm decides LU equivalence for graphs on n labelled vertices in Õ(n^6.38) bit operations and explicitly constructs the exact single-qubit unitaries whenever equivalence holds. It also counts the local-Clifford (LC) classes within any LU class, showing their number is a power of two computable within the same bound. This decides whether single-qubit Clifford gates reach every graph state in a given LU class and yields a counterexample otherwise. The method further decides LU equivalence of stabilizer codes encoding one logical qubit.

Conclusion: LU equivalence of graph states is decidable in deterministic polynomial time, closing a long-standing open question and extending the result to LC-class counting and stabilizer-code equivalence.

Abstract: Local-unitary (LU) equivalence asks whether two quantum states differ only by independent changes of basis on their qubits. For graph states, whether this relation can be decided in polynomial time has remained open for over a decade. We give a deterministic algorithm that decides LU equivalence for graphs on $n$ labelled vertices in $\widetilde O(n^{6.38})$ bit operations and constructs exact single-qubit unitaries whenever the states are equivalent. Building on Claudet and Perdrix's quasipolynomial algorithm, we replace the enumeration of vertex subsets by a compact system of constraints generated from pairs and triples. The remaining graph transformation is found by solving linear equations over the binary field. These new steps cost $\widetilde O(n^5)$ bit operations; the inherited graph preprocessing sets the overall bound. We also count the local-Clifford (LC) classes of graph states within any LU class: their number is a power of two, computable within the same bound. For any given graph state, this decides whether single-qubit Clifford gates reach every graph state in its LU class, and supplies a counterexample when they do not. The method also decides LU equivalence of stabilizer codes encoding one logical qubit.

</details>


### [38] [Quantum Krylov Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00537)
*Shunji Matsuura,Yoji Kawamura,Joseph Salfi,Satoshi Iso*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces Quantum Krylov Learning (QKL), a restricted-access framework that reconstructs model-independent spectral information about an unknown quantum system from single-temperature probe autocorrelators via operator Krylov structure, using only free evolution, probe preparation, and measurement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hamiltonians of natural quantum systems are typically inferred indirectly via model fitting, but direct extraction is preferable. In quantum sensing and boundary spectroscopy, only controllable probes access the system, and existing Hamiltonian learning protocols under such restricted access demand special symmetries, known parametric forms, or controlled operations beyond free evolution.

Method: QKL uses single temperature probe autocorrelators and their operator Krylov structure to extract model-independent spectral information. Instead of fitting a parametrized Hamiltonian, it reconstructs the operator Krylov Jacobi matrix representing dynamical modes accessible to the probe, assuming the unobserved system is at or near infinite temperature. The Jacobi matrix is reconstructed from the autocorrelation of a probe observable without oracle access to the Hamiltonian, requiring only free evolution under unknown H plus probe state preparation and measurement—no symmetry, parametric model, or controlled-unitary gates.

Result: The authors prove a Lieb--Robinson bound for the operator Krylov chain and derive a data-driven criterion for the required observation window. They also establish an end-to-end sample complexity guarantee: under generic conditions on spectral gaps and weights, the number of measurement shots needed to achieve spectral precision epsilon is polynomial in 1/epsilon.

Conclusion: QKL provides a symmetry-free, model-independent, controlled-operation-free approach to learning spectral properties of restricted-access quantum systems, with rigorous guarantees on observation window selection and sample complexity.

Abstract: The Hamiltonian of a natural quantum system is usually determined indirectly, by proposing microscopic models and comparing their predicted observables with experiment. A more direct approach is to extract information about the Hamiltonian from measurements on the system itself. In many settings, however, direct access to the full system is unavailable and measurements can be performed only through controllable probes, as in quantum sensing and boundary spectroscopy. Existing Hamiltonian learning protocols under such restricted access typically require a special symmetry, a known parametric form, or controlled operations beyond free evolution. We introduce Quantum Krylov Learning (QKL), a restricted-access framework that avoids these requirements. QKL uses single temperature probe autocorrelators and their operator Krylov structure to extract model-independent spectral information about the unknown system. Rather than fitting a parametrized Hamiltonian, it reconstructs the operator Krylov Jacobi matrix representing the dynamical modes accessible to the probe. The method assumes that the unobserved system is at, or near, infinite temperature. Under this condition, the Jacobi matrix can be reconstructed directly from the autocorrelation of a probe observable, without oracle access to the Hamiltonian. The required operations are free evolution under the unknown $H$, together with probe state preparation and measurement. The learning core therefore requires no symmetry, no parametric model, and no controlled-unitary gates. We show a Lieb--Robinson bound for the operator Krylov chain and derive a data-driven criterion for the required observation window. We also establish an end-to-end sample complexity guarantee: under generic conditions on the spectral gaps and weights, the number of measurement shots required to achieve spectral precision $\varepsilon$ is polynomial in $1/\varepsilon$.

</details>


### [39] [Unifying and Extending Strong Simulation of Quantum Circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00547)
*Floris Geerts,Rihan Hai,Matthias Lanzinger,Reinhard Pichler,Emanuel Sallinger,Daniel Unterberger*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper unifies exact classical quantum-circuit simulation under functional aggregate queries (FAQs), recovering treewidth, rank-width, and Clifford tractability, and introduces a new criterion, tensor layout symmetry width.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical simulation of quantum circuits has yielded many seemingly distinct tractability results (structural bounds like treewidth, algebraic/rank-width bounds, and special cases like Clifford circuits). The paper seeks a single unifying language that explains and connects these separate simulation guarantees, and that can systematically reveal new tractable regimes.

Method: Circuits are encoded as sum-product FAQ queries: gates become factors, internal wire variables are aggregated, and free boundary variables index transition amplitudes. Simulation is performed via an InsideOut evaluation scheme whose cost depends on the evaluation plan, the chosen semiring, and the representation of intermediate factors. Distinct tractability sources are exploited within this common scheme: explicit factor representations recover tensor-network treewidth bounds and finer sparsity-sensitive bounds via fractional covers; a formal phase semiring with compressed intermediate factors recovers rank-width-based simulation for compatible quadratic phase representations; for Clifford circuits, affine-quadratic factors are shown to be closed under multiplication and marginalization and remain polynomial in size. The framework also defines tensor layout symmetry width, combining local cut-rank with separator symmetry via exact tree-tensor representations, with a constructive evaluation bound.

Result: FAQ evaluation recovers the treewidth bound for tensor-network contraction and yields finer sparsity-sensitive fractional-cover bounds. Over the phase semiring, it recovers rank-width-based simulation for compatible quadratic phase representations. For Clifford circuits, it yields polynomial-time exact amplitude computation with no bounded-width assumption. The new parameter, tensor layout symmetry width, admits a constructive evaluation bound, and a circuit family is exhibited with bounded tensor layout symmetry width but unbounded phase-graph rank-width and circuit line-graph treewidth.

Conclusion: Representation-aware FAQ evaluation provides a common algorithmic foundation for exact classical simulation of quantum circuits and a systematic route to discovering new tractable simulation regimes.

Abstract: We establish functional aggregate queries (FAQs) as a unifying language for exact classical simulation of quantum circuits. A circuit becomes a sum-product query: factors encode gates, internal wire variables are aggregated, and free boundary variables index transition amplitudes. The central insight is that distinct sources of simulation tractability can be exploited within the same InsideOut evaluation scheme. The query specifies what is computed; the evaluation plan, semiring, and representation of intermediate factors determine the cost.
  This view unifies structural and algebraic simulation guarantees. With explicit factor representations, FAQ evaluation recovers the treewidth bound for tensor-network contraction and yields finer sparsity-sensitive bounds via fractional covers. Over a formal phase semiring, compressed intermediate factors recover rank-width-based simulation for compatible quadratic phase representations. For Clifford circuits, affine-quadratic factors are closed under multiplication and marginalization and remain polynomial in size, yielding polynomial-time exact amplitude computation without any bounded-width assumption.
  Beyond these recoveries, the framework yields a new tractability criterion: tensor layout symmetry width. This parameter combines local cut-rank with separator symmetry through exact tree-tensor representations. We give a constructive evaluation bound and exhibit a circuit family with bounded tensor layout symmetry width but unbounded phase-graph rank-width and circuit line-graph treewidth. These results establish representation-aware FAQ evaluation as a common algorithmic foundation for classical simulation and a systematic route to new tractable regimes.

</details>


### [40] [A polynomial-time classical sampler for noisy quantum circuits from statistical mechanics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00548)
*Jon Nelson,Joel Rajakumar,Chao Yin,Yifan F. Zhang,Michael J. Gullans*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proves that geometrically local circuits with unital gates and single-qubit depolarizing noise of strength p can be classically sampled in polynomial time once depth reaches Ω(p⁻¹ log p⁻¹), independent of system size—relaxing prior logarithmic-depth-in-system-size requirements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical simulation of noisy quantum circuits is key to understanding the limits of quantum advantage. Prior classical sampling algorithms for general circuits only worked when circuit depth grew logarithmically with system size, since noise then drove the global output state near a trivial state. The authors aim to show that much shallower, system-size-independent depths suffice.

Method: They map the output state of a geometrically local circuit—composed of unital operations interspersed with single-qubit depolarizing noise of strength p—to a polymer model in statistical mechanics, then combine a convergent cluster expansion with the hypercontractivity of the depolarizing channel to establish approximate classical samplability.

Result: They prove that any such geometrically local circuit with depolarizing noise strength p after depth Ω(p⁻¹ log p⁻¹) can be approximately sampled from by a polynomial-time classical computer, with the required depth independent of system size.

Conclusion: Local noise accumulation at system-size-independent depth is already sufficient for efficient classical sampling of noisy local circuits, tightening the boundary between classically simulable and quantum-advantage regimes.

Abstract: Developing classical simulation algorithms for noisy quantum circuits is essential to delineating the limits of quantum advantage. Existing classical sampling approaches for general circuits require circuit depths to grow logarithmically with system size, so that noise drives the global output state close to a trivial state. Here we show that local accumulation of noise at a depth independent of system size is already sufficient. Specifically, we prove that any geometrically local circuit composed of unital operations and interspersed with single-qubit depolarizing noise of strength $p$ after $Ω(p^{-1} \log p^{-1})$ depth can be approximately sampled from a polynomial-time classical computer. Our proof maps the output state of the circuit to a polymer model in statistical mechanics, and combines a convergent cluster expansion with hypercontractivity of the depolarizing channel.

</details>


### [41] [Optimal two-mode bosonic loss codes from finite group symmetry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00561)
*Argyris Giannisis Manes,Mahadevan Subramanian,Liang Jiang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Photon loss dominates noise in bosonic quantum hardware. The authors study fixed-total-photon-number qubit encodings in two bosonic modes and discover finite-group structure in optimal codes, deriving general Knill-Laflamme conditions for finite-group-invariant codes and constructing a conjectured infinite family attaining every loss distance at minimum photon number.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Photon loss is the dominant noise process in bosonic quantum hardware such as superconducting cavities. Fixed-total-photon-number encodings in two bosonic modes preserve the loss-detection advantage of dual-rail qubits while also enabling photon loss correction, making them attractive for hardware-efficient bosonic quantum error correction. However, optimal encoders and decoders in this setting were not well understood, and the structure underlying numerically found best codes was unclear.

Method: The authors optimize entanglement fidelity over arbitrary encoders and decoders for total photon numbers 4 ≤ n ≤ 25, revealing finite-group structure in every best-found code. Motivated by this, they derive necessary-and-sufficient Knill-Laflamme conditions for arbitrary finite-group-invariant codes, reducing construction to equations on representation multiplicity spaces. For every finite subgroup of SU(2) and every tensor rank, they explicitly construct the minimum number of operators needed to impose all loss-correction constraints. They also use exact MacWilliams-Farkas certificates, prove a monotonicity of the distance bound in total photon number, and identify a binary-polyhedral sequence with n_d = ⌈(3d²+1)/4⌉.

Result: The numerical optimization finds that of the best codes, 11 correspond to two-dimensional irreducible representations and 11 to reducible ones. The analytic constructions reproduce most numerical codes and predict constructions missed by the initial search. Exact MacWilliams-Farkas certificates show that the codes achieve maximum possible loss distance in 21 of 22 sectors, and the distance bound is proven monotone in total photon number. The constructed (n,d) = (28,6), (49,8), (76,10) codes give the smallest reported n for their respective distances, and all members through d = 9 attain the fixed-n LP distance bound.

Conclusion: Finite-group structure emerges in optimal fixed-total-photon-number bosonic encodings, enabling analytic construction of loss-correcting codes. The combination of explicit constructions, exact certificates, and symmetry-reduced constraint counting provides evidence for an infinite code family conjectured to attain every distance at the minimum photon number.

Abstract: Photon loss is a dominant noise process in bosonic quantum hardware, including superconducting cavities. Fixed-total-photon-number qubit encodings in two bosonic modes retain the loss-detection advantage of dual-rail qubits while supporting photon loss correction. Optimizing entanglement fidelity in this setting for $4\leq n\leq25$ over arbitrary encoders and decoders reveals finite-group structure in every best-found code: 11 correspond to two-dimensional irreducible representations and 11 to reducible ones. Motivated by this emergence, we derive the necessary-and-sufficient Knill-Laflamme conditions for arbitrary finite-group-invariant codes, reducing their construction to equations on representation multiplicity spaces. For every finite subgroup of $SU(2)$ and every tensor rank, we explicitly construct the minimum number of operators required to impose all corresponding loss-correction constraints. These results produce analytic counterparts to most numerical codes and predict constructions missed by the initial search. Exact MacWilliams-Farkas certificates show that our codes achieve the maximum possible loss distance in 21 of the 22 sectors, and we prove that the resulting distance bound is monotone in total photon number. Beyond the scan, we identify a binary-polyhedral sequence with photon number $n_d=\lceil(3d^2+1)/4\rceil$ and construct each corresponding code through distance $d=10$. To our knowledge, the constructed $(n,d)=(28,6),(49,8),(76,10)$ codes give the smallest reported $n$ for their respective distances; all members through $d=9$ attain the fixed-$n$ LP distance bound. Together, these constructions, certificates, and a symmetry-reduced constraint count provide evidence for an infinite code family conjectured to attain every distance at the minimum photon number.

### [42] [Characterizing unitaries via quasi-process functions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00579)

TL;DR: Every n-qubit unitary has a canonical "quasi-process function unitary" (qPFU) representation via its controlled single-qubit decomposition.   <summary>Details</summary>

Abstract: It is well-known that every n-qubit unitary can be decomposed into a product of controlled single-qubit unitaries. We show that the control conditions in such a decomposition naturally define a quasi-process function, an object that originally came up in the study of indefinite causal order. Here quasi-process functions yield a representation of every n-qubit unitary in a canonical form, namely, as a quasi-process function unitary (qPFU). We use this representation to show how quantum nonlocality without entanglement (QNLWE) can be understood in terms of unitaries that encode the trade-off between causal order and locality noted in R. Kunjwal and Ä. Baumeler, arXiv:2202.00440. Specifically, we obtain two sufficient (but not necessary) criteria---mutual exclusivity of control conditions (MECC) and Unambiguity---for a unitary to be a process function unitary (PFU), i.e., it admits a qPFU representation where the quasi-process function is a process function, an object that can model paradox-free classical causal loops. We also show the inequivalence of these criteria, neither implying the other. We then show that the circuit depth of MECC unitaries admits a characterization in terms of clique-partitioning problems on Hamming graphs and use this correspondence to provide bounds on the circuit depth of n-qubit MECC unitaries. Finally, we show how MECC unitaries provide a recipe for constructing a unitary purification of the associated process function, thereby providing a large class of unitary processes of interest in quantum causality. The generality of this causality-inspired representation of unitaries makes it a versatile tool for analyzing the structure of quantum circuits in other domains.

### [43] [Quantum key distribution using generalized contextuality against post-quantum eavesdroppers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00595)
*Daniel Centeno,Roberto D. Baldijão,Maria Ciudad Alañón,Yujie Zhang,Pedro Lauand,Elie Wolfe*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper investigates whether generalized contextuality in prepare-and-measure experiments can certify secret keys analogously to Bell nonlocality, showing contextuality is necessary but not sufficient for a positive key rate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bell nonlocality enables device-independent secret key certification, even against eavesdroppers restricted only by the no-signaling principle. The authors ask whether generalized contextuality, a form of nonclassicality accessible in prepare-and-measure scenarios on a single system, can provide a similar cryptographic resource.

Method: Alice and Bob establish operational equivalences among their preparation and measurement procedures, treated as theory-independent constraints that any physical intervention on the channel (including an adversary's) must respect. Eve is unrestricted and not assumed to be quantum. The optimal guessing probability against individual attacks is formulated as a finite linear program, yielding a worst-case asymptotic key rate from observed statistics and operational equivalences alone.

Result: Contextuality is proven necessary for a positive key rate but not sufficient. The (3,2)-parity-oblivious random access code, which yields secret key against a quantum eavesdropper, yields no certifiable key rate against a post-quantum one. Conversely, operational equivalences with no analogue among no-signaling constraints can strictly increase the asymptotic key rate, illustrated via the prepare-and-measure version of the standard CHSH protocol with an aligned key-generating setting.

Conclusion: Generalized contextuality plays a nuanced role in cryptography: it is a necessary but insufficient condition for certifiable secret key. The framework delineates when contextuality-based operational equivalences can and cannot support secure key generation against post-quantum adversaries.

Abstract: Bell nonlocality allows secret key to be certified without trusting the internal operation of the devices, and even against eavesdroppers constrained only by the no-signaling principle. We ask whether generalized contextuality, a notion of nonclassicality available in prepare-and-measure experiments on a single system, can play an analogous role. In our approach, Alice and Bob first establish operational equivalences among their local preparation and measurement procedures, which are then treated as theory-independent constraints that any physical intervention on the communication channel, including that of an adversary, must respect. Eve is otherwise unrestricted, that is, her intervention on the transmitted system is not assumed to be described by quantum theory. Within this adversarial model, we show that Eve's optimal guessing probability against individual attacks is the solution of a finite linear program, and hence that a worst-case asymptotic key rate follows from the observed statistics and the operational equivalences alone. We prove that contextuality is necessary for a positive key rate. However, we also show that it is not sufficient. Concretely, the (3,2)-parity-oblivious random access code, known to yield secret key against a quantum eavesdropper, yields none certifiable key rate against a post-quantum one. Conversely, operational equivalences with no analogue among no-signaling constraints can strictly increase the asymptotic key rate. We illustrate this with the prepare-and-measure version of the standard CHSH protocol with an aligned key-generating setting.

</details>


### [44] [Late-Time Power Laws in Fluorescence Decays: Quantum Decay or Alternative Mechanisms?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00603)
*Francesco Giacosa,Anna Kolbus,Krzysztof Kyziol,Magdalena Plodowska,Milena Piotrowska,Karol Szary,Arthur Vereijken*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work tests whether the late-time power law in survival probability P(t), predicted by quantum mechanics for unstable systems, can be explained by alternative nonextensive, continuous-lifetime, or energy-migration models, using time-resolved fluorescence spectroscopy data from erythrosine B and eosine Y. The fitted statistical and energy-migration parametrizations fail to reproduce key quantum features, notably the sharp exponential-to-power-law transition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The quantum-mechanical prediction of a late-time power law in the survival probability of an unstable state can also arise from unrelated phenomena (nonextensive statistics, continuous lifetime distributions, and energy-migration models in fluorescence). This ambiguity motivates confronting these alternative interpretations against real spectroscopic data to determine whether they can replicate the quantum-mechanical signatures.

Method: Time-resolved fluorescence spectroscopy experiments were performed with erythrosine B and eosine Y as fluorophores. The measured survival probability decays were fitted with specific nonextensive statistical parametrizations and energy-migration parametrizations, and the resulting fits were compared with the features expected under the quantum-mechanical description.

Result: The fitted statistical and energy-migration parametrizations cannot reproduce some of the features expected within the quantum-mechanical description, in particular the sharp transition from the exponential regime to the power-law regime.

Conclusion: Alternative statistical and energy-migration interpretations do not adequately account for the observed decay behavior, supporting the quantum-mechanical description of the late-time power law in these fluorophore systems, at least regarding the sharp exponential-to-power-law crossover.

Abstract: In quantum mechanics, the properties of an unstable system are closely related to its corresponding energy distribution. One of the predictions of the theory is the emergence of a late-time power law in the survival probability $P(t)$ of the unstable state. However, in complex systems, similar effects may emerge due to other phenomena. This is the case for nonextensive statistical models, for some continuous lifetime distribution models, and also for certain energy-migration models applied to fluorescence. In this work, these alternative interpretations are confronted with data from time-resolved fluorescence spectroscopy experiments with erythrosine B and eosine Y as fluorophores. It turns out that some of the features expected within the quantum-mechanical description, such as the sharp transition from the exponential to the power-law regime, cannot be reproduced by the specific statistical and energy-migration parametrizations fitted in the analysis.

</details>


### [45] [Engineering non-ergodic properties in two dimensional quantum many-body systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00635)
*Nyayabanta Swain,Gabriel Lemarié,Shaffique Adam*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Using an eigenstate-to-Hamiltonian construction, the authors engineer two 2D square-lattice spin Hamiltonians (axial vs. diagonal interaction geometry) from a common parent model, showing that geometry controls Hilbert-space structure and yields a crossover from ergodicity to non-ergodicity (axial) versus Hilbert-space fragmentation and scars (diagonal).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Non-ergodic quantum many-body dynamics can sustain quantum coherence far from equilibrium, but most known examples are found via intuition, analogy, or numerical search rather than systematic design. There is a lack of general methods for engineering robust non-ergodic dynamics in disorder-free interacting systems, particularly in higher dimensions where enhanced connectivity typically disfavors ergodicity breaking.

Method: An eigenstate-to-Hamiltonian construction approach is used to systematically engineer 2D quantum spin Hamiltonians with tunable non-ergodic properties. Starting from a common parent model and input eigenstate, two target Hamiltonians on the square lattice are constructed that differ in interaction geometry: axial versus diagonal. The resulting interaction geometry and coupling pattern are then related to Hilbert-space structure and dynamics.

Result: The axial model forms a connected Hilbert-space network and exhibits an anisotropy-driven crossover from ergodic to non-ergodic behavior via suppression of resonances. The diagonal model exhibits Hilbert-space fragmentation and, within its largest irreducible sector, signatures of quantum many-body scar dynamics, including towers of highly localized, low-entanglement eigenstates and long-lived coherent revivals.
