Conclusion: The work establishes a direct link between Hamiltonian design, Hilbert-space network structure, and emergent non-ergodic dynamics, offering a systematic route to engineering non-ergodic behavior in disorder-free higher-dimensional quantum many-body systems.

Abstract: Non-ergodic quantum many-body dynamics offers a route to persistent quantum coherence far from equilibrium, beyond the conventional expectations of thermalizing statistical mechanics. Most known examples have been identified through intuition, analogy, or numerical search, rather than by systematic Hamiltonian design. This leaves few general methods for engineering robust non-ergodic dynamics in disorder-free interacting quantum many-body systems, especially in higher dimensions where enhanced connectivity generally disfavors ergodicity breaking. Here, we use an eigenstate-to-Hamiltonian construction approach to systematically engineer two-dimensional quantum spin Hamiltonians with tunable non-ergodic properties. Starting from a common parent model and input eigenstate, we construct two target Hamiltonians on the square lattice that differ in their interaction geometry: axial versus diagonal. We show that the resulting interaction geometry and coupling pattern control the Hilbert-space structure and dynamics. The axial model forms a connected Hilbert-space network and exhibits an anisotropy-driven crossover from ergodic to non-ergodic behavior through the suppression of resonances. In contrast, the diagonal model exhibits Hilbert-space fragmentation and, within its largest irreducible sector, signatures of quantum many-body scar dynamics including towers of highly localized, low-entanglement eigenstates, and long-lived coherent revivals. These results establish a direct link between Hamiltonian design, Hilbert-space network structure, and emergent non-ergodic dynamics.

</details>


### [46] [Operator-language Feynman rules for driven-dissipative quantum systems: from mean field to non-Gaussian photon correlations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00667)
*Peter Ehlers,Phi Hung Nguyen,Daniel Soh*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper develops Feynman rules for Lindblad master equations in quantum optics, splitting the perturbation expansion into a kinematic shift algebra on Wick-ordered eigenoperators and a dynamical resolvent of the Gaussian unperturbed generator, then applies them to driven Kerr cavities, driven two-level atoms, Kerr rings/chains, and photon counting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Perturbation theory for open quantum systems governed by Lindblad master equations lacks a systematic, operator-level diagrammatic framework analogous to standard Feynman rules. The authors aim to provide such rules that exploit exactly solvable (mostly Gaussian) generators and remain efficient for many-body and counting problems.

Method: They construct a fixed algebra of elementary operator moves and closed-form propagators for perturbation theory about an exactly solvable generator. The expansion separates into (i) a kinematic part—the shift algebra on Wick-ordered eigenoperators O_mn = :(c†)^m c^n:, from which the vertex table for a given interaction is assembled—and (ii) a dynamical part entering only through the resolvent of the unperturbed generator, which is diagonal and additive for any stable, diagonalizable Gaussian L0, including thermal/squeezed reservoirs, correlated decay, and two-level emitters. The rules are extended to a counting-field-tilted generator for photon-counting cumulants.

Result: In the driven Kerr cavity the loop expansion coincides with the semiclassical expansion and the series is asymptotic. In a strongly driven two-level atom, dressed lines replace a finite-radius series with one accurate deep into saturation, one line per Mollow component. For Kerr rings and chains the cost is polynomial in the number of sites at fixed perturbative order. On a three-site ring the diagrams recover the non-Gaussian part of the three-photon correlation missed by the Gaussian cumulant closure; to tenth order they yield the smallest g^(3) error among compared methods for U ≤ 0.25κ at a cost below a fourth-order closure, though third- and fourth-order closures are more accurate at moderate coupling. The extended counting-field rules give photon-counting cumulants of a disordered eight-site chain beyond direct diagonalization; quantum-jump trajectories at three couplings agree to within 1.6 standard errors, within one at the strongest coupling, where they place the third cumulant six standard errors above the Gaussian value.

Conclusion: The proposed Feynman rules provide a systematic, scalable diagrammatic perturbation theory for Lindblad master equations, unifying kinematic and dynamical structure, matching or exceeding existing closures in accuracy and cost, and extending efficiently to many-body photon-counting statistics.

Abstract: We construct Feynman rules for Lindblad master equations in the operator language of quantum optics: a fixed algebra of elementary operator moves and closed-form propagators for perturbation theory about an exactly solvable, in most cases Gaussian, generator. The expansion separates into a kinematic part (the shift algebra on the Wick-ordered eigenoperators $O_{mn}=:(c^\dagger)^m c^n:$, from which the vertex table for a specified interaction is assembled) and a dynamical part that enters only through the resolvent of the unperturbed generator, diagonal and additive for any stable, diagonalizable Gaussian $L_0$, including thermal and squeezed reservoirs and correlated decay, and equally available for a two-level emitter, driven or undriven. In the driven Kerr cavity the loop expansion is the semiclassical expansion and the series is asymptotic. In a strongly driven two-level atom, dressed lines replace a series of finite radius by one accurate deep into saturation, one line per Mollow component. For Kerr rings and chains the cost is polynomial in the number of sites at fixed perturbative order. On a three-site ring the diagrams recover the non-Gaussian part of the three-photon correlation that the Gaussian cumulant closure lacks; carried to tenth order they give the smallest $g^{(3)}$ error of the methods compared for $U\leq0.25κ$ at a cost below that of a fourth-order closure, and third- and fourth-order closures are more accurate at moderate coupling. The same rules, extended to the counting-field-tilted generator, give the photon-counting cumulants of a disordered eight-site chain beyond direct diagonalization; quantum-jump trajectories at three couplings agree with them to within $1.6$ standard errors, within one at the strongest coupling, where they place the third cumulant six standard errors above the Gaussian value.

</details>


### [47] [Quantum Circuit Pruning: From NISQ Architectures to Fault-Tolerant Operations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00669)
*Pau Escofet,Carmen G. Almudéver,Sergi Abadal,Eduard Alarcón*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A routing-aware pruning strategy removes parametric two-qubit gates whose fidelity cost of execution (including routing overhead) exceeds their computational contribution, yielding gate count reductions up to 48.6% and fidelity improvements up to 47.7% on NISQ, with feasibility indicated for FTQC.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Two-qubit gates are the dominant source of error and routing overhead in quantum circuits, especially parametric ones whose small rotation angles contribute little computation yet incur full execution costs. Existing pruning methods are architecture-agnostic and ignore routing and hardware noise, so they miss the trade-off between a gate's computational value and its true execution cost.

Method: Each parametric two-qubit gate is evaluated by comparing an architecture-independent lower bound on the fidelity cost of omitting the gate against a hardware-specific model of the routing execution cost, separately for NISQ and FTQC settings; the pruning boundary is analytically characterized as a function of rotation angle, qubit distance, and hardware noise.

Result: Simulations on benchmark circuits show gate count reductions up to 48.6% and fidelity improvements up to 47.7% in the NISQ regime; a feasibility analysis indicates pruning remains relevant across a range of code distances and physical error rates for FT hardware.

Conclusion: Routing-aware pruning serves as a practical, unified compilation strategy applicable across the quantum computing stack, from NISQ to fault-tolerant architectures.

Abstract: We present a routing-aware pruning strategy for quantum circuits that selectively removes parametric two-qubit gates whose computational contribution is outweighed by the fidelity cost of their execution, and assess it across both Noisy Intermediate-Scale Quantum (NISQ) and fault-tolerant quantum computing (FTQC) architectures. Each two-qubit gate of a circuit is evaluated by comparing an architecture-independent lower bound on the fidelity cost of omitting the gate against a hardware-specific model for the routing execution cost in each setting. We additionally provide an analytical characterization of the pruning boundary as a function of rotation angle, qubit distance, and hardware noise. Simulations on benchmark circuits demonstrate gate count reductions of up to 48.6% and fidelity improvements of up to 47.7% in the NISQ regime, while a feasibility analysis on FT hardware indicates that pruning is expected to remain relevant across a range of code distances and physical error rates. These results establish routing-aware pruning as a practical and unified compilation strategy across the quantum computing stack.

</details>


### [48] [Beyond Feasibility: Finite-Depth Accessibility in Constrained QAOA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00736)
*Rushikesh Ubale,Gregory T. Byrd,Yasar Mulani,Sangram Deshpande*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces a finite-depth accessible set framework for constrained QAOA, showing that reachable objective quality—not just feasibility—predicts shallow-circuit performance with correlations up to 0.81.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Constraint-preserving mixers guarantee QAOA stays in a feasible subspace, but feasibility and global mixer connectivity alone do not reveal which feasible configurations a shallow circuit can actually reach. The authors target this gap by characterizing accessibility at the level of the executed circuit.

Method: The paper defines the finite-depth accessible set R_p(M, x_0) for a specified feasible initial state, finite depth, and finite mixer schedule, and shows the computational-basis support of the ideal QAOA circuit lies within it. From this it derives a parameter-independent objective ceiling and a normalized reachable-quality diagnostic Q_R. The framework is evaluated on constrained portfolio optimization and synthetic block-constrained quadratic problems using resource-matched block-local XY mixer schedules, with additional controlled and dependence-aware analyses.

Result: Across 207 favorable-tail CVaR QAOA configurations, Q_R shows the strongest association with realized shallow-QAOA performance among structural diagnostics, with Pearson r = 0.7003 and Spearman rho = 0.8057; the relationship remains positive in controlled and dependence-aware analyses and persists across feasible initialization, portfolio size, and objective family. A compilation-only study finds that phase-separator routing dominates the compiled two-qubit resource scale for dense portfolio Hamiltonians, and mixer schedules with comparable two-qubit cost can differ markedly in finite-depth accessible quality.

Conclusion: Objective-relevant finite-depth accessibility, captured by Q_R, is a complementary diagnostic for resource-bounded constrained QAOA, complementing feasibility and global connectivity criteria.

Abstract: Constraint-preserving mixers keep QAOA within a feasible subspace, but feasibility and global mixer connectivity do not determine which feasible configurations are available to a particular shallow circuit. We study constrained QAOA at the level of the circuit actually executed: a specified feasible initial state, finite depth, and finite schedule of mixer interactions. We define the finite-depth accessible set $R_p(M,x_0)$ and show that the computational-basis support of the ideal QAOA circuit is contained within it. This gives a parameter-independent objective ceiling $f_R^\star=\max_{x\in R_p}f(x)$ and a normalized reachable-quality diagnostic $Q_R$, separating accessible volume from the objective quality contained within it.
  We evaluate this framework using resource-matched block-local XY mixer schedules for constrained portfolio optimization and synthetic block-constrained quadratic problems. Across 207 favorable-tail CVaR QAOA configurations, $Q_R$ shows the strongest association with realized shallow-QAOA performance among the structural diagnostics considered, with Pearson and Spearman correlations of $0.7003$ and $0.8057$; the relationship remains positive under controlled and dependence-aware analyses. The distinction persists across changes in feasible initialization, portfolio size, and objective family.
  A compilation-only study further shows that, for the dense portfolio Hamiltonians considered here, phase-separator routing dominates the compiled two-qubit resource scale, while mixer schedules with comparable compiled two-qubit cost can exhibit markedly different finite-depth accessible quality. These results motivate objective-relevant finite-depth accessibility as a complementary diagnostic for resource-bounded constrained QAOA.

</details>


### [49] [Negative quasiprobabilities redistribute (and complex ones reduce) the information in unabsorbed photons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00740)
*Jonte R. Hance*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper challenges the claim that negative Kirkwood-Dirac quasiprobabilities enable quantum advantage in interaction-free measurement and postselected metrology. It shows that for a single photon, no interferometer or readout can beat direct transmission in Fisher information per absorbed photon, and negativity offers no advantage beyond concentrating information in weakly lit outputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Negative Kirkwood-Dirac quasiprobabilities have been credited with enabling quantum advantages in interaction-free measurement and postselected metrology. The authors aim to rigorously test this claim on the canonical task these tools were designed for: learning about a sample while minimizing absorption of light.

Method: The authors analyze the Kirkwood-Dirac distribution of path and output for a pure single photon passing once through an arbitrary interferometer. They compute the Fisher information about sample transmittance per absorbed photon, using the real part of the KD distribution (responding to absorption) and the imaginary part (responding to phase).

Result: For a single photon, the Fisher information per absorbed photon equals the direct-transmission value minus a sum of squared imaginary parts of the KD distribution—so no interferometer or readout beats direct transmission. Since the imaginary parts depend on the output basis (not the sample), a basis exists where every term is real, which is optimal. Additional information per absorbed photon arises only via phase response (requiring a thick or inhomogeneously broadened sample) or via many passes when absorptions go uncounted (e.g., a critically coupled resonator).

Conclusion: Negativity in the Kirkwood-Dirac distribution provides no genuine metrological advantage: it only concentrates information in weakly lit outputs and does not accumulate over multiple trials. A true single-shot advantage that does not accumulate comes from negativity, but accumulated advantage instead requires outputs that are dark without the object—a case that needs no negativity.

Abstract: Negative Kirkwood--Dirac quasiprobabilities are credited with the quantum advantage of interaction-free measurement and postselected metrology. We test that claim on the task interaction-free measurement was designed for: learning about a sample letting it absorb as little light as possible. The Kirkwood--Dirac distribution of path and output gives the linear response to a weak sample: its real part responds to absorption, its imaginary part to a phase. For a pure single photon passing once through any interferometer, the Fisher information about transmittance per absorbed photon is the direct-transmission value minus a sum of squared imaginary parts: no interferometer or readout beats direct transmission. Negativity plays no role beyond concentrating information in weakly lit outputs. The imaginary parts of the Kirkwood-Dirac distribution depend on the output basis here, not on the sample, so a basis exists in which every term is real, which is optimal. A single photon gains information per absorbed photon only through a phase response, which for an optical line needs a thick or inhomogeneously broadened sample, or through many passes when absorptions go uncounted, as shown by a critically coupled resonator. For detecting a weak absorber, negativity gives a single-shot advantage that does not accumulate over multiple trials; advantage instead accumulates when there are outputs which are dark without the object, a case which needs no negativity.

</details>


### [50] [esQueranto: Differentiable Structured Quantum Light for Automated Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00750)
*Marcello Armezzani,Tareq Jaouni,Pontus Lindgren,Sören Arlt,Mario Krenn,Xuemei Gu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces esQueranto, a differentiable JAX-based simulator unifying photon-number quantum optics with structured-light propagation to enable AI-driven automated experiment design in physics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Experiment design has historically been human-driven, but AI and computational methods now enable automated scientific discovery. This paradigm requires simulators expressive enough to represent diverse physical processes and couplings from which new experiments can be constructed.

Method: The authors develop esQueranto, a differentiable software implemented in JAX that brings photon-number quantum optics and structured-light propagation into a common description, allowing spatial light evolution to directly influence non-classical quantum states and their interference. JAX provides automatic differentiation and hardware-accelerated evaluation for optimization and automated search.

Result: The framework is demonstrated across a broad range of quantum-optical applications that exercise complementary aspects of its physical description.

Conclusion: By combining quantum and structured-light physics within a single differentiable simulator, esQueranto represents an important step towards a foundational simulator in physics.

Abstract: Uncovering new phenomena in nature requires experiments. For centuries, their design has been exclusively a human endeavor. Today, a new paradigm is emerging in which artificial intelligence and computational methods can design experiments themselves. Realizing this form of automated scientific discovery requires simulators that are sufficiently expressive to represent the diverse physical processes and couplings from which new experiments can be constructed. In this direction, we introduce \textsc{esQueranto}, a differentiable software that brings photon-number quantum optics and structured-light propagation into a common description, allowing the spatial evolution of light to directly influence non-classical quantum states and their interference. \textsc{esQueranto} is implemented in JAX, providing automatic differentiation and hardware-accelerated evaluation for optimization and automated search. We demonstrate the framework across a broad range of quantum-optical applications that exercise complementary aspects of its physical description. By combining quantum and structured-light physics within a single differentiable simulator, \textsc{esQueranto} takes an important step towards the dream of a foundational simulator in physics.

</details>


### [51] [Conditioning on Subalgebras - Entropy Duality and Generalized Quantum Stein's Lemma in von Neumann Algebras](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00772)
*Bjarne Bergh,Li Gao,Mizanur Rahaman*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper establishes duality relations for conditional entropies (Umegaki relative entropy, sandwiched- and Petz-Rényi divergences) relative to subalgebras of arbitrary von Neumann algebras, and applies these to prove an infinite-dimensional quantum Stein's lemma for finite-index subalgebras.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conditional entropy dualities are well understood for tensor-product subsystems in finite dimensions, but a framework covering general subalgebras and the fully infinite-dimensional setting was lacking. Extending this also promises an operational interpretation of relative entropy to a subalgebra beyond the finite-dimensional regime.

Method: Duality relations are developed for conditional entropies relative to subalgebras of arbitrary von Neumann algebras, covering the Umegaki relative entropy and the sandwiched- and Petz-Rényi divergences. These results generalize the finite-dimensional tensor-product dualities to general subalgebras and are lifted to the infinite-dimensional setting.

Result: The duality relations hold for general subalgebras and in infinite dimensions, and an infinite-dimensional extension of quantum Stein's lemma is obtained when the alternative hypothesis is the state space of a finite-index subalgebra.

Conclusion: The relative entropy to a subalgebra admits an operational interpretation as the optimal asymptotic error exponent in composite quantum hypothesis testing, the first such result beyond finite dimensions.

Abstract: We establish duality relations for conditional entropies relative to subalgebras of arbitrary von Neumann algebras, including the Umegaki relative entropy as well as the sandwiched- and Petz-Rényi divergences. Even in finite dimensions, these results extend the familiar dualities for conditional entropies from tensor-product subsystems to general subalgebras, while also lifting the framework to the fully infinite-dimensional setting. As an application, we establish an infinite-dimensional extension of quantum Stein's lemma when the alternative hypothesis is the state space of a finite-index subalgebra, which gives an operational interpretation of the relative entropy to a subalgebra as the optimal asymptotic error exponent in composite quantum hypothesis testing-the first beyond finite dimensions.

</details>


### [52] [Real-Time Adaptive Filtering and the Boxcar Limit in Superconducting Qubit Readout](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00783)
*Hans Johnson,Tanay Roy,Leonardo Bove,David van Zanten,Silvia Zorzetti,Jafar Saniie*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Adaptive FIR filtering (LMS/FPGA), window optimization, per-sample weighting, and sequential detection were evaluated against boxcar averaging for superconducting qubit readout. Window tuning gave the largest gains (+2.4% to +5.6%); filtering and per-sample weighting yielded little or no fidelity improvement beyond the boxcar limit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine when additional digital signal processing actually improves qubit readout fidelity, rather than merely denoising the readout waveform, by benchmarking several processing schemes against the standard boxcar-averager baseline.

Method: A real-time adaptive FIR filter trained by LMS on an FPGA quantum controller; frozen-filter readout shots compared offline with boxcar averaging; per-sample weighting, sequential (early-decision) detection, and matched-filter template construction from few labeled shots; sweeps of readout window start/duration on a 2D transmon (Q1) and a 3D ancilla qubit (Q2).

Result: Fixed FIR filters collapse to a scaled boxcar sum (apart from edge correction), so discrimination cannot improve. The frozen LMS filter retains 99.4% of the boxcar discrimination SNR at the 8 μs Q1 operating point, with no resolved fidelity gain. Window optimization improved contrast fidelity by +2.4% to +5.6%; per-sample weighting added up to +3.0% but only +0.33% beyond the tuned window. For Q2, the early-decision crossover lies near 1 μs; sequential testing decided most shots early within 0.5 percentage points of boxcar fidelity, and ~30 labeled shots per class sufficed for a near-optimal matched-filter template.

Conclusion: Gains come mainly from decision-stage improvements (optimal window selection and sequential detection) rather than from waveform denoising. The results provide an operating-point record that cleanly separates filter-based denoising from genuine decision-fidelity improvement.

Abstract: In dispersive readout of superconducting qubits, the common baseline is a boxcar averager: a uniformly weighted average over a fixed window followed by threshold-based state assignment. We ask when additional digital processing improves fidelity. We evaluated an adaptive finite-impulse-response (FIR) filter trained in real time by the least-mean-squares (LMS) algorithm on an FPGA quantum controller, and analyzed measured readout shots offline to compare the frozen filter with boxcar averaging and to test per-sample weighting and sequential detection.
  For the fixed FIR filters tested, summing the filtered samples gives the boxcar sum multiplied by one complex number, apart from a small edge correction (the kernel collapse). This scales state separation and noise equally, so discrimination cannot improve (the boxcar limit).
  The frozen LMS filter reduces trace noise without a resolved fidelity improvement at the tested readout lengths. Replayed on the same shots offline, each filtered shot carries 99.4% of the boxcar shot's discrimination signal-to-noise ratio at the 8 $μ$s operating point of the 2D transmon qubit (Q1).
  On Q1, optimizing the readout window's start and duration improves contrast readout fidelity by +2.4% to +5.6% across four readout lengths. Per-sample weighting adds up to +3.0% over full-window integration, and only +0.33% beyond the tuned window at 8 $μ$s.
  Remaining results are from a 3D ancilla qubit (Q2), which sits above the early-decision crossover that Q1 sits below. Hardware sweeps locate the crossover window near 1 $μ$s. Offline, the sequential test decided most shots early, with fidelity within 0.5 percentage points of the boxcar. About 30 labeled shots per class build a matched-filter template nearly as good as one from hundreds.
  The results form an operating-point record that separates waveform denoising from decision improvement.

</details>


### [53] [Remote state preparation of a single-spin state via hybrid spin-photon entanglement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00832)
*Yunge Jiang,Yehan Yu,Lili Song,Yan Mu,Chaoyun Peng,Mingfeng Wang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Proposes a remote state preparation protocol for a stationary electron-spin qubit using electron-spin/coherent-state hybrid entanglement, where the required coherent-state superposition projection is replaced by coupling to an auxiliary spin plus homodyne detection and spin measurement, yielding loss-tolerant, near-deterministic preparation with one bit of classical communication.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Remote state preparation exploits prior knowledge of a target state to cut the communication resources needed for quantum-state distribution. Single-photon encodings fail probabilistically under photon loss, so a loss-tolerant encoding is desirable. The conventional coherent-state protocol, however, requires projection onto superpositions of nonorthogonal coherent states, which is experimentally demanding.

Method: Hybrid entanglement between a stationary electron spin and a coherent-state light pulse is generated via spin-dependent reflection from a spin-cavity system. The problematic coherent-state-superposition projection is circumvented by coupling the transmitted pulse to an auxiliary spin, then performing homodyne detection and a spin projective measurement, avoiding direct superposition measurements and photon-number-resolving detection. Analytical treatment quantifies coherent-state nonorthogonality, fiber loss, and spin dephasing.

Result: In the ideal limit, deterministic preparation of a particular class of target states is achieved with only one bit of classical communication. Coherent-state encoding is inherently loss-tolerant: loss attenuates the amplitude, degrading fidelity gradually rather than causing probabilistic failure. Larger coherent-state amplitude improves distinguishability and fidelity but increases which-branch information leakage from photon loss, giving an optimal amplitude at each transmission distance. For experimentally relevant parameters, the optimized average fidelity stays well above the classical benchmark over tens of kilometers.

Conclusion: The protocol offers a practical, loss-tolerant route to remote state preparation of stationary spin qubits without demanding coherent-state-superposition or photon-number-resolving measurements, and a possible implementation using diamond nitrogen-vacancy centers is discussed.

Abstract: Remote state preparation is a fundamental quantum-information protocol that exploits prior knowledge of a target state to reduce the communication resources required for quantum-state distribution. Here, we propose a remote-state-preparation protocol for a stationary electron-spin qubit using hybrid entanglement between the electron spin and a coherent-state light pulse, generated by spin-dependent reflection from a spin-cavity system. Unlike single-photon encodings, the coherent-state encoding is naturally tolerant of photon loss: a loss channel attenuates the coherent-state amplitude, leading to a gradual reduction in preparation fidelity rather than probabilistic protocol failure. The central difficulty---implementing the projection onto superpositions of nonorthogonal coherent states required in the conventional protocol---is circumvented by coupling the transmitted pulse to an auxiliary spin, followed by homodyne detection and a spin projective measurement. The protocol therefore avoids direct coherent-state-superposition measurements and photon-number-resolving detection. In the ideal limit, deterministic preparation of a particular class of target states can be achieved using only one bit of classical communication. We analytically quantify the effects of coherent-state nonorthogonality, fiber loss, and spin dephasing on the preparation fidelity. Increasing the coherent-state amplitude improves state distinguishability and hence the preparation fidelity, but also enhances which-branch information leakage caused by photon loss, resulting in an optimal amplitude at each transmission distance. For experimentally relevant parameters, the optimized average fidelity remains well above the classical benchmark over distances of tens of kilometers. We also discuss a possible implementation using diamond nitrogen-vacancy centers.

</details>


### [54] [Neural Fourier Surrogates for Data Reuploading Quantum Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00841)
*Oliver Knitter,Jonathan Mei,Sang Hyub Kim,Chi Chen,Masako Yamada,Martin Roetteler*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces Neural Fourier Surrogates (NFS), a stochastic classical neural network that learns coefficients over the same finite Fourier series support as quantum neural networks, providing a natural classical baseline for evaluating QNN performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The exact boundary between classical and quantum advantage in quantum machine learning remains poorly understood. Direct comparison between QNNs and existing classical models is problematic because they encompass fundamentally different function classes, limiting broader insight into their differences.

Method: Inspired by Neural Quantum States and Random Fourier Features, the authors design Neural Fourier Surrogates (NFS), a stochastic classical neural network architecture that efficiently learns coefficients over the same finite Fourier series support as quantum neural networks. They test NFS on tabular benchmark datasets and analyze learned Fourier spectra on synthetic data.

Result: NFS is an effective classifier broadly competitive with established classical baselines, including a comparable Random Fourier Features model, and achieves comparable performance to data-reuploading QNNs. Additional analysis compares the learned Fourier spectra of QNNs and NFS on synthetic data.

Conclusion: NFS constitutes a natural classical baseline for evaluating QNN performance, enabling fairer and more informative comparisons between classical and quantum models.

Abstract: For quantum machine learning, the exact boundary between classical and quantum advantage is still poorly understood. Direct comparison between quantum neural networks (QNNs) and existing classical models, which encompass fundamentally different function classes, often fails to provide broader insight into the difference between the two. Inspired by the techniques of Neural Quantum States and Random Fourier Features, this work introduces Neural Fourier Surrogates (NFS), a stochastic classical neural network architecture for efficiently learning coefficients over the same finite Fourier series support as quantum neural networks. Testing on a selection of tabular benchmark datasets, we find that NFS is an effective classifier architecture broadly competitive with established classical baselines, including a comparable Random Fourier Features model, and possessing comparable performance to data-reuploading QNNs; combined with additional analysis comparing the learned Fourier spectra of QNNs and NFS on synthetic data, these results establish NFS as a natural classical baseline for evaluating QNN performance.

</details>


### [55] [Beyond IP = PSPACE and QIP = PSPACE: Interactive Proofs in Arbitrary Physical Theories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00847)
*Kishor Bharti*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Interactive proof power is studied in general probabilistic theories (GPTs). With classical messages, all theories decide PSPACE; with a quantum verifier and quantum messages, arbitrary super-quantum provers yield no advantage, preserving the three-message PSPACE result; with arbitrary message systems, the class can strictly exceed PSPACE.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The equalities IP = PSPACE and QIP = PSPACE (the latter with three messages) motivate a basic question: how much of an interactive proof's power derives from the underlying physical theory. The paper investigates interactive proofs in general probabilistic theories that subsume classical and quantum theory.

Method: Interactive proofs are analyzed within general probabilistic theories, varying two aspects: what the prover and verifier exchange (classical messages, quantum messages, or arbitrary systems) and how the theory defines efficient operations, under stated standard assumptions.

Result: When only classical messages are exchanged, protocols in every theory satisfying the standard assumptions decide exactly PSPACE. For protocols with a quantum verifier and quantum messages, letting the prover use any theory containing quantum theory does not increase the maximum acceptance probability, so the three-message PSPACE result remains valid against such provers. When messages may be arbitrary systems, the interactive-proof class can strictly exceed PSPACE.

Conclusion: The power of interactive proofs is robust to the underlying physical theory under classical or quantum message exchange, but can strictly surpass PSPACE when arbitrary physical systems may be communicated.

Abstract: The equalities IP = PSPACE and QIP = PSPACE, the latter achievable with three messages, raise a basic question: how much of an interactive proof's power comes from the underlying physical theory? We study interactive proofs in general probabilistic theories, which include classical and quantum theory. The answer depends on what the prover and verifier exchange and how the theory specifies efficient operations. When they exchange only classical messages, protocols in every theory satisfying our standard assumptions decide exactly PSPACE. For protocols with a quantum verifier and quantum messages, allowing a prover to use any theory containing quantum theory does not increase the maximum acceptance probability. Thus, the three-message PSPACE result remains valid against such provers. When messages may be arbitrary systems, the interactive-proof class can strictly exceed PSPACE.

</details>


### [56] [From Steane to A7: Quantum Codes from Invariant States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00865)
*Ian Teixeira*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper constructs a two-parameter family of single-error-correcting seven-ququart ((7,4,3))₄ codes with transversal Ã₇ symmetry, using an invariant-parent-state method based on maximal marginal mixing and Schur–Weyl sector weights.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The goal is to realize the finite component of a two-qubit super-golden gate set, i.e., to support non-Clifford operations transversally by applying the same gate to each physical ququart, thereby generalizing known code constructions beyond Clifford symmetries.

Method: Rather than directly searching for a codespace, the authors construct invariant parent states. Using a unitary t-group as local symmetry and a t-transitive permutation symmetry, maximal mixing of marginals on up to t sites is expressed as linear equations in Schur–Weyl sector weights; each feasible solution yields parents of pure distance at least t+1, and puncturing one site yields a q-dimensional code of distance at least t with transversal local symmetry. The Steane and nonstabilizer Fake Steane codes are analyzed as a circle of eight-qubit purifications with two fixed sector norms and one free relative phase; replacing Clifford symmetry by Ã₇ ≤ SU(4) gives three fixed norms and two free phases.

Result: The construction produces a two-parameter family of ((7,4,3))₄ ququart codes encoding two logical qubits, with transversal Ã₇ symmetry and supporting non-Clifford operations. The new ququart family arises from three fixed invariant-sector norms and two free relative phases, enabled by the nonabelian Ã₇ symmetry.

Conclusion: Continuous freedom in code constructions corresponds to free relative phases among invariant-sector norms fixed by error correction; enlarging the symmetry from Clifford to Ã₇ increases both the number of fixed norms and the number of free parameters, yielding new ququart codes suited to non-Clifford transversal gates.

Abstract: We construct a two-parameter family of single-error-correcting seven-ququart codes with transversal $\tilde{A_7}$ symmetry, realizing the finite component of a two-qubit super-golden gate set. These $((7,4,3))_4$ codes encode two logical qubits and support non-Clifford operations by applying the same gate to each physical ququart.
  Our method constructs invariant parent states rather than searching directly for a codespace. For $n$ systems of local dimension $q$, suppose the local symmetry is a unitary $t$-group and the permutation symmetry is $t$-transitive. Maximal mixing of all marginals on at most $t$ sites is then equivalent to linear equations in Schur--Weyl sector weights. Every feasible solution yields parents of pure distance at least $t+1$; puncturing one site produces a $q$-dimensional code of distance at least $t$ with the prescribed local symmetry acting transversally.
  Steane and the nonstabilizer Fake Steane code reveal why the construction leaves continuous freedom. They share the same prescribed transversal Clifford and permutation symmetries, and their eight-qubit purifications lie on a circle: error correction fixes two invariant-sector norms but leaves the relative phase free. Keeping the same permutation group and replacing Clifford symmetry by $\tilde{A_7}$ $\leq SU(4)$ gives three fixed sector norms and two free relative phases, producing the new ququart family.

</details>


### [57] [A Quantum Phase Neural Network with Multi-Inputs/Single-Output](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00868)
*Shuang Cong,Jinmin Yang,Sajede Harraz*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A 35-input/single-output quantum phase neural network (QPNN) is proposed to recognize 7×5 pixel letters "A" and "B", using quantum phase encoding and adaptive learning to achieve zero-error recognition while avoiding the 2^35 amplitude bottleneck.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide an effective way to solve the "exponential wall" problem in multi-input recognition, avoiding the bottleneck of generating all 2^35 complex exponential amplitudes.

Method: Each 35-bit (0/1) input is normalized and quantum-phase-encoded into a superposition state described by 35 quantum phases. A quantum rotation gate introduces adjustable phases, and a controlled NOT gate entangles the relative phase between two inputs. The input-output relationship is derived, and a phase-weight learning algorithm with adaptive learning rate is developed. Analytical solutions with recognition probability 1 (zero-error adjustable phase solutions, non-unique) are designed and verified on the Qiskit platform.

Result: Recognition probability of 1 is achieved; analytical expressions for all adjustable phase solutions with zero errors are derived and shown to be non-unique; experimental performance is verified on Qiskit.

Conclusion: Phase drive converts probability calculation into an analytical polynomial function with adjustable rotation angles, avoiding the exponential wall and offering a new implementation scheme for practical multi-input recognition problems.

Abstract: A 35 input/single output quantum phase neural network (QPNN) is proposed to recognize the English letters' A 'and' B 'composed 7*5 of pixels. Each node of the proposed QPNN has the input with 0 or 1. Through normalization and quantum phase encoding, each set of 35 with 0 or 1 digital inputs is encoded into a quantum superposition state described by 35 quantum phases. Then, a quantum rotation gate is used to introduce adjustable phase, and a controlled NOT gate is used to entangle the relative phase between the two inputs. The relationship between the input and output of the network is derived. This paper also derived the phase weight learning training algorithm with adaptive learning rate. The solutions with the recognition probability of 1 is designed, and the analytical expressions for all adjustable phase solutions with zero errors are not unique. The experimental performance is verified on the Qiskit platform. This paper uses phase drive to convert the probability calculation into an analytical polynomial function with adjustable rotation angles in the network, avoiding the bottleneck of generating all 2^35 complex exponential amplitudes and providing an effective way to solve the "exponential wall" problem, which provides a new implementation solution for the practical application of multi-input recognition problems.

</details>


### [58] [A Gate-Based Quantum Computing Framework for Codon Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00887)
*Fatemeh Ghasemi,Kion Kim*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work introduces a gate-based quantum computing framework for codon optimization by mapping the problem to an Ising Hamiltonian, solving it with VQE, SVQE, and QAOA, and benchmarking against exact diagonalization, classical optimization, and quantum annealing on a three-amino-acid case with IBM hardware demonstrations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Codon optimization is a challenging combinatorial optimization problem relevant to synthetic biology, protein expression, and biotechnology. Prior work has largely used quantum annealing, while gate-based quantum algorithms remain unexplored for this task.

Method: The optimization objective is formulated as an Ising Hamiltonian and expressed in Pauli operators compatible with gate-based quantum processors. The framework is evaluated using VQE, SVQE, and QAOA, with benchmarking against exact diagonalization, classical optimization, and quantum annealing. A three-amino-acid benchmark is used, and hardware demonstrations run on IBM Quantum processors.

Result: The implementations reproduce exact ground-state solutions on the three-amino-acid benchmark. The study identifies optimized ansatz selections, optimization strategies, initialization schemes, and hardware execution settings that yield reproducible performance on current noisy quantum hardware.

Conclusion: As a work in progress, this study establishes a reproducible computational framework for gate-based quantum codon optimization and provides a foundation for future investigations of larger protein sequences as quantum hardware and algorithms advance.

Abstract: Codon optimization is a challenging combinatorial optimization problem with important applications in synthetic biology, protein expression, and biotechnology. While quantum annealing has previously been explored for this problem, the application of gate-based quantum algorithms remains largely unexplored. In this work, we present a gate-based quantum computing framework for codon optimization by formulating the optimization objective as an Ising Hamiltonian and expressing it in terms of Pauli operators suitable for gate-based quantum processors. The proposed framework is investigated using the Variational Quantum Eigensolver (VQE), Sampling Variational Quantum Eigensolver (SVQE), and the Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA), with benchmarking against exact diagonalization, classical optimization, and quantum annealing. Using a three-amino-acid benchmark, the proposed implementations successfully reproduce the exact ground-state solutions while identifying optimized ansatz selections, optimization strategies, initialization schemes, and hardware execution settings that provide reproducible performance on current noisy quantum hardware. Hardware demonstrations are performed on IBM Quantum processors to evaluate the practical implementation of the proposed workflow. As a work in progress, this study establishes a reproducible computational framework for gate-based quantum codon optimization and provides the foundation for future investigations of larger protein sequences as quantum hardware and algorithms continue to advance.

</details>


### [59] [Telecom-Band Optical Transitions of Erbium Atoms in Rare-Gas Solids](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00918)
*Evgenii Zaitsev,Alec Dinerstein,Shan Zou,Charles Peretti,Yutian Wen,Wei Guo,Gregory V. Hartland,Yizhong Huang,Dafei Jin*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Neutral Er atoms isolated in solid Ne and Ar at 6 K show narrow near-infrared transitions, including telecom-band inner-shell emission with millisecond-scale lifetime in Ne.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rare-earth atoms like Er have inner-shell near-infrared transitions, some within telecom bands, that are largely host-insensitive due to shielding by filled outer shells. Cryogenic rare-gas solids (solid Ne, Ar) offer high chemical purity and a nearly nuclear-spin-free environment, making them attractive hosts for preserving optical and spin coherence, motivating the isolation of neutral Er atoms in these matrices.

Method: Neutral Er atoms were isolated in solid Ne and Ar at 6 K. Their emission was characterized using steady-state and time-resolved photoluminescence spectroscopy, with thermal annealing applied to modify the matrix environment.

Result: Several near-infrared transitions between 1000–1500 nm were observed in both hosts, including inner-shell emission in the telecom O and E bands. The 4f→5d transition near 1299 nm forms a narrow multiplet consistent with crystal-field splitting, decaying on a microsecond timescale. The 4f→4f transition near 1437 nm shows a matrix shift below 1 cm⁻¹ in both hosts; in solid Ne its inhomogeneous linewidth is bounded by spectrometer resolution to <22 GHz, and its PL lifetime reaches up to 78.9 ms, close to the estimated radiative limit. Annealing extends the 1437 nm lifetime in Ne and suppresses phonon sidebands relative to zero-phonon lines in Ar.

Conclusion: Rare-gas solids are promising hosts for long-lived telecom-band Er emission, providing a foundation for future spin-photon interfaces and hybrid quantum architectures.

Abstract: Rare-earth (RE) atoms, such as erbium (Er), have inner-shell optical transitions in the near-infrared, some of which are in the telecom bands. These transitions are largely insensitive to the host environment due to effective shielding by the filled outer shells. Cryogenic rare-gas (RG) solids, such as solid neon (Ne) and argon (Ar), combine high chemical purity with a nearly nuclear-spin-free environment, making them attractive solid hosts for preserving optical and spin coherence. In this work, we isolate neutral Er atoms in solid Ne and Ar at 6 K and characterize their emission by steady-state and time-resolved photoluminescence (PL) spectroscopy. In both hosts, we observe several near-infrared transitions between 1000--1500 nm, including inner-shell emission in the telecom O and E bands. The $4f \rightarrow 5d$ transition near 1299 nm forms a multiplet of narrow components, consistent with crystal-field splitting, and decays on a microsecond timescale. The $4f \rightarrow 4f$ transition near 1437 nm shows a matrix shift below 1 cm$^{-1}$ in both hosts. In solid Ne, its inhomogeneous linewidth is bounded by the spectrometer resolution to < 22 GHz, and its PL lifetime reaches up to 78.9 ms, close to the estimated radiative limit. Thermal annealing extends the 1437 nm lifetime in Ne and suppresses the phonon sidebands relative to the zero-phonon lines in Ar. These results identify RG solids as promising hosts for long-lived telecom-band Er emission and provide a foundation for future spin-photon interfaces and hybrid quantum architectures.

</details>


### [60] [TATVA: A Reinforcement Learning Framework for Quantum Circuit Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00945)
*Om Bhamare,Aryan Jadhav,Manas Shinde,Prithvi Shinde*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: TATVA is a reinforcement learning system that synthesizes quantum circuits for high-fidelity quantum state preparation, using dual RL agents (DQN and PPO) with a parallel architecture and Qiskit's statevector simulator, achieving up to 5 qubits with 0.999999 fidelity and compact circuits, followed by post-hoc depth optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-fidelity quantum state preparation is a critical initial step in quantum computing, as errors can propagate and affect final computational results. A key challenge is designing quantum circuits that achieve the target state accurately with fewer gates. Traditional approaches rely on predefined rules and mathematical decompositions, which struggle to handle diverse and complex target states.

Method: TATVA formulates circuit synthesis as a sequential decision problem where agents select one gate at a time and compute fidelity after each applied gate, keeping the gate yielding the highest fidelity. The system employs a parallel architecture with two RL agents—Deep Q-Network (DQN) and Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO)—integrated with Qiskit's statevector simulator. After reaching target fidelity, a post-hoc optimization step reduces circuit depth while preserving fidelity. Performance is assessed via fidelity, success rate, gate count, and circuit depth.

Result: TATVA synthesizes circuits for up to 5 qubits, achieving a fidelity of 0.999999 and producing compact circuits. It also performs post-hoc depth optimization without degrading fidelity, and generalizes to target states unseen during training, demonstrating effective learning beyond the training set.

Conclusion: TATVA demonstrates that reinforcement learning enables automatic quantum circuit synthesis with high fidelity and compact gate counts, and that RL agents can generalize to novel target states. This approach offers a viable alternative to rule-based and decomposition-based circuit design for complex quantum state preparation.

Abstract: One of the most important initial steps in quantum computing is high-fidelity quantum state preparation, because errors in it can affect the final computational result. Therefore, one of the major challenges is to design a quantum circuit that can achieve the desired target state accurately with a smaller number of gates. Traditionally, circuits are designed using predefined rules and mathematical decompositions, but this makes it difficult to design circuits for different and complex target states. This paper presents TATVA, a reinforcement learning (RL) system for synthesizing quantum circuits. TATVA views circuit synthesis as a sequential decision problem in which the agents select one gate at a time and calculate the fidelity after each applied gate, with the gate producing the highest fidelity selected for the circuit. The system introduces a parallel architecture that uses two RL agents, Deep Q-Network (DQN) and Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO), along with Qiskit's statevector simulator. The system can synthesize up to 5 qubits while achieving a fidelity of 0.999999 and producing compact circuits. After achieving the desired fidelity, it performs a post-hoc optimization step that reduces the circuit depth while preserving the achieved fidelity. The circuit is finalized using fidelity, success rate, gate count, and circuit depth. Target states that are not used during training are used to test whether the system has learned effectively and can generalize beyond the training states. This approach aims to enable automatic quantum circuit synthesis with high fidelity using reinforcement learning.

</details>


### [61] [Sublinear-depth Quantum Simulation of Electrons with Atomic Orbitals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00950)
*Jakob Günther,Aram W. Harrow*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work shows that Trotter steps for atomic-orbital electronic-structure Hamiltonians can be implemented in polylogarithmic circuit depth without ancillas, achieving sublinear simulation depth and a total gate count of N^{5/3+o(1)}, matching the best known scaling for any basis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Atomic orbitals are the standard compact, flexible basis for molecular electronic structure, but they yield Hamiltonians with up to O(N^4) terms and little structure, making it hard to compete with plane-wave and real-space-grid quantum simulation methods. The paper aims to close this complexity gap.

Method: The authors combine: (1) a rigorous treatment of orbital localization, (2) a hierarchical Hamiltonian decomposition based on the Fast Multipole Method, and (3) shallow quantum Fourier arithmetic circuits, to construct an ancilla-free Trotter step.

Result: A Trotter step for atomic orbitals is implementable in depth O(polylog(N)) without ancillas. Bounding the Trotter error gives sublinear simulation depth, and the total gate count is N^{5/3+o(1)}.

Conclusion: The total gate count matches the best scaling known for any basis, demonstrating that atomic-orbital-based quantum simulation can be competitive with plane-wave and real-space-grid approaches.

Abstract: Discretizing electronic degrees of freedom into atomic orbitals is the default choice to accurately model electrons in molecules in a compact and flexible way. We consider the task of simulating such electronic structure models on a quantum computer for $N$ orbitals, keeping the number of orbitals per atom constant. Atomic orbitals give rise to Hamiltonians with potentially up to $\mathcal{O}(N^4)$ terms and little structure, which made it challenging to match the complexity of methods based on plane waves and real-space grids. In this work, we show that a Trotter step for atomic orbitals can be implemented in depth $\mathcal{O}(\mathrm{polylog}(N))$ without ancillas by combining a rigorous treatment of orbital localization, a hierarchical Hamiltonian decomposition based on the Fast Multipole Method, and shallow quantum Fourier arithmetic circuits. By bounding the Trotter error, we obtain sublinear simulation depth, and our total gate count $N^{5/3 + o(1)}$ matches the best scaling known for any basis.

</details>


### [62] [Exact $T$-counts of Toffoli layers from an isotropy bound](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01024)
*Arul Rhik Mazumder*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper proves that a layer of m disjoint CCZ gates (the diagonal part of a parallel Toffoli layer) requires exactly 6m+1 T gates in any Hadamard-free Clifford+T circuit with clean ancillas, establishing the first optimality proof for general m, and generalizes this to an "isotropy floor" lower bound on T-count for diagonal level-three gates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The T-count dominates the cost of fault-tolerant Clifford+T computation, so tight lower bounds on T-count are crucial for assessing optimality of synthesis and optimization. Prior matching constructions by Campbell and Howard gave T-counts such as 7 (m=1), 13 (m=2), and 4m+3 (fan-out), but no proof existed that these were optimal for general m.

Method: The authors prove an isotropy constraint: for a pure-cubic phase, the vectors recording which T gates touch each qubit span a totally isotropic subspace. This yields the isotropy floor δ ≥ 2(n−d*)−r for every diagonal level-three gate, computed from the phase polynomial in polynomial time. A separate parity argument raises the floor to 2ν+1 on non-Clifford pure-cubic gates.

Result: A layer of m disjoint CCZ gates needs exactly 6m+1 T gates, matching the Campbell–Howard construction and proving optimality for general m. The floor is never below stabilizer nullity ν (equal to n−d* on this class). On the output of the TODD optimizer for 24 benchmark circuits, the floor certifies 193 of 311 merged phase-polynomial blocks optimal (186–193 across five seeds), versus 113 for nullity.

Conclusion: The isotropy floor provides a polynomial-time-computable, tight lower bound on T-count for diagonal level-three gates, surpassing stabilizer nullity. It also extends, under stated conditions, to circuits whose only internal Hadamards appear in unitarily uncomputed temporary AND blocks, while under adaptive feedforward only t ≥ ν is proved.

Abstract: The $T$-count is the dominant cost of fault-tolerant Clifford+$T$ computation. We prove that a layer of $m$ disjoint CCZ gates, the diagonal core of a parallel Toffoli layer, needs exactly $6m+1$ $T$ gates in every Hadamard-free Clifford+$T$ circuit with clean ancillas. Campbell and Howard gave the matching construction. To our knowledge this is the first proof that it is optimal for general $m$ (for $m=1$ the value $7$ is classical, and Campbell and Howard state the value $13$ at $m=2$). On every controlled unitary the same floor comes within one of their exact count, and it recovers their $4m+3$ for a fan-out of $m$ Toffolis from one control. The proof rests on an isotropy constraint: for a pure-cubic phase, the vectors recording which $T$ gates touch each qubit span a totally isotropic subspace. In general the constraint gives the isotropy floor $δ\ge2(n-d^{\ast})-r$ for every diagonal level-three gate, computed from the phase polynomial in polynomial time. The floor is never below stabilizer nullity $ν$, which equals $n-d^{\ast}$ on this class, and a separate parity argument raises it to $2ν+1$ on non-Clifford pure-cubic gates. On the output of the TODD optimizer for the $24$ benchmark circuits it completes, the floor certifies $193$ of its $311$ merged phase-polynomial blocks optimal for their Hadamard layering ($186$ to $193$ across five optimizer seeds), against $113$ for nullity. The floor also holds, under stated conditions, for circuits whose only internal Hadamards form unitarily uncomputed temporary AND blocks, while under adaptive feedforward only $t\geν$ is proved.

</details>


### [63] [Device-Independent Conference Keys from Parity-Extended Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01025)
*Suvradip Chakraborty,Ronak Ramachandran,Aniruddha Sen*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces Parity-G games, a framework extending any two-player non-local game (with an optimal strategy where one player measures Pauli observables) to N players while preserving quantum and classical values, enabling new device-independent conference key agreement (DI-CKA) protocols. Applied to the Mermin-Peres Magic Square Game, it yields the Parity Magic Square Game and the first DI-CKA protocol based on pseudo-telepathy, proven secure against coherent attacks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing DI-CKA protocols rely on a single Bell inequality (typically a multiparty CHSH variant), whereas DI-QKD has exploited a much richer variety of non-local games. It was unclear how to transfer this diversity of games to the conference (multiparty) setting.

Method: The authors define Parity-G games, which extend any two-player game G to N players for arbitrary N, provided G admits an optimal strategy in which one player measures Pauli observables. The extension preserves the quantum and classical values of G, and the security of the N-party protocol reduces to analysis of the two-player game alone. They instantiate this with the Mermin-Peres Magic Square Game to obtain the Parity Magic Square Game, and construct a DI-CKA protocol from it, proving security against coherent attacks.

Result: The framework recovers the Parity-CHSH game of Ribeiro, Murta and Wehner as a special case. Applied to the Magic Square Game, it produces a new N-player pseudo-telepathy game, the Parity Magic Square Game, in which ideal devices win every round. The resulting DI-CKA protocol is the first based on a pseudo-telepathy game, yields up to two key bits per round, and at low noise achieves a key rate exceeding that of the Parity-CHSH-based DI-CKA protocol.

Conclusion: Parity-G games provide a general bridge from the rich landscape of two-player non-local games to multiparty DI-CKA, subsuming prior constructions and enabling the first pseudo-telepathy-based DI-CKA protocol with improved key rates at low noise.

Abstract: Device-independent conference key agreement (DI-CKA) lets a group of parties establish a shared secret key from untrusted quantum devices, with security certified by non-locality. Existing DI-CKA protocols are each built around a single Bell inequality, typically a multiparty variant of the CHSH game. DI-QKD protocols, in contrast, have been built from a much richer landscape of non-local games, and it has remained unclear how to carry this landscape over to the conference setting. We introduce $\textit{Parity-$G$ games}$, which extend any two-player game $G$ to $N$ players, for every $N$, provided $G$ has an optimal strategy in which one player measures Pauli observables. The extension preserves the quantum and classical values of $G$, and the security of the resulting $N$-party protocol follows from an analysis of the two-player game alone. Our framework recovers the Parity-CHSH game of Ribeiro, Murta and Wehner (Phys. Rev. A, 2018) as a special case. Applied to the Mermin--Peres Magic Square Game, it yields a new $N$-player pseudo-telepathy game, the $\textit{Parity Magic Square Game}$, which ideal devices win in every round. We use it to construct the $\textit{first}$ DI-CKA protocol based on a pseudo-telepathy game. We prove the protocol secure against coherent attacks. It produces up to two key bits per round, and at low noise its key rate exceeds that of the DI-CKA protocol based on the Parity-CHSH game.

</details>


### [64] [Function-like pseudorandom unitaries generate pseudorandom quantum processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01038)
*Zitai Xu,Rahul Arvind,Kishor Bharti,Tobias Haug,Dax Enshan Koh,Wai-Keong Mok,Andrew Tanggara,Alexey V. Gorshkov*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces pseudorandom function-like unitaries (PRFUs): a reusable family of independently random-looking quantum operations generated from a single short key and public labels. It defines classical- and coherent-label access under adaptive quantum queries, gives constructions from post-quantum pseudorandom functions and pseudorandom unitaries (classical labels) and from indexed path-recording with quantum-secure function/permutation primitives (coherent labels), and shows applications to pseudorandom channels, quantum combs with private memory, unitary gluing, quantum authentication, masked QRAM, and random multi-time dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Haar-random unitaries are the canonical model of generic random quantum evolution but have exponential description and circuit complexity. Pseudorandom unitaries efficiently emulate a single Haar-random unitary, yet many tasks need an entire reusable family of independently random-looking operations rather than one fixed unitary. Existing notions do not provide an efficient, keyed, label-indexed family, so the paper aims to extend quantum pseudorandomness from individual operations to efficiently generated families of random-looking quantum dynamics.

Method: The authors introduce pseudorandom function-like unitaries (PRFU), which use a single short key to generate a family of unitaries indexed by public labels. They separate classical-label and coherent-label access models and establish security under adaptive quantum queries. For classical labels, they give a generic construction combining a post-quantum pseudorandom function with a pseudorandom unitary. For coherent labels, they introduce an indexed path-recording framework to control interference across labels and prove security of a construction based on quantum-secure function and permutation primitives. They then derive pseudorandom channels and quantum combs with private memory, and combine PRFUs with unitary gluing for a one-key family of pseudorandom unitaries with post-key-generation register widths.

Result: PRFUs are shown to generate pseudorandom channels and quantum combs with private memory that remain secure against adaptive interventions and concurrent sessions. Combining PRFUs with unitary gluing yields a one-key family of pseudorandom unitaries whose supported register widths can be chosen after key generation. Applications demonstrated include nonce-resolved quantum authentication, coherently masked QRAM queries, and efficient emulation of random multi-time dynamics.

Conclusion: The work extends quantum pseudorandomness from individual operations to efficiently generated families of random-looking quantum dynamics. PRFUs provide a keyed, label-indexed, reusable primitive with security under adaptive quantum queries in both classical- and coherent-label settings, and they underpin several cryptographic and simulation applications.

Abstract: Haar-random unitaries provide a canonical model of generic random quantum evolution, but they typically have exponential description and circuit complexity. Although pseudorandom unitaries efficiently emulate a single Haar-random unitary, many tasks require an entire reusable family of independently random-looking operations. We introduce pseudorandom function-like unitaries (PRFU), which generate such a family indexed by public labels using only a single short key. We distinguish classical- and coherent-label access and establish security under adaptive quantum queries. For classical labels, we provide a generic construction from a post-quantum pseudorandom function and a pseudorandom unitary. For coherent labels, we use an indexed path-recording framework to analyze interference across labels and prove security of a construction based on quantum-secure function and permutation primitives. We further show that PRFUs generate pseudorandom channels and quantum combs with private memory, which are secure against adaptive interventions and concurrent sessions. Combining PRFUs with unitary gluing yields a one-key family of pseudorandom unitaries whose supported register widths can be chosen after key generation. Further applications include nonce-resolved quantum authentication, coherently masked QRAM queries, and efficient emulation of random multi-time dynamics. Our results extend quantum pseudorandomness from individual operations to efficiently generated families of random-looking quantum dynamics.

</details>


### [65] [Learned Parallel Bit-Flipping Sequential Belief Propagation Decoding of Quantum LDPC Codes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01068)
*Mohsen Moradi,Taejoon Kim,Remi A. Chou*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes a learned parallel bit-flipping sequential BP decoder for QLDPC codes, using a quaternary score (syndrome gain, quantized Pauli log-likelihood penalty, Q-table lookahead) to fork independent learned BP continuations in parallel, improving reliability with low latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: QLDPC codes enable low-overhead fault-tolerant quantum computation, but need fast, low-complexity, reliable decoders. Standard flooding BP fails to converge on QLDPC codes due to short cycles, degeneracy, and symmetric decoding trajectories. Learned sequential BP improves convergence via an RL policy for variable-node update ordering, but a single learned trajectory remains sensitive to unfavorable local Pauli decisions.

Method: Run learned sequential BP for a fixed number of iterations. If the syndrome is unsatisfied, construct quaternary bit-flipping candidates, each flipping the current Pauli decision of one qubit, scored by a new quaternary score combining syndrome gain, a quantized Pauli log-likelihood penalty, and Q-table lookahead at hypothetical post-flip states. Selected candidates initialize independent learned sequential BP continuations from the same decoder state, executed in parallel.

Result: Simulations on representative QLDPC codes over the depolarizing channel show improved reliability of learned sequential BP with a parallel low-latency structure; testing multiple candidates mainly increases parallel hardware resources rather than sequential decoding latency.

Conclusion: The proposed learned parallel bit-flipping sequential BP decoder enhances the reliability of learned sequential BP while retaining a parallel low-latency structure, offering a promising direction for practical QLDPC decoding.

Abstract: Quantum low-density parity-check (QLDPC) codes are promising candidates for low-overhead fault-tolerant quantum computation, but their practical use requires fast, low-complexity, and reliable decoders. Belief propagation (BP) is attractive because of its local message-passing structure, yet standard flooding BP often suffers from convergence failures on QLDPC codes due to short cycles, degeneracy, and symmetric decoding trajectories. Learned sequential BP improves convergence by using a reinforcement-learning policy to choose variable-node update orders, but a single learned trajectory can still be sensitive to unfavorable local Pauli decisions. We propose a learned parallel bit-flipping sequential BP decoder with a new quaternary score combining syndrome gain, a quantized Pauli log-likelihood penalty, and Q-table lookahead at hypothetical post-flip states. Our decoder first runs learned sequential BP for a fixed number of iterations. If the syndrome is not satisfied, it constructs quaternary bit-flipping candidates, each corresponding to changing the current Pauli decision of one qubit. The selected candidates initialize independent learned sequential BP continuations from the same decoder state. Since these continuations are independent, they can be executed in parallel, so testing several candidates mainly increases parallel hardware resources rather than sequential decoding latency. Simulations on representative QLDPC codes over the depolarizing channel show that the proposed decoder improves the reliability of learned sequential BP while having a parallel low-latency structure.

</details>


### [66] [The stationarity test: a framework for learning quantum many-body systems from their thermal states](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01074)
*Thiago Bergamaschi*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a "stationarity test" that connects quantum Gibbs sampling to Hamiltonian learning; using it, the authors give the first structure-learning algorithm for lattice Hamiltonians at all temperatures, the first coefficient-learning algorithm from thermal metastable states, and a refinement of a thermal area law.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning the Hamiltonian interactions of a quantum system from samples of its Gibbs states is a foundational problem at the intersection of quantum learning theory and many-body physics, but prior methods lacked algorithms for structure learning across all temperatures and for learning from metastable states.

Method: The authors draw connections to quantum Gibbs sampling, introducing a stationarity test that guesses a Hamiltonian and measures the rate-of-change of local observables under the associated detailed-balanced quantum Markov chain [CKG23]. Technically, the arguments rely on new approximate locality and convexity properties of the quantum Fisher information of these Gibbs sampling algorithms.

Result: The stationarity test yields: (1) the first structure-learning algorithm for the interaction graph of lattice Hamiltonians at all temperatures from Gibbs state copies; (2) the first algorithm for learning lattice Hamiltonian coefficients from thermal metastable states modeled as local minima of free energy; and (3) a refinement of the recent thermal area law for metastable states [BCV25] valid in the thermodynamic limit.

Conclusion: The proposed learning algorithms are rigorous, time-efficient, and nearly sample-optimal in system size and accuracy, establishing the stationarity test as a versatile tool for Hamiltonian learning.

Abstract: The task of learning the Hamiltonian interactions governing a quantum system, given samples of its thermal (or 'Gibbs') states, is a foundational question at the intersection of quantum learning theory and many-body physics. In this paper, we draw connections to the quantum Gibbs sampling literature to introduce a natural learning algorithm we call the stationarity test: which simply "guesses" the Hamiltonian, and measures the rate-of-change of local observables, under the associated detailed-balanced quantum Markov chain [CKG23]. We leverage the stationarity test to address the following applications:
  1. To give the first learning algorithm for the underlying interaction graph, i.e. structure learning, of lattice Hamiltonians at all temperatures, given copies of their Gibbs states.
  2. To give the first learning algorithm for the coefficients of a lattice Hamiltonian, given only copies of its thermal metastable states, modeled as the "local minima" of the free energy.
  3. In addition, we present a refinement to the recent area law for thermal metastable states [BCV25], which holds in the thermodynamic limit.
  Our learning algorithms are rigorous, time-efficient, and nearly sample-optimal in system size and accuracy. At a technical level, our arguments are based on new approximate locality and convexity properties for the quantum Fisher information of these Gibbs sampling algorithms.

</details>


### [67] [Quantum synchronization in atom-cavity coupled systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01094)
*Katha Haldar,Anushree Dey,Saikat Ghosh,Bimalendu Deb*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A driven spin-1 atomic ensemble interacting with two driven cavity modes can exhibit limit cycles or quantum synchronization depending on drive strengths, visualized via SU(2) and spin-1 Husimi-Q functions, corroborated by U(1) symmetry breaking, Wigner functions, and coherence measures. Field synchronization and Wehrl mutual information are found to be mutually exclusive.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Coupled atom-cavity systems are promising platforms for quantum technologies, yet the emergent collective dynamics of multi-mode cavities interacting with multi-level atoms under driving and dissipation remain underexplored. Understanding limit-cycle and quantum synchronization behavior in such driven dissipative systems could enable quantum advantages, e.g., in quantum metrology.

Method: The authors study a CQED setup with an ensemble of spin-1 atoms driven by a classical field and coupled to two driven cavity modes. They introduce a generalized two-mode field Husimi-Q function based on SU(2) coherent states and a spin-1 Husimi-Q function for the atomic state. They further analyze U(1) symmetry breaking, the Wigner quasi-probability distribution, and coherence properties of the reduced field density matrix. They also compute the Wehrl mutual information between the two modes.

Result: Depending on cavity drive strengths and other parameters, both the atomic subsystem and the two-mode cavity field display limit-cycle or quantum synchronization. The phase-space Husimi-Q functions reveal these behaviors, corroborated by U(1) symmetry breaking, Wigner distributions, and coherence properties. Notably, field synchronization and Wehrl mutual information are mutually exclusive.

Conclusion: The work reveals an intricate interplay between limit-cycle and quantum synchronization in driven dissipative interacting quantum systems, and identifies mutual exclusivity between field synchronization and Wehrl mutual information. These findings are relevant for extracting quantum advantages in quantum metrology using coupled atom-cavity systems.

Abstract: We consider a cavity quantum electrodynamics (CQED) setup where an ensemble of spin-1 atoms driven by a classical field interacts with two driven cavity field modes. We show that the two sub-systems - the atoms and the two-mode cavity field, exhibit limit-cycle or quantum synchronization depending on the cavity drive strengths and other system parameters. In order to visualize the limit cycle and synchronization in the field, we introduce generalized two-mode field Husimi-Q function using SU(2) coherent states. For atomic spin states, we make use of spin-1 Husimi-Q function to visualize the same. We further corroborate the occurrence of field synchronization and limit cycle by analyzing $U(1)$ symmetry breaking, Wigner quasi-probability distribution, coherence properties of reduced field density matrix. Our results reveal intriguing interplay between the limit-cycle and quantum synchronization behavior, highlighting emergent quantum phenomena in driven dissipative interacting quantum systems. Additionally, we calculate Wehrl mutual information between the two modes and find that the field synchronization and Wehrl mutual information are mutually exclusive. Our study would be important to extract quantum advantages in quantum metrology using coupled atom-cavity systems.

</details>


### [68] [Constant-Per-Layer-Depth MPS-Pretrained Ansatz for Noisy Distributed Quantum Processors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01099)
*Seongpyo Hong,Woodo Lee,Yong-Su Kim,Seung-Sup B. Lee,Junghyun Lee*

Main category: quant-ph
