TL;DR: This work compares MPS-pretraining circuit architectures (ladder, mixed-canonical, brick-wall) for variational quantum algorithms and extends them to distributed modular processors, showing brick-wall minimizes noise/ZNE overhead and distributed circuits can match or beat single-device performance when communication idle time is short versus coherence time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Distributed quantum processors could scale variational algorithms beyond single devices, but circuit depth, communication overhead, and noise constrain their performance. A principled approach is needed to co-design tensor-network pretraining, circuit scheduling, and communication topology for noisy modular hardware.

Method: The authors compare ladder, mixed-canonical, and brick-wall realizations of matrix-product-state (MPS) pretraining under matched per-layer two-qubit-block resources. They evaluate ideal VQE performance, gradient scales, circuit duration, idle-time decoherence, and ZNE overhead. They then extend MPS-pretrained circuits to modular processors with one communication qubit per QPU, using a nearest-neighbor QPU-path schedule that keeps per-layer depth constant as QPUs are added.

Result: Despite comparable ideal VQE performance and gradient scales, the shallower brick-wall architecture reduces circuit duration, idle-time decoherence, and zero-noise-extrapolation (ZNE) overhead, yielding superior noisy and ZNE-assisted performance. Distributed circuits whose inter-QPU links realize long-range interactions of the target Hamiltonian match or outperform the single-processor brick-wall under noise when communication idle time is short compared with the coherence time.

Conclusion: The findings establish hardware-aware co-design of tensor-network pretraining, circuit scheduling, and communication topology as a guiding principle for variational quantum computation on noisy modular hardware.

Abstract: Distributed quantum processors could scale variational algorithms beyond single devices, but circuit depth, communication overhead, and noise limit their performance. We compare ladder, mixed-canonical, and brick-wall realizations of matrix-product-state (MPS) pretraining with matched per-layer two-qubit-block resources. Despite comparable ideal variational quantum eigensolver performance and gradient scales, the shallower brick-wall architecture reduces circuit duration, idle-time decoherence, and zero-noise-extrapolation (ZNE) overhead, yielding superior noisy and ZNE-assisted performance. We then extend MPS-pretrained circuits to modular processors with one communication qubit per quantum processing unit (QPU); a nearest-neighbor QPU-path schedule keeps the per-layer depth constant as QPUs are added. Distributed circuits whose inter-QPU links realize long-range interactions of the target Hamiltonian match or outperform the single-processor brick-wall under noise when communication idle time is short compared with the coherence time. These results establish hardware-aware co-design of tensor-network pretraining, circuit scheduling, and communication topology as a principle for variational quantum computation on noisy modular hardware.

</details>


### [69] [Dissipation-Sensitivity Trade-Off in Dissipative Bosonic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01112)
*Kohei Yoshimura,Ryusuke Hamazaki*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper derives a universal lower bound on the steady-state entropy production rate in terms of the quantum Fisher information gain over a thermal reference for driven single-mode bosonic systems, showing a trade-off between dissipation cost and metrological sensitivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sensitivity beyond the thermal limit is desirable for quantum metrology, but maintaining such non-thermal states under a thermal environment requires continuous driving and dissipation. The relationship between this thermodynamic cost and the achievable metrological gain was not previously quantified, motivating a link between steady-state thermodynamics and quantum metrological performance.

Method: For Gaussian states, the authors rigorously derive a lower bound on the steady-state entropy production rate expressed in terms of the quantum Fisher information gain relative to a thermal reference state in a driven single-mode bosonic system under a thermal environment. They supplement the analytical derivation with numerical investigations including Kerr nonlinearities to test validity beyond the Gaussian regime.

Result: A universal trade-off is established: greater sensitivity than a thermal state necessitates a corresponding minimum dissipation. The derived bound holds rigorously for Gaussian states, and numerical results with Kerr nonlinearities indicate the bounds can persist beyond the Gaussian regime.

Conclusion: The results establish a new connection between steady-state thermodynamics and quantum metrological performance, indicating an intrinsic dissipation cost for maintaining enhanced sensitivity in thermal environments.

Abstract: We discover a universal trade-off between the dissipation required to maintain a quantum state more sensitive than a thermal one and the sensitivity gain for a driven single-mode bosonic system under a thermal environment. Specifically, for Gaussian states, we rigorously derive a lower bound on the entropy production rate in the steady state in terms of the quantum Fisher information gain relative to a thermal reference state. Numerical investigations of Kerr nonlinearities further support that these bounds can hold beyond the Gaussian regime. Our results establish a new connection between steady-state thermodynamics and quantum metrological performance.

</details>


### [70] [Multivariate Quantum Signal Processing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01125)
*Guang Hao Low*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces Multivariate Quantum Signal Processing (QSP) for designing matrix-valued transformations of multiple noncommuting nonnormal block-encoded matrices, extending univariate QSP/QSVT to multi-oracle settings via a multiport IIR filter transfer-function framework that resolves or improves query complexity of several open multi-oracle problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Univariate QSP/QSVT provide powerful approximation-theoretic guarantees for single block-encoded operators, but many practical problems involve multiple noncommuting, nonnormal block-encoded matrices. Existing multi-oracle techniques lack a unified analytic design methodology with uniform guarantees, efficient circuits, and near-optimal query complexity. The authors aim to bridge this gap by extending QSP to the multivariate setting.

Method: They develop Multivariate QSP with the analytic structure of multiport Infinite-Impulse-Response (IIR) filters, establishing a transfer-function methodology for quantum query algorithms. The framework provides a complete characterization of achievable transfer functions, a principled library of analytic IIR components, and efficiently computable analytic bounds that hold uniformly across the oracle promise. These yield quantum circuits with logarithmic error dependence and near-unity success probability.

Result: The framework improves or resolves per-oracle query complexity for several open multi-oracle problems: (1) Hamiltonian simulation under H=sum_j H_j with weighted quasinorm cost ~t<lambda,C>_{1/2}+O(||C||_1 log(1/eps)); (2) sparse simulation with optimal time-norm Theta(sqrt(d)||H||_{1->2} t) and additive error dependence; (3) solving linear systems and (4) differential equations under minimal matrix assumptions with optimal weighted state-preparation and multi-term cost; (5) ground-state preparation and energy estimation; (6) generalized eigenvalue problems. Applications include exact simulation of downfolded Hamiltonians with reduced cost via fractional dynamic correlation energy from coarse-grained Hamiltonians, classical-data-input-reduction for multiscale graph classification via Kron reduction, and online generalization with anisotropic Kalman filtering of streaming quantum data.

Conclusion: Multivariate QSP generalizes QSP/QSVT to multiple noncommuting nonnormal block-encoded matrices, providing a transfer-function design methodology with approximation-theoretic guarantees paralleling the univariate case. It delivers near-optimal or optimal query complexities for a broad set of open multi-oracle problems, with applications to downfolded Hamiltonian simulation, multiscale graph classification, and online quantum data filtering.

Abstract: We develop Multivariate Quantum Signal Processing for designing matrix-valued transformations of multiple noncommuting nonnormal block-encoded matrices. Our framework equips quantum transducers with the analytic structure of multiport Infinite-Impulse-Response (IIR) filters and establishes a transfer-function methodology for designing quantum query algorithms. Efficiently computable analytic bounds that hold uniformly across the oracle promise yield efficient quantum circuits with logarithmic error dependence and near-unity success probability, paralleling the approximation-theoretic guarantees of univariate QSP/QSVT. We provide a complete characterization of achievable transfer functions and a principled library of analytic IIR components. Our modular designs greatly improve or resolve the per-oracle query complexity of open multi-oracle problems: (1) Hamiltonian simulation under $H=\sum_j H_j$ with weighted quasinorm cost $\approx t\langle\vecλ,\vec C\rangle_{1/2}+\mathcal{O}(\|\vec C\|_1\log(1/ε))$ for block-encoding normalizations $\vecλ$ and costs $\vec C$; (2) sparse simulation with optimal time--norm $Θ(\sqrt{d}\|H\|_{\rm 1\to2}t)$ and additive error dependence; (3) solving linear systems and (4) differential equations under minimal matrix assumptions with optimal weighted state-preparation and multi-term cost; (5) ground-state preparation and energy estimation; (6) generalized eigenvalue problems. We apply these to exactly simulate downfolded Hamiltonians with leading cost $t\langle\vecλ_{\rm eff},\vec C_{\rm eff}\rangle_{1/2}$ further reduced by fractional dynamic correlation energy contributions from coarse-grained Hamiltonians, and show similar classical-data-input-reduction results for multiscale graph classification via Kron reduction. We also show the online generalization with anisotropic Kalman filtering of streaming quantum data.

</details>


### [71] [Classical Hardness of Learning Functions of Hamiltonians](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01141)
*Sota Hashimoto,Akinori Kawachi*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper rigorously proves average-case classical hardness for two distribution-specific Hamiltonian function learning problems, f_cos,π(λ)=cos(πλ) and f_exp,β(λ)=e^{-βλ}, under the assumption that factoring random RSA moduli is average-case hard.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Morohoshi et al. proposed Hamiltonian function learning—predicting Tr[f(H)ρ] from classical descriptions of H and ρ—and gave an efficient quantum algorithm, but left rigorous average-case classical hardness unproven. This paper closes that gap.

Method: The authors establish a classical reduction: any efficient classical randomized learner under squared loss whose hypotheses are classically polynomial-time evaluable for either the cosine or exponential variant would yield a classical randomized polynomial-time algorithm for factoring random RSA moduli.

Result: Under the assumption of average-case hardness of factoring random RSA moduli, no efficient classical randomized learner can solve either distribution-specific Hamiltonian function learning problem when hypotheses are classically evaluable in polynomial time.

Conclusion: This work provides the missing rigorous average-case classical hardness evidence for Hamiltonian function learning, complementing the existing efficient quantum learning algorithm and strengthening the case for quantum advantage in this learning task.

Abstract: Morohoshi, Nakayama, Manabe, and Mitarai proposed a physically motivated quantum machine learning problem in which the goal is to predict quantities of the form $\operatorname{Tr}[f(H)ρ]$ from classical descriptions of a Hamiltonian $H$ and a quantum state $ρ$, where $f$ is an unknown function. We call this problem Hamiltonian function learning in this paper. They constructed an efficient quantum learning algorithm under suitable conditions, while leaving a rigorous proof of average-case classical hardness open. In this paper, we rigorously prove the average-case classical hardness for two distribution-specific Hamiltonian function learning problems for $f_{\cos,π}(λ)=\cos(πλ)$ and $f_{\exp,β}(λ)=e^{-βλ}$ discussed in the paper of Morohoshi et al. under the assumption of the average-case hardness of factoring random RSA moduli. More specifically, we show that an efficient classical randomized learner under squared loss whose output hypotheses are evaluable in classical polynomial time for either problem would yield a classical randomized polynomial-time algorithm for factoring random RSA moduli.

</details>


### [72] [Robustness hierarchy of bipartite quantum correlations under noisy dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01163)
*Shakib Daryanoosh*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper develops a robustness framework for the hierarchy of Bell nonlocality, EPR steering, and entanglement, ordering their robustness measures and linking robustness to noise-induced transition times under Lindblad dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum correlations are fragile under noise, so quantitative measures are needed not only to detect their presence but also to track their degradation during physical evolution.

Method: The authors formulate a robustness framework for the hierarchy of Bell nonlocality, EPR steering, and entanglement, establish an ordering of the corresponding robustness measures, relate robustness to noise-induced transition times, and express the instantaneous robustness decay along Lindblad dynamics as a directional derivative determined by both the evolution and the robustness functional.

Result: Applications to isotropic depolarizing noise and amplitude damping illustrate the resulting resource transition times and the directional dependence of steering robustness, respectively.

Conclusion: The framework offers an approach to characterizing the degradation and lifetime of bipartite quantum correlations under noisy dynamics.

Abstract: Quantum correlations are fragile under noise, motivating quantitative measures that track not only their presence but also their degradation during physical evolution. We develop a robustness framework for the hierarchy of Bell nonlocality, EPR steering, and entanglement, establishing an ordering of the corresponding robustness measures and relating robustness to noise-induced transition times. We further formulate the instantaneous robustness decay along Lindblad dynamics as a directional derivative determined by both the evolution and the robustness functional. Applications to isotropic depolarizing noise and amplitude damping illustrate, respectively, the resulting resource transition times and the directional dependence of steering robustness. The framework provides an approach to characterizing the degradation and lifetime of bipartite quantum correlations under noisy dynamics.

</details>


### [73] [Vector chiral order and dynamical quantum phase transitions in an Ising chain with dimerized anisotropic Gamma interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01167)
*Yu-Hong Yan,Shi-Qiao Wu,Kun-Liang Zhang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Dimerized anisotropic Gamma interactions in a transverse-field Ising chain produce vector chiral order and dynamical quantum phase transitions, with a gapless critical line and quench-dependent periodic or aperiodic critical-time sequences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper explores how dimerized off-diagonal (Gamma) exchange, beyond conventional Ising and transverse-field physics, can enrich equilibrium ordering and dynamical critical phenomena in quantum spin chains.

Method: The authors study a transverse-field Ising chain with dimerized anisotropic Gamma interactions, analyzing the competition between Gamma anisotropy and the transverse field both with and without Ising interactions, using vector spin chirality and the vector chiral order parameter.

Result: The phase diagram contains positive and negative vector chiral phases and a paramagnetic phase. Without Ising interactions, a gapless critical line with no finite-size drift appears, where spin-spin correlations and subsystem entanglement entropy differ from Ising criticality. Dynamical quantum phase transitions show periodic or aperiodic critical-time sequences depending on the quench path.

Conclusion: Dimerized off-diagonal exchange enriches both equilibrium ordering and dynamical critical behavior in a quantum Ising chain, yielding vector chiral order, a distinct gapless critical line, and quench-path-dependent dynamical quantum phase transitions.

Abstract: We investigate a transverse-field Ising chain with dimerized anisotropic Gamma interactions and demonstrate that, both in the absence and presence of Ising interactions, the competition between Gamma anisotropy and the transverse field gives rise to vector chiral order and dynamical quantum phase transitions. The phase diagram comprises positive and negative vector chiral phases and a paramagnetic phase, characterized by the vector spin chirality and the vector chiral order parameter. In the absence of Ising interactions, we identify a gapless critical line that exhibits no finite-size drift, along which the spin-spin correlations and subsystem entanglement entropy display behavior distinct from that of Ising criticality. Furthermore, we find that the critical times of dynamical quantum phase transitions form periodic or aperiodic sequences, depending on the quench path. Our findings highlight how dimerized off-diagonal exchange enriches both equilibrium ordering and dynamical critical behavior in a quantum Ising chain.

</details>


### [74] [Rapid mixing of quantum spin chains at any finite temperature](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01190)
*Leeseok Kim*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A quasi-local quantum Gibbs sampler based on the Petz recovery map on constant-length contiguous blocks mixes rapidly to the Gibbs state of any 1D finite-range Hamiltonian at all finite temperatures in O(log(n/ε)) time, with a proof via order-1 quantum Wasserstein distance; it also yields O(polylog(n/ε))-depth quantum circuits for Gibbs state preparation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum Gibbs samplers are key tools for preparing thermal states, but rigorous mixing-time guarantees for local, experimentally feasible samplers are scarce. The paper aims to establish fast (logarithmic) mixing for a quasi-local sampler and to enable efficient circuit preparation of 1D Gibbs states.

Method: The sampler performs each update by tracing out a contiguous block of constant length and reconstructing it with the Petz recovery map, following early proposals by Kastoryano and Brandão. The mixing analysis directly bounds the distance between the updated state and the target Gibbs state using the quantum Wasserstein distance of order 1.

Result: The sampler mixes to the Gibbs state of any 1D finite-range Hamiltonian at all finite temperatures in O(log(n/ε)) time, and Gibbs states can be prepared with O(polylog(n/ε)) circuit depth.

Conclusion: The authors hope these techniques—particularly the Wasserstein-distance-based analysis—will facilitate the analysis and design of other quantum Gibbs samplers, extending beyond the 1D finite-range, finite-temperature setting.

Abstract: We prove that a quasi-local quantum Gibbs sampler rapidly mixes to the Gibbs state of any 1D finite-range Hamiltonian at all finite temperatures in $\mathcal{O}\left(\log (n/\varepsilon)\right)$ time. Each update traces out a contiguous block of constant length and reconstructs it using the Petz recovery map, following early proposals for quantum heat-bath Gibbs samplers by Kastoryano and Brandão~\cite{kastoryano2016quantum}. Our proof directly bounds the distance between the updated state and the target Gibbs state using quantum Wasserstein distance of order $1$. We further provide $\mathcal{O}(\text{polylog}(n/\varepsilon))$ depth of quantum circuits to prepare these Gibbs states. We hope these techniques will facilitate the analysis and design of other Gibbs samplers.

</details>


### [75] [Fermionic Gaussian Scrooge Ensembles in Deep Thermalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01209)
*Ning Sun,Pengfei Zhang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces the fermionic Gaussian Scrooge ensemble to describe deep thermalization in free-fermion systems, showing it emerges universally where chaotic systems would instead converge to the standard Scrooge ensemble.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep thermalization studies the projected ensemble of pure states generated by measuring part of a many-body wave function. In chaotic systems this ensemble universally converges to the Scrooge ensemble (maximally random given the average density matrix). Free-fermion systems are nonchaotic and constrained to Gaussian states, so the standard Scrooge ensemble does not apply, motivating a Gaussian analogue tailored to free-fermion dynamics.

Method: The authors define the fermionic Gaussian Scrooge ensemble as a distortion of the Gaussian Haar ensemble by the average density matrix, and analyze its moments, revealing an enlarged symmetry of Gaussian states in the replicated Hilbert space. They test this ensemble in two settings: an analytical proof for the SYK$_2$ model at arbitrary evolution times, and numerical simulations of random Gaussian circuits with charge conservation.

Result: The projected ensemble of the SYK$_2$ model is analytically shown to follow the fermionic Gaussian Scrooge ensemble at arbitrary evolution times, and numerical evidence shows the same ensemble emerges at sufficiently long times in random Gaussian circuits with charge conservation.

Conclusion: The fermionic Gaussian Scrooge ensemble provides a universal description of deep thermalization in free-fermion systems, extending deep-thermalization theory beyond chaotic dynamics to the Gaussian-constrained regime.

Abstract: Measuring part of a many-body wave function generates a projected ensemble of pure quantum states on the unmeasured subsystem. Recent advances in deep thermalization have shown that, in chaotic systems, this ensemble universally converges to the maximally random ensemble compatible with its average density matrix, known as the Scrooge ensemble. By contrast, free-fermion systems are nonchaotic, and their quantum states are constrained to remain Gaussian. Motivated by this distinction, we introduce the fermionic Gaussian Scrooge ensemble to describe deep thermalization in generic free-fermion systems. This ensemble is defined as a distortion of the Gaussian Haar ensemble by the average density matrix, and its moments reveal an enlarged symmetry of Gaussian states in the replicated Hilbert space. We demonstrate the emergence of the fermionic Gaussian Scrooge ensemble in two complementary settings: (1) we analytically prove that the projected ensemble of the SYK$_2$ model follows the fermionic Gaussian Scrooge ensemble at arbitrary evolution times; (2) we provide numerical evidence that it emerges at sufficiently long times in random Gaussian circuits with charge conservation. Our results establish the fermionic Gaussian Scrooge ensemble as a universal description of deep thermalization in free-fermion systems.

</details>


### [76] [Experimental quantification of quantum coherence for a set of quantum states](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01227)
*Tianle Zheng,Liangsheng Li,Wenting Zhou,Chengjie Zhang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work experimentally verifies basis-independent quantum coherence for a set of quantum states using a Sagnac interferometer, showing excellent agreement with theory and revealing that BB84 states exhibit maximal set coherence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum coherence is typically studied for individual states, but a basis-independent framework for coherence across a set of quantum states is less explored experimentally. The authors aim to directly verify such a set-coherence theory and connect it to practical protocols like BB84 and quantum secure direct communication.

Method: The authors perform a direct experimental verification of quantum coherence for sets of quantum states in a basis-independent manner. They use a Sagnac interferometer to experimentally quantify the quantum coherence for two sets of experimental states, comparing the measurements with theoretical predictions. They also introduce a novel application of set coherence to the BB84 protocol.

Result: The experimental results match the theory of quantum coherence for a set of quantum states perfectly. The authors show that the states employed in the BB84 protocol exhibit maximal set coherence.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate the validity and applicability of basis-independent set coherence, pave the way for wider applications of quantum coherence for sets of quantum states, and suggest utility in quantum key distribution protocols and probabilistic quantum cloning.

Abstract: We present a direct experimental verification of quantum coherence for a set of quantum states in a basis-independent manner. We find that the theory of quantum coherence for a set of quantum states matches our experimental results perfectly and can be applied to protocols like BB84 and quantum secure direct communication. Using a Sagnac interferometer, we experimentally quantify the quantum coherence for two sets of experimental states. Moreover, we introduce a novel application of set coherence, illustrating that the states employed in the BB84 protocol exhibit maximal set coherence. Our results pave the way for wider applications of quantum coherence for a set of quantum states, including quantum key distribution protocols and probabilistic quantum cloning.

</details>


### [77] [Linear-Time Encodable Quantum Codes near the CSS GV Bound](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01277)
*Rachel Yun Zhang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper constructs quantum CSS codes achieving a rate-distance tradeoff approaching the CSS Gilbert–Varshamov (GV) bound, with a log-depth, linear-gate encoding circuit inspired by classical repeat-multiple-accumulate codes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum CSS codes with good rate-distance tradeoff are central to quantum error correction, but existing constructions often lack practical, low-complexity encoders. The goal is to close the gap toward the CSS GV bound while keeping encoding efficient.

Method: The construction is inspired by Brehm and Resch and is a quantum analogue of classical repeat-multiple-accumulate (RMA) codes. The encoder consists of a single constant-depth outer quantum circuit followed by several classical accumulation and inverse-accumulation layers, yielding a quantum circuit of logarithmic depth and a linear number of gates.

Result: The resulting quantum CSS codes achieve a rate-distance tradeoff approaching the CSS GV bound, and are encodable by a logarithmic-depth circuit with a linear number of gates.

Conclusion: Quantum CSS codes with near-optimal rate-distance tradeoff and highly efficient, simple encoders can be constructed by adapting classical repeat-multiple-accumulate ideas, providing a practical route to good quantum error-correcting codes.

Abstract: We construct quantum CSS codes with rate-distance tradeoff approaching the CSS GV bound, which can be encoded by a quantum circuit of logarithmic depth and a linear number of gates. Our construction is heavily inspired by a work of Brehm and Resch and can be viewed as a quantum analogue of classical repeat multiple accumulate codes. The encoder has a particularly simple structure, consisting of a single constant depth outer quantum circuit followed by several classical accumulation and inverse-accumulation layers.

</details>


### [78] [Preservability of Measurement Incompatibility: Purification, Activation, and a No-Go Theorem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01327)
*Chao-Hsien Wu,Franco Nori,Huan-Yu Ku*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work studies the purification and activation of measurement incompatibility preservability, introducing robustness-based resource monotones, proving a no-go theorem for pre-filtering activation, and showing stochastic activation via post-filtering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Measurement incompatibility is a fundamental quantum resource, but system-environment interactions can degrade or destroy it (measurement-incompatibility-annihilating channels). While the ability of noisy dynamics to preserve incompatibility has been characterized via the resource theory of measurement incompatibility preservability, it remained unclear how to purify this preservation and how to activate it from an annihilating channel.

Method: The authors formulate robustness-based resource monotones as figures of merit within the resource theory of measurement incompatibility preservability. They analyze pre-filtering and post-filtering operations acting on the channel to test whether preservation of measurement incompatibility can be enhanced or activated.

Result: Pre-filtering operations can strengthen measurement incompatibility preservability but cannot activate it from an incompatibility-annihilating channel (a no-go theorem). In contrast, an incompatibility-annihilating channel can be stochastically activated via post-filtering operations.

Conclusion: The results establish a practical framework for exploiting measurement incompatibility in quantum information processing, distinguishing the capabilities of pre- versus post-filtering in purifying and activating incompatibility preservability.

Abstract: Measurement incompatibility is a fundamental quantum resource that enables advantages in many quantum information tasks, including cryptography and communication. However, unavoidable interactions between a system and its environment can degrade or even completely destroy measurement incompatibility; such a process is referred to as a measurement-incompatibility-annihilating channel. Recently, the capability of noisy quantum dynamics to preserve measurement incompatibility has been characterized within the resource theory of measurement incompatibility preservability. This motivates studying how to purify the preservation of measurement incompatibility and how to activate it from a measurement-incompatibility-annihilating channel. To this end, we first introduce our figures of merit as robustness-based resource monotones within this resource theory. We demonstrate that while pre-filtering operations can strengthen measurement incompatibility preservability, they cannot activate it from an incompatibility-annihilating channel, establishing a no-go theorem. Furthermore, we explicitly demonstrate that an incompatibility-annihilating channel can be stochastically activated via post-filtering operations. Our results provide a practical framework for exploiting measurement incompatibility in quantum information processing.

</details>


### [79] [Achieving Identical Stored Energy in Cascaded Collisional Quantum Battery Charging: Analytical Result](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01332)
*Longyan Gong,Jing Zhang,Yongtao Li*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work studies a two-qubit quantum battery under cascaded collision models and proposes adaptive measurement on the charger after each collision to suppress trajectory-dependent stored-energy fluctuations, achieving complete suppression for excited-state chargers but proving full suppression impossible for general superposition chargers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Charging uniformity across distinct trials is essential for reliable quantum batteries, yet intrinsic quantum stochasticity fundamentally threatens this goal, motivating a scheme to control trajectory-dependent fluctuations in stored energy.

Method: The authors analyze a two-qubit quantum battery within cascaded collision models and introduce an adaptive measurement on the charger after each collision, producing two conditional battery states with equal expected stored energy. They analytically derive conditions for suppressing stored-energy fluctuations for chargers in the excited state versus general superposition states, and support the analysis with numerical simulations.

Result: For chargers prepared in the excited state, complete suppression of trajectory-dependent stored-energy fluctuations is analytically proven attainable. For chargers prepared in general superposition states, full suppression is shown to be fundamentally prohibited due to battery-phase-dependent recursive dynamics. Numerical simulations agree quantitatively with the theoretical predictions.

Conclusion: Adaptive measurement on the charger offers a feasible pathway toward high-uniformity quantum-battery charging, though achieving full charging uniformity is subject to intrinsic constraints depending on the charger's initial state.

Abstract: Charging uniformity across distinct trials is an essential requirement for reliable quantum batteries. Nevertheless, intrinsic quantum stochasticity poses a fundamental challenge to this goal. This work investigates a two-qubit quantum battery within the framework of cascaded collision models. We propose an adaptive measurement on the charger after each collision, yielding two conditional battery states of equal expected stored energy. For chargers prepared in the excited state, we analytically prove that complete suppression of trajectory-dependent stored-energy fluctuations is attainable; for chargers prepared in general superposition states, our theory reveals that full suppression is fundamentally prohibited owing to battery-phase-dependent recursive dynamics. Numerical simulations are in excellent quantitative agreement with theoretical predictions. These findings provide a feasible pathway toward high-uniformity quantum-battery charging.

</details>


### [80] [Vacuum entanglement in a time-dependent electric field](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01394)
*Álvaro Álvarez-Domínguez,Luis J. Garay,Mercedes Martín-Benito,Iker Sanz-González*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper studies how a time-dependent homogeneous electric field affects quantum correlations between two disjoint regions in the vacuum of a charged scalar field, linking correlation dynamics to Schwinger pair production.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how external electromagnetic backgrounds, through the Schwinger effect, modify the quantum correlations and entanglement structure of the charged-field vacuum across spatially separated regions.

Method: Constructing localized observables in two disjoint regions and tracking the time evolution of vacuum correlations under a time-dependent homogeneous electric field, relating the dynamics to particle-antiparticle creation via the Schwinger effect.

Result: The electric field increases the mixedness of the states and enhances total correlations, while the distillable entanglement between the regions decreases, vanishing at a finite time for sufficiently intense pulses in the non-perturbative pair-production regime. The external background also induces anisotropy, giving the correlation distribution a nontrivial directional dependence.

Conclusion: Strong electric fields degrade distillable entanglement between disjoint regions even as total correlations grow, with entanglement disappearing at finite times for intense pulses; background-induced anisotropy makes the correlation structure directionally dependent.

Abstract: We analyze the correlations of the vacuum state of a charged scalar field by constructing localized observables in two disjoint regions. We consider a time-dependent homogeneous electric field and examine the time evolution of correlations by relating it with the particle-antiparticle creation through the Schwinger effect. Our results show that the electric field increases the mixedness of the states and enhances total correlations, while the distillable entanglement among the regions decreases, vanishing at a finite time for sufficiently intense pulses within the non-perturbative pair-production regime. We also discuss the effect of anisotropy induced by the external background, showing that the distribution of correlations acquires a nontrivial directional dependence.

</details>


### [81] [Optimal initial states for quantum Fisher information in linearized cavity optomechanics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01417)
*Wangjun Lu,Qing Yu,Ying Li,Cuilu Zhai,Rui Zhang,Zhao-Hui Peng,Shiqing Tang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper determines optimal initial states for estimating the single-photon coupling in a linearized cavity-optomechanical system via quantum Fisher information, showing that in the red-detuned regime the optimum reduces to maximizing the variance of the excitation-exchange operator, with closed-form solutions including Fock, phase-matched squeezed vacuum, and two-mode NOON-like states, and extends the analysis to the blue sideband, cavity loss, and a realistic experiment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Estimating the single-photon coupling is central to cavity optomechanics, but the optimal probe states for this metrological task had not been fully characterized. A rigorous optimization based on quantum Fisher information was needed to identify the fundamental sensitivity limits and the states that attain them.

Method: The authors linearize the cavity-optomechanical Hamiltonian and compute the quantum Fisher information for the single-photon coupling. In the red-detuned (beam-splitter) regime, for pure inputs the Fisher information equals four times the variance of the excitation-exchange operator, reducing optimization to variance maximization with a closed-form solution. Optimization is performed over the mechanical reference state (Fock references, phase-matched squeezed vacuum via a Lagrange-dual bound), jointly over both modes at fixed total excitation number, and extended to the blue sideband, cavity loss, and a state-of-the-art quantum-coherent coupling experiment. Exact diagonalization in truncated Fock spaces is used for verification.

Result: In the red-detuned regime with a mechanical Fock reference the probe is degenerate—any state saturating the photon-number constraint is optimal—and a Fock reference with many phonons enhances per-photon sensitivity linearly. For a fixed arbitrary mechanical reference the optimum is a phase-matched squeezed vacuum. Jointly optimizing both modes gives a Heisenberg bound quadratic in energy, saturated by equal superpositions of the two extremal generator eigenstates (a two-mode NOON state after a balanced beam splitter), while balanced product Fock states achieve only linear enhancement. The blue sideband has an unbounded two-mode-squeezing spectrum, making the entangled optimum impossible and the balanced product Fock state exactly optimal at fixed energy. Under cavity loss information accumulation becomes linear on the cavity-lifetime timescale, with a renewal-optimal duration of about two and a half lifetimes. For a state-of-the-art experiment the per-hour relative precision lies between one part in a million and ten parts in a million, with number and thermal references most robust.

Conclusion: Optimal initial states for estimating the single-photon coupling in a linearized cavity-optomechanical system are fully characterized by quantum Fisher information. The red-detuned regime admits closed-form optima, including linear per-photon enhancement with Fock references and a quadratic Heisenberg bound saturated by two-mode NOON-like states; the single-photon input is the simplest saturating case. The blue sideband and cavity-loss scenarios are also solved, and a realistic quantum-coherent coupling experiment yields per-hour relative precisions in the 10^-6 to 10^-5 range.

Abstract: We find the optimal initial states of a linearized cavity-optomechanical system for estimating the single-photon coupling by quantum Fisher information. In the red-detuned (beam-splitter) regime the interaction exchanges excitations between the two modes, and for pure inputs the Fisher information is four times the variance of the excitation-exchange operator, reducing the optimization to variance maximization with a closed-form solution. With the mechanical mode in a Fock state the probe is degenerate: any state saturating the photon-number constraint is optimal, and a Fock reference with many phonons enhances the per-photon sensitivity linearly. For an arbitrary fixed mechanical reference it becomes a phase-matched squeezed vacuum, from an exact bound on that coherence obtained by a Lagrange-dual argument. Jointly optimizing both modes at fixed total excitation number, the quantum Fisher information obeys a Heisenberg bound quadratic in the energy, saturated by equal superpositions of the two extremal eigenstates of the generator, equivalently a two-mode NOON state after a balanced beam splitter; balanced product Fock states reach only a linear enhancement. Exact diagonalization in truncated Fock spaces confirms all the results, the familiar single-photon input being the simplest saturating case. Three extensions are worked out: the blue sideband has an unbounded two-mode-squeezing spectrum, so the entangled optimum becomes impossible and the balanced product Fock state is exactly optimal at definite energy; under cavity loss the information accumulation turns linear on the cavity-lifetime timescale, at a renewal-optimal duration of about two and a half lifetimes; and for a state-of-the-art quantum-coherent coupling experiment the per-hour relative precision lies between one part in a million and ten parts in a million, number and thermal references being the most robust.

</details>


### [82] [Robust and leakage-resilient device-independent oblivious transfer in MiniQCryp](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01421)
*Zhili Chen,Rahul Jain,YaoNan Zhang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Assuming post-quantum one-way functions, the authors construct device-independent (DI) oblivious transfer (OT) and bit commitment from untrusted quantum devices operated by honest parties using only trusted classical computation. The protocols are simulation-secure against quantum polynomial-time adversaries, sequentially composable, and tolerate constant or inverse-polylogarithmic fault rates depending on the regime. The OT is complete for secure computation, yielding DI protocols for any efficiently computable classical functionality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Device-independent cryptography offers strong security guarantees by treating quantum devices as untrusted, but constructing DI oblivious transfer and bit commitment under minimal complexity assumptions with composable security and fault tolerance remains challenging. The paper aims to achieve these primitives assuming only post-quantum one-way functions, supporting non-IID devices and arbitrary entanglement.

Method: A single protocol skeleton is used for both OT and commitment in two regimes. One regime assumes isolated laboratories with coordinate-local measurements in the honest receiver's device, tolerating a constant rate of honest-device faults; the other allows polylogarithmically many qubits of adaptive leakage between laboratories and arbitrary joint measurements, tolerating an inverse-polylogarithmic fault rate. Each elementary DI call uses a fresh isolated batch of polylogarithmically many device coordinates, with polynomial total device use. Techniques include a commit-and-prove functionality, disjoint audits, an affine consistency check, classical equivocation for the commitment extractor, and a selector-aware parallel-repetition bound for the Magic Square game derived from the two-round threshold theorem of Kundu and Tan.

Result: DI OT and bit commitment are achieved with simulation-based security against quantum polynomial-time adversaries and sequential composition with efficient simulators. The commitment has efficient simulators against both parties and yields DI coin tossing with abort. Since OT is complete for secure computation, the construction yields a DI protocol with abort for every efficiently computable classical functionality on a fixed number of parties, secure against static corruption of any proper subset.

Conclusion: The work establishes DI OT and bit commitment from post-quantum one-way functions, with composable security, fault tolerance, and full completeness for secure computation, thereby enabling device-independent protocols for general classical functionalities under static corruptions.

Abstract: Assuming post-quantum one-way functions, we construct device-independent (DI) oblivious transfer (OT) and bit commitment: honest parties use only trusted classical computation to operate untrusted quantum devices, which may share arbitrary entanglement and behave non-IID. Security is simulation-based against quantum polynomial-time adversaries and composes sequentially with efficient simulators. One protocol skeleton serves both, in two regimes. With isolated laboratories and coordinate-local measurements in the honest receiver's device, it tolerates a constant rate of honest-device faults. With polylogarithmically many qubits of adaptive leakage between the laboratories and arbitrary joint measurements, it tolerates an inverse-polylogarithmic rate. Each elementary DI call uses a fresh, isolated batch of polylogarithmically many device coordinates, and total device use in the compiled OT protocol is polynomial. The commitment has efficient simulators against both parties and yields DI coin tossing with abort.
  Because OT is complete for secure computation, the construction yields a DI protocol, with abort, for every efficiently computable classical functionality on a fixed number of parties, secure against static corruption of any proper subset of them.
  The commitment's extractor changes a public parity relation through classical equivocation and leaves the device execution, hence its leakage, unchanged. A commit-and-prove functionality, disjoint audits, and an affine consistency check link the certified correlations to ideal OT. Sender security rests on a selector-aware parallel-repetition bound for the Magic Square game, which we derive from the two-round threshold theorem of Kundu and Tan.

</details>


### [83] [Unflattening by Flattening -- How Input Distributions Shape Output Variance in Angle-Encoded Circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01446)
*Melvin Strobl,Gabriel Mejia,Eileen Kuehn,Achim Streit*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work shows that for angle-encoded product states, output variability depends on "input purity" (overlap with the circuit's dynamical Lie algebra), giving variance guarantees of order 1/n for matchgate and off-diagonal families, and constructs a classical preprocessing certificate that predicts mean output variance before circuit execution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Barren plateaus cause loss gradients in parameterized quantum circuits to vanish exponentially with qubit count, obstructing training. The authors seek to characterize how the input distribution, rather than only the circuit architecture, governs output variation and trainability.

Method: They introduce algebraic input purity, the input's overlap with the circuit's dynamical Lie algebra, and analyze output variance under specified readouts with Haar or exact group 2-design sampling. They examine matchgate circuits on n qubits, the off-diagonal family, independent uniform angles, and binary/ternary weighted encodings. A classical preprocessing certificate based on a shared random rotation and a computable mean-purity threshold is constructed, validated by numerical experiments.

Result: Matchgate circuits retain output variance of order 1/n for every pure product input. The off-diagonal family yields zero output on computational-basis inputs at any depth and for every parameter choice; a count of diagonal Pauli strings identifies these zero-output endpoints. Independent uniform angles give mean variance of the same order as matchgates. For any fixed dataset of nonzero inputs with at least 2n coordinates, the certificate accepts a shared random rotation when dataset mean purity passes a computable threshold, certifying mean output variance of order 1/n before execution with a constant expected number of trials. Encodings recover independent-angle mean purity from one uniform scalar input.

Conclusion: Input distribution, captured by algebraic input purity, controls output variance and can be certified classically beforehand. However, the guarantee concerns output variance averaged over inputs, whereas successful learning additionally depends on the task and on information preserved by the encoding.

Abstract: Barren plateaus hinder training of parameterized quantum circuits by making loss gradients exponentially small in the number of qubits. For angle-encoded product states, we show how the input distribution affects output variation through algebraic input purity, i.e. the input's overlap with the circuit's dynamical Lie algebra. With the specified readouts and Haar or exact group 2-design sampling, matchgate circuits on $n$ qubits retain output variance of order $1/n$ for every pure product input. The off-diagonal family instead has zero output on computational-basis inputs at any depth and for every parameter choice. Independent uniform angles yield mean variance of the same order as matchgates. A count of diagonal Pauli strings identifies these zero-output endpoints. For any fixed dataset of nonzero inputs with at least $2n$ coordinates, we construct a classical preprocessing certificate. A shared random rotation is accepted only when the dataset's mean purity passes a computable threshold. This certifies mean output variance of order $1/n$ for the off-diagonal family before circuit execution, with a constant expected number of trials. Binary and ternary weighted encodings also recover the independent-angle mean purity from one uniform scalar input. Numerical experiments examine training behavior and extensions to larger algebras. The guarantee concerns output variance averaged over inputs, while successful learning also depends on the task and information preserved by the encoding.

</details>


### [84] [Unbounded separation between definite and indefinite causal order in finite-dimensional quantum metrology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01462)
*Yanglin Hu,Zi-Shen Li,Giulio Chiribella,Yuxiang Yang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper shows that indefinite causal order yields arbitrarily large (unbounded) advantages over definite causal order in finite-dimensional quantum metrology, specifically in the finite sample regime, for estimating a geometric phase associated with discrete displacement operators.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Indefinite causal order is known to provide an unbounded advantage in measuring a geometric phase of a harmonic oscillator, but this is tied to infinite dimensions. Its finite-dimensional analogue was thought to be limited to bounded constant-factor advantages, leaving open whether genuinely unbounded advantages can exist in finite dimensions.

Method: The authors analyze estimation of a geometric phase associated with two sets of N displacements generated by discrete position and momentum operators on a d-dimensional quantum system. They compare strategies with indefinite versus definite causal order, and prove an approximate Weyl relation for discrete Gaussian wavepackets as a technical tool.

Result: They establish an unbounded separation: for any constant R, there exist N and d=Ω(N²) such that an indefinite-order strategy uses an initial probe with R times less energy than any definite-order strategy achieving the same mean squared error, provided the number of shots ν=O(exp(πd/16)/poly(d)). The energy saving grows arbitrarily large with the problem parameters.

Conclusion: Contrary to recent expectations, arbitrarily large advantages from indefinite causal order do arise for finite-dimensional systems, at least in the finite sample (bounded shots) regime, with energy savings scaling without bound. The approximate Weyl relation for discrete Gaussian wavepackets is a result of independent technical interest.

Abstract: Indefinite causal order is known to offer enhancements in quantum metrology, notably including an unbounded advantage in the measurement of a geometric phase of a harmonic oscillator. This advantage, however, is specific to the infinite dimensional setting, and its finite dimensional analogue remains elusive, with recent findings suggesting that advantages in finite dimensions may be fundamentally limited to bounded constant factors. Here we show that, in fact, arbitrarily large advantages arise for finite dimensional systems in the finite sample regime. Specifically, we establish an unbounded separation between definite and indefinite causal order in the estimation of a geometric phase associated to two sets of $N$ displacements generated by discrete position and momentum operators on a $d$-dimensional quantum system: for any given constant $R$, there exist values of $N$ and $d=Ω(N^2)$ such that a strategy with indefinite order uses an initial probe with $R$ times less energy than the probe required by every strategy with definite order achieving the same mean squared error, whenever the number of measurement shots $ν$ is bounded as $ν=\mathcal{O}(\exp(πd/16)/\mathrm{poly}(d))$. In other words, indefinite order offers an energy saving that grows arbitrarily large with the parameters of the problem. To prove this result, we establish an approximate Weyl relation for discrete Gaussian wavepackets, which is of independent technical interest.

</details>


### [85] [Zero- Versus Infinite-Temperature Damping in Variational Quantum Circuits: Feature Scale, Sampling Cost, and Frame Gauge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01466)
*Vu-Quoc-Minh Nguyen,Tuan-Vu Truong,Hoang-Long Nguyen,Trung-Khanh Le*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The Pauli twirl of amplitude damping is shown to equal generalized amplitude damping at infinite temperature; comparing AD and its twirls in variational circuits isolates a zero-temperature feature-scale bias that is largely removable by a trainable output scale but reappears as a measurement-shot cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Amplitude damping (AD) is non-unital, whereas its Pauli twirl is unital; understanding how this difference affects variational circuits is important because AD is the dominant noise in superconducting hardware, yet many error-mitigation and noise-modeling approaches rely on twirled (unital) approximations. The paper aims to isolate and quantify the specific effect of the non-unital part of AD on variational quantum circuits.

Method: The authors analytically characterize Pauli twirling of AD, identifying it with generalized amplitude damping at infinite temperature (retaining the contraction, removing the non-unital term). They compare AD versus its twirls in variational/classifier circuits using random parameters and trainable output scales, deriving closed-form expressions for feature floors under strong damping and the temperature dependence tanh(ħω/2k_BT) at fixed T1, with simulations up to eight qubits.

Result: For random parameters features under AD settle on a floor (set by the last layer at strong damping) with a closed form; under the twirls features shrink by a constant factor per layer. A trainable output scale removes most accuracy differences, leaving AD ahead of its twirls by at most ~3 percentage points. However, the removed gap reappears as measurement-shot cost: with 10^3 shots per image, a four-qubit classifier under AD at p=0.3 stays within 1.5 points of noiseless accuracy, while twirled classifiers lose up to 33. At weaker damping the separation depth grows roughly as (np)^{-1} ln(1/p). The damping direction is a gauge when damping follows complete entangling layers with trainable circuit boundaries, but becomes physical inside a decomposed two-qubit gate in an eigensolver.

Conclusion: The non-unital zero-temperature bias of AD mainly acts through the scale of features, making it largely gauge-like and correctable by a trainable output scale in variational settings. Nevertheless, the underlying difference resurfaces as a substantial measurement-shot overhead, and it becomes physically consequential inside decomposed two-qubit gates (e.g., in eigensolvers), indicating that twirled approximations can be misleading depending on circuit structure and readout constraints.

Abstract: The Pauli twirl of amplitude damping (AD) is generalized amplitude damping at infinite temperature: it keeps the contraction of AD and removes its non-unital term, so comparing the two in variational circuits isolates the zero-temperature bias, which acts mainly through the scale of the features. For random parameters, features under AD settle on a floor, which at strong damping is set by the last layer, has a closed form, and at fixed $T_1$ falls with temperature as $\tanh(\hbarω/2k_BT)$; under the twirls they shrink by a constant factor per layer, up to eight qubits. A trainable output scale removes most of the resulting accuracy differences, leaving AD ahead of its twirls by at most about three percentage points in our simulations; what it removes reappears as a cost in measurement shots: trained and tested with $10^3$ shots per image, a four-qubit classifier under AD at $p=0.3$ stays within 1.5 points of noiseless accuracy, while the twirled classifiers lose up to 33. At weaker damping the separation depth grows roughly as $(np)^{-1}\ln(1/p)$. The damping direction is a gauge when the damping follows complete entangling layers and the circuit boundaries are trainable; in an eigensolver it becomes physical inside a decomposed two-qubit gate.

</details>


### [86] [Continuous-Process Randomized Compilation for Quantum Process Tensors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01521)
*He Wang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces continuous-process randomized compilation (CPRC) in the continuous process tensor (cPT) framework, extending randomized compiling from discrete gate boundaries to continuous-time, memory-bearing quantum processes to tailor non-Markovian errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional discrete randomized compiling only tailors errors with random control frames at logical-gate boundaries, so it cannot capture intra-instrument control evolution or persistent system-environment coupling. In memory-bearing environments, errors correlate across time rather than being confined to single gates.

Method: The authors build CPRC on the cPT framework, unifying random unitary control trajectories, pointwise-covariant finite-duration instruments, and environmental dynamics on a single time axis. They study four protocols: fixed Pauli refresh, bounded Cayley paths, unitary Brownian motion, and Ornstein-Uhlenbeck driving. The theory is numerically validated in a qubit model via output probabilities, operator conditional mutual information, and all two-particle coefficients for qubit and thermal Drude baths.

Result: Three key results are obtained: (1) pointwise covariance makes each trajectory exactly reproduce the target logic in the absence of system-environment coupling, while a closed-frame counterexample shows endpoint-only compensation induces first-order instrument errors; (2) independent Pauli conjugations over full intervals diagonalize error histories at boundaries, but classical labels retain environment-induced correlations, confirmed by an exact static-bath solution; (3) a total-variation error bound is derived for full adaptive output records under bounded centered coupling and mixing conditions. Convergence of trajectory-averaged cPT coefficients is proven in fixed particle sectors for finite non-Gaussian environments and Gaussian baths with time-integrable covariance, including the uncut Drude spectrum.

Conclusion: The work extends randomized compiling from discrete circuits to continuous-time memory-bearing quantum processes, offering a unified framework, quantitative tools, implementable schemes, and benchmarks for non-Markovian error tailoring.

Abstract: Traditional discrete randomized compiling (RC) tailors errors with random control frames at logical-gate boundaries, but fails to capture intra-instrument control evolution or persistent system-environment coupling. In memory-bearing environments, errors correlate across times, not just single gates. We build continuous-process randomized compilation (CPRC) in the continuous process tensor (cPT) framework, unifying random unitary control trajectories, pointwise-covariant finite-duration instruments, and environmental dynamics on one time axis. We study four protocols: fixed Pauli refresh, bounded Cayley paths, unitary Brownian motion, Ornstein-Uhlenbeck driving. We obtain three key results: (1) Pointwise covariance ensures each trajectory exactly reproduces the target logic without system-environment coupling; a closed-frame counterexample proves endpoint-only compensation causes first-order instrument errors. (2) Independent Pauli conjugations over full intervals diagonalize error histories at boundaries, but classical labels retain environment-induced correlations, confirmed by an exact static-bath solution. (3) We derive a total-variation error bound for full adaptive output records under bounded centered coupling and mixing conditions. We prove convergence of trajectory-averaged cPT coefficients in fixed particle sectors for finite non-Gaussian environments and Gaussian baths with time-integrable covariance, including the uncut Drude spectrum. We numerically validate the theory in a qubit model via output probabilities, operator conditional mutual information, and all two-particle coefficients for both qubit and thermal Drude baths. This work extends RC from discrete circuits to continuous-time memory-bearing quantum processes, offering a unified framework, quantitative tools, implementable schemes, and benchmarks for non-Markovian error tailoring.

</details>


### [87] [Efficient learning of quantum interactions from thermal metastable states](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01538)
*Bingrun Wang,Qi Ye,Chi-Fang Chen*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper reformulates finite-temperature many-body learning from exact Gibbs states (intractable to prepare) to metastable states of detailed-balanced Lindbladians, extending Gibbs-state learning algorithms to achieve nearly optimal sample and computational complexity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning quantum interactions from finite-temperature many-body systems is central to quantum platforms. Prior rigorous, efficient protocols assume exact Gibbs states as input, but exact Gibbs states are computationally intractable to prepare and may not faithfully represent generic finite-temperature quantum systems. Real systems coupled to a heat bath often remain stuck at an approximate stationary (metastable) state long before true equilibration, motivating a more physically and algorithmically consistent formulation.

Method: The authors formulate learning from metastable states of detailed-balanced master equations (Lindbladians) arising from system-bath interactions. They distill the algorithmic mechanism and structural condition underlying Gibbs-state learning and extend it in full to metastable states.

Result: The proposed approach attains nearly optimal sample and computational complexity in terms of system size and precision. The work also sharpens notions of metastability and develops a unified framework for finite-temperature learning.

Conclusion: Learning from metastable Lindbladian states offers a physically and algorithmically consistent alternative to Gibbs-state learning, achieving nearly optimal complexity and providing a unified framework for finite-temperature quantum learning.

Abstract: Learning quantum interactions from finite-temperature many-body systems is a central task in emerging quantum platforms. Recently, the problem of learning from lattice quantum Gibbs states has found rigorous, efficient protocols. Nevertheless, exact Gibbs states, as the input premise, are in fact computationally intractable to prepare and may not faithfully represent generic finite-temperature quantum systems. In contrast, a system coupled to a heat bath can be stuck at an approximate stationary state (metastable state) long before it truly equilibrates. Here, we formulate a physically and algorithmically consistent alternative: learning from such metastable states of detailed-balanced master equations (Lindbladians) arising from system-bath interactions. We distill the algorithmic mechanism and structural condition underlying Gibbs-state learning and extend it in full to metastable states, attaining nearly optimal sample and computational complexity (in the system size and the precision). More broadly, we sharpen notions of metastability and develop a unified framework for finite-temperature learning.

</details>


### [88] [Molecular Dynamics with Nuclear Effects on Quantum Computers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01549)
*Lukas Haßfurth,Juliane Heitkämper,Elias Walter,Birger Horstmann*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A hybrid quantum-classical ab-initio molecular dynamics algorithm incorporating nuclear quantum effects via the nuclear-electronic orbital method, using VQE on quantum hardware for energies and forces, reproduces experimental vibrational spectra and renders proton shuttling in the Zundel ion barrierless.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nuclear quantum effects are essential for describing proton transfer and hydrogen bonding, yet incorporating them into quantum chemistry calculations is often computationally prohibitive on classical hardware. Quantum computers offer a promising alternative because their space requirements scale linearly with system size.

Method: The authors propose a hybrid quantum-classical algorithm for ab-initio molecular dynamics that includes nuclear quantum effects through the nuclear-electronic orbital (NEO) method. Ground state energies and forces are evaluated on the quantum computer with a variational quantum eigensolver (VQE), while molecular geometries are updated classically. The approach is validated on H2, H2O, and the Zundel ion H5O2+, and compact hardware-efficient ansatz circuits are compared against UCCSD ansatzes.

Result: Simulated vibrational spectra are compared with experimental data. Including nuclear quantum effects makes the proton shuttling motion in the Zundel ion effectively barrierless, and errors in the simulated frequencies improve significantly. Hardware-efficient ansatz circuits yield results comparable to the more accurate UCCSD ansatzes.

Conclusion: The results suggest that the proposed hybrid quantum-classical NEO-based molecular dynamics algorithm can capture nuclear quantum effects accurately and may be feasible to execute on near-term quantum devices using compact hardware-efficient ansatzes.

Abstract: Nuclear quantum effects are critical for describing proton transfer and hydrogen bonding, but their incorporation into quantum chemistry calculations is often computationally prohibitive on classical hardware. A promising alternative are quantum computers due to their linear scaling in the space requirements with system size. We introduce a novel hybrid quantum-classical algorithm for ab-initio molecular dynamics that incorporates nuclear quantum effects via the nuclear-electronic orbital method. The proposed algorithm evaluates ground state energies and forces on the quantum computer using a variational quantum eigensolver, while the molecular geometries are updated classically. We validate our approach through simulations of $\text{H}_2$, $\text{H}_2\text{O}$ and the Zundel ion $\text{H}_5\text{O}_2^+$, comparing the simulated vibrational spectra with experimental data. Upon inclusion of nuclear quantum effects, the proton shuttling movement in the Zundel ion becomes effectively barrierless, and errors in the simulated frequencies improve significantly. Employing compact hardware-efficient ansatz circuits we achieve results comparable to the more accurate UCCSD ansatzes, which hints towards the feasibility of executing our algorithm on near-term quantum devices.

</details>


### [89] [Efficient certification of time-reversal symmetry requires entanglement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01555)
*Zhenhuan Liu,Zhenyu Du,Yifan Tang,Zi-Wen Liu,Jens Eisert,Ingo Roth*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper formulates a Bell-inequality-like test of time-reversal symmetry using only forward access and trusted quantum operations, proving that distinguishing time-reversal-symmetric circular ensembles from Haar-random n-qubit dynamics requires Ω(min{2^{n/2}, 2^{n−e}}) queries for classically adaptive protocols, with maximally entangled probes and SWAP measurements reducing the cost to a constant.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Time-reversal symmetry is a fundamental principle of physics describing the invariance of physical laws under reversal of the direction of time. The authors aim to test this antiunitary symmetry using only forward access and trusted quantum operations, bridging quantum information science and fundamental physics by quantitatively connecting entanglement to time-reversal symmetry.

Method: The authors devise a Bell-inequality-like test in which entanglement converts temporal input–output relations into measurable spatial exchange symmetry. They analyze n-qubit unitary dynamics and prove query-complexity lower bounds for classically adaptive protocols, where e = min{e_s, e_m} with e_s and e_m being the probe and measurement logarithmic entanglement negativities. They also develop a test for arbitrary fixed, compatible probes and measurements, relating query complexity to logarithmic negativities, and optimize the probe and measurement to match the lower-bound scaling in the high-entanglement regime.

Result: Reliably distinguishing time-reversal-symmetric circular ensembles from Haar-random dynamics requires Ω(min{2^{n/2}, 2^{n−e}}) queries for any classically adaptive protocol. Maximally entangled probes and SWAP measurements reduce this cost to a constant number of queries. The query complexity of the general test is related to the logarithmic negativities of the probes and measurements, matching the lower-bound scaling in the high-entanglement regime via optimization.

Conclusion: The results establish a quantitative connection between entanglement and time-reversal symmetry, bridging two central concepts in quantum information science and fundamental physics.

Abstract: Time-reversal symmetry is a fundamental principle of physics describing the invariance of physical laws under reversal of the direction of time. We formulate a Bell-inequality-like test of this antiunitary symmetry using only forward access and trusted quantum operations: entanglement converts temporal input--output relations into measurable spatial exchange symmetry. For $n$-qubit unitary dynamics, we prove that reliably distinguishing the time-reversal-symmetric circular ensembles from Haar-random dynamics requires $Ω(\min\{2^{n/2},2^{n-e}\})$ queries for any classically adaptive protocol. Here, $e=\min\{e_{\mathrm s},e_{\mathrm m}\}$ with $e_{\mathrm s}$ and $e_{\mathrm m}$ representing the probe and measurement logarithmic entanglement negativities, respectively. Maximally entangled probes and SWAP measurements reduce this cost to a constant number of queries. Furthermore, we develop a time-reversal symmetry test for arbitrary fixed, compatible probes and measurements, relate its query complexity to their logarithmic negativities, and match the lower-bound scaling in the high-entanglement regime by optimizing the probe and measurement. Our results establish a quantitative connection between entanglement and time-reversal symmetry, bridging two central concepts in quantum information science and fundamental physics.

</details>


### [90] [Adversarial Robustness in Fake Quantum Simulators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01574)
*Marc Maußner,Volker Reers*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A dual-phase study benchmarks Qiskit Aer throughput across hardware and evaluates PGD attacks with adversarial retraining under realistic noise, finding that 50/50 adversarial-to-benign retraining is essential for robust 4-qubit classifiers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: QML models are increasingly deployed on noisy simulators, but their performance scalability and adversarial robustness under realistic noise conditions remain insufficiently quantified.

Method: Dual-phased experimental study: (1) benchmarking computational throughput of Qiskit's Aer simulation engine across varying hardware architectures; (2) evaluating Projected Gradient Descent (PGD) attacks and adversarial retraining strategies under noise-model-based fake simulators, up to 8 qubits.

Result: Runtime tradeoffs and scaling behavior for medium-scale simulations (up to 8 qubits) are quantified; high adversarial-to-benign retraining ratios (50/50) are essential for practical robustness of 4-qubit classifiers under realistic noise.

Conclusion: Scalability of Aer simulation is hardware-dependent, and adversarial robustness of QML classifiers under realistic noise critically requires balanced adversarial retraining at high ratios.

Abstract: This paper investigates the performance scalability and adversarial robustness of Quantum Machine Learning (QML) models deployed on noise-model-based fake simulators. We conduct a dual-phased study, first benchmarking the computational throughput of Qiskit's Aer simulation engine across varying hardware architectures, and second, evaluating the effectiveness of Projected Gradient Descent (PGD) attacks and adversarial retraining strategies under realistic noise conditions. Our results quantify the runtime tradeoffs and scaling behavior for medium-scale simulations (projected up to 8 qubits) and demonstrate that high adversarial-to-benign retraining ratios (50/50) are essential for achieving practical model robustness for 4-qubit classifiers under realistic noise conditions.

</details>


### [91] [Electromagnetic Side-Channel Vulnerability in QKD Equipment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01609)
*Mikio Fujiwara,Katsumi Fujii,Fumie Ono,Masakazu Ono,Akihisa Tomita*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Electromagnetic emissions from a prototype time-bin decoy-state BB84 QKD device were measured at 0 m and 3 m in an anechoic chamber; the transmitter dominates leakage, and quantum states are distinguishable at the third harmonic of the 1241.6 MHz clock.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world QKD network nodes may be exposed to side-channel attacks, notably electromagnetic leakage, so the practical emission characteristics of a QKD device need empirical characterization.

Method: Electromagnetic spectra and waveforms were measured from a prototype QKD device implementing a time-bin decoy-state BB84 protocol with Y and Z bases, at distances of 0 m and 3 m from both transmitter and receiver inside an anechoic chamber.

Result: The transmitter was identified as the primary source of electromagnetic leakage. Distinct differences between the generated quantum states were observed at the third harmonic of the 1241.6 MHz clock.

Conclusion: The findings indicate a measurable EM side-channel in the QKD prototype, with state-dependent leakage at a specific harmonic, which could inform countermeasures for practical QKD network nodes.

Abstract: Actual quantum key distribution network nodes may be vulnerable to side-channel attacks such as electromagnetic leakage. In this study, we measured electromagnetic emissions from a prototype quantum key distribution device implementing a time-bin decoy-state BB84 protocol with Y and Z bases. We measured the electromagnetic spectra and waveforms at distances of 0 m and 3 m from the transmitter and receiver inside an anechoic chamber. The results showed that the transmitter was the primary source of electromagnetic leakage, and distinct differences were observed between the quantum states generated at the third harmonic of the 1241.6 MHz clock.

</details>


### [92] [Dissipation-Enabled Operator-Norm Locality Bounds for Bose-Hubbard Hamiltonians](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01669)
*Marius Lemm,Tim Möbus*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Local dissipation (on-site ℓ-photon loss) restores an operator-norm Lieb–Robinson bound in the dissipative Bose–Hubbard model, yielding an almost-ballistic bound, local adiabatic and channel approximations, a thermodynamic limit, and efficient digital quantum simulation—all uniform in the input state, unlike closed bosonic lattice systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Closed bosonic lattice systems generically fail to satisfy operator-norm Lieb–Robinson bounds uniformly in the initial state, because the information-propagation velocity grows with local boson occupancy and can become macroscopically large. The authors aim to regularize this unbounded propagation by adding local dissipation.

Method: They study the dissipative Bose–Hubbard model governed by a Lindbladian with on-site ℓ-photon loss (ℓ > 2). Dissipation rapidly depletes highly occupied sites, regularizing the state on a Sobolev-type scale of local particle moments. The resulting moment bounds diverge as time → 0 but remain integrable near zero for sufficiently low orders. The framework is extended to cat-code dissipation, and for initial states in the code space a local adiabatic approximation uniform in the total volume is proved.

Result: They establish an almost-ballistic operator-norm Lieb–Robinson bound for the dissipative Bose–Hubbard model. Additional consequences include a local channel approximation, existence of a thermodynamic limit, and efficient digital quantum simulation. For cat-code dissipation with code-space initial states, a volume-uniform local adiabatic approximation is obtained.

Conclusion: Local dissipation enforces state-uniform operator-norm Lieb–Robinson bounds in bosonic lattice systems, mirroring the uniform-in-input-state behavior of quantum spin systems, in contrast to closed Bose–Hubbard systems. This makes local channel approximation, thermodynamic limits, and efficient digital quantum simulation available uniformly in the input state.

Abstract: Closed bosonic lattice systems do not generally admit operator norm Lieb--Robinson bounds uniformly in the initial state. The reason is that the information propagation velocity can grow with the local boson occupancy, which can be macroscopically large. Here, we show that local dissipation restores an operator-norm Lieb--Robinson bound.
