We consider the dissipative Bose--Hubbard model described by a Lindbladian operator with on-site $\ell$-photon loss, $\ell>2$ and, for our main result, we establish an almost-ballistic Lieb-Robinson bound. Dissipation rapidly depletes highly occupied sites, thus regularizing the state on the Sobolev-type scale of local particle moments. The resulting moment bounds diverge as time approaches zero but remain integrable near zero at sufficiently low orders. We extend these ideas to treat cat-code dissipation and, for initial states in the code space, we prove a local adiabatic approximation uniform in the total volume. Further consequences include local channel approximation, a thermodynamic limit, and efficient digital quantum simulation. The point is that these applications are now available \textit{uniformly in the input state}, as for quantum spin systems, but in contrast to closed Bose--Hubbard systems.

</details>


### [93] [A Code-Agnostic Graph Neural Network Decoder from the Detection Error Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01683)
*Federico Alberto Astolfi,Guido Pupillo*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: POLYMECHANON is a GNN decoder for quantum error correction that takes only the detection error model (DEM) as input, enabling it to decode any stabilizer code under any DEM-expressible noise model without architectural redesign, outperforming correlated MWPM and matching or surpassing BP+OSD across surface and qLDPC codes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing quantum decoders are typically tied to specific codes or noise models, requiring redesign when codes or noise assumptions change. A decoder that generalizes to arbitrary stabilizer codes and noise models would simplify deployment and enable real-time decoding.

Method: The DEM is represented as a tripartite graph of detectors, error mechanisms, and logical observables, with all input features derived from the quantum code itself. The same GNN architecture is trained on the DEM, so only the DEM changes when decoding a new code or noise model. Evaluation covers rotated surface code, high-rate qLDPC codes, timing, cross-code generalization, and confidence-based post-selection.

Result: On the rotated surface code, up to 25% fewer logical failures than correlated MWPM under phenomenological and circuit-level noise. On qLDPC codes, matches BP+OSD on smaller codes and surpasses it on larger ones (up to 16% fewer logical failures on [[130,4,6]]). Decoding time is error-rate-independent (a few ms/shots on a single GPU vs. tens of ms for BP+OSD on one CPU core). A single cross-family model beats uncorrelated MWPM on seen graph-like codes and stays within 10% of BP+OSD on others, with limited generalization to unseen codes, especially larger ones. Probabilistic outputs enable post-selection, reducing surface-code logical error rate by over an order of magnitude while retaining >90% of shots.

Conclusion: A DEM-driven GNN decoder provides a code- and noise-agnostic, real-time-capable solution for quantum error correction, with competitive accuracy and post-selection benefits, requiring only a new DEM rather than decoder redesign when codes or noise models change.

Abstract: We present POLYMECHANON, a graph neural network (GNN) decoder for quantum error correction whose only input is the detection error model (DEM) of a quantum code under a given noise model. We represent the DEM as a tripartite graph of detectors, error mechanisms and logical observables, where every input feature is computed by using the quantum code as data rather than design choice. In this way, the same architecture decodes in principle any stabiliser code, under any noise model that can be expressed as a detection error model, once trained on the DEM. We test this approach along four directions. Firstly, on the rotated surface code the decoder outperforms correlated MWPM under both phenomenological and circuit-level noise, with up to $25\%$ fewer logical failures. Secondly, on a family of high-rate qLDPC codes it matches BP+OSD on the smaller codes and surpasses it on the larger ones, with up to $16\%$ fewer logical failures on $[\![130,4,6]\!]$. Thirdly, its decoding time does not depend on the physical error rate, whereas that of BP+OSD grows with it: on $[\![130,4,6]\!]$ its effective cost per shot is of the order of a few ms on a single GPU against a few tens of milliseconds for BP+OSD on a single CPU core, and its fixed computational graph makes it a candidate for real-time decoding on neutral-atom processors. Finally, a single model trained across codes of different families outperforms uncorrelated MWPM on the graph-like codes seen during training and stays within $10\%$ of BP+OSD on the others, while it tends to fail to generalize to unseen codes, particularly larger ones. Its probabilistic output enables confidence-based post-selection, lowering the logical error rate by more than an order of magnitude on the surface code while keeping more than $90\%$ of the shots. Since only the DEM changes, new codes and noise models can be decoded without redesigning the decoder.

</details>


### [94] [Mentor-Initiated Asymmetric Bidirectional Quantum Teleportation Protocol for Arbitrary Qubit States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01684)
*Sugata Adhya,Benchao Yang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A mentor-initiated bidirectional asymmetric quantum teleportation protocol is proposed that removes the Bell-like restriction on the Bob-to-Alice two-qubit state, allowing an arbitrary two-qubit state to be teleported; entangled resources are built from standard gates and demonstrated via Qiskit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing mentor-initiated bidirectional asymmetric quantum teleportation schemes restrict the two-qubit state transmitted from Bob to Alice to a Bell-like form α|00⟩+β|11⟩, which limits the class of states that can be teleported.

Method: A mentor-initiated bidirectional asymmetric quantum teleportation protocol is constructed, with the required entangled resources generated using standard quantum gates; the protocol is validated through a quantum-circuit implementation in Qiskit.

Result: The protocol successfully teleports an arbitrary two-qubit state from Bob to Alice within the mentor-initiated framework, generalizing beyond the Bell-like state previously required.

Conclusion: The proposed scheme broadens the applicability of mentor-initiated bidirectional asymmetric quantum teleportation by supporting arbitrary two-qubit states, with gate-based entangled resource construction and Qiskit circuit verification.

Abstract: In this paper, a mentor-initiated bidirectional asymmetric quantum teleportation protocol is proposed. Unlike existing schemes that restrict the two-qubit state transmitted from Bob to Alice to a Bell-like form $α|00\rangle+β|11\rangle$, our protocol allows an arbitrary two-qubit state to be teleported from Bob to Alice within the mentor-initiated framework. The required entangled resources are constructed using standard quantum gates, and the protocol is illustrated through its quantum-circuit implementation in Qiskit.

</details>


### [95] [Predicting electric-field noise in ion traps using fluctuation electrodynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01693)
*Markus Teller,Da An,Alberto M. Alonso,Philip C. Holz,Philipp Schindler,Hartmut Häffner,Tracy E. Northup*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A method is presented to predict electric-field noise from bulk fluctuations in dielectric and metallic materials, applicable to arbitrary trap geometries and depending only on energy loss. Applied to a floating-electrode ion trap, dielectric noise dominates and is predicted within a factor of four of experiment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Electric-field noise in microfabricated surface ion traps causes gate errors in trapped-ion quantum computers, yet it has been difficult to predict.

Method: Model electric-field noise originating from fluctuations in the bulk of dielectric and metallic materials, valid for arbitrary trap geometries, with energy loss as the sole relevant material property. Apply to a trap with a floating electrode and to typical surface trap designs, analyzing electrode shielding.

Result: For the floating-electrode trap, dielectric contributions are dominant, and the model predicts observed noise within a factor of four. For typical surface traps, the noise and electrode shielding behavior are analyzed.

Conclusion: The approach provides a geometry-general, energy-loss-based prediction of bulk electric-field noise in surface ion traps, matching measurement within a factor of four and offering insight into electrode shielding for trap design.

Abstract: Electric-field noise in microfabricated surface ion traps contributes to gate errors in trapped-ion quantum computers, but this noise has been challenging to predict. Here, we present a method to predict the electric-field noise arising from fluctuations in the bulk of dielectric and metallic materials. The method is valid for arbitrary trap geometries, and the only relevant material property is energy loss. We apply it to an ion trap with an electrically floating electrode and find the contribution from dielectrics to be dominant. Our model predicts the observed noise for this trap within a factor of four. In addition, we apply our model to typical surface trap designs and analyze how the electrode structures shield the noise.

</details>


### [96] [Efficiently Optimizing the Quantum Value of Bell Inequalities using Batched Gradient Descent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01699)
*Xinyu Xu,Ping Zhu,Weikang Li,Pierre Pocreau,Dalu Ding,Dawei Ding*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a batched gradient descent (BGD) optimizer for the quantum value of Bell inequalities, using differentiable search over feasible quantum strategies and direct tensor contraction on a GPU, scaling to over a thousand inputs or outputs within minutes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Numerical methods for Bell inequalities must scale with the number of parties, inputs, and outputs to study large-scale inequalities, design experiments, and enable applications like randomness generation and multi-agent coordination. Existing general-purpose optimizers such as the see-saw method struggle with even a dozen inputs and outputs.

Method: A BGD optimizer that performs differentiable search over feasible quantum strategies, generating states and projective measurements from unconstrained variables. The Bell expression is evaluated by direct tensor contraction, avoiding construction of the prohibitively large Bell operator. This makes parallel random restarts inexpensive and naturally GPU-implementable. The classical value is also computed by GPU strategy enumeration or a mixed-integer linear program formulation.

Result: Compared with the see-saw method, the BGD optimizer achieves significant speedups across a wide variety of Bell inequality families. On a GPU, it optimizes inequalities with more than a thousand inputs or outputs within minutes, and efficiently computes the corresponding classical values.

Conclusion: The optimizers can serve as numerical evaluation tools for Bell experiments and multi-agent coordination problems modeled by Bell inequalities with many inputs and outputs, a common feature of real-world scenarios such as high frequency trading and distributed systems.

Abstract: Exploring the world of Bell inequalities requires numerical methods that scale effectively with the number of parties, inputs, and outputs. Such methods can be used to study large-scale Bell inequalities beyond the reach of analytic methods, to design experiments for violating more complicated Bell inequalities, and to enable applications of Bell inequality violation. Such applications include randomness generation and multi-agent coordination with restricted communication. However, current general purpose optimizers such as the see-saw method struggle to handle Bell inequalities with only a dozen inputs and outputs. We introduce an optimizer for the quantum value of a Bell inequality based on batched gradient descent (BGD). More precisely, our BGD optimizer is a differentiable search over feasible quantum strategies in which states and projective measurements are generated from unconstrained variables. The Bell expression is evaluated by a direct tensor contraction without forming the prohibitively large Bell operator. This formulation makes parallel random restarts inexpensive and is thus naturally implemented by a GPU. We evaluate our optimizer against the see-saw method and find a significant speedup for a wide variety of Bell inequality families. Implemented on a GPU, our BGD optimizer can optimize Bell inequalities in our numerical experiments with more than a thousand inputs or outputs within minutes. For these large-scale Bell inequalities, we also efficiently compute the classical value by enumerating possible strategies using a GPU or expressing the optimization as a mixed-integer linear program. Our optimizers can serve as numerical evaluation tools for Bell experiments and multi-agent coordination problems modeled by Bell inequalities with many inputs and outputs, a common feature of real-world scenarios such as high frequency trading and distributed systems.

</details>


### [97] [Exact first-detection probability in a locally monitored solvable quantum circuit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01703)
*Cecilia De Fazio,Igor Lesanovsky,Gabriele Perfetto*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Exact analytical results for first-detection probabilities in the monitored Floquet-quantum East model reveal geometric statistics for infrequent monitoring and deviations driven by measurement-induced correlations for frequent monitoring.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Local measurements and unitary Floquet dynamics compete in monitored quantum many-body systems, but exact characterizations of how measurement backaction reshapes detection statistics remain rare. This work targets that gap using an integrable deterministic Floquet model.

Method: The authors study the deterministic Floquet-quantum East model under local stroboscopic measurements. They derive exact analytical first-detection probabilities via a renewal equation relating the first-detection probability to the subsystem Loschmidt echo, which encodes the effective open dynamics of the monitored qubits. Return probabilities are controlled by the monitored subregion size, enabling access via local mid-circuit measurements on digital quantum simulators.

Result: Two regimes emerge from the competition between the measurement probing time and the relaxation timescale of the unitary circuit dynamics: infrequent monitoring yields geometric first-detection statistics, whereas frequent monitoring induces correlations causing deviations from geometric behavior.

Conclusion: The work provides a rare exact characterization of how local measurement backaction reshapes detection statistics in an interacting quantum many-body system, with experimentally accessible return probabilities through local mid-circuit measurements.

Abstract: We study the interplay between local stroboscopic measurements and the unitary quantum circuit dynamics of the deterministic Floquet-quantum East model. We derive exact analytical results for the probability of first detecting a subset of qubits back in their initial state. This is achieved via a renewal equation linking the first-detection probability to the subsystem Loschmidt echo, which encodes the effective open dynamics of the monitored qubits. The resulting statistics exhibit two regimes set by the competition between the measurement probing time and the relaxation timescale of the unitary circuit dynamics: while infrequent monitoring leads to geometric first-detection statistics, frequent monitoring induces correlations that manifest as deviations from this geometric behavior. These results provide a rare exact characterization of how local measurement backaction reshapes detection statistics in an interacting quantum many-body system. In our local protocol, return probabilities are controlled by the size of the monitored subregion and can therefore be accessed on digital quantum simulators via local mid-circuit measurements.

</details>


### [98] [Less precise but less noisy: local circuits for momentum-space state preparation and measurement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01704)
*Etienne Granet,Henrik Dreyer*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: For tight-binding chain ground state preparation on Quantinuum H2, adiabatic evolution outperforms the Fermionic Fourier Transform (FFT) beyond a critical system size N at equal gate count and depth, due to FFT's higher noise sensitivity from its long-range couplings. A cheaper, less noisy momentum measurement scheme also beats FFT for spectral function measurement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum algorithms are typically optimized for gate count or circuit depth, but these metrics may not capture noise sensitivity—an equally decisive factor for real hardware performance.

Method: Compare adiabatic evolution and FFT for tight-binding chain ground state preparation at matched gate count and circuit depth on Quantinuum System Model H2. Attribute performance differences to momentum resolution and coupling range, and introduce a momentum measurement scheme with coarser resolution but lower cost and noise.

Result: Beyond a threshold system size N, adiabatic evolution reaches significantly lower energies than FFT at equal gates and depth. FFT's high momentum resolution (1/N) requires long-range couplings that propagate errors faster. The proposed momentum measurement scheme outperforms FFT for spectral function measurement on H2.

Conclusion: Noise sensitivity should be treated as a first-class optimization criterion for quantum algorithms, beyond gate count and circuit depth. Coarser momentum resolution can be a worthwhile trade for reduced noise sensitivity in physical applications.

Abstract: Quantum algorithms are usually optimized for gate count or circuit depth. We find on Quantinuum System Model H2 quantum computer that for a tight-binding chain ground state preparation, there is a system size $N$ beyond which the adiabatic evolution reaches significantly lower energies than the Fermionic Fourier Transform (FFT), with the same number of gates, and with the same circuit depth. We attribute this high noise sensitivity of the FFT to its high precision, being able to distinguish momenta by $1/N$. This high resolution in momentum space requires long-range couplings in real space, which propagates errors faster. In contrast, although local and physical circuits such as the adiabatic evolution have a coarser momentum resolution, they also propagate errors more slowly. For physical applications, high momentum resolution is rarely required and is often worth trading for low noise sensitivity. We also introduce a momentum measurement scheme that although less precise than FFT, is less costly and less noisy. We show that it achieves better performance than FFT for spectral function measurement on Quantinuum System Model H2 quantum computer. Our work emphasizes the importance of reducing the noise sensitivity of quantum algorithms, beyond the number of gates or circuit depth.

</details>


### [99] [Near-optimal quantum query lower bounds on bipartiteness and expansion testing in the bounded-degree graph model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01752)
*Chandrima Kayal,Sayantan Sen,Dániel Szabó*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work establishes essentially tight quantum query lower bounds of Ω̃(N^{1/3}) for both bipartiteness and expansion testing in the bounded-degree graph model, matching the known upper bounds and thereby completely characterizing their quantum query complexity up to polylogarithmic factors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bipartiteness and expansion testing are canonical graph property testing problems. Classically both require Θ̃(√N) queries (Goldreich–Ron). Ambainis, Childs, and Liu (2011) gave quantum algorithms using Õ(N^{1/3}) queries and proved only Ω̃(N^{1/4}) lower bound for expansion testing, leaving open whether bipartiteness admits an exponential quantum advantage. No progress had been made for over a decade, motivating the resolution of these gaps.

Method: The authors employ the polynomial method, as in Ambainis–Childs–Liu, but introduce intermediate problems related to the main testing problems via reductions and perform a more precise analysis of the resulting polynomials to derive near-optimal lower bounds.

Result: Essentially tight Ω̃(N^{1/3}) quantum query lower bounds are proven for both bipartiteness testing and expansion testing.

Conclusion: Combined with the known Õ(N^{1/3}) upper bounds, these results completely characterize the quantum query complexity of bipartiteness and expansion testing in the bounded-degree model up to polylogarithmic factors, closing the long-standing open problem.

Abstract: In this work, we study bipartiteness and expansion testing, two canonical problems in graph property testing in the bounded-degree model through the lens of quantum query complexity. In the classical setting, it is known that $\widetildeΘ(\sqrt{N})$ queries are necessary and sufficient for both these testing problems (Goldreich and Ron, 1999, 2000 & 2002), where $N$ denotes the number of vertices of the input graph. Due to their significance, (Ambainis, Childs, and Liu, 2011) initiated the study of these problems in the quantum setting and designed quantum algorithms for bipartiteness and expansion testing that perform $\widetilde{O}(N^{1/3})$ queries, showing a polynomial speedup. They also proved that $\widetildeΩ(N^{1/4})$ queries are necessary for expansion testing, but the possibility of an exponential quantum advantage for bipartiteness testing remained open. Despite significant effort, there has been no improvement in these results in the last decade and a half. In this work, we prove essentially tight $\widetildeΩ(N^{1/3})$ quantum query lower bounds for both bipartiteness and expansion testing, thereby completely characterizing the quantum query complexity of these problems up to polylogarithmic factors. While our proofs use the polynomial method similarly to Ambainis, Childs, and Liu, we use intermediate problems that we relate to the main problems via reductions, and perform a more precise analysis of the resulting polynomials, leading to the near-optimal lower bounds.

</details>


### [100] [Efficient quantum phase estimation with adaptive entanglement-assisted Hadamard test](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01772)
*Hengzhun Chen,Benchi Zhao,Yingzhou Li*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces the adaptive entanglement-assisted Hadamard test (AEHT), which iteratively refines the reference phase to progressively amplify the phase signal, overcoming the accuracy-limited amplification of conventional EHT and suppressing bias under imperfect eigenstate preparation, while measuring cost by device-restart count.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The conventional entanglement-assisted Hadamard test (EHT) estimates a quantum phase by amplifying the phase signal, but its feasible amplification is fundamentally limited by the accuracy of the reference phase, making it inefficient in the high-precision regime. Additionally, under imperfect eigenstate preparation, a systematic bias is unavoidable when using conventional EHT.

Method: The authors propose the adaptive entanglement-assisted Hadamard test (AEHT), an algorithm that iteratively refines the reference phase, thereby enabling progressively stronger amplification as the iteration proceeds. They also adopt device-restart count, rather than shot count, as the cost measure for quantum phase estimation to better reflect physical implementation considerations.

Result: Numerical experiments confirm the effectiveness of the proposed AEHT compared with conventional methods under the device-restart count measure. The AEHT suppresses the systematic bias arising in the imperfect eigenstate preparation scenario and unlocks the full amplification power of large entangled states.

Conclusion: By unlocking the full amplification power of large entangled states, the work offers an efficient method to estimate high-precision quantum phase on near-term quantum processors.

Abstract: The entanglement-assisted Hadamard test (EHT) is a practical method for estimating a quantum phase by amplifying the phase signal. However, the feasible amplification is fundamentally limited by the accuracy of the reference phase, such that the method is inefficient in the high-precision regime. In this work, we propose an algorithm, called adaptive entanglement-assisted Hadamard test (AEHT), that iteratively refines the reference phase, enabling progressively stronger amplification as the iteration goes by. We further consider the imperfect eigenstate preparation scenario, where a systematic bias is unavoidable when estimating the quantum phase with the conventional EHT. Such a bias can be suppressed by the proposed AEHT. Moreover, taking physical implementation into consideration, we adopt device-restart count to measure the cost of quantum phase estimation, rather than shot count. The numerical experiments confirm the effectiveness of the proposed AEHT compared with conventional methods under this measure. By unlocking the full amplification power of large entangled states, this work offers an efficient method to estimate high-precision quantum phase on near-term quantum processors.

</details>


### [101] [QUFIG: GNN-Based Prediction of Quantum Fault Injection Vulnerabilities with Gate-Level Precision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01777)
*Shihan Zhao,Qiying Li,Ben Dong,Qian Wang,Yuntao Liu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: QUFIG is a learning-guided prioritization framework that uses a circuit-DAG-based GNN to predict gate-level vulnerability scores to injected faults, ranking gate-fault pairs to efficiently identify high-impact vulnerabilities under limited fidelity budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The growing scale and accessibility of quantum hardware have introduced reliability and security challenges, such as run-time fault injection attacks in cloud-based quantum computing. Existing methods fail to identify vulnerabilities with gate-level precision or adapt to run-time environments.

Method: The work formulates gate-level fault analysis as a learning-guided prioritization problem under restricted fidelity budgets. It employs a circuit-DAG-based GNN backbone to predict the vulnerability score of each gate to each fault type, where the score is defined as the impact of the gate-fault pair on circuit fidelity. Gate-fault pairs are then ranked by their vulnerability scores.

Result: Experiments on QASMbench and HamLib MaxCut show that QUFIG recovers high-impact vulnerable gate-fault pairs with fewer inspections than random and depth-based heuristics, reducing the number of gates requiring inspection by 2.9--19.8% while maintaining effective fault identification.

Conclusion: QUFIG enables quantum circuit designers to identify vulnerabilities and apply targeted defenses more efficiently, addressing gate-level fault analysis under run-time conditions.

Abstract: The growing scale and accessibility of quantum hardware exposed new reliability and security challenges in the quantum computing workflow, such as the run-time fault injection attacks in cloud-based quantum computing platforms. However, existing works fail to identify vulnerabilities with gate-level precision or adapt to run-time environments. In this work, we formulate gate-level fault analysis as a learning-guided prioritization problem under restricted fidelity budgets. The framework uses a circuit-DAG-based GNN backbone to predict the vulnerability score of each gate to each type of injected fault, defined as the impact of the gate-fault pair on circuit fidelity. The gate-fault pairs are then ranked by their vulnerability score. Experiments on QASMbench and HamLib MaxCut show that QUFIG recovers high-impact vulnerable gate-fault pairs with fewer inspections than random and depth-based heuristics. Our results show that QUFIG can reduce the number of gates requiring inspection by 2.9--19.8% while maintaining effective fault identification, allowing quantum circuit designers to identify vulnerabilities and apply targeted defenses more efficiently.

</details>


### [102] [Non-Markovian effects on informational steady states](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01782)
*Jacob Werner*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work studies how non-Markovian memory effects affect informational steady states (ISSs) using continuously monitored collision models with two-qubit ancillas, finding that steady-state information gain per measurement is negatively correlated with the degree of non-Markovianity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ISSs—arising when information gain from continuous measurements balances information loss to the environment—have mainly been studied in Markovian settings. How memory (non-Markovian) effects influence their formation and steady-state properties remains largely unexplored.

Method: The authors investigate non-Markovian effects on ISSs within the framework of continuously monitored collision models, modeling the environment as a sequence of two-qubit ancillas. They numerically characterize how system-ancilla and ancilla-ancilla interaction parameters affect both the degree of non-Markovianity and the steady-state information gain.

Result: For this specific model, the steady-state information gain per measurement is negatively correlated with the degree of non-Markovianity. The interaction parameters (system-ancilla and ancilla-ancilla) are shown to influence both the degree of non-Markovianity and the steady-state information gain.

Conclusion: Memory effects can suppress steady-state information gain in this collision model, revealing a trade-off between non-Markovianity and the information acquired at the informational steady state.

Abstract: Informational steady states (ISSs) arise when the rate at which information is gained from continuous measurements is exactly balanced by the rate at which information from earlier measurements is lost to the environment. While ISSs have been studied in Markovian settings, relatively little is known about how memory effects influence their formation and steady-state properties. In this work, we investigate non-Markovian effects on ISSs within the framework of continuously monitored collision models. We model the environment as a sequence of two-qubit ancillas and find that, for this specific model, the steady-state information gain per measurement is negatively correlated with the degree of non-Markovianity. Numerical results further characterize how the system-ancilla and ancilla-ancilla interaction parameters influence both the degree of non-Markovianity and the steady-state information gain.

</details>


### [103] [Learnt Attacks on Quantum Key Distribution under Channel Noise and Device Drift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01792)
*Marcel Mordarski,Benjamin Gras,Abdelrahman Shehata,Daniel Budina,Roberto Bondesan*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper quantifies the advantage of adaptive eavesdropping on QKD links whose channel noise drifts between recalibrations, framing the attacker as a constrained MDP that learns gate structure and rotation angles jointly.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: QKD links are provisioned from security analyses of stationary channels, but actual devices drift between recalibrations. It was previously unquantified whether an eavesdropper who cannot alter the channel's own noise can still gain by following that drift. Prior learning-based attacks (e.g., Decker et al.) searched only a parametrised circuit on a fixed gate template against a fixed channel, leaving the joint gate-structure search and drifting-channel setting open.

Method: Adaptive eavesdropping is posed as a constrained Markov decision process: the attacker selects one circuit per round, the noise level follows an Ornstein–Uhlenbeck process, and the abort condition is a budget over each block of rounds. The gate structure and rotation angles are searched jointly, yielding circuits compact enough to form a discrete action set, which extends the construction to noise models lacking a known template, including the amplitude damping channel. The value of adaptation is bounded by the best fixed circuit and a dynamic-programming upper bound.

Result: On device-independent E91 under bilateral depolarising noise, a reinforcement-learning attacker raises her Holevo information from 0.135 for the best fixed circuit to 0.348 at zero detection, 98% of the upper bound. On BB84 under a drifting bit-flip channel, she exceeds a conservative noise-indexed rule by 0.024 in fidelity, reaching 99% of the upper bound. Under stationary noise, the attacker's gain from basis asymmetry changes sign between an averaged and a per-basis error-rate constraint.

Conclusion: The search, started from random gate sequences, recovers the analytical cloners and the collective-attack key rate, and meets the lower bound of the Winick–Lütkenhaus–Coles objective from above.

Abstract: Quantum key distribution (QKD) links are provisioned from security analyses of stationary channels, whereas the devices that determine the channel drift between recalibrations. Whether an eavesdropper who cannot alter the channel's own noise gains by following that drift has not been quantified. Adaptive eavesdropping is posed here as a constrained Markov decision process in which the attacker selects one circuit per round while the noise level follows an Ornstein--Uhlenbeck process and the abort condition is a budget over each block of rounds. The value of adaptation is bounded by the best fixed circuit and a dynamic-programming upper bound. The actions are learnt attacks. Whereas Decker et al. trained a parametrised circuit on a fixed gate template against a fixed channel, here the gate structure and rotation angles are searched jointly. This yields circuits compact enough to form a discrete action set, extending the construction to noise models lacking a known template, including the amplitude damping channel. On device-independent E91 under bilateral depolarising noise, a reinforcement-learning attacker raises her Holevo information from $0.135$ for the best fixed circuit to $0.348$ at zero detection, $98\%$ of the upper bound. On BB84 under a drifting bit-flip channel, she exceeds a conservative noise-indexed rule by $0.024$ in fidelity, reaching $99\%$ of the upper bound. Under stationary noise, the attacker's gain from basis asymmetry changes sign between an averaged and a per-basis error-rate constraint. The search, started from random gate sequences, recovers the analytical cloners and the collective-attack key rate, and meets the lower bound of the Winick--Lütkenhaus--Coles objective from above.

</details>


### [104] [Collision models: Markovian and Non-Markovian impurity models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01814)
*Jacob Werner*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces "Markovian impurity models," a class of collision models where information backflow is periodically blocked. Using the BLP measure, it shows that non-Markovianity is monotonic in the impurity number p, implying a critical p for the Markovian-to-non-Markovian transition, and numerically studies how this critical p depends on interaction strengths.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Collision models offer a flexible framework for open quantum systems and non-Markovianity, but a scenario in which information backflow is periodically blocked—motivated by physical settings—had not been systematically modeled. The authors aim to capture such settings via a new model class.

Method: The authors define Markovian impurity models: collision models with p impurities that periodically block information backflow. They quantify non-Markovianity with the Breuer-Laine-Piilo (BLP) measure and prove a monotonicity relation between p-impurity and (p+1)-impurity models. Numerical simulations explore the dependence of the critical p on system-ancilla and ancilla-ancilla interaction strengths.

Result: If a p-impurity model is non-Markovian, the corresponding (p+1)-impurity model is also non-Markovian, implying a critical p separating Markovian from non-Markovian dynamics. Numerically, increasing either the system-ancilla or ancilla-ancilla interaction strength generally lowers the critical p (with exceptions); sufficiently weak ancilla-ancilla interactions yield Markovian dynamics even without impurities.

Conclusion: Markovian impurity models provide a tractable framework linking impurity number to non-Markovianity via a critical p, with interaction strengths controlling the transition. The complementary non-Markovian impurity model is briefly introduced.

Abstract: Collision models provide a flexible framework for studying open quantum systems and introducing non-Markovianity. We present a novel class of collision models, which we term Markovian impurity models, motivated by physical scenarios in which information backflow is periodically blocked. Using the Breuer-Laine-Piilo (BLP) measure of non-Markovianity, we show that if a $p$-impurity model is non-Markovian, then the corresponding $(p+1)$-impurity model is also non-Markovian, implying the existence of a critical $p$ at which the process transitions from Markovian to non-Markovian. Numerical simulations are used to investigate how this critical parameter depends on the system-ancilla and ancilla-ancilla interaction strengths. We find that, altough exceptions exist, increasing either interaction strength generally lowers the critical $p$, while sufficiently weak ancilla-ancilla interactions lead to a regime in which the dynamics remain Markovian even in the absence of impurities. Finally, we briefly introduce the complementary non-Markovian impurity model.

</details>


### [105] [QPI-DeepONet-MAC: A Scalable and Stable Hybrid Classical-Quantum Architecture for Physics-Informed Deep Operator Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01824)
*Said Lantigua,José Valencia,Gilson Giraldi,Renato Portugal,Jonas Maziero*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: QPI-DeepONet-MAC is a hybrid quantum-classical physics-informed operator network with provable universal approximation, barren-plateau-free trainability conditions, sensor-count lower bounds, and a coherence-preserving regularization scheme.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: General operator learning for parametric PDEs is a core AI-for-physics challenge. PI-DeepONets embed governing equations but suffer optimization difficulties in high dimensions, while quantum extensions add representational capacity yet risk barren plateaus that impede training.

Method: The authors introduce QPI-DeepONet-MAC, integrating parameterized quantum circuits into PI-DeepONets via a multiplicative-and-additive coupling (MAC) of classical representations and quantum expectation values. They analytically prove universal operator approximation, derive quantum parameter gradient bounds giving explicit barren-plateau avoidance conditions and a trainability scaling criterion, derive a sensor-count lower bound tied to input regularity, domain dimension, and PDE dynamical growth, and propose an informational regularization based on quantum coherence and state fidelity dynamics.

Result: The architecture retains universal operator approximation, establishes explicit conditions to avoid barren plateaus with a trainability scaling criterion, links sampling requirements to input regularity, dimension, and PDE growth, and shows that coherence/fidelity-based regularization prevents coherence loss and preserves quantum structure during training.

Conclusion: These results provide a theoretical foundation for quantum-enhanced physics-informed operator learning and position QPI-DeepONet-MAC as a scalable, stable framework for solving parametric PDEs.

Abstract: General operator learning for parametric partial differential equations (PDEs) is a fundamental challenge at the intersection of artificial intelligence and physics-based modeling. Physics-informed Deep Operator Networks (PI-DeepONets) incorporate governing equations into learning, but face optimization difficulties in high dimensions. Quantum extensions offer additional representational capacity, yet their trainability can be hindered by barren plateaus. Here, we introduce QPI-DeepONet-MAC, a hybrid quantum-classical architecture that integrates parameterized quantum circuits into PI-DeepONets through a multiplicative-and-additive coupling (MAC) of classical representations and quantum expectation values. We prove analytically that the proposed architecture retains universal operator approximation capabilities. Furthermore, we derive bounds on quantum parameter gradients, establishing explicit conditions to avoid barren plateaus alongside a scaling criterion for trainability. Additionally, we derive a lower bound on the number of sensors required for a target accuracy, directly linking sampling requirements to input function regularity, domain dimension, and PDE dynamical growth. Finally, we propose an informational regularization scheme based on quantum coherence and state fidelity dynamics to prevent coherence loss and preserve quantum structure during training. Together, these results establish a theoretical foundation for quantum-enhanced physics-informed operator learning and position QPI-DeepONet-MAC as a scalable, stable framework for solving parametric PDEs.

</details>


### [106] [Experimental realization of Wheeler's delayed-choice experiment with dual selections](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01832)
*Xiaowan Yang,Xinglei Yu,Liangsheng Li,Xinzhi Zhao,Tianle Zheng,Chengjie Zhang,Chuan-Feng Li,Guang-Can Guo*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The authors implement a delayed-choice experiment with entangled photons, controlling both beam splitters of a Mach-Zehnder interferometer via path encoding, and observe single-photon wave-particle behavior plus a wave-particle quantum superposition state.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Wheeler's delayed-choice experiment probes wave-particle duality, but conventional versions control only the second beam splitter. Extending control to both beam splitters, including their quantum superposition, enables a richer test of wave-particle behavior.

Method: Entangled photons are used to realize a delayed-choice experiment with dual selections. Both beam splitters in the Mach-Zehnder interferometer are experimentally controlled, with the presence and absence of the second beam splitter simultaneously controlled through path encoding.

Result: The experiment reveals the wave-particle behavior of single photons under different configurations of the two beam splitters and shows that when both beam splitters are in a quantum superposition of present and absent, photons enter a wave-particle quantum superposition state.

Conclusion: Dual-selection delayed-choice control of both beam splitters demonstrates tunable wave-particle behavior and a wave-particle quantum superposition state, extending Wheeler's delayed-choice scheme.

Abstract: Wheeler's delayed-choice experiment demonstrates wave-particle duality of particles using different experimental configurations of a Mach-Zehnder interferometer. In a quantum version of this experiment, the wave-particle behavior of photons can be observed by controlling presence or absence of the second beam splitter. Here, we implement a delayed-choice experiment with dual selections based on entangled photons, experimentally controlling both of two beam splitters in the Mach-Zehnder interferometer, where the presence and absence of the second beam splitter are simultaneously controlled through path encoding. Our experiment reveals the wave-particle behavior of single photons under different configurations of two beam splitters. We further discuss the scenario when two beam splitters are in a quantum superposition of being present and absent, photons will be in a wave-particle quantum superposition state.

</details>


### [107] [Toward Optimal Circuit Depth for Geometrically Local Hamiltonian Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01839)
*Zhenyu Shen,Yusen Wu,Penghui Yao,Xiao Yuan,Yukun Zhang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper constructs a nearest-neighbor quantum circuit simulating time-independent finite-range lattice Hamiltonians in depth O(T log(nT/ε)) and gate count O(nT log(nT/ε)), reducing prior polylogarithmic overhead to a single logarithm, and proves a matching Ω(log n/log log n) depth lower bound for uniform XXZ Hamiltonians, establishing near-optimal circuit depth for lattice Hamiltonian simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Digital quantum simulation of geometrically local Hamiltonians should preserve much of the parallelism inherent in the underlying dynamics. Prior work by Haah, Hastings, Kothari, and Low incurred polylogarithmic circuit-depth overhead; reducing this overhead and understanding the fundamental limits set by locality and precision is central to efficient quantum simulation.

Method: The authors exploit spatial localization within a Lieb–Robinson decomposition onto logarithmically scaled blocks. In each block, the error of a constant-order product formula is compensated by a correction that yields exponential accuracy, implementable with depth proportional to the block diameter—introducing no extra asymptotic depth scaling beyond that demanded by spatial localization. They also derive an unconditional circuit-depth lower bound for uniform XXZ Hamiltonians via a locality-based argument.

Result: A nearest-neighbor circuit simulating an n-qubit, time-independent, finite-range Hamiltonian with normalized local interaction strength on a fixed-dimensional lattice for time T ≥ 1 achieves depth O(T log(nT/ε)) and gate count O(nT log(nT/ε)), reducing polylogarithmic overhead to a single logarithm. An unconditional lower bound of Ω(log n/log log n) depth is proved for uniform XXZ Hamiltonians, matching the O(log n) upper bound up to a doubly logarithmic factor.

Conclusion: Spatial locality controls not only the parallelism but also the cost of high precision, yielding near-optimal circuit depth for lattice Hamiltonian simulation in this regime and clarifying the roles of locality and precision in the circuit complexity of quantum dynamics, suggesting broader principles for spatially constrained quantum computation.

Abstract: Understanding the dynamics of interacting quantum systems is a central goal of quantum simulation. For geometrically local Hamiltonians, locality suggests that digital simulation should retain much of the parallelism of the underlying dynamics. For a target accuracy $ε$, we construct a nearest-neighbor quantum circuit that simulates the dynamics of an $n$-qubit, time-independent, finite-range Hamiltonian with normalized local interaction strength on a fixed-dimensional lattice for time $T\ge1$, with circuit depth $O(T\log(nT/ε))$ and gate count $O(nT\log(nT/ε))$. This reduces the polylogarithmic overhead of Haah, Hastings, Kothari, and Low to a single logarithm. On logarithmic-scale blocks from the Lieb--Robinson decomposition, a shallow but highly accurate implementation is essential for reducing the circuit depth. Our key observation is that spatial localization also controls the cost of high precision. In each block, the error of a constant-order product formula is compensated for by a correction, yielding exponential accuracy. The correction can be implemented with depth proportional to the block diameter, introducing no additional asymptotic depth scale beyond that required by spatial localization.
  Furthermore, we prove an unconditional circuit-depth lower bound for uniform XXZ Hamiltonians: at fixed evolution time $T$ in the stated regime and sufficiently small fixed error $ε$, any nearest-neighbor quantum circuit simulating these dynamics requires depth $Ω(\log n/\log\log n)$. This matches our $O(\log n)$ upper bound up to a doubly logarithmic factor, establishing near-optimal circuit depth for lattice Hamiltonian simulation in this regime. Together, these results clarify the role of locality and precision in determining the circuit complexity of quantum dynamics and suggest broader principles for spatially constrained quantum computation.

</details>


### [108] [Trapdoored Clifford Operators and Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01848)
*Minki Hhan,Hojune Lee*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces trapdoored Clifford operator distributions that are computationally indistinguishable from uniformly random Cliffords yet can be sampled and implemented in near-linear time given a trapdoor, based on a variant of the learning parity with noise (LPN) assumption. These constructions enable faster random-Clifford-based protocols, resolve an open problem on trapdoored finite-field matrices, and yield worst-case to average-case hardness reductions for matrix multiplication and Clifford circuit synthesis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Uniformly random n-qubit Clifford operators are widely used in quantum computing (randomized benchmarking, classical shadows, quantum authentication), but sampling and implementing them incurs near-quadratic complexity due to the size of the Clifford group. This cost is a bottleneck for applications relying on random Cliffords, motivating a way to generate operators that behave like uniformly random Cliffords while being much faster to sample and implement.

Method: The authors construct a cryptographic primitive: a distribution of trapdoored Clifford operators whose samples are computationally indistinguishable from uniformly random Cliffords, yet are efficiently samplable and implementable given the trapdoor. Security relies on a variant of the learning parity with noise (LPN) assumption. The constructions support fast tableau action on Pauli labels for classical simulation and can be optimized for polylogarithmic-depth implementation. As a building block, they construct trapdoored matrices over finite fields supporting efficient multiplication by both a matrix and its inverse, resolving an open question of Vaikuntanathan and Zamir [SODA'26].

Result: The constructions yield near-linear-time sampling and implementation of (pseudo)random Cliffords, fast tableau action on Pauli labels, and polylogarithmic-depth implementability. They also provide trapdoored finite-field matrices with efficient forward and inverse multiplication. These enable faster protocols based on random Cliffords and worst-case to average-case reductions, showing that batching Clifford circuits (applying the same Clifford to multiple registers) is at least as hard as worst-case matrix multiplication, even when synthesis succeeds on only a small constant fraction of random Cliffords, with extension to approximate implementations by general quantum circuits.

Conclusion: Trapdoored Clifford distributions offer a cryptographic route around the near-quadratic complexity of uniform random Cliffords, achieving near-linear-time sampling/implementation under an LPN variant while remaining computationally indistinguishable from uniform. Beyond speeding up random-Clifford protocols, they establish new worst-case to average-case hardness results, notably for batched Clifford circuit synthesis (including approximate, general-quantum-circuit implementations), and resolve a prior open problem on trapdoored finite-field matrices.

Abstract: Random Clifford operators have numerous applications in quantum computing, including randomized benchmarking, classical shadows, and quantum authentication. However, sampling and implementing uniformly random $n$-qubit Clifford incur near-quadratic complexity due to the size of Clifford group.
  We introduce a cryptographic way to overcome these barriers: trapdoored Clifford operator distributions whose samples are computationally indistinguishable from uniformly random Cliffords, yet implementing them can be much faster given the trapdoor. We construct a distribution of trapdoored Clifford operators whose elements can be sampled and implemented in near-linear time under a variant of the learning parity with noise assumption. Our constructions allow fast tableau action on Pauli labels for classical simulation, and also can be optimized to admit polylogarithmic-depth implementation. Along the way, we construct trapdoored matrices over finite fields that support efficient multiplication by both a matrix and its inverse, resolving an open question left by Vaikuntanathan and Zamir [SODA'26].
  We use these constructions to obtain faster protocols based on random Cliffords. We also explore their applications to the worst-case to average-case reductions for matrix and Clifford problems including the iterated matrix multiplication and Clifford circuit synthesis. In particular, we show the hardness of batching Clifford circuits: synthesizing circuits that apply the same Clifford to multiple registers is at least as hard as worst-case matrix multiplication, even when synthesis succeeds on a small constant fraction of random Cliffords. This extends to approximate implementations by general quantum circuits.

</details>


### [109] [Quantum Optimal Transport Barycenters: Existence, Duality, and Gaussian Rigidity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01855)
*Augusto Gerolin,Zhiyi Lin*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper develops a Quantum Optimal Transport (QOT) barycenter framework for quantum states, establishing existence and duality for general transport costs on separable Hilbert spaces, and proves a Gaussian rigidity theorem: with a faithful Gaussian input, the 2-quantum Wasserstein barycenter is unique among all quantum states and Gaussian.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Wasserstein barycenters are central in classical optimal transport, and analogous quantum notions (quantum Wasserstein barycenters) have been proposed. However, a unified treatment covering both quantum-state and quantum-channel formulations, along with existence, duality, and rigidity (whether Gaussian inputs force a Gaussian unique barycenter), was lacking.

Method: The authors build a QOT barycenter framework as an analog of Wasserstein barycenters, proving existence and duality for a broad class of possibly unbounded transport costs on separable Hilbert spaces. Specializing to canonical quadratic cost operators recovers 2-quantum Wasserstein barycenters in both state and channel formulations (Caglioti–Golse–Mouhot–Paul; De Palma–Trevisan). For Gaussian inputs, the problem is reduced to finite-dimensional convex optimization over covariance matrices, and a state-reconstruction principle under covariance complementary slackness upgrades covariance uniqueness to full quantum-state uniqueness.

Result: The barycenter problem admits a Gaussian minimizer and reduces to a finite-dimensional convex optimization over covariance matrices. A global rigidity theorem is proved: if at least one Gaussian input is faithful, the barycenter is unique among all quantum states and is necessarily Gaussian. Faithfulness is sufficient but not necessary—families of pure inputs still yield a unique barycenter, while partially pure nonfaithful Gaussian inputs may admit multiple barycenters.

Conclusion: The framework unifies existing 2-quantum Wasserstein barycenter formulations and provides global Gaussian rigidity results, with faithfulness as a sufficient (but not necessary) condition for uniqueness, and complementary slackness as the key tool linking covariance and state uniqueness.

Abstract: We develop a Quantum Optimal Transport (QOT) barycenter framework for quantum states, as an analog of Wasserstein barycenters~\cite{AguCar}, and establish existence and duality results for a broad class of possibly unbounded transport costs on separable Hilbert spaces. Our framework provides, in particular, a unified treatment of $2$-quantum Wasserstein (QW) barycenters in both the quantum-state and quantum-channel formulations by specializing to the canonical quadratic cost operators associated with the $2$-quantum Wasserstein distances of Caglioti--Golse--Mouhot--Paul~\cite{caglioti2021towards,Golse-Mouhot-Paul:2016} and De Palma--Trevisan~\cite{DPaTre19}.
  The central results concern Gaussian input states and, in particular, Gaussian rigidity: whether Gaussian input states force the 2-QW barycenter itself to be Gaussian and uniquely determined when the minimization is taken over all quantum states. We first show that the barycenter problem admits a Gaussian minimizer and reduces to a finite-dimensional convex optimization problem over covariance matrices.
  The main difficulty is that uniqueness of the optimal covariance does not, in general, imply uniqueness of the underlying quantum state. We bridge this gap through a state-reconstruction principle under covariance complementary slackness that upgrades uniqueness of the optimal covariance to uniqueness of the full quantum state, and thereby prove a global rigidity theorem: if at least one Gaussian input is faithful, then the barycenter is unique among all quantum states and is necessarily Gaussian. Faithfulness is sufficient but not necessary: families of pure inputs still determine a unique barycenter, whereas partially pure nonfaithful Gaussian inputs may admit multiple barycenters.

</details>


### [110] [Approximation theorems for fermionic Gaussian states](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01860)
*Amir-Reza Negari,Farzin Salek,Zoltán Zimborás,Aram Harrow,Patrick Hayden,Jens Eisert*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: For fermionic Gaussian states, the admissibility condition on Majorana covariance matrices yields a quantitative monogamy constraint on two-point correlations, from which product-state, de Finetti, and recoverability approximations are derived directly at the covariance level.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many-body physics broadly expects that sufficiently distributed interactions or correlations reduce to an effective mean-field description, but a quantitative version of this principle at the level of fermionic Gaussian states and their covariance matrices was lacking.

Method: Exploit the admissibility condition for fermionic (Majorana) covariance matrices to derive a quantitative monogamy constraint on two-point correlations—a fixed region has only a bounded covariance budget to distribute among many disjoint regions—and apply this principle to quadratic fermionic Hamiltonians on high-degree graphs, to k-copy Gaussian states with graded fermionic exchangeability, and to spatially clustering fermionic Gaussian states.

Result: Product-state approximations to ground-state energy and finite-temperature free energy with error O(D^{-1/2}) for D-regular graphs; a finite fermionic Gaussian de Finetti theorem with O(k/n) trace-norm closeness to the product of the one-copy marginal (exact product structure under infinite exchangeability); and for exponentially clustering mutual information, decay of conditional mutual information across a separating buffer as O(r^{-1}), giving an O(r^{-1/2}) trace-norm recovered-state approximation via recoverability.

Conclusion: Product and de Finetti approximations and recoverability estimates for fermionic Gaussian systems can all be derived directly from covariance-level structure, providing a unified quantitative mean-field principle for such states.

Abstract: A recurring principle in many-body physics is that sufficiently distributed interactions or correlations lead to an effectively mean-field description. For fermionic Gaussian states, we establish a quantitative version of this principle directly at the level of Majorana covariance matrices. The main observation is that the admissibility condition for fermionic covariance matrices imposes a quantitative monogamy constraint on two-point correlations: a fixed region has only a bounded covariance budget to distribute among many disjoint regions. We use this principle in three directions. First, for quadratic fermionic Hamiltonians on finite simple graphs of high degree, we obtain product-state approximations to both the ground-state energy and the finite-temperature free energy, with error of order \(D^{-1/2}\) for \(D\)-regular graphs. Second, we prove a finite fermionic Gaussian de Finetti theorem: a \(k\)-copy Gaussian state that is \(n\)-exchangeable under graded fermionic copy permutations is \(O(k/n)\)-close in trace norm to the product of its one-copy marginal. In particular, infinite exchangeability within the Gaussian class implies exact product structure. Third, for spatial fermionic Gaussian states with exponentially clustering mutual information, we prove that the conditional mutual information across a separating buffer decays as \(O(r^{-1})\), and hence, by recoverability, the state admits an \(O(r^{-1/2})\) trace-norm approximation by a recovered state. These results show that product and de Finetti approximations and recoverability estimates for fermionic Gaussian systems can all be derived directly from covariance-level structure.

</details>


### [111] [Fourier Symmetrization for Geometric Quantum Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01874)
*Letao Wang,Abdel Lisser,Sreejith Sreekumar,Zeno Toffano*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work analyzes how symmetry shapes the expressivity of quantum Fourier models (QFMs) via their Fourier spectrum, showing symmetrization groups frequencies into orbits and sums coefficients, and demonstrates that randomized encoding enables invariant models without ancilla or extra depth, with QPINNs achieving lowest errors under hard boundary constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Geometric quantum machine learning incorporates symmetry into quantum models, yet how symmetry shapes expressivity and guides effective model design remains insufficiently understood.

Method: The authors use the Fourier representation of QFMs, organizing the frequency spectrum into orbits and summing Fourier coefficients within each orbit into a symmetrized coefficient. They analyze variance under independent exact 2-design trainable layers and bound deviations for single-layer QFMs with ε-approximate 2-design layers. They examine the hyperoctahedral group for orbit growth, show elementary abelian 2-group symmetrization yields pure multivariate Chebyshev polynomial bases, and introduce randomized encoding to implement invariant models without ancilla qubits or twirling circuit depth. Models are evaluated as QPINNs on screened Poisson and stationary viscous Hamilton-Jacobi equations.

Result: When trainable layers form independent exact 2-designs, the variance of each symmetrized coefficient equals the sum of coefficient variances in its orbit; for ε-approximate 2-design single-layer QFMs, a bound on deviation is derived that can be exponentially tighter than an existing bound. Hyperoctahedral orbit growth can mitigate vanishing expressivity, and under hard boundary constraints QPINNs using exact symmetrization and randomized encoding achieve the lowest mean errors in the two benchmarks, respectively.

Conclusion: Symmetry organizes QFM Fourier spectra into orbits whose symmetrized coefficients admit variance identities and tighter deviation bounds, enabling orbit growth to counter vanishing expressivity and yielding Chebyshev bases. Randomized encoding realizes invariant models efficiently, and QPINNs leveraging these techniques attain best results on two PDE benchmarks under hard boundary constraints.

Abstract: Geometric quantum machine learning incorporates symmetry into quantum models, but how symmetry shapes their expressivity and guides effective model design remains insufficiently understood. We address this question through the Fourier representation of quantum Fourier models (QFMs). Symmetry organizes the frequency spectrum into orbits and sums the Fourier coefficients within each orbit into a symmetrized coefficient. When QFM trainable layers form independent exact 2-designs, the variance of each symmetrized coefficient equals the sum of the coefficient variances in its orbit. For single-layer QFMs with $\varepsilon$-approximate 2-design trainable layers, we bound the deviation from this identity. The resulting bound for individual Fourier coefficients can be exponentially tighter than an existing bound. The hyperoctahedral group provides an example of orbit growth that can mitigate vanishing expressivity of the symmetrized coefficients. Symmetrization of QFMs over an elementary abelian 2-group also yields pure multivariate Chebyshev polynomial basis functions. We introduce randomized encoding, which implements invariant models without ancilla qubits or the additional circuit depth for quantum twirling. We evaluate the models as quantum physics-informed neural networks (QPINNs) on two-dimensional screened Poisson and stationary viscous Hamilton-Jacobi equations. Under hard boundary constraints, QPINNs using exact symmetrization and randomized encoding achieve the lowest mean errors in the two benchmarks, respectively.

</details>


### [112] [Floquet-Universal Hamiltonian Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01878)
*Emilio Onorati,Harriet Apel,Michael M. Wolf,Toby Cubitt*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper develops a theory of Floquet simulation, using periodically driven Hamiltonians to synthesize time-independent ones, achieving full and constructive characterization of Floquet-universal Hamiltonians with only O(1) local interaction strengths.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Analogue and digital Hamiltonian simulation are rooted in two distinct regimes: time-independent Hamiltonians (analogue) and fully controllable time-dependent quantum circuits (digital). Time-periodic Hamiltonians represent an intermediate regime that has lacked a unifying theory. The paper aims to bridge this gap by establishing a theory of Floquet simulation, motivated also by practical concerns—analogue simulation via time-independent Hamiltonians often requires impractical multi-scale local interaction strengths.

Method: The authors use periodically driven (time-periodic) Hamiltonians to synthesize time-independent target Hamiltonians. They prove that a set of interactions S can Floquet-simulate every Hamiltonian in the Lie algebra Lie(S), yielding a complete and constructive characterization of Floquet-universal Hamiltonians. The construction uses only O(1) local interaction strengths and ratios thereof, and requires a range of driving frequencies scaling polynomially with system size for important classes such as k-local lattice Hamiltonians.

Result: A set of interactions S can Floquet-simulate every Hamiltonian in Lie(S). The paper gives a complete, constructive characterization of Floquet-universal Hamiltonians able to produce any target Hamiltonian, using only O(1) local interaction strengths and avoiding impractical multi-scale strengths. Driving frequencies scale polynomially with system size for k-local lattice Hamiltonians. This yields numerous BQP-completeness and QMA-hardness results for natural physical properties and quantities in Floquet physics.

Conclusion: Floquet simulation forms an intermediate regime between analogue and digital Hamiltonian simulation, offering a practical route to universal Hamiltonian synthesis with O(1) local interaction strengths. It has immediate complexity-theoretic implications, including BQP-completeness and QMA-hardness results for natural properties studied in Floquet physics.

Abstract: Analogue and digital Hamiltonian simulation represent two distinct approaches to making one quantum system replicate the physical properties of another, with consequences ranging from practical applications to complexity theory and quantum gravity. They are rooted in two distinct regimes of quantum dynamics: time-independent Hamiltonians for the former; fully controllable time-dependent quantum circuits in the latter. Time-periodic Hamiltonians represent an intermediate regime. In this work, we establish a theory of Floquet simulation where we use periodically driven Hamiltonians to synthesise time-independent ones.
  We show that a set of interactions $S$ can Floquet-simulate every Hamiltonian in the Lie algebra $\mathrm{Lie}(S)$, and consequently we give a complete and constructive characterisation of Floquet-universal Hamiltonians that are able to produce any target Hamiltonian. Notably, our construction uses only $O(1)$ local interaction strengths and ratios thereof, avoiding the impractical multi-scale local interaction strengths often required in analogue simulation via time-independent Hamiltonians. Instead, the construction requires a range of driving frequencies which scales polynomially with system size for important classes such as $k$-local lattice Hamiltonians. This has immediate complexity-theoretic implications, including numerous BQP-completeness and QMA-hardness results for natural physical properties and quantities studied in Floquet physics.

</details>


### [113] [Exponential quantum advantages for decoded quantum interferometry in the streaming setting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01902)
*Kewen Wu,Guangxu Yang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: DQI is adapted to a streaming generalization of OPI, achieving a 93% approximation using one pass, polylogarithmic space and polylogarithmic time per entry, while classical algorithms achieving 76% require polynomial space.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Space is a central resource; storing and manipulating a massive input is challenging, especially given the fault-tolerant overhead of logical qubits. The paper asks whether DQI yields quantum advantages in memory, and whether this can be proven unconditionally.

Method: The authors study a natural generalization of OPI using Hermite interpolation and Hasse derivatives: the task is to find a low-degree polynomial satisfying as many value and derivative constraints as possible. They adapt the DQI algorithm to the streaming setting.

Result: Quantum efficiency: the adapted DQI algorithm satisfies 93% of the constraints, reads the input stream in one pass, uses polylogarithmic space, and has polylogarithmic computation time per stream entry. Classical hardness: any classical algorithm satisfying just 76% of the constraints requires polynomial space, even with polynomially many passes and unlimited time.

Conclusion: The result provides a complete tradeoff curve for tunable parameters and implies that DQI has provable quantum advantages for the original OPI problem.

Abstract: Decoded quantum interferometry (DQI) is a polynomial-time quantum algorithm introduced by Jordan et al. (Nature 2025). For a natural optimization problem, known as optimal polynomial intersection (OPI), it achieves approximation guarantees in regimes where all known classical algorithms require exponential time.
  Besides time, space is another central resource: storing and manipulating a massive input can be very challenging, especially when logical qubits carry substantial fault-tolerant implementation overhead. This motivates the following question: does DQI yield quantum advantages in memory, and can we prove it unconditionally?
  We give an affirmative answer to this question in the streaming setting. In particular, we consider a natural generalization of OPI using Hermite interpolation and Hasse derivatives, which asks for a low-degree polynomial satisfying as many constraints on its values and derivatives as possible. As a concrete example, we show
  [Quantum efficiency.] An adaptation of the DQI algorithm produces a polynomial satisfying $93\%$ of the constraints; moreover, it only reads the input stream in one pass, uses polylogarithmic space, and has polylogarithmic computation time per stream entry.
  [Classical hardness.] Any classical algorithm that produces an answer satisfying just $76\%$ of the constraints requires polynomial space, even if it can read the input stream with polynomially many passes and can use unlimited time.
  Our result provides a complete tradeoff curve for the tunable parameters, and implies that DQI has provable quantum advantages for the original OPI problem.

</details>


### [114] [Loss-tolerant distributed lattice surgery using fusion networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01923)
*Felix Burt,Richard Meister,Sheng-Ku Lin,Kuan-Cheng Chen,Michael Hanks,Roberto Bondesan,M. S. Kim,Kin K. Leung*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper uses ZX calculus to design hybrid syndrome extraction protocols for networked quantum processing units, demonstrating that fusion- and measurement-based approaches can achieve a 50% interface-erasure threshold and improve interface distances for distributed rotated surface code lattice surgery, though interface-distance advantages only materialize under low local noise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Networking matter-based QPUs is key to scaling fault-tolerant quantum computers, which requires distributed logical operations across photonic links. However, noise in these links is characteristically different from and stronger than in local QPUs due to photon loss and probabilistic linear-optical operations. Measurement- and fusion-based quantum computing are inherently robust against loss and probabilistic photonic operations, so they could complement circuit-based error correction at the interface between networked QPUs.

Method: The authors use ZX calculus transformations to construct hybrid syndrome extraction protocols and apply them to distributed rotated surface code lattice surgery. They analyze interface thresholds for several protocols, convert erasure thresholds into photon-loss thresholds, and probe subthreshold performance using fusion boosting.

Result: Several protocols attain a 50% interface-erasure threshold when local noise is absent, including circuit-based lattice surgery using fused Bell pairs and hybrid protocols using linear cluster states. Relative to a straight Bell-pair interface geometry, the hybrid protocols increase the merge-observable interface distance from d+1 to 2d+1 and restore the perpendicular-observable interface distance from floor((d+1)/2) to d. The authors calculate how the threshold decreases with truncated resource states, map correctable regions under resource-state errors, local circuit noise, and fusion erasure, and convert erasure thresholds into photon-loss thresholds. At circuit and resource-state error rates of 10^-3, local errors largely mask the interface-distance advantage; as local noise falls to 10^-4 and below, the linear-chain protocols improve more rapidly with decreasing erasure.

Conclusion: Interface distance enhancements are effective in low local noise regimes. At higher local noise (10^-3), local errors largely mask the advantages of the improved interface distances, whereas at reduced local noise (10^-4 and below), the linear-chain protocols improve more rapidly as erasure decreases.

Abstract: Networking matter-based quantum processing units (QPUs) offers a promising route to scaling fault-tolerant quantum computers. This requires distributed logical operations to be performed across photonic links, where noise is characteristically different from and stronger than in local QPUs owing to photon loss and probabilistic linear-optical operations. Measurement- and fusion-based quantum computing are designed to be robust against loss and probabilistic photonic operations, suggesting they could complement circuit-based error correction at the interface between networked QPUs. We use ZX calculus transformations to construct hybrid syndrome extraction protocols and apply them to distributed rotated surface code lattice surgery, demonstrating that several protocols attain a $50\%$ interface-erasure threshold when local noise is absent, including circuit-based lattice surgery using fused Bell pairs and hybrid protocols using linear cluster states. Relative to a straight Bell-pair interface geometry, the hybrid protocols increase the merge-observable interface distance from $d+1$ to $2d+1$ and restore the perpendicular-observable interface distance from $\lfloor(d+1)/2\rfloor$ to $d$. We calculate how the threshold decreases when using truncated resource states and map correctable regions under resource-state errors, local circuit noise, and fusion erasure. We then convert these erasure thresholds into photon-loss thresholds and probe subthreshold performance using fusion boosting. At circuit and resource-state error rates of $10^{-3}$, local errors largely mask the interface-distance advantage. As local noise is reduced to $10^{-4}$ and below, the linear-chain protocols improve more rapidly with decreasing erasure, suggesting that interface distance enhancements are effective in low local noise regimes.

</details>


### [115] [Avoiding Exponentially Large Groups with Open Quantum System Technology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01932)
*Jihong Cai,Advith Govindarajan,Marius Junge*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes state preparation and channel universality via open quantum systems, replacing global unitary control with a small poly(n)-dimensional Lie group acting on n+3 qubits, combined with partial trace and environmental state preparation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Preparing arbitrary quantum states and channels conventionally requires control over the full unitary group on n qubits, which is exponentially large. The authors seek a cheaper control paradigm that exploits open quantum system resources to reduce the required group to polynomial size.

Method: Identify a small Lie group G in (n+3) qubits whose Lie algebra has dimension poly(n), derived from local Pauli strings. State preparation proceeds by repeatedly applying partial trace, environment state preparation, and group operations. A single open-system magic interaction Hamiltonian is identified whose unitary group, combined with the open-system technology, yields transitivity on the space of all density matrices. Channel universality is achieved using a larger environment.

Result: Every state can be approximated from any fixed input state using the small group together with partial trace and environmental state preparation. Adding one open-system magic interaction Hamiltonian achieves full transitivity on densities. All channels on n qubits can be approximated with a larger environment, giving channel universality.

Conclusion: Open quantum system resources enable a new landscape of cheap and expensive states, densities, and channels, with control complexity polynomial rather than exponential in n.

Abstract: We propose a novel approach to state preparation by embracing the full power of open quantum systems. Instead of working with the whole unitary group in $n$ qubits, we identify a small Lie group $G$ in $(n+3)$ qubits. The dimension of its Lie algebra is $\text{poly}(n)$. Every state can be approximated starting from any fixed input state by repeatedly using partial trace and state preparation in the environment alongside group operations.
