Moreover, we also identify a single (open system magic) interaction Hamiltonian whose unitary group can be combined with open system quantum technology mentioned above to achieve transitivity on the space of all densities. Similarly, we show that all channels on $n$ qubits can be approximated by using a larger environment, achieving channel universality. The interesting small groups we identify are derived from Lie algebras of local Pauli strings and lead to a new landscape of cheap and expensive states, densities, and channels.

</details>


### [116] [Optimal query complexity for fractional quantum evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01940)
*Anthony Yuezhang Liu,Adam Wesołowski,Jayne Thompson,Mile Gu,Lirandë Pira*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proves a matching lower bound for the fractional quantum query problem, showing the optimal query complexity is Θ(1/δ · log(1/ε)), which confirms that the existing QSVT construction is asymptotically optimal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The fractional query problem—implementing U^t = e^{itH} for noninteger t given oracle access to U = e^{iH}—had an upper bound of O((1/δ) log(1/ε)) queries via quantum singular value transformation, but no matching lower bound was known. Closing this gap determines the true optimal query complexity.

Method: The authors reduce any N-query circuit to the approximation of e^{itθ} by a trigonometric polynomial of degree O(N), then apply the Remez inequality to derive the lower bound. They additionally provide an alternative proof constructing a linear functional that annihilates the approximant space.

Result: They establish a lower bound of Ω_τ((1/δ) log(1/ε)) matching the QSVT upper bound, yielding optimal complexity Θ_τ((1/δ) log(1/ε)). The alternative functional-based argument gives a Ω_τ(log(1/ε)) bound that is uniform in δ.

Conclusion: The optimal query complexity for the fractional query problem is Θ_τ((1/δ) log(1/ε)), proving that the QSVT construction is asymptotically optimal.

Abstract: Given oracle access to an unknown unitary $U=e^{iH}$ , the fractional query problem asks how many queries are required to implement a noninteger power $U^t=e^{itH}$, $0<t<1$, when the spectrum is separated from the branch cut by a gap $δ$. Quantum singular value transformation gives an upper bound of $O\!\left(\frac{1}δ\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$ queries for approximation error $\varepsilon$. We prove a matching lower bound for arbitrary query algorithms. Our argument reduces any $N$-query circuit to the approximation of $e^{itθ}$ by a trigonometric polynomial with degree bounded by $O(N)$, together with Remez inequality. This allows us to establish the lower bound of $Ω_τ\!\left(\frac{1}δ\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$. Consequently, the optimal query complexity for fractional query problem is $Θ_τ\!\left(\frac{1}δ\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$, showing that the known QSVT construction is asymptotically optimal. We also give an alternative lower bound proof based on constructing a linear functional that annihilates the approximant space, yielding a $Ω_τ\!\left(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$ bound uniform to $δ$.

</details>


### [117] [Universality Sacrifices Reliability in Classical-Quantum Channel Coding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01941)
*Kaito Watanabe,Masahito Hayashi,Takaya Matsuura,Hao-Chung Cheng*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Universal channel coding cannot generally attain the channel-aware optimal high-rate reliability for classical-quantum channels, because the unitary rotation of the output system is unknown. The channel-aware and universal error exponents are governed by the Petz and sandwiched Rényi divergences, which coincide for commuting outputs but differ for noncommuting ones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classically, universal codes achieve both capacity and optimal high-rate reliability without a full channel description. The paper asks whether this compatibility extends to classical-quantum (cq) channels, i.e., whether optimal channel-aware reliability is achievable by a fixed universal coding scheme.

Method: The authors exhibit a family of cq channels for which no fixed coding scheme attains the channel-aware optimal reliability, due to ignorance of the unitary rotation of the output system. They derive a converse bound on reliability for unitary-invariant decoders and construct a channel-independent encoder-decoder pair achieving a universally achievable bound matching the converse in the high-rate regime.

Result: The optimal channel-aware and universal error exponents are governed by the Petz and sandwiched Rényi divergences, respectively. These divergences coincide for commuting outputs but differ for noncommuting ones. The converse bound for unitary-invariant decoders can be strictly smaller than the optimal channel-aware error exponent.

Conclusion: Universality preserves optimal reliability classically but can reduce it quantumly, revealing a fundamental reliability cost of performing the classical-quantum channel coding task universally.

Abstract: Universal channel coding enables communication without a complete description of the channel. For classical channels, universal codes can attain both capacity and the optimal high-rate reliability. We show that this compatibility fails for classical-quantum channels in general; that is, the optimal reliability in the channel-aware scenario is not always achievable with universal coding due to the ignorance of the unitary rotation of the output system. We exhibit a family of classical-quantum channels for which one cannot achieve the channel-aware optimal reliability by a fixed coding scheme. We further derive a converse bound on the reliability for unitary-invariant decoders, a natural assumption for the universal coding scheme, that can be strictly smaller than the optimal channel-aware error exponent. Conversely, we construct a channel-independent encoder-decoder pair and establish a universally achievable bound on the reliability that matches this converse bound in the high-rate regime, thereby characterizing the optimal universal reliability. Specifically, the channel-aware and universal exponents are governed by the Petz and sandwiched Rényi divergences, respectively. These divergences coincide for commuting outputs but differ for noncommuting ones, explaining why universality preserves optimal reliability classically but can reduce it quantumly. Our results showcase the fundamental reliability cost of performing the classical-quantum channel coding task universally.

</details>


### [118] [Hardware-Efficient Ground-State Preparation using Variational Imaginary-Time Majorana Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01954)
*Federico Santona,Manuel G. Algaba,Aeishah Ameera Anuar,Anna M. Wernbacher,Prachi Sharma,Fedor Šimkovic*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: VIME is a classical pre-training algorithm extending OVQITE to molecular electronic structure via a single compiled Majorana-propagation surrogate graph, paired with a compressed c-tUPS ansatz that achieves chemical accuracy at far lower resource cost than ADAPT-VMPE.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Compact ground-state preparation circuits are essential for early fault-tolerant quantum chemistry, motivating a need for resource-efficient algorithms that can prepare accurate molecular ground states with reduced circuit cost.

Method: VIME extends operator-projected variational quantum imaginary-time evolution (OVQITE) to molecular electronic structure using a single compiled Majorana-propagation (MP) surrogate graph. It is paired with a newly developed compressed tiled Unitary Product State (c-tUPS) ansatz to reduce both classical simulation and quantum state-preparation costs.

Result: For the strongly correlated ruthenium complex TLD-1411, active-space calculations up to 52 qubits reach chemical precision against DMRG references, with energy errors ~400× smaller than ADAPT-VMPE. Across the multireference acene series up to heptacene (60 qubits), VIME matches or improves MP-reported ADAPT-VMPE accuracy with up to 26× fewer CNOTs and 167× lower two-qubit depth.

Conclusion: Imaginary-time classical pre-training of compressed variational ansätze is established as a promising route to resource-efficient molecular ground-state preparation.

Abstract: Compact ground-state preparation circuits are essential for early fault-tolerant quantum chemistry. We introduce Variational Imaginary-time Majorana Evolution (VIME), a classical pre-training algorithm that extends operator-projected variational quantum imaginary-time evolution (OVQITE) to molecular electronic structure using a single compiled Majorana-propagation (MP) surrogate graph. We pair VIME with a compressed tiled Unitary Product State (c-tUPS) ansatz that we develop to reduce both classical simulation and quantum state-preparation costs. In the strongly correlated ruthenium complex TLD-1411, active-space calculations requiring up to 52 qubits achieve chemical precision against DMRG reference energies, with energy errors approximately $400\times$ smaller than those from ADAPT-VQE-based variational Majorana-propagation (ADAPT-VMPE). Across the strongly multireference acene series up to heptacene (60 qubits), VIME matches or improves on the MP-reported energy accuracy of the ADAPT-VMPE comparison points with up to $26\times$ fewer CNOTs and $167\times$ lower two-qubit depth. These results establish imaginary-time classical pre-training of compressed variational ansätze as a promising route to resource-efficient molecular ground-state preparation.

</details>


### [119] [Fully tolerant product state testing and closest product state learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01979)
*Zongbo Bao,Jonas Helsen,Tuyen Nguyen*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper provides a time-efficient, tolerant product-state tester and an improved closest product state learner for unknown $n$-qudit states, both using random coloring and blockwise spectral projection, with copy complexity independent of $n$ for testing and $\widetilde{O}((nd)^2)2^{\widetilde{O}(1/\varepsilon^8)}$ for learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Testing whether an unknown $n$-qudit state is close to or far from any product state, and learning a near-optimal product state, are fundamental problems where prior methods may be inefficient in time or require many copies. The work aims for time efficiency and copy complexity independent of $n$ for tolerant testing, and substantially better copy complexity for learning.

Method: The method uses a random coloring argument: partitioning $[n]$ into $q$ parts so that the squared overlap with the closest product state w.r.t. the partition exceeds the global optimum by at most an additive $O(1/q)$. This reduces the problem to tolerant testing of $q$ parties with growing local dimensions. Combined with blockwise spectral projection, it shows that the natural $k$-copy generalization of Harrow & Montanaro's product state test is an efficient tolerant tester. For learning, the same techniques plus a qudit variant of the Bakshi et al. high-fidelity product state learning algorithm and a sampling technique based on Werner's optimal cloning channel yield an $\varepsilon$-approximately optimal product state.

Result: A time-efficient tolerant product-state tester requiring an $n$-independent number of copies is obtained. For closest product state learning, the algorithm takes $\widetilde{O}((nd)^2)2^{\widetilde{O}(1/\varepsilon^8)}$ copies and produces an $\varepsilon$-approximately optimal product state.

Conclusion: Random coloring and blockwise spectral projection are powerful tools for product-state testing and learning, enabling $n$-independent copy complexity for tolerant testing and substantially improved copy complexity for learning.

Abstract: We address the problem of testing whether an unknown $n$-qudit state $ρ$ is $a$-close to a product state or $b$-far away from any product state, as measured in terms of state overlap. We provide a time-efficient algorithm to solve this problem that requires an $n$-independent number of copies of the unknown state. Our random coloring argument shows that for any unknown state there always exists a partition of $[n]$ into $q$ parts such that the square of the overlap with the closest product state with respect to this partition is only an additive factor $O(1/q)$ larger. This reduces the problem to tolerant testing of $q$ parties, with potentially growing local dimensions. Combining this insight with blockwise spectral projection arguments we can show that the natural $k$-copy generalization of Harrow & Montanaro's product state test provides an efficient tolerant tester.
  We use the same random coloring and blockwise spectral projection techniques to obtain a substantially improved algorithm for closest product state learning. In particular we give an algorithm that takes in $\widetilde{O}\big((nd)^2\big)\, 2^{\widetilde{O}(1/\varepsilon^8)}$ copies of the unknown state and produces an $\varepsilon$-approximately optimal product state. The key technical components of this learner are a qudit variant of the Bakshi et al. high-fidelity product state learning algorithm and a sampling technique based on Werner's optimal cloning channel.

</details>


### [120] [On the Metastability of the Mean-Field Interchange Model for Local Dimension $\geq 3$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01987)
*Sergio Escobar,Lin Lin,Michael Ragone,Kevin D. Stubbs*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper analyzes the Davies dynamics of the mean-field interchange model with local dimension d≥3 and single-site generalized Pauli couplings, showing that thermalization is fast (polynomial) at both low and high temperatures but breaks down (exponentially slow) in the interval between two inverse temperatures β_min(d) and β=d, where a static first-order phase transition lies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum systems are often believed to thermalize slowly at low temperatures due to metastable phases. Yet there are cases where further cooling restores polynomial-time thermalization. The paper seeks to identify and rigorously demonstrate such a behavior and delineate the dynamical phase diagram separating fast and slow thermalization regimes.

Method: Analysis of the Davies generator for the mean-field interchange model with local dimension d≥3 and single-site generalized Pauli couplings. The proof employs Schur-Weyl duality to decompose the Davies generator into a classical Markov generator on Young diagrams, plus remaining symmetry sectors whose gap is Ω(n^{-1}).

Result: The dynamical phase diagram is governed by two distinguished inverse temperatures, β_min(d) and β=d, with the static first-order phase transition lying between them. Outside the interval (β_min(d), d), the spectral gap of the Davies generator is bounded below by an inverse polynomial in system size n, yielding polynomial-time thermalization. Inside this interval, the gap vanishes exponentially in n, indicating metastability. The exponential slowdown arises entirely from the Young diagram chain.

Conclusion: Cooling can restore fast thermalization even when an intermediate temperature regime suffers metastable, exponentially slow dynamics. The spectral gap behavior of the Davies generator fully characterizes the dynamical phase diagram, and the obstruction to fast mixing is localized to the Young diagram Markov chain.

Abstract: Many quantum systems are believed to thermalize slowly at low temperatures due to the existence of metastable phases. However, there are cases where further cooling can restore polynomial-time thermalization: we demonstrate that the Davies dynamics of the mean-field interchange model with local dimension $d\geq 3$ and single-site generalized Pauli couplings exhibits this behavior. The dynamical phase diagram is beholden to two distinguished inverse temperatures, $β_{\mathrm{min}}(d)$ and $β=d$, with the static first-order phase transition lying between them. At low and high temperatures surrounding the interval $(β_{\mathrm{min}}(d),d)$, the spectral gap of the Davies generator is bounded below by an inverse polynomial in system size $n$. Meanwhile, in this interval, the gap vanishes exponentially in $n$, indicating metastability. The proof uses Schur-Weyl duality to decompose the Davies generator into a classical Markov generator on Young diagrams, and the restriction to the remaining symmetry sectors whose gap is $Ω(n^{-1})$. Thus, exponential slowdown arises entirely from the Young diagram chain.

</details>


### [121] [Geometry of Knill-Laflamme Coefficients for Pauli Error Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01992)
*Baisong Sun,Ningping Cao,Yiu Tung Poon,Bei Zeng*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper develops a geometric framework for Knill-Laflamme coefficients, showing that representation multiplicity can lift active expectation data to exact scalar compressions, unifying commuting Pauli families and subsystem stabilizer codes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Knill-Laflamme coefficients characterize exact quantum error detection via scalar compressions of error operators onto the code space. For prescribed Pauli observables and fixed code dimension, attainable coefficient vectors form a joint higher-rank numerical range whose Euclidean norm image is the signature spectrum. How operator representation, code dimension, and detection constraints govern this geometry was not well understood.

Method: The authors study the geometry of attainable Knill-Laflamme coefficient vectors. Their main theorem identifies representation multiplicity as sufficient to lift active expectation data to exact scalar compressions, requiring each occupied block to have multiplicity at least the code dimension times the rank of the corresponding active state. They analyze convexity and connectedness of the coefficient range, apply the framework to commuting Pauli families and subsystem stabilizer codes, and construct parent-Hamiltonian realizations of exact detecting codes as degenerate ground spaces.

Result: When multiplicity suffices to realize all active states, the coefficient range equals the full active expectation body, hence is convex and connected, with a closed interval as signature spectrum. Sufficient common-eigenspace degeneracy yields polytopes for commuting Pauli families; protected logical subsystems supply multiplicity spaces for noncommuting gauge observables. Examples show increasing code dimension can make a range shrink, collapse, or become empty, while increasing multiplicity can turn a Bloch sphere into a filled ball. A three-qubit example shows noncommuting detection constraints reduce a tetrahedral range to its barycenter and four vertices, yielding a disconnected signature spectrum.

Conclusion: The framework provides a structural account of Knill-Laflamme coefficient geometry, unifying commuting Pauli families and subsystem stabilizer codes, and motivates sharper criteria for connected coefficient ranges and interval signature spectra under structured error models.

Abstract: Knill-Laflamme coefficients characterize exact quantum error detection through scalar compressions of error operators to the code space. For prescribed Pauli observables and a fixed code dimension, attainable coefficient vectors form a joint higher-rank numerical range, whose Euclidean norm image is the signature spectrum. We study how operator representation, code dimension, and detection constraints govern this geometry. Our main theorem identifies representation multiplicity as sufficient to lift active expectation data to exact scalar compressions: a lift exists whenever each occupied block has multiplicity at least the code dimension times the rank of the corresponding active state. When multiplicity suffices to realize all active states, the coefficient range is the full active expectation body and is therefore convex and connected, with a closed interval as its signature spectrum. This framework unifies commuting Pauli families, where sufficient common-eigenspace degeneracy yields polytopes, and subsystem stabilizer codes, where protected logical subsystems supply multiplicity spaces for noncommuting gauge observables. Explicit examples show how increasing the code dimension can make a range shrink, collapse, or become empty, while increasing multiplicity can turn a Bloch sphere into a filled ball. In the subsystem setting, parent-Hamiltonian constructions realize exact detecting codes as degenerate ground spaces, selecting distinguished vectors and continuous paths within these ranges. A final three-qubit example shows additional noncommuting detection constraints reduce a tetrahedral range to its barycenter and four vertices, producing a disconnected signature spectrum. The results provide a structural framework for Knill-Laflamme coefficient geometry and motivate sharper criteria for connected coefficient ranges and interval signature spectra under structured error models.

</details>


### [122] [Operational transformation rates of quantum states](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02025)
*Giulia Mazzola,Renato Renner*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper relaxes the iid assumption for quantum state copies by defining an operational set of states indistinguishable from a tensor power, shows via a de Finetti-type argument that this set is asymptotically almost-iid, and introduces operational analogues of asymptotic transformation rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The standard description of many copies of a quantum state as a tensor power relies on the iid assumption. However, operationally, an experimentalist cannot generally exclude imperfections at unknown positions or correlations between copies, so the iid assumption is not justified a priori. The work aims to place asymptotic quantum resource manipulation on a more operationally sound footing by relaxing this assumption.

Method: The authors relax the iid assumption by considering a whole set of resource states fundamentally indistinguishable from a tensor power state, i.e., states that appear to the experimentalist like many copies of the given quantum state. They use a de Finetti-type argument to characterize this operational set.

Result: The operational set of indistinguishable states can asymptotically be represented by almost-iid states. Building on this, the authors introduce operational analogues of asymptotic transformation rates, including entanglement cost and distillation.

Conclusion: Assuming exact iid copies is not operationally necessary; an indistinguishable set of states behaves asymptotically as almost-iid. This yields operational definitions of asymptotic transformation rates and raises the open question of whether these operational rates coincide with their iid counterparts.

Abstract: Typically, multiple copies of a quantum state are described by a tensor power of that state. However, from an operational point of view, an experimentalist cannot, in general, rule out imperfections at unknown positions or correlations between copies, so assuming independent and identically distributed (iid) copies is not justified a priori. In the following, we relax the iid assumption by considering a whole set of resource states that are fundamentally indistinguishable from a tensor power state, and in that sense look like many copies of the quantum state to the experimentalist. Using a de Finetti-type argument, we show that this operational set of states can asymptotically be represented by almost-iid states. As an application, we introduce operational analogues of asymptotic transformation rates, such as entanglement cost and distillation. This raises the question of whether these rates coincide with their iid counterparts.

</details>


### [123] [(A Variant of) Clifford Circuit Synthesis is NP-Complete](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02029)
*Luna Lima Keller,Richard Kueng*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Optimal Clifford circuit synthesis is proven NP-hard, completing the complexity landscape where Clifford simulation/equivalence are in P but depth-optimal synthesis is NP-complete.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Optimal circuit synthesis—finding the shortest-depth circuit for a given functionality over a fixed gate set—is central to quantum and classical hardware design. The classical case is well understood (heuristics and hardness), but optimal quantum circuit synthesis is far less understood, so the authors aim to establish rigorous complexity-theoretic hardness for the quantum (Clifford) case.

Method: The proof has two parts: (i) a reduction from 3-edge colorability on 3-regular graphs to a circuit synthesis problem involving only CZ gates; (ii) a proof that adding other elementary Clifford gates (Hadamard, phase, CNOT) cannot improve the optimal circuit depth.

Result: A variant of optimal Clifford synthesis is NP-hard, giving a matching hardness result to the known 3-SAT-based heuristics.

Conclusion: The complexity-theoretic picture of Clifford circuits is sharpened: simulation and equivalence checking are in P, whereas deciding whether a Clifford unitary admits an implementation within a prescribed depth is NP-complete.

Abstract: Optimal circuit synthesis is the problem of finding the shortest-depth circuit representation of a given functionality with respect to a pre-specified elementary gate set. This is a central problem in both quantum and classical hardware design. While the classical version is very well understood -- both in terms of heuristics and rigorous hardness assertions -- much less is known about optimal quantum circuit synthesis.
  We focus on optimal Clifford circuit synthesis, for which various heuristics are known, e.g. via reduction to 3-SAT. Our main result supplies a matching hardness result: a variant of optimal Clifford synthesis is NP-hard. The proof proceeds in two parts: (i) reduce 3-edge colorability on 3-regular graphs to a circuit synthesis problem that only involves CZ gates, (ii) prove that the availability of additional elementary Clifford gates -- most notably: Hadamard, phase and CNOT -- cannot lead to further improvements of the optimal circuit depth. Our work sharpens the complexity-theoretic understanding of Clifford circuits: simulation and equivalence checking are in P, whereas deciding whether a Clifford unitary admits an implementation within a prescribed depth is NP-complete.

</details>


### [124] [Sparse Hamiltonian simulation with optimal dependence on the maximum column Euclidean norm](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02030)
*Zecheng Li,Chunhao Wang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A quantum algorithm simulates d-sparse Hermitian Hamiltonians using O(tΛ√d + √d log(2/ε)) sparse-oracle queries, removing subpolynomial overhead and matching worst-case lower bounds, with applications to quantum linear systems and black-box unitary implementation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior algorithms, notably Low's [STOC 2019], contained a subpolynomial overhead in the query complexity for simulating sparse Hamiltonians. The goal is to remove this overhead and approach the known worst-case lower bounds, achieving optimal query complexity.

Method: The paper provides a quantum algorithm simulating a d-sparse Hermitian Hamiltonian H with known upper bound Λ on max column Euclidean norm ‖H‖_{1→2}. It uses O(tΛ√d + √d log(2/ε)) sparse-oracle queries, replacing the subpolynomial overhead with an additive logarithmic precision term. A spectral-norm bound may substitute for Λ. Gate counts are linear in query scale plus polynomial/log factors.

Result: For tΛ ≥ 1/2, simulation with operator-norm error ε uses O(tΛ√d + √d log(2/ε)) queries, matching the worst-case lower bound when d>1 and tΛ ≥ log(2/ε). Applications yield O(κ√d polylog(κ/ε)) queries for d-sparse quantum linear systems (‖A‖≤1, ‖A^{-1}‖≤κ), and O(√d log(2/ε)) queries for black-box unitaries with ≤d nonzero entries per row/column, optimal at constant error and resolving an open question, giving Θ(√N) for arbitrary N×N unitaries.

Conclusion: The algorithm achieves near-optimal query complexity for sparse Hamiltonian simulation, eliminating subpolynomial overheads and matching lower bounds. It improves quantum linear system solving and resolves the open question on black-box unitary implementation posed by Berry and Childs [QIC 2012].

Abstract: We give a quantum algorithm for simulating a $d$-sparse Hermitian Hamiltonian $H$, assuming a known upper bound $Λ$ on its maximum column Euclidean norm $\|H\|_{1\to2}$. For $tΛ\ge1/2$, simulation with operator-norm error $ε$ uses \[
  O\!\left(tΛ\sqrt d+\sqrt d\log(2/ε)\right) \] sparse-oracle queries. This removes the subpolynomial overhead in Low's algorithm [STOC 2019], replacing it with an additive logarithmic precision term. For $d>1$ and $tΛ\ge\log(2/ε)$, the bound matches the worst-case lower bound. A known spectral-norm upper bound may also be used in place of $Λ$. The number of 1- and 2-qubit gates is linear in the query scale, up to oracle costs and polynomial overhead in the input bit lengths and logarithmic precision parameters.
  As applications, we obtain $O(κ\sqrt d\,\mathrm{polylog}(κ/ε))$ queries for solving $d$-sparse quantum linear systems with $\|A\|\le1$ and $\|A^{-1}\|\leκ$, under standard sparse and state-preparation access. We also give a gate-efficient implementation of black-box unitaries with at most $d$ nonzero entries per row and column using $O(\sqrt d\log(2/ε))$ queries, given sparse access to the unitary and its adjoint. At constant error, the query bound is optimal and yields $Θ(\sqrt N)$ queries for arbitrary $N\times N$ unitaries, resolving the open question on black-box unitary implementation posed by Berry and Childs [QIC 2012].

</details>


### [125] [Adaptivity is all you need: Optimal stabilizer learning using just single-copy measurements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02031)
*L. Bittel,J. Eisert,W. Gong,A. A. Mele,L. Schatzki*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Adaptive single-copy Clifford measurements achieve Θ(n) sample complexity for learning stabilizer states, closing the gap with two-copy Bell sampling and extending to low-stabilizer-nullity states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stabilizer states are central to quantum computing, but their learnability shows a gap: Θ(n) copies suffice with two-copy Bell measurements, while non-adaptive single-copy measurements require Ω(n²). The paper aims to determine whether adaptivity can close this gap, and to extend learnability to states beyond exact stabilizers.

Method: A polynomial-time adaptive algorithm using single-copy Clifford measurements. Related techniques yield a sample-optimal single-copy tolerant tester, and an analysis of the testing tradeoff with k qubits of quantum memory. The adaptive mechanism is generalized to states of stabilizer nullity at most r, including Clifford circuits with a bounded number of T gates.

Result: Adaptivity fully closes the gap: arbitrary n-qubit stabilizer states are learned from Θ(n) single-copy Clifford measurements, matching optimal Bell-sampling sample complexity without multi-copy measurements. A sample-optimal single-copy tolerant tester is obtained, with optimal testing tradeoff Θ(n−k+1/ε) at infidelity ε using k qubits of quantum memory. States of stabilizer nullity at most r are learned with O(n2^r) single-copy measurements.

Conclusion: Adaptive single-copy measurements are as powerful as multi-copy Bell sampling for stabilizer-state learning, establishing adaptivity as a key resource and providing sample-optimal learning and testing, with extensions to low-stabilizer-nullity states and bounded-T-gate Clifford circuits.

Abstract: Stabilizer states are central to quantum computing, underlying quantum error correction, benchmarking, and efficient classical simulation. Yet their learnability exhibits a striking gap: an $n$-qubit stabilizer state can be learned from $Θ(n)$ copies using two-copy Bell measurements, whereas non-adaptive single-copy measurements require $Ω(n^2)$ copies. Here we show that adaptivity completely closes this gap. We give a polynomial-time adaptive algorithm that learns an arbitrary $n$-qubit stabilizer state from $Θ(n)$ single-copy Clifford measurements, matching the optimal sample complexity of Bell sampling without any multi-copy measurements. The same ideas yield a sample-optimal single-copy tolerant tester and, with $k$ qubits of quantum memory, the optimal testing tradeoff $Θ(n-k+1/\varepsilon)$ at infidelity $\varepsilon$. Finally, we show that this adaptive mechanism extends beyond exact stabilizer states: states of stabilizer nullity at most $r$, including states prepared by Clifford circuits with a bounded number of $T$ gates, can be learned using $O(n2^{r})$ single-copy measurements.

</details>


### [126] [Nonlocality without entanglement for multipartite quantum measurements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02032)
*Satyaki Manna,Debashis Saha*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper establishes a measurement analogue of nonlocality without entanglement, showing that certain product measurements are perfectly distinguishable jointly but not under LOCC, with hierarchies, directionality, and optical realizations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nonlocality without entanglement (NLWE) is known for quantum states, but its measurement analogue has not been clearly established. The authors aim to show that this phenomenon emerges in much simpler settings for measurements, and to explore the structure of LOCC discrimination of measurements.

Method: The authors analyze pairs of two-qubit product measurements under joint access versus LOCC-restricted access. They compare adaptive LOCC protocols against non-adaptive ones using six bipartite qutrit product measurements. They also examine directional LOCC distinguishability and construct n-qubit product measurement pairs indistinguishable across any bipartition even with arbitrary joint operations per side, plus an optical realization of quantum data hiding.

Result: A pair of two-qubit product measurements is perfectly distinguishable jointly but not via LOCC. Adaptive LOCC protocols strictly outperform non-adaptive ones for six bipartite qutrit product measurements. LOCC distinguishability can be intrinsically directional. Pairs of n-qubit product measurements are globally perfectly distinguishable but not across any bipartition, even allowing arbitrary joint operations on each side. Quantum data hiding via process NLWE can be implemented with elementary optical components.

Conclusion: Measurement-based nonlocality without entanglement arises in simple, low-dimensional settings, exhibits adaptive advantages and directional asymmetry, and generalizes to strong bipartition-independent indistinguishability. It also enables practical quantum data hiding with basic optical elements.

Abstract: We show that a pair of two-qubit product measurements can be perfectly distinguished when accessed jointly, yet cannot be perfectly distinguished when their local components are distributed between distant parties restricted to local quantum operations and classical communication (LOCC). This establishes a measurement analogue of nonlocality without entanglement that, unlike its state-based counterpart, emerges in a remarkably simple setting. We further uncover a hierarchy of LOCC discrimination strategies: adaptive protocols, in which the probe used at one site may depend on classical information obtained from another, can strictly outperform restricted non-adaptive protocols, as demonstrated by a set of six bipartite qutrit product measurements. We also show that LOCC distinguishability of bipartite measurements can be intrinsically directional, depending on which party initiates the protocol. More generally, we construct pairs of $n$-qubit product measurements that are globally perfectly distinguishable but cannot be perfectly distinguished across any bipartition, even when all parties on either side of the bipartition are allowed arbitrary joint quantum operations. Finally, we show how quantum data hiding based on quantum-process nonlocality without entanglement can be realized using elementary optical components.

</details>


### [127] [Complexity and Applications of Nearest Stabilizer Product State Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02037)
*Daniel Grier,Hakop Pashayan,Luke Schaeffer*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper studies maximizing the overlap between a stabilizer state and a product of single-qubit stabilizer states, giving a complete complexity classification: after accounting for Clifford symmetries, 9 distinct sets S exist, and all but the two simplest yield NP-complete problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The optimization problem over stabilizer product states arises in multiple contexts, but its complexity was not fully understood. The authors aim to unify applications—classical simulation runtime bounds, entanglement measures, and low-rank matrix completion—and to determine the exact computational complexity of the nearest stabilizer product state problem.

Method: The problem is formulated as maximizing |⟨ψ|φ1,...,φn⟩|² over |φi⟩ ∈ S, given an n-qubit stabilizer state |ψ⟩ and a set S of single-qubit stabilizer states. The authors vary S and reduce the classification to 9 distinct cases by factoring out symmetries in the Clifford group, then prove complexity results for each case.

Result: The problem has applications to tighter runtime bounds for certain classical simulation algorithms, measures of entanglement, and the complexity of low-rank matrix completion. A complete complexity classification shows that, among the 9 distinct sets S up to Clifford symmetries, all but the two simplest are NP-complete.

Conclusion: The nearest stabilizer product state problem is fully classified: most natural choices of S make it NP-complete, with only the two simplest cases being tractable, highlighting broad computational hardness across its applications.

Abstract: Consider the following optimization problem over stabilizer product states: given an $n$-qubit stabilizer state $\left|ψ\right\rangle$ and a set of single-qubit stabilizer states $S$, maximize $\left|\langle ψ| φ_1, \ldots, φ_n \rangle\right|^2$ over single-qubit stabilizer states $\left|φ_i\right\rangle \in S$. By varying the set $S$, we show that solutions to this problem can be useful in a variety of settings: tighter runtime bounds for certain classical simulation algorithms; measures of entanglement; and the complexity of low-rank matrix completion. Moreover, we give a complete complexity classification of this nearest stabilizer product state problem. After accounting for the symmetries in the Clifford group, there are $9$ distinct possible sets $S$, and we show that all but the two simplest of these are $\NP$-complete.

</details>


### [128] [Fermionic Gaussianity can be tested with mode-independent sample-complexity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02050)
*Maxwell West,Martin Larocca*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a general technique for upper-bounding the acceptance probability of 2-copy tests for states far from a target family, yielding optimal sample complexity for deciding fermionic Gaussianity and Slater determinant states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deciding whether an unknown quantum state belongs to or is far from a given family is a fundamental problem in quantum information theory. While natural 2-copy tests often accept states in the target family, bounding acceptance for far states—and thus the sample complexity of the decision problem—is typically much harder. The authors aim to close this gap for important families such as fermionic Gaussian and Slater determinant states.

Method: The authors develop a simple new technique for upper-bounding the acceptance probability of 2-copy tests for general families, which in turn upper-bounds the sample complexity of the underlying decision problem. They apply this framework to test fermionic Gaussianity and Slater determinant states, analyzing the well-known Bell sampling procedure within their bounds.

Result: Deciding whether an unknown pure n-mode state is fermionic Gaussian, or at least ε-far in trace distance from all Gaussian states, can be accomplished with the optimal number Θ(ε⁻²) of copies. The analysis shows Bell sampling for testing fermionic Gaussianity is optimal, even against protocols using arbitrary collective and adaptive measurements. The analogous decision problem for Slater determinant states is also solvable with mode-independent sample complexity.

Conclusion: The proposed framework provides a general and powerful tool for bounding the sample complexity of quantum state decision problems via 2-copy tests. It establishes the optimality of Bell sampling for fermionic Gaussianity testing and extends to Slater determinant states, demonstrating broad applicability to testing structured quantum state families.

Abstract: Deciding whether an unknown quantum state either belongs to, or is far from, a given family of states is a natural question of quantum information theory. In many such cases, there is a natural 2-copy test which always accepts whenever the state indeed belongs to the target family; it is often much more difficult, however, to bound the probability of acceptance for states that are far from the family. Here we develop a simple new technique for upper bounding this probability for general families, and therefore upper bounding the sample-complexity of the decision problem itself. As a particularly striking example of this framework, we show that deciding whether an unknown pure $n$-mode state is fermionic Gaussian, or at least $\varepsilon$-far in trace distance from all Gaussian states, can be accomplished using the optimal number $Θ(\varepsilon^{-2})$ of copies. Remarkably, our analysis applies to the well-known Bell sampling procedure for testing fermionic Gaussianity, which we therefore show to be optimal, even compared to protocols making arbitrary collective and adaptive measurements. As a second example, we show that the analogous decision problem for the family of Slater determinant states can also be solved with mode-independent sample-complexity.

</details>


### [129] [Sequential Capacity of Quantum Processes with Finite Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02068)
*Yibin Wang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper defines "sequential response capacity" to quantify how many adaptive testing stages can distinguish quantum processes by a prescribed probability gap, and derives scaling laws: with fixed sizes and resolution, capacity grows like K log K for quantum devices (via time-dependent phase rotations on one qubit) versus only linearly for fixed-basis classical stochastic processes; noise analysis shows residual phase-flip probability sets the coherence timescale limiting this logarithmic enhancement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how complex the responses of a quantum device can become as it runs longer with fixed internal memory, and to characterize the advantage of quantum over classical processes in sequential discrimination tasks.

Method: Define sequential response capacity (number of adaptive testing stages using fresh runs that separate possible processes by a prescribed response-probability gap) and derive tight laws relating capacity to run length and probability resolution. Construct protocols using time-dependent phase rotations on a single visible qubit with no extra internal memory, yielding response probabilities of exactly zero or one. Compare with classical stochastic processes measuring in a fixed basis each step, and analyze phase sequences selected by a stored classical label under independent Pauli noise.

Result: For fixed system and memory sizes, capacity scales as K log K at fixed resolution (K = number of time steps per run), with matching upper and lower bounds. Classical fixed-basis processes achieve only linear capacity. Under ideal controls and weak residual phase noise, matching capacity bounds hold at a fixed small gap, identifying the inverse residual phase-flip probability as the coherence timescale limiting the extra logarithmic growth.

Conclusion: Quantum devices with fixed memory achieve a logarithmic (K log K) enhancement in sequential response capacity over classical processes, realized by phase rotations on a single qubit without extra memory; this enhancement is ultimately limited by decoherence, with the inverse residual phase-flip probability serving as the relevant coherence timescale.

Abstract: How complex can the responses of a quantum device become as it runs longer with a fixed internal memory? We quantify this complexity through sequential response capacity: how many adaptive testing stages, each using a fresh run, can continue to separate possible processes by a prescribed gap in response probabilities. For fixed system and memory sizes, we establish a tight law relating this capacity to run length and probability resolution. At fixed resolution, the capacity grows on the order of $K\log K$, where $K$ is the number of time steps in each run. Our construction attains this growth using time-dependent phase rotations on a single visible qubit with no additional internal memory; its tests give response probabilities exactly zero or one. Under the same tests, classical stochastic processes that measure in a fixed basis at every step have only linear capacity at fixed sizes and resolution. For phase sequences selected by a stored classical label, we then quantify how known independent Pauli noise changes this logarithmic enhancement. With ideal controls and weak residual phase noise after correction, we prove matching capacity bounds at a fixed small probability gap. These bounds identify the inverse residual phase-flip probability as the coherence timescale that limits the extra logarithmic growth.

</details>


### [130] [Universal Bound and Phase Transition in Many-Body Fermionic Non-Gaussianity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02075)
*Masahiro Hoshino,Ryota Matsuda,Yuto Ashida*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Using magic Rényi entropy (MRE) as a non-Gaussianity measure, the authors derive its universal upper bound and Haar mean, show typical Haar-random states reach maximal MRE density ln(4/3) per Majorana, and uncover a first-order transition in non-Gaussianity growth under real/imaginary-time SYK evolution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fermionic non-Gaussianity is a resource for universal quantum computation generated by quantum many-body interactions, but how it grows toward its maximal value and whether it has distinct signatures beyond conventional thermodynamic quantities is unclear.

Method: They use the magic Rényi entropy as a non-Gaussianity measure, derive universal bounds and the Haar mean analytically, and evolve a Gaussian state in imaginary and real time under the Sachdev-Ye-Kitaev Hamiltonian, varying the time durations.

Result: Typical Haar-random states attain the maximal MRE density of ln(4/3) per Majorana in the large-system limit. MRE can differ extensively between states with identical covariance matrices. A first-order transition appears, marked by a kink in MRE density and spontaneous breaking of the permutation symmetry among the four replicas used to evaluate MRE.

Conclusion: The identified transition represents a qualitative change in the state's non-Gaussianity that is not reflected in the thermal free energy, revealing non-Gaussianity as an order parameter with features invisible to standard thermodynamic probes.

Abstract: Fermionic non-Gaussianity is a resource for universal quantum computation that can be generated by interactions in quantum many-body systems. Using the magic Rényi entropy (MRE) as a measure of non-Gaussianity, we derive its universal upper bound and Haar mean, proving that typical Haar-random states attain the maximal MRE density of $\ln(4/3)$ per Majorana in the large-system limit. We also show that the MRE can differ extensively between states with identical covariance matrices. To investigate how non-Gaussianity grows toward this maximal density, we evolve a Gaussian state in imaginary and real time under the Sachdev-Ye-Kitaev Hamiltonian. By varying the imaginary- and real-time durations, we identify a first-order transition marked by a kink in the MRE density and spontaneous breaking of the permutation symmetry among the four copies used to evaluate the MRE. This transition represents a qualitative change in the non-Gaussianity of the state that is not reflected in the thermal free energy.

</details>


### [131] [A computational phase diagram for the transverse field Ising model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02079)
*Thuy-Duong Vuong*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper gives a randomized classical algorithm to approximate the partition function and Gibbs-state observables of the transverse field Ising model in polynomial time when Δ(J)·tanh(βη)/η ≤ 1, and proves exp(o(n))-hardness of approximation beyond this threshold.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding when quantum Gibbs states and partition functions of the transverse field Ising model can be efficiently approximated classically is central to quantum many-body physics and complexity theory. Prior work lacks a sharp characterization of the tractable regime, particularly in terms of the coupling matrix spectral width and transverse field strength, so the authors aim to delineate the boundary between classical tractability and hardness.

Method: For H = ½ Σ J_ij Z_i Z_j + Σ h_i Z_i + η Σ X_i, with Δ(J) the spectral width of the coupling matrix J, the authors design a randomized classical algorithm that approximates the partition function Z(β) = Tr(e^{-βH}) to relative error ε under the condition Δ(J)·tanh(βη)/η ≤ 1. They also extend this approach to approximate Pauli string observables of the Gibbs state within additive error, and to diagonal X-basis observables within relative error. A complexity-theoretic reduction establishes hardness when the condition fails.

Result: When Δ(J)·tanh(βη)/η ≤ 1, Z(β) can be approximated to relative error ε ∈ (0,1) in time polynomial in n, β, model parameters, and ε^{-1}. Gibbs-state Pauli string observables are approximable with arbitrarily small additive error, and X-diagonal observables with arbitrarily small relative error. Conversely, when Δ(J)·tanh(βη)/η > 1, approximating Z(β) within an exp(o(n))-multiplicative factor is NP-hard, implying no efficient classical or quantum algorithm is likely under standard assumptions.

Conclusion: The condition Δ(J)·tanh(βη)/η = 1 acts as a sharp threshold separating efficient classical approximability of the transverse field Ising model's partition function and Gibbs observables from hardness of approximation, providing a complete complexity dichotomy for this model.

Abstract: We study the transverse field Ising model, defined by the Hamiltonian $H =\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i, j\in [n]} J_{ij} Z_i Z_j +\sum_{i=1}^n h_i^z Z_i + η\sum_{i} X_i$ where $J $ is the symmetric interaction matrix, and $η$ is the transverse field strength. Let $Δ(J)=λ_{\max}(J)-λ_{\min}(J)$ be the spectral width of $J.$ When the inverse temperature $β\geq0$ satisfies $Δ(J)\cdot\frac{\tanh(βη)}η\leq1$, we give a randomized classical algorithm that approximates the partition function $Z(β)=\operatorname{Tr}(e^{-βH})$ to a given relative error $ε\in(0,1)$ in time polynomial in $n$, $β$, the model parameters, and $ε^{-1}$. When $ Δ(J) \cdot \frac{\tanh(βη)}η > 1 ,$ we show that approximating $ Z(β)$ within an $\exp(o(n))$-multiplicative factor is $\textbf{NP}$-hard, and thus unlikely to admit an efficient classical or quantum algorithms under standard complexity theoretic assumptions. Furthermore, in the regime $Δ(J)\cdot \frac{\tanh(βη)}η\leq 1,$ we provide an efficient randomized classical algorithm that approximates Pauli string observables of the Gibbs state $ ρ_β= \frac{e^{-βH}}{\operatorname{Tr}(e^{-βH})}$ within an arbitrarily small additive error. In the special case when the observable is also diagonal in the $X$-basis, i.e. $P \in \{I, X\}^{\otimes n}$, the algorithm further achieves arbitrarily small relative error.

</details>


### [132] [Strong Simulation of 1D Quantum Circuits via Reduced Transition Matrices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02082)
*Matilde Grassi,Stefano Carignano,Luca Tagliacozzo,Jacopo De Nardis*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces the Sweeping RTM algorithm to approximately strong-simulate single output probabilities of 1D chaotic brick-wall circuits, finding that the required bond dimension grows only subexponentially over the accessible time window at fixed relative precision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Direct tensor-network evolution of pure states under chaotic unitary circuits is severely limited by the growth of spatial entanglement. The authors target a more restricted and tractable task: approximate strong simulation of a specified output probability at finite relative precision, avoiding full-state evolution.

Method: The Sweeping RTM algorithm, a transverse tensor-network contraction based on reduced transition matrices (RTMs), is used to evaluate p(x|y)=|<x|U(T)|y>|^2 for given input and output bit strings y and x. It jointly compresses the left and right temporal boundary states, seeking a probability that converges across spatial cuts and as bond dimension increases.

Result: For chaotic 1D brick-wall circuits at fixed relative target precision, numerical evidence shows the bond dimension needed for stable estimates grows subexponentially over the accessible time window.

Conclusion: The approach provides a direct route to classical probability queries for chaotic quantum circuits, with potential applications to benchmarking and learning tasks.

Abstract: Tensor networks are powerful tools for simulating quantum many-body systems, but the growth of spatial entanglement severely limits the direct evolution of pure states under chaotic unitary circuits. Here we consider a more targeted task: the approximate strong simulation of a specified output probability of a 1D chaotic brick-wall circuit at finite relative precision. Given input and output bit strings $\by$ and $\bx$, we evaluate $p(\bx|\by)=|\bra{\bx}U(T)\ket{\by}|^2$ using the Sweeping RTM algorithm, a transverse tensor-network contraction based on reduced transition matrices (RTMs). The algorithm compresses the left and right temporal boundary states jointly, seeking an output probability that converges across spatial cuts and as the bond dimension is increased. For chaotic one-dimensional brick-wall circuits at a fixed relative target precision, we find numerical evidence that the bond dimension required to obtain stable estimates grows subexponentially over the accessible time window. Our findings open a direct route to classical probability queries for chaotic quantum circuits, with potential applications to benchmarking and learning tasks.

</details>


### [133] [Stationarity as a One-Mode Constraint on Quantum Correlation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02090)
*Itai Panas*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A regularized Hartree-Fock argument shows that the scalar energy of a stationary state is one-mode sufficient: a single retained pair mode plus a one-mode exclusion axiom reproduces He-like coefficients and energies despite a microscopically complex return spectrum.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stationary quantum states exhibit a single dynamical phase factor and one scalar energy even when the internal response is spread across a large Hilbert space. The authors aim to explain this one-ness of stationary time evolution, not by debating the value or observability of an absolute phase, but by identifying what structural property of the stationary mean field allows energetic compression to a single mode.

Method: In regularized Hartree-Fock (reg-HF), the link to the stationary mean field is set up before any one-mode reduction. The Gaussian HF representation induces a primitive-product relative-coordinate Gaussian measure that fixes a covariance; at this covariance the distribution is simultaneously minimum-Fisher-information and maximum-Shannon-differential-entropy, and saturates the Stam bound. A compatible Gaussian scale resolution of the Coulomb operator and virial completion convert the regularized pair displacement into a stationary-compatible response. Stationarity then motivates a one-mode constraint on the terminal scalar correlation response, formalized by a one-mode exclusion axiom: covariance from response components orthogonal to the retained pair mode is excluded from the terminal scalar energy property, without claiming the full response is one-dimensional. Helium is used as the minimal nontrivial test, with a parameter-free retained Coulomb carrier and a finite-Z insertion.

Result: The parameter-free retained Coulomb carrier $-δ/e$ together with the finite-$Z$ insertion $8δ/9$ closely reproduces independent He-like coefficients and energies. An explicit Bethe-Goldstone calculation yields a broad non-single-denominator return spectrum, yet its scalar energy is exactly moment-compressible.

Conclusion: The results separate microscopic spectral complexity from one-mode energetic sufficiency, supporting the reg-HF hypothesis that stationary scalar energy can be captured by a single retained mode under the one-mode exclusion axiom.

Abstract: A stationary quantum state has one dynamical phase factor and one scalar energy even when its internal response spans a large Hilbert space. Our argument concerns not the value or observability of an absolute phase, but the one-ness of stationary time evolution. In regularized Hartree--Fock (reg-HF), the connection to the stationary mean field is established before any one-mode reduction: the primitive-product relative-coordinate Gaussian measure induced by the Gaussian HF representation fixes a covariance and, at that covariance, is simultaneously the minimum-Fisher-information and maximum-Shannon-differential-entropy distribution and the equality case of the Stam bound. The Coulomb operator admits a compatible Gaussian scale resolution, and virial completion converts the resulting regularized pair displacement into a stationary-compatible response. Stationarity motivates a one-mode constraint on the terminal scalar correlation response. The decisive reg-HF hypothesis is a one-mode exclusion axiom: covariance carried by response components orthogonal to the retained pair mode is excluded from the terminal scalar energy property, without asserting that the unrestricted response is one dimensional. Helium provides the minimal nontrivial test. The parameter-free retained Coulomb carrier $-δ/e$ and finite-$Z$ insertion $8δ/9$ closely reproduce independent He-like coefficients and energies, while an explicit Bethe--Goldstone calculation displays a broad non-single-denominator return spectrum even though its scalar energy is exactly moment-compressible. The result separates microscopic spectral complexity from one-mode energetic sufficiency.

</details>


### [134] [One-Way Quantum Symmetric Private Information Retrieval Protocol From A Single Database Server Using NISQ Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02093)
*Xiang Zou,H. F. Chau*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper shows symmetric private information retrieval (SPIR) is possible with information-theoretic security when the server and user are restricted to NISQ devices that measure qubits on arrival and lack long-term quantum memory, even against an all-powerful eavesdropper.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SPIR with a single server is impossible for parties with unrestricted quantum power. The authors seek a setting where information-theoretic SPIR becomes achievable, exploiting realistic hardware constraints (NISQ devices, no long-term quantum memory, immediate measurement) on the server and user while leaving the eavesdropper all-powerful.

Method: The server sends BB84 states one way; the user performs unambiguous discrimination and thus learns a random subset of bits. After a joint random permutation into blocks, the user announces the conclusive locations of one privately chosen block. The server hashes and takes a random parity of those locations in each block; the permutation makes it exponentially unlikely that one location set fits two blocks. Security is proved via an explicit size rule for the announced set (database privacy), secrecy against the eavesdropper, and user privacy against a server preparing prescribed BB84 states and announcing truthfully.

Result: Secrecy against the eavesdropper, database privacy, and user privacy are proven exactly for single photons and up to a small multi-photon term over a tolerated noise channel. The only added equipment beyond BB84 is a passive linear-optical measurement with one ancillary mode. For a 10^4-bit database and database privacy 10^-6, blocks of about a thousand qubits suffice over a tolerated noise channel. Certifying an arbitrary memoryless user and committing to the permutation require larger auxiliary transmissions, quantified with finite keys. Database privacy extends to decoy-state weak coherent pulses, and simulations show the bound is nearly tight.

Conclusion: Information-theoretic SPIR is achievable under NISQ memory restrictions with a single server, using BB84 states plus minimal linear-optics hardware. The scheme provides proven security and practical block sizes, extends to decoy-state weak coherent pulses, and its privacy bound is nearly tight per simulations.

Abstract: Symmetric private information retrieval (SPIR) lets an inquirer obtain one specific bit only, without leaking any information about its index to the database owner. With a single server, this is impossible for parties with unrestricted quantum power. We show that it becomes possible, with information-theoretic security, when the server and the user hold only noisy intermediate-scale quantum (NISQ) devices without long-term quantum memory and measure each qubit on arrival, while the eavesdropper remains all-powerful; this is a stricter form of the memory restriction of the bounded- and noisy-quantum-storage models. The server sends BB84 states one way; the user discriminates them unambiguously and so knows a random subset of the bits. After a joint random permutation into blocks, the user announces the conclusive locations of one privately chosen block, and the server hashes and takes a random parity of these locations in every block; the permutation makes it exponentially unlikely that one set of locations fits two blocks. We prove secrecy against the eavesdropper, database privacy through an explicit size rule for the announced set, and user privacy against a server who prepares the prescribed BB84 states and announces truthfully, exactly for single photons and up to a small multi-photon term over a tolerated noise channel. The only addition to BB84 equipment is a passive linear-optical measurement with one ancillary mode. For a $10^{4}$-bit database and database privacy $10^{-6}$, blocks of about a thousand qubits suffice over a tolerated noise channel for a user who applies the prescribed measurement; certifying an arbitrary memoryless user and committing to the permutation need larger auxiliary transmissions, which we quantify with finite keys. Database privacy extends to decoy-state weak coherent pulses, and simulations show that the bound is nearly tight.

</details>


### [135] [Quantum state preparation for weighted d-DNNF](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02094)
*Steef Hegeman,Joon Hyung Lee,Alfons Laarman*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: For quantum states described by weighted d-DNNF circuits, a quantum circuit preparing the state can be constructed in linear time (up to complex arithmetic).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The quantum state preparation problem asks: given a description of a quantum state, can one efficiently generate a quantum circuit that computes (prepares) that state? Efficient state preparation is foundational for quantum algorithms and quantum simulation, motivating the search for state representations that admit fast, systematic circuit synthesis.

Method: The authors exploit weighted d-DNNF (deterministic, decomposable pseudo-Boolean circuits) as the classical description language for quantum states. They construct a synthesis procedure that converts these circuits into a corresponding quantum circuit, achieving linear time complexity up to complex arithmetic.

Result: States described by weighted d-DNNF admit a quantum circuit computing them that can be obtained in linear time, up to the cost of complex arithmetic operations.

Conclusion: Weighted d-DNNF provides a tractable representation for quantum state preparation: the associated quantum circuit can be built efficiently in linear time, extending efficient circuit synthesis to this structured circuit class.

Abstract: The quantum state preparation problem is to, given a description of a quantum state, efficiently generate a quantum circuit computing the state. We show that for quantum states described by weighted d-DNNF (deterministic, decomposable pseudo-Boolean circuits) a quantum circuit computing the state can be obtained in linear time up to complex arithmetic.

</details>


### [136] [A No-Go Theorem for Order-Two Clifford Electric-Magnetic Duality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02097)
*Shunta Takahashi,Zhi Li,Beni Yoshida*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: For the Z2 toric code, any locality-preserving Clifford realization of electric-magnetic exchange cannot have order two; for ZN toric codes, an order-two Clifford realization exists for odd N but is impossible for even N.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Electromagnetic duality acts as an order-two operation on anyon types and as a Hadamard-type logical transformation on encoded quantum information. The paper asks whether this order-two duality can be realized microscopically as an order-two Clifford operation, i.e., whether the emergent duality's group law lifts faithfully to its microscopic realization.

Method: A general proof for the Z2 toric code showing that any locality-preserving Clifford realization of electric-magnetic exchange cannot have order two, without assuming translation symmetry, restricted system sizes, or a fixed vertex-plaquette stabilizer pairing. The proof locally emulates the unavoidable crossing between electric and magnetic strings, analogous to a cross-cap in a non-orientable manifold. The analysis is extended to the ZN toric code.

Result: For the Z2 toric code, no locality-preserving order-two Clifford realization of electric-magnetic exchange exists. For the ZN toric code, an order-two Clifford realization exists for odd N, while it is impossible for even N.

Conclusion: The group law of an emergent electromagnetic duality need not lift faithfully to its microscopic Clifford realization.

Abstract: Electromagnetic duality in the toric code has order two at the level of anyon types and acts as a Hadamard-type logical transformation on the encoded quantum information. Here, we ask whether it can likewise be realized microscopically as an order-two Clifford operation. For the $\mathbb{Z}_2$ toric code, we prove that any locality-preserving Clifford realization of electric-magnetic exchange cannot have order two. Our proof is fully general and requires neither translation symmetry with restricted families of system sizes nor a fixed pairing between vertex and plaquette stabilizers. Geometrically, the proof locally emulates the unavoidable crossing between electric and magnetic strings, similar to a cross-cap in a non-orientable manifold. Extending the problem to the $\mathbb{Z}_N$ toric code, we find that an order-two Clifford realization exists for odd $N$, whereas it is impossible for even $N$. Our results show that the group law of an emergent electromagnetic duality need not lift faithfully to its microscopic Clifford realization.

</details>


### [137] [An exponential separation between entanglement-assisted and unassisted one-way quantum communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02099)
*Ryan Anselm,Srijita Kundu,Olivier Lalonde,Ashwin Nayak*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper resolves a longstanding open question in quantum communication complexity by exhibiting an exponential separation in the one-way setting for a total Boolean function: it can be computed with O(log n) bits of classical communication with prior entanglement, but requires Ω(n^{1/3}) qubits of quantum communication without entanglement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A central question in quantum communication complexity is whether entanglement can provide a significant advantage for communication tasks. Such separations were previously known only for relational problems and, in the simultaneous message passing model, for partial functions; it remained unresolved whether any separation exists for a total Boolean function.

Method: The authors construct a family of total Boolean functions f_n: {0,1}^n × {0,1}^n → {0,1} based on a special case of the subgroup membership problem, first studied in the communication setting by Aaronson, Le Gall, Russell, and Tani, and analyze their communication complexity with and without entanglement.

Result: The constructed functions can be computed with O(log n) bits of one-way classical communication given prior entanglement, but require Ω(n^{1/3}) qubits of one-way quantum communication without entanglement, yielding an exponential separation for a total Boolean function.

Conclusion: This work provides the first exponential separation for a total Boolean function in the one-way communication model, resolving the longstanding open question of whether entanglement can dramatically reduce communication for total Boolean functions.

Abstract: A longstanding question in quantum communication complexity is whether some task can be accomplished with a small amount of communication in the presence of entanglement, yet require much more quantum communication in the absence of entanglement. Separations of this nature were previously known for relational problems and, in the simultaneous message passing model, for partial functions. But it has remained unresolved whether any such separation exists for a total Boolean function. We resolve this question with an exponential separation in the one-way setting: we exhibit a family of total Boolean functions $f_n\colon \{0,1\}^n \times \{0,1\}^n \to \{0,1\}$ that can be computed with $O(\log n)$ bits of one-way classical communication given prior entanglement, but that require $Ω(n^{1/3})$ qubits of one-way quantum communication without entanglement. Our function is a special case of the subgroup membership problem, first studied in the communication setting by Aaronson, Le Gall, Russell, and Tani.

</details>


### [138] [On the pseudorandomness of simple quantum processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02100)
*Jesko Dujmovic,Jonas Haferkamp,Alexander Poremba*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper studies the quantum analog of Gowers' conjecture on local random reversible operations, showing that unitary designs do not generically imply pseudorandomness and refuting the unitary analog of the Hoory–Magen–Myers–Rackoff conjecture.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Gowers conjectured that repeatedly composing local random reversible operations yields permutations indistinguishable from random. The authors aim to make progress on this longstanding conjecture by studying its unitary quantum analog.

Method: They construct efficiently samplable families of distributions on one- and two-qubit gates that, after T=O_t(n^2 log^2 n) independent steps, yield an approximate unitary t-design with negligible error, while an efficient quantum algorithm distinguishes it from random using only O_t(log^2 n) queries. They also build a stronger separation between unitary designs and pseudorandom unitaries at polynomially bounded moments using highly structured ensembles.

Result: Statistical moment matching via unitary designs does not generically lead to pseudorandomness, even for simple quantum processes. This refutes the unitary analog of the Hoory–Magen–Myers–Rackoff conjecture. A stronger separation at polynomially bounded moments is achieved, though requiring highly structured ensembles.

Conclusion: Caution is advised when using unitary designs to model information scrambling in black-hole physics, since even maximally scrambled systems can exhibit structure accessible to efficient experiments. New conjectures are proposed for how pseudorandomness can plausibly emerge within simple quantum processes such as random quantum circuits.

Abstract: Can simple processes appear highly complex? Gowers (Comb. Prob. Comp. '96) conjectured that repeatedly composing local random reversible operations can yield global permutations that are indistinguishable from random. In this work, we study the unitary quantum analog of this question, in an attempt to make new progress on this longstanding conjecture.
  Our first result shows that statistical moment matching in the form of unitary designs does not generically lead to pseudorandomness---even for the simplest quantum processes: for every fixed $t$, we give an efficiently samplable family $\{ν_n\}_n$ of distributions on one- and two-qubit gates such that, after $T=O_t(n^2\log^2 n)$ independent steps, the resulting $n$-qubit ensemble is an approximate unitary $t$-design with negligible error $\exp(-Ω(\log^2 n))$, yet an efficient quantum algorithm distinguishes it from random using only $O_t(\log^2 n)$ queries. This refutes the unitary analog of the Hoory--Magen--Myers--Rackoff conjecture (ICALP '04) for permutations.
  Our second result is a stronger separation between unitary designs and pseudorandom unitaries at polynomially bounded moments; our counterexample, however, requires highly structured ensembles, in contrast with the simple local walks from before. This suggests caution when using unitary designs to model information scrambling in black-hole physics, as even maximally scrambled systems can exhibit structure which is accessible to efficient experiments. Motivated by these findings, we then propose new conjectures for how pseudorandomness can plausibly emerge within simple quantum processes, such as random quantum circuits.

</details>


### [139] [Time-space lower bounds for breaking quantum cryptography](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02101)
*Fangqi Dong,Alex Lombardi*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proves near-optimal time-space lower bounds for breaking quantum cryptography in the random oracle model, showing that $n$ qubits of communication (binary phase states) provide security against preprocessing attacks with space up to $N^2$, demonstrating a new advantage of quantum over classical cryptography.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Post-quantum one-way functions have best known bounds of $O((T^2 + ST)/N)$ with a trivial attack at $S=N$, limiting security against preprocessing attacks. The paper aims to show quantum cryptography can do better, motivating tighter time-space tradeoff bounds and a simpler methodology.

Method: Express the optimal preprocessing attack as the operator norm of a random matrix, then bound this in expectation over the random oracle via the trace-moment method. Interpret trace moments using compressed oracles (Zhandry, Crypto 2019) and analyze them. This simplifies and generalizes Liu's (Eurocrypt 2023) approach.

Result: A $T$-query adversary with $S$ qubits of non-uniform advice recovers a random key from the $n$-qubit binary phase state with probability at most $O((T^2 + \sqrt{ST})/N)$. Additional results: (1) tightened QROM post-quantum PRG bound $O(T^2/N + \sqrt{ST/N})$; (2) extended LMW24 unitary synthesis lower bound to adversaries with one arbitrary function query plus polynomially many adaptive random oracle queries; (3) tight $O(\sqrt{S}/N)$ bound for pseudorandomness of random binary phase states against space $S$ distinguishers.

Conclusion: Quantum cryptography (binary phase states) achieves security against preprocessing attacks with space up to $N^2$ using only $n$ qubits of communication, versus $N$ for classical cryptography. The trace-moment/compressed-oracle methodology yields near-optimal bounds and generalizes prior work.

Abstract: We prove near-optimal time-space lower bounds for breaking quantum cryptography in the random oracle model. Specifically, we show that a $T$-query adversary with $S$ qubits of non-uniform advice can recover a random key $k$ from the $n$-qubit binary phase state $|ψ_k\rangle \propto \sum_{x} R(k,x) |x\rangle$ with probability at most $O(\frac{T^2 + \sqrt{ST}}{N})$ for $N=2^n$. In contrast, the best known bound for post-quantum one-way functions is $O(\frac{T^2 + ST}{N})$, with a trivial attack at $S = N$. This demonstrates a new advantage of quantum cryptography over classical cryptography: $n$ qubits of communication suffice for security against preprocessing attacks with space up to $N^2$ rather than $N$. Our methodology is simple: express the optimal preprocessing attack as the operator norm of a random matrix, and bound this value in expectation over the random oracle via the trace-moment method. These trace moments have a natural interpretation using compressed oracles [Zhandry, Crypto 2019], which we then analyze. This can be viewed as a simplification and generalization of the approach of Liu [Eurocrypt 2023] for proving time-space tradeoffs for breaking post-quantum cryptography. We also prove the following results: (1) We tighten Liu's analysis of post-quantum PRGs in QROM, achieving a distinguishing advantage bound of $O(\frac{T^2}N + \sqrt{\frac{ST}N})$. (2) For unitary synthesis, we extend the one-query lower bound of Lombardi-Ma-Wright [STOC 2024] to hold against adversaries that can make one arbitrary function query along with polynomially many (adaptive) queries to the random oracle, either before or after the function query. This also interprets the original LMW24 result in terms of compressed oracles. (3) Finally, we prove a tight $O(\frac{\sqrt{S}}N)$ bound for the pseudorandomness of random binary phase states against space $S$ distinguishers.

</details>


### [140] [Unitary Schur Sampling of Qudits via Random SWAP Tests: Hunt for Antisymmetry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02103)
*Shrigyan Brahmachari,David Jakab,Henry D. Pfister,Iman Marvian*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Unitary Schur sampling of arbitrary permutation-invariant mixed states on n qudits can be done using only pairwise SWAP tests, with O(n·min{n,d}³·log²(n/ε)) tests achieving ε diamond-distance error, via a "search for antisymmetry" that traverses a Young diagram.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Circuit-based Schur sampling conventionally requires coherent representation-theoretic primitives (Clebsch-Gordan transforms, generalized phase estimation, or symmetric-group quantum Fourier transforms), which are complex to implement. The paper seeks a simpler, measurement-based protocol that avoids these coherent operations while retaining full unitary Schur sampling functionality.
