Method: The protocol repeatedly identifies and extracts the largest antisymmetric subsystem using only two-qudit projective measurements onto the symmetric and antisymmetric subspaces (pairwise SWAP tests). This reframes unitary Schur sampling as a search for antisymmetry, interpretable as a stochastic traversal of a Young diagram. It preserves the SU(d)-irrep state while preparing a canonical pure state in the multiplicity subsystem. The authors then apply this to quantum purity amplification (QPA) by combining multiple noisy copies of a pure state.

Result: The protocol achieves error ε in diamond distance using O(n·min{n,d}³·log²(n/ε)) random SWAP tests. For QPA, the optimal purified state is obtained by retaining a single designated output qudit and discarding the rest.

Conclusion: Unitary Schur sampling can be implemented purely with pairwise SWAP tests, eliminating the need for coherent representation-theoretic operations, and this antisymmetry-search formulation yields an efficient protocol with a clean application to quantum purity amplification.

Abstract: Schur sampling is a fundamental primitive for extracting permutation-invariant information from many-body quantum systems and has broad applications in quantum information science. Circuit-based implementations typically rely on coherent representation-theoretic operations such as Clebsch-Gordan transforms, generalized phase estimation, or quantum Fourier transforms over the symmetric group. We show that unitary Schur sampling of arbitrary permutation-invariant mixed states on $n$ $d$-dimensional qudits can instead be implemented using only pairwise SWAP tests, namely, two-qudit projective measurements onto the symmetric and antisymmetric subspaces. Our protocol achieves error $ε$ in diamond distance using $O(n\min\{n,d\}^{3}\log^2(n/ε))$ random SWAP tests. The central idea is to repeatedly identify and extract the largest antisymmetric subsystem. This turns unitary Schur sampling into a search for antisymmetry and gives the algorithm a natural interpretation as a stochastic traversal of a Young diagram. The protocol preserves the $\mathrm{SU}(d)$-irrep state while preparing a canonical pure state in the multiplicity subsystem. As an application, we consider quantum purity amplification (QPA), in which multiple noisy copies of a pure quantum state are combined to produce a state of higher purity. We show that the optimal purified state can be obtained by retaining a single designated output qudit and discarding the rest.

</details>


### [141] [Quantum Advantage for Two-Party Differential Privacy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02113)
*Daniel Alabi,Emil T. Khabiboulline*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces information-theoretically private quantum protocols for two-party Hamming distance where both parties must output the same estimate. In Klauck's honest, nonpreemptive, message-preserving model, the authors give an O(n)-communication quantum protocol with pure ε quantum differential privacy (QDP) achieving expected error at most 2/sinh ε + γ for every γ>0, and show that quantum communication achieves O(1) information-theoretic error, matching classical accuracy only available under computational assumptions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical information-theoretic protocols for two-party Hamming distance require Ω(√n) error under pure differential privacy and Ω(√n/log n) error under strong approximate differential privacy, whereas computational security permits O(1) error. The paper aims to close this gap information-theoretically by exploiting quantum communication.

Method: The main construction uses a guarded coherent round trip and an equal-Gram rigidity principle that prevents an honest player from retaining input-dependent complementary information. The authors also separate this model from weaker prescribed-channel privacy (which admits an exact classical realization) and from fully retention-robust security (against which measurement-and-abort attacks remain possible).

Result: An O(n)-communication quantum protocol achieves pure ε QDP expected error at most 2/sinh ε + γ for every γ>0. For approximate (ε, δ) QDP, an exact hockey-stick divergence calculation yields strictly smaller error while preserving the O(1)-versus-Ω(√n/log n) separation for δ=o(1/n).

Conclusion: Quantum communication achieves O(1) information-theoretic error for two-party Hamming distance, matching the accuracy classically available only under computational assumptions. Preservation of non-orthogonal quantum messages is identified as a resource for privacy.

Abstract: We introduce information-theoretically private quantum protocols for two-party Hamming distance when both parties must output the same estimate. Classically, for input length $n$, information-theoretic protocols require $Ω(\sqrt{n})$ error under pure differential privacy and $Ω(\sqrt{n}/\log n)$ error under strong approximate differential privacy, whereas computational security permits $O(1)$ error. In Klauck's honest, nonpreemptive, message-preserving model, we give an $O(n)$-communication quantum protocol with pure $\varepsilon$ quantum differential privacy (QDP) and expected error at most $\frac{2}{\sinh \varepsilon}+γ$, for every $γ>0$. For approximate $(\varepsilon, δ)$ QDP, an exact hockey-stick divergence calculation yields strictly smaller error, while preserving the $O(1)$-versus-$Ω(\sqrt{n}/\log n)$ separation for $δ=o(1/n)$. Thus, quantum communication achieves $O(1)$ information-theoretic error, matching the accuracy available classically only under computational assumptions.
  The main construction uses a guarded coherent round trip and an equal-Gram rigidity principle that prevents an honest player from retaining input-dependent complementary information. We separate this model from weaker prescribed-channel privacy, which already admits an exact classical realization, and from fully retention-robust security, against which measurement-and-abort attacks remain possible. Therefore, we identify preservation of non-orthogonal quantum messages as a resource for privacy.

</details>


### [142] [Local random quantum circuits converge to the Porter-Thomas distribution in polynomial depth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02125)
*Aniruddha Sen,Nicholas Hunter-Jones*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Polynomial-depth brickwork random circuits converge inverse-polynomially in total variation distance to the Porter-Thomas distribution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Porter-Thomas statistics characterize random quantum states and chaotic many-body systems, and are central to random circuit sampling and quantum advantage demonstrations. Convergence to Porter-Thomas in low-depth random circuits is assumed but lacks a rigorous proof.

Method: The authors analyze the output distribution over a fixed bitstring of a local random quantum circuit built from nearest-neighbor Haar random gates. The proof relies on moment bounds from approximate designs, analytic estimates for characteristic functions, and a local anticoncentration property for inverse moments.

Result: For any m ≥ 0, the distribution corresponding to circuits of depth O(n^{2m+1} log(n)) is at most O(1/n^m) far in total variation distance from the Porter-Thomas distribution.

Conclusion: The output distribution of polynomial-depth brickwork random circuits converges inverse-polynomially in total variation distance to the Porter-Thomas distribution, providing a rigorous convergence result supporting random circuit sampling.

Abstract: Porter-Thomas statistics are a characteristic feature of the output distribution of random quantum states and, more broadly, chaotic quantum many-body systems. Convergence to Porter-Thomas plays a central role in random circuit sampling and experimental demonstrations of quantum advantage, where the output statistics of low-depth random quantum circuits are expected to be approximately Porter-Thomas, despite the absence of a rigorous proof of convergence. We show that the output distribution of polynomial-depth brickwork random circuits converges inverse-polynomially in total variation distance to the Porter-Thomas distribution. Specifically, consider the output probability distribution over a fixed bitstring of a local random quantum circuit, constructed from nearest-neighbor Haar random gates. Then, for any $m \geq 0$, the distribution corresponding to circuits of depth $O(n^{2m+1}\log(n))$ is at most $O(1/n^m)$ far in total variation distance from the Porter-Thomas distribution. Our proof uses moment bounds from approximate designs, analytic estimates for characteristic functions, and a local anticoncentration property for inverse moments.

</details>


### [143] [Spiking neural networks for streaming qubit readout](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02129)
*Barry M. Dillon,Aqib Javed,Jim Harkin,Patryk Dabkowski,Benjamin Lienhard*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Spiking neural network discriminators enable fast, accurate, streaming superconducting qubit-state readout, outperforming matched filtering and nearing full-trace ANN accuracy while being FPGA-deployable in real time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fast and accurate qubit-state assignment is essential for feedback, calibration, and error correction in quantum processors. In superconducting platforms, frequency-multiplexed readout makes this task intrinsically multivariate, as measured traces encode crosstalk, qubit-state relaxation events, and transient nonidealities not fully captured by conventional matched filtering.

Method: The authors introduce spiking neural network (SNN) discriminators for superconducting qubit readout. The networks process the measurement window in successive time chunks, exploiting temporal structure and updating classification scores as data arrive rather than waiting until the end of the readout window. They use quantisation-aware training and hls4ml synthesis for FPGA implementation.

Result: The spiking networks outperform matched-filter discrimination and approach the accuracy of a full-trace artificial neural network. Each FPGA inference update can be completed before the next readout chunk arrives.

Conclusion: SNNs provide a streaming, time-resolved estimate of the qubit state that evolves as the readout signal is acquired, establishing them as a promising route to low-latency, real-time qubit readout on FPGA hardware, with broader implications for time-critical quantum-control and scientific-inference applications.

Abstract: Fast and accurate qubit-state assignment is essential for feedback, calibration, and error correction in quantum processors. In superconducting platforms, frequency-multiplexed readout makes this task intrinsically multivariate as measured traces can encode crosstalk, qubit-state relaxation events, and other transient nonidealities that are not fully captured by conventional matched filtering. Here, we introduce spiking neural network (SNN) discriminators for superconducting qubit readout. By processing the measurement window in successive time chunks, the networks exploit temporal structure and update classification scores as data arrive, rather than waiting until the end of the readout window. The spiking networks outperform matched-filter discrimination and approach the accuracy of a full-trace artificial neural network. Beyond reaching the performance of artificial neural networks, the key advantage of SNNs is that they provide a streaming, time-resolved estimate of the qubit state that evolves as the readout signal is acquired. Using quantisation-aware training and hls4ml synthesis, we further demonstrate that each FPGA inference update can be completed before the next readout chunk arrives. These results establish spiking neural networks as a promising route to low-latency, real-time qubit readout on FPGA hardware, with broader implications for time-critical quantum-control and scientific-inference applications.

</details>


### [144] [Quantum impurity models: easy at equilibrium, universal in motion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02130)
*Srinivasan Arunachalam,Sergey Bravyi,Anirban Chowdhury,Arkopal Dutt,Alexandru Gheorghiu,Zhi Li*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Equilibrium properties of constant-size fermionic quantum impurity models are classically efficient to approximate, while their time evolution is BQP-complete, revealing a sharp computational dichotomy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum impurity models describe a small interacting subsystem coupled to a large free-fermion bath and are central to many-body and condensed-matter physics. Understanding whether their ground energy, thermal equilibrium, and dynamics can be efficiently computed classically versus quantumly is of fundamental complexity-theoretic interest, especially given that the best prior classical algorithm for ground energy ran only in quasi-polynomial time.

Method: The authors develop classical algorithms exploiting the exponential suppression of multi-particle bath excitations, using a basis organized by energy scale and Krylov depth. They also construct a universality reduction in which a stationary quantum processor is programmed by an incoming stream of freely propagating fermions, showing time evolution is BQP-complete for time-independent Hamiltonians with fixed constant impurity size.

Result: (1) Ground energy of an n-mode impurity model with constant-size impurity is approximable to additive error ε in poly(n,1/ε) time, improving on the previous quasi-polynomial algorithm. (2) At inverse temperature β, the Helmholtz free energy and a classical description of the thermofield double state are computable to precision ε in poly(n,β,1/ε) time. (3) Simulating time evolution e^{-iHt} is BQP-complete, even for time-independent H with fixed constant impurity size.

Conclusion: Quantum impurity models exhibit a sharp equilibrium-versus-dynamics dichotomy: equilibrium and thermal properties are classically tractable, whereas dynamics encode universal quantum computation. This establishes a clean complexity separation and a fermionic programming model for quantum processors.

Abstract: A quantum impurity model describes a small interacting subsystem embedded into a large bath of free fermions. Here we study the computational complexity of calculating the ground energy, thermal equilibrium, and dynamical properties of these models. Our work reveals a sharp contrast: equilibrium properties can be efficiently approximated by classical means, whereas time evolution can implement a universal quantum computation. More precisely, let $H$ be the Hamiltonian of an impurity model with $n$ fermionic modes and a constant-size impurity. We show that: (1) the ground energy of $H$ can be approximated to additive error $\varepsilon$ by a classical algorithm with runtime $\textsf{poly}(n,1/\varepsilon)$, improving on the quasi-polynomial runtime of the best previously known algorithm; (2) at inverse temperature $β$, the Helmholtz free energy and a classical description of the thermofield double state can be computed to precision $\varepsilon$ in time $\textsf{poly}(n,β,1/\varepsilon)$; (3) simulating the time evolution $e^{-iHt}$ is $\textsf{BQP}$-complete, for $H$ that is time-independent and has a fixed, constant impurity size. Our algorithms exploit exponential suppression of multi-particle bath excitations in a basis organized by energy scale and Krylov depth. Our universality construction realizes a stationary quantum processor whose program arrives in a stream of freely propagating fermions.

</details>


### [145] [Optimal transducers using symmetries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02133)
*Benoît Dubus,Julien Ladeuze,Jérémie Roland*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper uses the symmetry group of state-conversion problems to simplify the construction of optimal transducers, providing covariant catalysts and block-diagonal unitaries, and derives optimal transducers for quantum algorithmic primitives like search, amplitude amplification, and amplitude estimation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transducers offer a powerful quantum computing framework where optimal transduction complexity equals the adversary bound and they compose exactly without error-control overhead. However, constructing efficient or optimal transducers remains hard because it requires solving the adversary SDP and explicitly constructing the corresponding unitary for an algorithm.

Method: Leveraging the symmetry group of state-conversion problems: (1) a symmetrization argument proving an optimal catalyst can always be chosen covariant under a representation of the symmetry group; (2) proving the transducer intertwines two different group representations, so it can be chosen block diagonal in the isotypic decomposition of the Hilbert space.

Result: Optimal transducers with optimal constants are derived for widely used quantum algorithmic primitives, including unstructured search, amplitude amplification, and amplitude estimation.

Conclusion: The approach extends prior work using representation theory for adversary lower bounds to the systematic construction of optimal quantum algorithms, simplifying both solving the adversary SDP and constructing the explicit unitary.

Abstract: Transducers (Belovs, Jeffery and Yolcu, 2024) are a quantum computing framework describing a quantum algorithm as a unitary converting an input state into a target state using a catalyst, an auxiliary vector that is left unchanged. They are a powerful tool in quantum algorithm design, especially in the context of quantum query complexity: feasible points of the (dual) adversary semidefinite program directly translate into transducers and the optimal transduction complexity is equal to the adversary bound, i.e. the Las Vegas complexity, which is known to characterize bounded-error quantum query complexity. Moreover, contrary to bounded-error algorithms, transducers compose exactly, which limits overheads due to controlling errors in algorithms constructed by composition. Constructing efficient, let alone optimal, transducers in terms of quantum query complexity nevertheless remains a hard task since it still requires solving the adversary SDP and constructing the unitary to obtain an explicit algorithm. In this paper, we show how using the symmetry group of state-conversion problems simplifies both steps. First, using a symmetrization argument, we prove an optimal catalyst can always be chosen covariant under a representation of the symmetry group. Second, we prove that the transducer intertwines two different representations of the group and can thus be chosen block diagonal in the isotypic decomposition of the Hilbert space. Using those methods, we then derive optimal transducers, with optimal constants, for different widely used quantum algorithmic primitives, such as unstructured search, amplitude amplification and amplitude estimation. Our approach extends previous work on the use of representation theory to compute adversary lower bounds (Høyer, Lee, and {\v S}palek, 2007; Ambainis, Magnin, Roetteler and Roland, 2011) to the systematic construction of optimal algorithms.

</details>


### [146] [A Generalized quantum Stein lemma on von Neumann algebras](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02134)
*Li Gao*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The authors prove a generalized quantum Stein lemma for i.i.d. normal states against convex, tensor-stable families on arbitrary von Neumann algebras, showing the worst-case type-II error exponent equals the regularized relative entropy with a strong converse, at every type-I error tolerance in (0,1).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum Stein's lemma characterizes optimal asymptotic hypothesis-testing rates between states, but prior results are largely restricted to finite-dimensional or specific operator-algebraic settings. Extending it to arbitrary von Neumann algebras, against general convex tensor-stable alternatives and allowing regularized relative entropy, addresses the need for a robust operational characterization in infinite-dimensional and general algebraic settings.

Method: The proof combines three ingredients: (1) the integral representation of relative entropy via hockey-stick divergences, (2) a modular testing bound, and (3) a convex minimax argument. The setting is i.i.d. normal states tested against convex, tensor-stable families on arbitrary von Neumann algebras, assuming existence of an alternative with finite relative entropy from the null state.

Result: Assuming an alternative state with finite relative entropy from the null state, for every type-I error tolerance ε ∈ (0,1), the worst-case type-II error exponent is achieved by the regularized relative entropy, and this holds with a strong converse.

Conclusion: The quantum Stein lemma generalizes to i.i.d. normal states against convex, tensor-stable families on arbitrary von Neumann algebras, establishing the regularized relative entropy as the exact asymptotic worst-case type-II error exponent with a strong converse at all ε ∈ (0,1).

Abstract: We prove a generalized quantum Stein lemma for i.i.d. normal states against convex, tensor-stable families on arbitrary von Neumann algebras. Assuming the existence of an alternative state with finite relative entropy from the null state, we show that, at every type-I error tolerance $\varepsilon\in (0,1)$, the worst case type-II error exponent is achieved with the regularized relative entropy with a strong converse. The proof combines the integral representation of relative entropy by hockey-stick divergences, a modular testing bound, and convex minimax argument.

</details>


### [147] [Random Quantum Circuits Beyond Moment Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02135)
*Shih-Han Hung*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work proves optimal bounds on how accurately strong approximate unitary k-designs reproduce the individual output probability distributions of Haar-random circuits, showing moment matching alone gives an O(sqrt(2^n/(k(k+2^n)))+eps) Kolmogorov-distance error to the Porter-Thomas law, and that local invariance yields exponential improvement with total-variation guarantees, with applications to Shannon entropy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Random quantum circuits are used to efficiently mimic ideal random quantum evolution, typically via approximate unitary designs that match Haar moments up to order k. However, it was unclear how well such moment matching reproduces the full distributions of individual output probabilities, not just low moments, and whether a better uniform bound is possible. The paper aims to quantify this distributional accuracy and identify the additional structure needed to improve it.

Method: The authors analyze strong epsilon-approximate unitary k-designs on n qubits and bound the Kolmogorov distance between the distribution of each individual output probability and the finite-dimensional Porter-Thomas distribution (a beta distribution with parameters 1 and 2^n-1). They prove the general bound is O(sqrt(2^n/(k(k+2^n)))+epsilon) and show it is optimal up to constant factors. They then consider designs with local invariance under unitary translations on log k + O(1) qubits and derive exponential-in-k improvements in Kolmogorov distance and total variation distance. They also translate marginal-distribution accuracy into a bound on expected Shannon entropy.

Result: For every strong epsilon-approximate unitary k-design on n qubits, the distribution of each individual output probability lies within O(sqrt(2^n/(k(k+2^n)))+epsilon) in Kolmogorov distance of the Porter-Thomas distribution; this bound is optimal up to constants, so a substantially better uniform bound requires extra structure. If the design is invariant under unitary translations on log k + O(1) qubits, the error improves to 2^{-Omega(k)}+O(epsilon) in Kolmogorov distance and 2^{-Omega(k)}+O(epsilon log(2/epsilon)) in total variation distance. As an application, if each output probability's marginal is within Kolmogorov distance eta of its Haar counterpart, the expected Shannon entropy deviates from the Haar value by O(sqrt(eta)).

Conclusion: Moment matching alone guarantees only a limited, optimal distributional accuracy for individual output probabilities, while local invariance provides an exponential improvement and stronger total-variation guarantees. These results characterize both what approximate unitary designs guarantee distributionally and what extra structure improves it, and they imply controlled deviation of expected Shannon entropy from the Haar value.

Abstract: Random quantum circuits aim to efficiently reproduce the statistical properties of ideal random quantum evolution. One approach is to construct approximate unitary designs, which match the moments of Haar-random unitaries up to a prescribed order with controlled error. In this work, we establish quantitative guarantees for how accurately these designs reproduce the full distributions of individual output probabilities. We show that, for every strong $\varepsilon$-approximate unitary $k$-design on $n$ qubits, the distribution of each individual output probability is within $O\left(\sqrt{\frac{2^n}{k(k+2^n)}}+\varepsilon\right)$ in Kolmogorov distance of the finite-dimensional Porter-Thomas distribution, a beta distribution with parameters $1$ and $2^n-1$. This bound is optimal up to constant factors, implying that a substantially better uniform bound requires additional structure. We further show that local invariance yields an exponential improvement in the dependence on $k$, with guarantees also in the stronger metric of total variation: every strong $\varepsilon$-approximate unitary $k$-design invariant under unitary translations acting on $\log k+O(1)$ qubits achieves error $2^{-Ω(k)}+O(\varepsilon)$ in Kolmogorov distance and $2^{-Ω(k)}+O(\varepsilon\log(2/\varepsilon))$ in total variation distance. These results identify both the distributional accuracy guaranteed by moment matching and the additional structure that substantially improves it. As an application, we show that if the marginal distribution of each output probability of a strong approximate design is within Kolmogorov distance $η$ of its Haar counterpart, then the expected Shannon entropy of the output distribution differs from its Haar value by $O(\sqrtη)$.

</details>


### [148] [One-Shot any Code](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02137)
*Andrew C. Yuan*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Any single-shot code is self-correcting, and any [[n,k]] CSS QLDPC code can be transformed into an [[nm,k]] CSS QLDPC code with single-shot quantum error correction and an O(log m) parallel-time local decoder, achieving threshold p_RG and error suppression Ω(m^α), with enhancement when the input code has good parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Single-shot quantum error correction (SS-QEC) is desirable because it tolerates measurement errors without repeated syndrome extraction, but constructing CSS QLDPC codes with single-shot decoders, good thresholds, and efficient local decoding remains challenging. The paper aims to provide a general transformation that upgrades arbitrary CSS QLDPC codes to single-shot correctable codes with strong error suppression and fast local decoding.

Method: The authors first prove that any single-shot code is self-correcting via a simple argument. They then construct a transformation mapping any [[n,k]] CSS QLDPC code into an [[nm,k]] CSS QLDPC code by a suitable product/expansion (parameter m), equipping it with a single-shot decoder that is local and runs in O(log m) parallel time. They analyze performance under joint local stochastic physical and measurement noise below a constant threshold p_RG, and study how the error suppression composes with that of the input code's single-shot decoder.

Result: For the transformed [[nm,k]] CSS QLDPC code, the logical failure probability over T correction rounds is O(T n) exp[-Ω(m^α)] for a constant α>0, below a constant threshold p_RG under joint local stochastic physical and measurement noise, with threshold p_RG and error suppression Ω(m^α). If the input code has a single-shot decoder with threshold p_c>0 and suppression Ω(n^β), the output code enhances suppression to Ω(m^α n^β) while retaining the decoder-independent threshold p_RG, making it enhanced when p_c < p_RG.

Conclusion: The work establishes a general method to promote any CSS QLDPC code to a single-shot quantum error-correcting code with an efficient local parallel decoder, constant threshold, and provable error suppression. It also shows error-suppression enhancement and decoder-independent thresholds, providing a flexible framework for constructing high-performance single-shot QLDPC codes.

Abstract: We first provide a simple proof showing that any single-shot code is self-correcting. We then show that any $[[n,k]]$ CSS quantum low-density parity-check (QLDPC) code can be transformed into an $[[nm,k]]$ CSS QLDPC code with single-shot (SS) quantum error correction and an efficient local decoder which runs in $O(\log m)$ parallel time. Below a constant threshold $p_{\rm RG}>0$ for joint local stochastic physical and measurement noise, the logical failure probability over $T$ correction rounds is bounded by \begin{equation*}
  O(T n)\exp[-Ω(m^α)] \end{equation*} for a constant $α>0$; we say that the \textit{threshold} is $p_{\rm RG}$ and \textit{error suppression} is $Ω(m^α)$. Moreover, if the input code is equipped with a single-shot decoder with threshold $p_c>0$ and error suppression $Ω(n^β)$, then the output code \textit{enhances} the error suppression to $Ω(m^α n^β)$, while maintaining the decoder-independent threshold $p_{\rm RG}$, and thus \textit{enhanced} if $p_c < p_{\rm RG}$.

</details>


### [149] [A provable quantum advantage for approximate optimization via decoded quantum interferometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02145)
*Maximilian J. Kramer,Elies Gil-Fuster,Benjamin D. M. Jones,Jens Eisert,Franz J. Schreiber*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work proves a strict oracle separation showing Decoded Quantum Interferometry (DQI) provably outperforms all polynomial-time classical algorithms on a task called folded optimal polynomial intersection (folded OPI), with a modified DQI achieving an even stronger quantum advantage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: DQI is a novel quantum paradigm for approximate optimization with strong performance guarantees, exploiting duality between optimization and coding theory. A central open question is whether DQI can provably outperform all polynomial-time classical algorithms, which this paper addresses.

Method: The authors consider folded OPI, where acceptance sets are randomly chosen and accessed via membership oracles. They build on Jordan et al.'s DQI framework, extend Yamakawa and Zhandry's classical lower-bound method from exact search to approximation, and draw on developments by Sun and Wootters, Horinaga and Yamakawa, and Jo to design a modified DQI algorithm.

Result: A strict gap is established between the approximation ratio of any polynomial-time classical algorithm and that of DQI. The modified DQI algorithm yields an even larger gap. For code rate 0.3, DQI and the modified algorithm achieve expected scores of approximately 0.85 and 0.95, while exceeding the classical threshold of 0.65 with constant probability requires super-polynomially many classical membership queries.

Conclusion: DQI provably outperforms all polynomial-time classical algorithms in an oracle setting, and a modified version strengthens this quantum separation, demonstrating a genuine provable quantum advantage for approximate optimization.

Abstract: Decoded quantum interferometry (DQI) is a novel paradigm for tackling approximate optimization problems on quantum computers. This framework comes with strong performance guarantees and exploits a well-established duality between optimization and coding theory. A central question, however, is whether DQI can actually provably outperform all polynomial-time classical algorithms. In this work, we establish such an advantage in an oracle setting: we consider an optimization task called folded optimal polynomial intersection (folded OPI), where the acceptance sets are chosen randomly and accessed through membership oracles. We establish a strict gap between the approximation ratio achievable by any polynomial-time classical algorithm and the approximation ratio achieved by the DQI algorithm. Our proof builds on Jordan et al.'s DQI framework for approximate optimization and extends the classical lower-bound method underlying Yamakawa and Zhandry's exact-search oracle separation to approximation. Building on recent developments by Sun and Wootters, Horinaga and Yamakawa, and Jo, we further show that a modified version of the DQI algorithm achieves a strictly larger gap on the folded OPI problem, yielding an even stronger quantum separation. As a concrete example, for code rate $0.3$, DQI and the modified algorithm achieve expected scores of approximately $0.85$ and $0.95$, respectively. In contrast, exceeding the classical threshold of $0.65$ by any fixed amount with constant probability on sampled instances requires super-polynomially many classical membership queries.

</details>


### [150] [Polynomial-time additive-error estimation of output probabilities for shallow quantum circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02146)
*Matthew Coudron,Michael J. Gullans,Jon Nelson,Joel Rajakumar,Shi Jie Samuel Tan*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A deterministic classical algorithm estimates the output probability $|\langle x|U|0^n\rangle|^2$ of a constant-depth quantum circuit to additive error $\varepsilon$ in $\mathrm{poly}(n,1/\varepsilon)$ time, improving prior quasi-polynomial and sub-exponential bounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior classical simulation/estimation methods for constant-depth quantum circuits required super-polynomial time ($n^{O(\log n)}$ generally, $n^{O(\log\log n)}$ for geometrically local, $n^{O(1)}$ only for 2D local). This work aims to remove these limitations and achieve genuinely polynomial-time estimation for arbitrary connectivity.

Method: A deterministic classical algorithm based on a novel simulation/estimation technique for constant-depth circuits with bounded fan-in gates and arbitrary connectivity (no locality assumption).

Result: Estimates the single output probability $|\langle x|U|0^n\rangle|^2$ to additive error $\varepsilon$ in $\mathrm{poly}(n,1/\varepsilon)$ time, improving over $n^{O(\log n)}$, $n^{O(\log\log n)}$ (geometrically local), and $n^{O(1)}$ (2D local) previous bounds.

Conclusion: Constant-depth quantum circuits with bounded fan-in and arbitrary connectivity admit efficient classical estimation of individual output probabilities, shrinking the gap between classical simulability and quantum advantage for shallow circuits.

Abstract: We give a deterministic classical algorithm that estimates $|\langle x|U|0^n\rangle|^2$ to additive error $\varepsilon$ in $\mathrm{poly}(n, 1/\varepsilon)$ time, where $U$ is a constant-depth quantum circuit comprised of gates with bounded fan-in and arbitrary connectivity, and $x$ is an arbitrary $n$-bit output string. This improves over prior state-of-the-art algorithms that takes $n^{O(log(n))}$ time for the same task, $n^{O(log(log(n))}$ when $U$ is geometrically local, and $n^{O(1)}$ for 2D geometrically-local circuits.

</details>


### [151] [The Robustness of QAC0](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02154)
*Daniel Grier,Jackson Morris,Kewen Wu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper studies the robustness of QAC^0, showing that its non-zero error tolerance can be eliminated via exact amplitude amplification in the many-copies setting, and that QAC^0 is robust to gate-set restrictions—every QAC^0 circuit can be efficiently approximated using only generalized Toffoli, S, and Hadamard gates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors question two foundational assumptions about QAC^0: whether the non-zero error typically permitted in QAC^0 circuits computing Boolean functions is actually necessary, and to what extent QAC^0's computational power depends on the availability of arbitrary single-qubit gates at any circuit position.

Method: For the error-elimination question, they remove the error inherent in the parallel W-test of Grier–Morris–Wu via a novel application of exact amplitude amplification in the many-copies context. For the gate-set question, they show that any QAC^0 circuit can be approximately implemented by a QAC^0 circuit composed solely of generalized Toffoli, S, and Hadamard gates, constructible efficiently from a classical description of the original circuit.

Result: Exact QAC^0 can exactly simulate TC^0 with polynomially many copies of the classical input. For every fixed prime p, exact QAC^0, EQAC^0, can compute total Boolean functions outside of AC^0[p]. Additionally, every QAC^0 circuit admits an efficient approximate implementation using only generalized Toffoli, S, and Hadamard gates.

Conclusion: The non-zero error in QAC^0 is not necessary, as it can be eliminated entirely, strengthening the computational power of exact QAC^0. Furthermore, QAC^0 is robust to severe restrictions on its single-qubit gate set, since generalized Toffoli, S, and Hadamard gates suffice for efficient approximate simulation of any QAC^0 circuit.

Abstract: In this work we study the robustness of $\mathsf{QAC}^0$ with respect to error tolerance and modifications to its gate-set. First, we investigate whether the non-zero error typically allowed for $\mathsf{QAC}^0$ circuits computing Boolean functions is truly necessary. We show that the error inherent in the parallel $W$-test of \cite{grier_morris_wu} can be eliminated entirely via a novel application of exact amplitude amplification in the many-copies context. Consequently, we find that $\mathsf{QAC}^0$ can \textit{exactly} simulate $\mathsf{TC}^0$ with polynomially many copies of the classical input and that for every fixed prime $p$ exact $\mathsf{QAC}^0$, $\mathsf{EQAC}^0$, can compute total Boolean functions outside of $\mathsf{AC}^0[p]$.
  Second, we ask to what extent the computational power of $\mathsf{QAC}^0$ follows from the fact that arbitrary single-qubit gates may be used at any point in the circuit. We find that $\mathsf{QAC}^0$ is in fact robust to restrictions on which single-qubit gates are permitted: every $\mathsf{QAC}^0$ circuit can be approximately implemented by a $\mathsf{QAC}^0$ circuit consisting of just generalized Toffoli, $S$, and Hadamard gates. Moreover, this approximating circuit can be constructed efficiently from a classical description of the original circuit.

</details>


### [152] [Learning Many-Body Hamiltonians Using a Local Probe](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02157)
*Suying Liu,Zitai Xu,Alexey V. Gorshkov,Xiaodi Wu,Yu-Xin Wang,Zhi-Yuan Wei*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A single measurable qubit, aided by fast control and a connected reference backbone, suffices to learn all O(N) parameters of a bounded-degree two-body Hamiltonian on N qubits at the Heisenberg limit, using robust QSP-synthesized SWAP gates and a parallel architecture with total query time Õ(N).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Hamiltonian learning protocols typically require direct measurement access to the entire system, which is impractical for large many-body systems where only a local measurement interface is available.

Method: The protocol uses fast single-qubit control plus a connected reference backbone so one measurable qubit accesses the whole system. Robust SWAP gates synthesized via quantum signal processing coherently transfer states evolving under distant Hamiltonian parameters to the measurable qubit, requiring no prior calibration of intermediate-link parameters. A parallel learning architecture is employed for an N-qubit chain.

Result: All O(N) independent parameters of a bounded-degree two-body Hamiltonian are learned at the Heisenberg limit. The parallel architecture achieves total query time Õ(N) for an N-qubit chain while retaining Heisenberg-limited precision scaling, matching fundamental precision and information-propagation lower bounds up to logarithmic factors. The framework extends to arbitrary bounded-degree interaction graphs.

Conclusion: The results establish a scalable route to learning an extensive number of many-body Hamiltonian parameters through only a local measurement interface.

Abstract: Hamiltonian learning provides a systematic framework for reconstructing unknown quantum dynamics. However, existing protocols typically assume direct measurement access to the entire system. With fast single-qubit control and a connected reference backbone, we show that a single measurable qubit suffices to learn all $O(N)$ independent parameters of a bounded-degree two-body Hamiltonian on $N$ qubits at the Heisenberg limit. Crucially, our protocol uses robust SWAP gates synthesized by quantum signal processing, enabling coherent transfer of states evolving under distant Hamiltonian parameters to the measurable qubit. This transfer requires no prior calibration of the Hamiltonian parameters of the intermediate links. A parallel learning architecture achieves total query time $\widetilde{O}(N)$ for an $N$-qubit chain, while retaining Heisenberg-limited precision scaling. On the chain, these scalings match the fundamental precision and information-propagation lower bounds up to logarithmic factors. The framework further extends to arbitrary bounded-degree interaction graphs. Our results establish a scalable route to learning an extensive number of many-body Hamiltonian parameters through only a local measurement interface.

</details>


### [153] [Polynomial-time classical and quantum simulation of quantum impurity models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02167)
*Jiaqing Jiang,Nathan Ju,Ojas Parekh,Chaithanya Rayudu,Andrew Zhao*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work maps the classical/quantum complexity boundary for quantum impurity models: static equilibrium properties (ground-state energy, partition function) are classically tractable in polynomial time, while dynamical properties (nonequilibrium Green's functions) are classically hard yet quantumly easy, even at finite temperature.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum impurity models are paradigmatic for interacting quantum matter and serve as computational primitives in electronic-structure methods (e.g., embedding/dynamical mean-field approaches). However, the precise boundary between classically and quantumly tractable simulation for this problem class was unclear, leaving open where quantum advantage can and cannot be expected.

Method: Computational complexity analysis of simulating impurity models (small interacting fermion subsystem coupled to a large noninteracting bath). The authors devise classical algorithms for static properties and establish hardness results (via complexity-theoretic reductions, e.g., encoding the full power of quantum computation) for dynamical properties.

Result: Static properties are classically efficient: (1) ground-state energy to additive precision δ in poly(n, δ⁻¹) time (improving prior quasipolynomial bounds to polynomial), and (2) partition function at inverse temperature β to relative precision δ in poly(n, β, δ⁻¹) time (first rigorous polynomial-time guarantee for thermal equilibrium). Dynamical properties are classically hard but quantumly easy: computing nonequilibrium Green's functions captures the full power of quantum computation, even at finite temperature.

Conclusion: Superpolynomial quantum speedups for static impurity properties are ruled out, whereas simulating impurity physics out of equilibrium offers a concrete avenue for quantum advantage.

Abstract: Quantum impurity models are paradigmatic models of interacting quantum matter, as well as key computational primitives for modern electronic-structure methods. They describe a small subsystem of interacting fermions coupled to a large, noninteracting bath. We perform a comprehensive study of the computational complexity of simulating impurity models, delineating the boundary between classical and quantum tractability for this class of problems. Our main finding is that static properties of quantum impurity models can be calculated efficiently on a classical computer. Specifically, we give classical algorithms that (1) estimate the ground-state energy to additive precision $δ$ in time $\mathrm{poly}(n,δ^{-1})$, and (2) estimate the partition function at inverse temperature $β$ to relative precision $δ$ in time $\mathrm{poly}(n,β,δ^{-1})$, where $n$ is the system size. These results improve the previous best-known complexity for ground-state energy estimation from quasipolynomial to polynomial time, while establishing for the first time rigorous polynomial-time guarantees for simulating impurity models in thermal equilibrium. On the other hand, we find that simulating dynamical properties of impurity models is hard for classical computers but easy on a quantum computer. As a canonical example, we show that computing their nonequilibrium Green's functions captures the full power of quantum computation, even at finite temperature. Taken together, our results rule out superpolynomial quantum speedups for computing static properties, but provide an avenue for quantum advantage in simulating impurity physics out of equilibrium.

</details>


### [154] [Robust exponential lower bounds for fermionic and bosonic Gaussian ranks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02172)
*Fuchuan Wei,Kong-Wing Wu,Zhengwei Liu,Zi-Wen Liu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper establishes robust exponential lower bounds on Gaussian rank for bosonic and fermionic systems, proving that non-Gaussianity universally entails exponential Gaussian decomposition complexity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical simulation via coherent decomposition into tractable free states has complexity governed by the decomposition rank, but strong lower bounds on this rank are notoriously difficult—exemplified by the open qubit stabilizer rank problem. This work extends the inquiry to the Gaussian setting in bosonic and fermionic systems to determine whether non-Gaussianity imposes fundamental simulation barriers.

Method: The authors study the Gaussian rank problem in bosonic and fermionic systems. For fermions, their proof reduces the general case to four modes combined with Majorana spectral bounds. For bosons, they employ Gaussian postselection together with an entropy-based rank bound.

Result: They prove that for every pure non-Gaussian state on finitely many modes (with definite parity in the fermionic case), the approximate border Gaussian rank of its tensor powers grows at least exponentially at any fixed norm error below one. Explicit exponential lower bounds are derived for the four-mode fermionic GHZ state and the bosonic single-photon state; the bosonic results require no energy assumption on the target state.

Conclusion: Non-Gaussianity universally entails exponential Gaussian decomposition complexity, establishing fundamental limitations on decomposition-based classical simulation of bosonic and fermionic systems.

Abstract: The power and limitations of classical simulation are central to understanding quantum computational advantages. A leading simulation paradigm is based on coherent decomposition into classically tractable free states where the decomposition rank determines the simulation complexity. Proving strong lower bounds on this number is a notoriously difficult and mathematically rich problem, as exemplified by the qubit stabilizer rank problem. Here, we study the Gaussian version of this problem in both bosonic and fermionic systems and establish robust exponential lower bounds on Gaussian rank. In particular, we prove that for every pure non-Gaussian state on finitely many modes, with definite parity in the fermionic case, the approximate border Gaussian rank of its tensor powers grows at least exponentially at any fixed norm error below one. Our proofs combine reduction to four modes with Majorana spectral bounds for fermions, and Gaussian postselection with an entropy-based rank bound for bosons. As concrete examples, we derive explicit exponential lower bounds for the four-mode fermionic GHZ state and the bosonic single-photon state. The bosonic results require no assumption on the energy of the target state. Our results show that non-Gaussianity universally entails exponential Gaussian decomposition complexity, setting fundamental limitations on decomposition-based classical simulation of bosonic and fermionic systems.

</details>


### [155] [From Permutation Symmetry to Communication Bounds and Additivity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02176)
*Zahra Baghali Khanian,Debbie Leung,Graeme Smith*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Permutation invariance eliminates the asymptotic advantage of correlations across channel uses for finite-dimensional memoryless channels: the optimized coherent information per use converges to the single-use maximum and is attained at finite block length. A strong converse for pure-source entanglement generation follows, and a common variational structure unifies completely bounded norms, sandwiched Renyi channel quantities, and output-entropy bounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Correlations across channel uses can enhance quantum communication rates, so optimizing over arbitrarily large blocks is a central difficulty in computing quantum capacity. The paper aims to control this superadditive advantage and to unify several seemingly distinct operational quantities under a symmetry-based framework.

Method: The authors exploit full permutation invariance together with De Finetti reduction and permutation covariance, embedding completely bounded norms, sandwiched Renyi channel quantities, and output-entropy quantities into a shared variational framework.

Result: The optimized coherent information per use converges to the single-use maximum and attains its supremum at finite block length, so asymptotic optimization reduces to a single channel use, though finite-block superadditivity remains possible. A strong converse holds for pure-source entanglement generation under permutation-invariant inputs. Multiplicativity and additivity of the studied norms and channel quantities are recovered via alternative proofs. For permutation-invariant inputs, correlations cannot reduce output entropy per use below the single-use minimum for Renyi orders greater than one and for von Neumann entropy.

Conclusion: Permutation symmetry sharply limits the benefit of cross-use correlations for finite-dimensional memoryless channels, reducing asymptotic optimization to a single use while leaving finite-block superadditivity viable. The same De Finetti-based variational framework systematically yields multiplicativity, additivity, strong-converse, and output-entropy results.

Abstract: Correlations across channel uses can improve quantum communication rates, making optimization over arbitrarily large blocks a central difficulty in determining quantum capacity. We show how full permutation invariance limits this advantage for every finite-dimensional memoryless channel. The optimized coherent information per use converges to the single-use maximum and consequently attains its supremum at a finite block length. Thus finite-block superadditivity remains possible, while the asymptotic optimization reduces to a single channel use. We also establish a strong converse for pure-source entanglement generation with permutation-invariant inputs: at any fixed rate above the single-use coherent-information maximum, the fidelity tends to zero. Beyond these communication bounds, we identify a common structure underlying completely bounded norms and the sandwiched Rényi channel quantities governing discrimination against a replacer and entanglement-assisted communication. De Finetti reduction and permutation covariance yield systematic alternative proofs of their known multiplicativity and additivity results within a shared variational framework. We further show that, for permutation-invariant inputs, correlations cannot sustain an asymptotic reduction in output entropy per use below the single-use minimum. This entropy limit holds for Rényi orders greater than one and for the von Neumann entropy, extending the role of symmetry from communication bounds to the control of output entropy.

</details>


### [156] [Learning SYK Hamiltonians](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02178)
*Anurag Anshu,Srinivasan Arunachalam,Sitan Chen,Yeongwoo Hwang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper studies learning the dense SYK Hamiltonian from Gibbs state copies, overcoming the failure of locality/degree-based algorithms via the model's random mean-field structure, achieving polynomial-sample and quasipolynomial-time learning guarantees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Hamiltonian learning algorithms rely on geometric locality or bounded interaction degree, which do not apply to the SYK model since each quartic interaction overlaps with Θ(n^3) others. The dense, all-to-all coupling structure is a fundamental obstruction that motivates finding a new approach exploiting the model's randomness.

Method: Exploit the random mean-field structure of the SYK model rather than locality or bounded degree. Prove a high-probability (over SYK couplings) guarantee for full Hamiltonian recovery at any constant temperature with polynomial sample complexity. Additionally construct a distinct quasipolynomial-time learning algorithm for sufficiently small constant inverse temperature, qualitatively different from the sample-efficient one.

Result: At any constant temperature, the entire SYK Hamiltonian can be learned to inverse-polynomial accuracy with high probability over the couplings, using polynomially many samples. For sufficiently small constant inverse temperature, a quasipolynomial-time algorithm is obtained that is qualitatively distinct from the sample-efficient algorithm.

Conclusion: Dense, high-overlap Hamiltonians like SYK, intractable for locality- or degree-based methods, are nonetheless learnable by leveraging their random mean-field structure. This yields polynomial-sample learnability at constant temperature and a separate quasipolynomial-time algorithm at low inverse temperature.

Abstract: We study the problem of learning the dense Sachdev--Ye--Kitaev (SYK) Hamiltonian from copies of its Gibbs state. Existing algorithms for Hamiltonian learning typically rely on geometric locality or bounded interaction degree and therefore do not apply to SYK, where each quartic interaction overlaps with $Θ(n^3)$ others. We show that this obstruction can be overcome by exploiting the random mean-field structure of the model. At any constant temperature, we prove that with high probability over the SYK couplings, the entire Hamiltonian can be learned to inverse-polynomial accuracy using polynomially many samples. Furthermore, when the inverse temperature is restricted to be a sufficiently small constant, we construct a quasipolynomial-time learning algorithm which is qualitatively different from the sample-efficient algorithm.

</details>


### [157] [Single-Particle Spectral Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02183)
*Adrian Chapman,Charles Derby,Steven T. Flammia,Yeongwoo Hwang,Joel Klassen,Calum McCartney*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper studies learning the weights of a Hamiltonian whose non-interacting single-particle structure is hidden by an unknown global unitary. It proves the problem is DQC1-hard, then proposes SPICES, a Hadamard-test-based algorithm that recovers the spectral distribution in Wasserstein distance via classical postprocessing exploiting the link between quasiparticle energies and Fourier frequencies of partition-function zeros.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hamiltonians with hidden non-interacting structure are well-motivated in materials modeling, where quasiparticle spectra underpin classical methods such as mean-field theory, Green's functions, and density functional theory. The goal is to recover the single-particle energies E_i without reconstructing the obfuscating unitary U, despite the problem's apparent simplification from the non-interacting promise.

Method: The authors formalize learning weights E_i of H = U†(Σ_{i∈I} E_i n_i + H_O)U, with non-interacting modes I and a spectator H_O on disjoint modes O, hidden by an unknown global unitary U. They establish DQC1-hardness, then introduce SPICES (single-particle inference via contour estimators), combining a Hadamard test with a classical postprocessing procedure that maps single-particle quasiparticle energies to Fourier frequencies governing zeros of the partition function at complex inverse temperature.

Result: The learning problem is shown to be DQC1-hard, indicating that the non-interacting promise does not trivialize it. Under certain natural assumptions, SPICES efficiently recovers the spectral distribution in Wasserstein distance.

Conclusion: Since quasiparticle spectra appear ubiquitously in downstream materials calculations and SPICES is simple and practical to execute, the method is expected to be a promising route to quantum applications in materials simulation.

Abstract: We study Hamiltonian learning in a novel setting, where we are promised a physical structure that is obfuscated by a global unitary. In particular, we consider the task of learning the weights $E_i$ of a Hamiltonian $H=U^{\dagger}(\sum_{i \in I} E_i n_i + H_O)U$ comprised of a set of non-interacting modes $I$ and an arbitrary ``spectator'' $H_O$ acting on a disjoint set of modes $O$. The non-interacting structure is hidden by an unknown global unitary $U$, and our goal is to recover the $E_i$ without reconstructing $U$. Such Hamiltonians are well-motivated in the setting of materials modeling, where they find application for classical methods such as mean-field theory, Green's functions, and density functional theory. We show that this problem is DQC1-hard, despite the apparent simplification given by the non-interacting structure. We then introduce SPICES (single-particle inference via contour estimators), which combines a Hadamard test with a novel classical postprocessing procedure to efficiently recover the spectral distribution in Wasserstein distance under certain natural assumptions. The method exploits the correspondence between single-particle energies of the individual quasiparticles and the Fourier frequencies governing zeros of the partition function at complex inverse temperature. As quasiparticle spectra feature ubiquitously in downstream materials calculations, and with SPICES being simple and practical to execute, we expect that SPICES is a promising route to quantum applications in materials simulation.

</details>

### [158] [Hyperbolic lattices with mass disorder: Phases and phase transitions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02192)
*Sheersh Sen,Christopher A. Leong,Bitan Roy*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: The paper studies mass-disorder effects on electronic states in three hyperbolic lattices ({10,3}, {8,3}, {8,4}) within nearest-neighbor tight-binding models, finding a Dirac liquid, Fermi liquid, and flat band respectively, and mapping their disorder-driven phase transitions via kernel polynomial method calculations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hyperbolic lattices host unusual flat and dispersive bands with distinct zero-energy DOS behaviors (vanishing, finite, diverging), but the impact of mass disorder on these emergent electronic states and their quantum phase transitions remains unexplored.

Method: Nearest-neighbor tight-binding models on plaquette-centered {10,3}, {8,3}, and {8,4} hyperbolic lattices on a Poincaré disk with open boundary conditions; average and typical density of states computed via extensive kernel polynomial method (KPM) numerical calculations, exploiting the emergent bipartite nature to introduce mass disorder.

Result: The three lattices exhibit vanishing, finite, and diverging zero-energy DOS, yielding a Dirac liquid, Fermi liquid, and flat band respectively. The hyperbolic Dirac liquid stays stable under weak mass disorder, undergoes a semimetal-to-metal quantum phase transition at moderate disorder, then an Anderson metal-to-insulator transition at stronger disorder. The other two systems show only the metal-to-insulator transition. Critical exponents near all transitions are close to those mediated by on-site potential disorder.

Conclusion: Mass disorder drives distinct disorder-tuned quantum phase transitions in hyperbolic lattices, with the Dirac liquid displaying an intermediate metallic phase absent in the Fermi liquid and flat-band cases; universality classes resemble those of on-site potential disorder.

Abstract: Nearest-neighbor (NN) tight-binding models (TBMs) on plaquette-centered $\{ 10,3\}$ (Schläfli symbol), $\{ 8,3\}$, and $\{ 8,4\}$ hyperbolic lattices on a Poincaré disk with open boundary conditions display a vanishing, a finite, and a diverging density of states (DOS) near zero energy (band center), respectively, yielding a Dirac liquid, a Fermi liquid, and a flat band. Emergent bipartite nature of these lattices within the framework of NN-TBMs, allows us to scrutinize the impact of mass disorder on the electronic states and DOS therein. From extensive numerical calculations of the average and typical DOS using the kernel polynomial method, we show that hyperbolic Dirac liquid remains stable against weak mass disorder, undergoing a semimetal-to-metal quantum phase transition at moderate disorder, followed by an Anderson metal-to-insulator transition at even stronger disorder. The remaining two systems display only the latter transition. Critical exponents near all these transitions are found to be close to the ones mediated by on-site potential disorder.

</details>
